# OF GEBER
## THE MOST PENE-
### TRATING PHILOSOPHER, THE SUMMARY OF PER-

->*fection of the mastery <term>Magistery</term> <note>original: "magisterij"; in alchemy, the "magistery" refers to the "Great Work" or the master-process of transmuting base metals into gold or achieving spiritual perfection.</note> in its own nature, recently published from*<-
->*a copy in the Vatican Library, most thoroughly corrected*<-
->*from every side, including certain Chapters, Ves-*<-
->*sels, and Furnaces <note>original: "Fornacum"; the technical description of these furnaces was essential for the practical "laboratory" side of alchemy.</note>, which were omitted in a volume previously*<-
->*most faultily printed. Also the Books of the Investi-*<-
->*gation of the mastery, and the Testament of the same*<-
->*Geber <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a Persian polymath often considered the father of early chemistry.</note>, as well as the Golden Booklet of Three Words,*<-
->*and of Avicenna <note>original: "Auicennæ"; the Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian physician whose work on minerals and geology was frequently appended to alchemical corpora.</note>, the supreme physician*<-
->*and most acute phi-*<-
->*losopher, the*<-
->*addition on*<-
->*Minerals,*<-
->*most highly*<-
->*purified.*<-

<term>Summa</term> <note>A comprehensive treatise or "summary" of a specific field of knowledge, popular in medieval and Renaissance academic writing.</note>

<term>Castigatissima</term> <note>Translated here as "most highly purified"; while it literally means "most corrected" or "chastened" (referring to the text), in an alchemical context it carries a double meaning of being chemically purified or refined.</note>

<summary>This page is the formal title page for a 16th-century edition of alchemical works attributed to Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan). It highlights that this edition is superior to previous versions because it was corrected using a manuscript from the Vatican Library and includes previously missing sections on alchemical tools and furnaces.</summary>
<keywords>Geber, Jabir ibn Hayyan, alchemy, Summa perfectionis magisterii, Avicenna, Ibn Sina, Vatican Library, alchemical furnaces, transmutation, Mineralia</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page and provides the first visual representation of the "Vessels and Furnaces" mentioned in the previous text. It serves as a practical frontispiece for the technical instructions that follow in Geber's treatise.</meta>

# ILLUSTRATION OF THE DISTILLATORY APPARATUS

->[Full-page woodcut depicting a Renaissance chemical laboratory setup]<-

This detailed illustration provides a visual guide to the primary tools of the alchemist’s workshop. At the center is a large, circular **brick furnace**, designed to provide the steady, controlled heat necessary for the "Mastery."

The process shown here is <term>Distillation</term> <note>The fundamental chemical process of heating a liquid to create vapor, then cooling that vapor to collect it in a purified liquid form.</note>, utilizing the following components:

*   **The Furnace**: Built with arched openings at the base for air circulation and fuel management. Two rectangular vents at the top allow smoke and excess heat to escape.
*   The <term>Alembic</term> <note>original: "alambicus"; the rounded or conical cap placed over the heating vessel to catch rising vapors.</note>: The vessel resting directly on the heat source.
*   **The Delivery Tube**: A long, curved neck extending from the alembic, designed to allow vapors to cool as they travel away from the furnace.
*   The <term>Cucurbit</term> <note>original: "cucurbita"; a receiving vessel named for its gourd-like shape. In this illustration, it sits to the left, collecting the "spirit" or essence of the distilled substance.</note>: The vessel at the end of the tube where the final product is gathered.

*This apparatus was the "engine" of the alchemical laboratory, used to purify substances and search for the hidden essences of metals and minerals.*

<summary>A full-page woodcut illustration of a 16th-century distillation apparatus, including a brick furnace, alembic, and receiving vessel. It illustrates the practical "furnaces and vessels" described in Geber's alchemical text.</summary>

<keywords>distillation, furnace, alembic, cucurbit, alchemy, laboratory equipment, Renaissance science, Geber</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page follows the technical illustration of the furnace and distillation apparatus. Having shown the physical tools of the trade, the text now shifts to a cautionary preface addressed to students of alchemy, likely written by the librarians or keepers of the Vatican collection where this manuscript was held.</meta>

# THE CUSTODIANS OF THE LIBRARY
## *To the Students of Vatican Alchemy*
### *to be truly wise.*

**S**INCE *many followers of <term>Alchemy</term> <note>The medieval and early modern precursor to chemistry, focused on the transformation of matter and the creation of the "Philosophers' Stone."</note> of every class are found in these times, who scrutinize the hidden causes of natural philosophy so that they might draw out the <term>Philosophers' Stone</term> <note>original: "lapis philosophorum"; a legendary substance supposed to be able to change base metals into gold and grant eternal life.</note> (as it is commonly called) from paper-based obscurities, and wring it from the hands of <insert>Arnald</insert> Raynald <note>Arnald of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note>, Raymond <note>Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher and theologian to whom many alchemical texts were falsely attributed.</note>, **Geber**, and some Monks—not to mention even the highest Theologians—the cleverer among them by interpreting perversely, those more inclined by affirming ambiguities, the stupider by believing in chimeras, the swifter by experimenting, and the lavish by pouring out and losing their money; they are ended with this result: that they waste their time, promise vain things, blather bombastic words, and are held as a joke and object of derision by all, so that finally they squander their money in place of the Stone. Hence an art so rare—indeed a science so supernatural—is held and believed to be an illusion and mere deception. Nor is it a wonder, when students of this divine invention assiduously read **Geber** and admire him as if he were their own **Aristarchus** <note>Aristarchus of Samothrace was a famous Greek scholar known for his rigorous and often harsh criticism of literature; here, it means the alchemists treat Geber as their ultimate, infallible authority.</note>: they cling to him, keep watch over him, and devote themselves to him, just as **Alexander of Macedon** <note>Alexander the Great.</note> was accustomed to do with the **Education of Cyrus** <note>original: "pedia Cyri"; Xenophon’s biography of Cyrus the Great, which Alexander reportedly kept with him at all times as a manual for leadership.</note>. And since that author [Geber] abounds with infinite errors, and is carried about as one who is blind, crippled, or rather as someone else entirely; and like a **Scyllan satyr** <note>A reference to a monstrous or wild figure; here used to describe Geber's writing as harsh, animalistic, or incomprehensible.</note>, he does not speak but gnashes his teeth; he does not teach but unteaches, and [nurtures?] <unclear>lactitan...</unclear> the mind* <note>The text breaks off here mid-word; "lactitan" likely relates to "lactitante," meaning to suckle or nurture, suggesting the text "feeds" the mind in a deceptive way.</note>

<summary>A preface by the Vatican Library custodians warning alchemy students about the dangers of misinterpreting complex texts and squandering their wealth. It specifically criticizes the obscure and "erroneous" writing style of the famous alchemist Geber.</summary>

<keywords>Vatican Library, Alchemy, Philosophers' Stone, Arnald of Villanova, Raymond Lull, Geber, Alexander the Great, Criticism, Scientific Deception</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface from the previous page, which warned of the failures of misguided alchemists. The text here uses classical metaphors of healing and resurrection to describe the restoration of the "true" alchemical art, which the authors claim to be reviving from the Vatican's archives. The catchword "fusa" at the bottom completes the final word "infusa" (infused).</meta>

# INDEX

*...wrapped in a mist of [superstition] and overturned. We have decided to come to the aid of your vain labors and expenses by healing the wounds of this art, by applying eye-salve* <note>original: "collyrium"; a medicinal lotion for the eyes, used here metaphorically for clearing one's spiritual or intellectual vision.</note> *to bleary eyes, by eliminating the <term>dyscrasia</term>* <note>An ancient medical term referring to an imbalance of the four bodily humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile), believed to cause disease.</note>, *and by restoring more beautiful things to the lopped-off limbs (just as was done for <term>Pelops</term>)* <note>In Greek myth, Pelops was cut into pieces and served as a feast for the gods, but was later restored to life with an ivory shoulder.</note>, *so that at last—like the ram boiled by <term>Medea</term>* <note>The sorceress Medea demonstrated her power to restore youth by cutting up an old ram and boiling it with magic herbs, from which it emerged as a young lamb.</note>—*it may be seen to take shape, grow young, be brought to life, shine, and be returned entirely to itself. This is done by drawing forth <term>Machaon</term>* <note>The son of Asclepius and a legendary healer in the Trojan War; here, he represents the healing power of true knowledge.</note> *from the Vatican library as if from the Trojan Horse.*

*He who, sweating over this cure, watches most attentively, and who rescues the one mourned* <note>original: "conclamatum"; a reference to the Roman custom of calling the name of the deceased three times to ensure they were truly dead.</note> *from the gates of <term>Proserpina</term>* <note>The Queen of the Underworld; this signifies bringing the "dead" art of alchemy back to life.</note> *and restores him to life, light, and brightness, and to you: flatter this charming man, and admire him as he discourses so learnedly and profoundly. His words promise and bestow secret doctrine, his sentences pearls, and his precepts the <term>obrizum</term>* <note>A technical term for pure, refined gold.</note> *of these matters.*

*However, you—most of whom are searchers into the riches of nature—shall judge how much you owe to our diligence. To you, as you walked through <term>Cimmerian darkness</term>* <note>A proverbial expression for absolute darkness, referring to a mythical people said to live in perpetual mist and gloom.</note>, *we have provided the lamp of <term>Cleanthes</term>* <note>A Stoic philosopher who worked as a water-carrier by night so he could study philosophy by day; his "lamp" represents tireless study in the face of hardship.</note>, *and those who were cast down into the cave of <term>Trophonius</term>* <note>An oracle located in a subterranean cave; visitors were said to emerge so terrified that they lost the ability to laugh.</note> *we have led out and brought into the light. If ever the hard-won knowledge of such a rare, hidden, and celestial treasure reaches you, you will be the judge.*

*In the meantime, read often: drink this in and sweat over it, and by your persistence keep the gods entreated, so that this most secret truth—covered by such great lies and obscurities, called by every name in the world, held by few through books, yet always dearly loved; perceived by even fewer through a teacher, yet nonetheless never abandoned; and infused* <note>original: "infusa", completed by the catchword "fusa".</note> *into the very few through revelation...*

<summary>This page concludes the preface by using mythological allegories (Medea, Pelops, Machaon) to describe the "healing" and restoration of alchemical knowledge from the Vatican archives. It positions the editors as guides who lead students out of intellectual darkness into the light of "true" alchemy.</summary>
<keywords>Medea, Pelops, Machaon, Proserpina, Cimmerian darkness, Vatican Library, alchemical restoration, pure gold, secret revelation</keywords>

# INDEX

*...infused, yet not despaired of, may be opened to you. So that you may have and give thanks for the copy from the Vatican library—most corrected in every respect, from which this most adorned offspring* <note>original: "fætura"; a metaphor meaning the "birth" or "offspring" of the printing press, referring to this specific edition.</note> *has come forth—and for our labor undertaken on your behalf, purchase at any price this new and complete <term>Geber</term>* <note>The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan. In the 16th century, the "Pseudo-Geber" corpus was considered the highest authority on alchemical practice.</note>, *a most rich source of <term>gold mines</term>* <note>original: "aurifodinarum"; used here metaphorically to describe the text as a source of immense alchemical wealth and secrets.</note>. *And expect shortly better, rarer, and more deeply-disputing <term>authors</term>* <note>A promise to the reader that more rare alchemical manuscripts from the same collection will be published soon.</note>, *and*
->*Farewell.*<-

# I N D E X

## OF THE FIRST BOOK OF THE SUMMA
### Of Geber, the most clear-sighted philosopher,
### Preface on the perfection of the mastery in its own nature.

Chapter 1. Concerning the obstacles by which the practitioner of this work is hindered, in general discourse.
Chapter 2. Concerning obstacles on the part of the body. <note>This likely refers to the physical health and stamina required for long alchemical operations.</note>
Chapter 3. Concerning obstacles on the part of the soul. <note>Geber argues that an alchemist must possess specific mental and moral qualities, such as patience and a lack of greed, to succeed.</note>
Chapter 4. Concerning obstacles arising from accidental chance.
Chapter 5. Concerning those things which the practitioner <term>artificem</term> <note>Literally "the artist"; in alchemical texts, this refers to the skilled operator who assists nature in the laboratory.</note> must consider.
Chapter 6. Concerning the persuasions of the Sophists. <note>"Sophists" here refers to those who use clever but false logic to argue that alchemy is impossible.</note>
Chapter 7. Concerning the natural principles upon which nature founds its action.
Chapter 8. Concerning the solutions to the persuasions of the Sophists.
Chapter 9. Concerning the reasons of those who deny the art based on given premises.
Chapter 10. Concerning those who suppose the art exists in <term>spirits</term> <note>In alchemy, "spirits" are volatile substances that can be vaporized by heat, specifically mercury, sulfur, arsenic, and sal ammoniac.</note>.
Chapter 11. Concerning those who suppose the art exists in bodies, stones, salts, alums, <term>boraxes</term> <note>original: "baurachijs"; refers to various alkaline salts used as fluxes to help metals melt.</note>, or growing things <note>Likely referring to the "vegetable" or organic kingdom.</note>.
Chapter 12. Concerning the natural principles of Mercury, Sulfur, and Arsenic.
Chapter 13. Concerning Sulfur.
Chapter 14. Concerning Arsenic.
Chapter 15. Concerning Mercury.
Chapter 16. Concerning the metallic bodies which are the effects of the principle...

<summary>This page provides a table of contents for the first book of Geber's "Summa Perfectionis," outlining the physical and mental requirements for an alchemist and the basic chemical principles of the work.</summary>
<keywords>Index, Geber, alchemy, mastery, sulfur, arsenic, mercury, alums, borax, sophistry, natural principles</keywords>

| **Of Ceration.** <note><term>Ceration</term> comes from the word for wax (cera); it is the process of softening a hard, dry substance until it has a waxy, meltable consistency.</note> | **Chap. 19.** |

# THE CONTENTS OF THE FOURTH BOOK

->The Preface of the most insightful Philosopher Geber and<-
->King of India concerning Medicines in a universal<-
->discourse. And concerning the five<-
->differences of their<-
->perfection.<-
<note>Geber, the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, was often legendary referred to as a King of India or of the Persians in medieval European texts.</note>

*Of the preparation of the substance of Quicksilver in the*

# I N D E X

*Of the medicines of Mars in the first order. Chapter 5.* <note>In the alchemical tradition, Mars refers to iron. The "orders" of medicines refer to the potency of the elixir: the first order only changed the surface or specific qualities of a metal.</note>
*Of the medicines of the Moon in the first order. Chapter 6.* <note>The Moon refers to silver.</note>
*Of the medicines of the second order. Chapter 7.* <note>Second order medicines were thought to change the substance of a metal more deeply, though still imperfectly.</note>

->Chapter 8.<-
*Of the correction of defects after the administration of*
*medicines of the second order. Chapter 9.*
*Of the medicines of Quicksilver, and their entry*
->Chapter 10.<- <note>Quicksilver refers to mercury. "Entry" refers to the elixir's ability to penetrate and merge with the metal being transformed.</note>
*Of the administration of the third order. Chapter 11.* <note>The third order is the most perfect medicine, capable of total and permanent transmutation into gold or silver.</note>
*Of the administration of the Solar and Lunar medicines.*
->Chapter 12.<- <note>The Sun (Sol) refers to gold, and the Moon (Luna) to silver.</note>
*Of the examinations by which it is known whether the*
*mastery is in perfection. Chapter 13.* <note><term>Magisterium</term> (mastery) refers to the completed alchemical process or the "Great Work."</note>
*Of the examination by cupel. Chapter 14.* <note><term>Cineritium</term> refers to cupellation, a refining process where metals are heated in a "cupel" of bone ash to separate precious metals from base ones.</note>
*Of the examination by cement. Chapter 15.* <note>Cementation was a method of purifying gold by heating it while packed in a mixture of salts or "cements."</note>
*Of the examination by ignition. Chapter 16.* <note>A test of a metal's purity based on how it reacts to intense heat and whether it loses weight or changes color.</note>
*Of the examination by fusion. Chapter 17.* <note>A test focused on the melting point and fluidity of the metal.</note>
*Of the examination by the exposure of bodies to sharp*
*vapors. Chapter 18.* <note>original: "uapores acutos". This involves testing a metal's resistance to corrosion by exposing it to acidic fumes, such as those from vinegar.</note>
*Of the examination by extinction. Chapter 19.* <note>Extinction is the process of quenching a red-hot metal in a liquid to observe its reaction and resulting hardness.</note>
*Of the examination in the mixture of Sulfur. Chapter 20.*
*Of the examination of Calcination and the Reduction of bodies.*
->Chapter 21.<- <note><term>Calcination</term> involves burning a metal to a powder, while <term>Reduction</term> is the process of returning that powder back to its metallic form.</note>
*Of the examination in the easy and difficult mixing of Quicksilver*
->Chapter 22.<-

## *Recapitulation of the whole work in one Summary. Chapter 23:*

## THE BOOK OF INVESTIGATION
->*of the mastery of Geber the most*<-
->*clear-sighted philosopher, and King of India*<- <note>original: "Indiæ Regis". In medieval alchemical tradition, Geber (the Latinized name for Jabir ibn Hayyan) was often legendary titles, including King of India or King of the Persians, to emphasize his authority and antiquity.</note>
->**Preface.**<-

**Of the preparation of common salt. Chapter 1.**
**Of the preparation of salt of glass. Chapter 2.** <note>original: "Salis Vitri". This likely refers to "sandiver" or "glass-gall," a salty byproduct found on the surface of molten glass in furnaces, used as a flux.</note>
**Of the preparation of saltpeter. Chapter 3.** <note>original: "Salis nitri". Potassium nitrate, a key ingredient for making strong acids.</note>
**Of the preparation of rock salt. Chapter 4.** <note>original: "Salis gemmæ". Literally "gem salt," referring to clear, crystalline halite.</note>
**Of the preparation of alkali salt. Chapter 5.** <note>original: "Salis alcali". This usually refers to potassium carbonate obtained from the ashes of burnt plants.</note>
**Of the preparation of sal ammoniac. Chapter 6.** <note>original: "Salis armoniaci". Ammonium chloride, a substance highly valued in alchemy for its ability to sublimate and act on metals.</note>
**Of the preparation of salt of tartar. Chapter 7.** <note>original: "Salis tartari". Potassium carbonate derived from the crusty deposits left in wine barrels.</note>
**Of the preparation of salt of urine. Chapter 8.**
**Of the preparation of ice or rock alum. Chapter 9.** <note>original: "Aluminis glaciæ uel rocæ". Alum was used primarily as a mordant in dyeing and as an astringent.</note>
**Of the preparation of Yemen alum. Chapter 10.** <note>original: "Aluminis iameni". A high-quality grade of alum named after its supposed source in Yemen.</note>
**Of the preparation of plumose alum. Chapter 11.** <note>original: "Aluminis plumosæ". "Feathered" or fibrous alum; often used in historical texts to describe asbestos or similar mineral fibers that did not burn.</note>
**Of the preparation of black ink. Chapter 12.** <note>original: "Atramenti nigri". In this chemical context, it refers to vitriol (iron sulfate) used to make black dyes and inks.</note>
**Of the preparation of copperas. Chapter 13.** <note>original: "Cuperosæ". A general name for metallic sulfates, specifically green vitriol.</note>
**Of the preparation of Roman vitriol. Chapter 14.** <note>original: "Vitrioli Romani". A specific, high-purity copper sulfate known for its blue color.</note>
**Of the preparation of ceruse. Chapter 15.** <note>original: "Cerussæ". White lead, a pigment produced by exposing lead to vinegar vapors.</note>
**Of the preparation of Spanish white. Chapter 16.** <note>original: "Albi hispanici". A white pigment or cosmetic powder, often made from tin or bismuth.</note>
**Of the preparation of verdigris. Chapter 17.** <note>original: "Viridis æris". Literally "green of copper," the green pigment formed by the weathering of copper.</note>
**Of the preparation of saffron. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . Chapter 18.** <note>original: "Croci". In alchemy, "saffron" (crocus) refers to a fine yellow or orange powder produced by calcining a metal, such as Crocus Martis (iron oxide).</note>

->FIRST<-

# THE SUMMARY OF THE FIRST BOOK OF GEBER,
## THE MOST INSIGHTFUL PHILOSOPHER,
### regarding the perfection of the <term>Magistery</term> <note>The "Magistery" refers to the masterful process of alchemical transformation, specifically the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> in its own nature. The Introduction.

*ALL our knowledge, which we have condensed from the sayings of the ancients in various compilations within our volumes, we shall here gather into a single <term>Summa</term> <note>A "Summa" is a comprehensive manual or compendium that summarizes a branch of knowledge.</note>. And what was left incomplete in the books written by us, we have sufficiently compensated for in this tradition of our book, and we have supplied their deficiency with brief speech. And what was hidden by us in one part, we have made manifest in that same part in this our volume, so that the completion of such an excellent and noble part of philosophy might be opened to the wise. Know therefore, most dear son, that in this work, the entire operation of the art is sufficiently contained in general chapters and universal discussion, without any omission. By God, whoever shall have worked according to this book will rejoice to have found the true end of this art. But know, most dear one, that he who is ignorant of the natural principles within himself is already far removed from our art: because he does not have a true root upon which to found his intention. And he who knows his natural principles and all causes, [yet] has not yet attained the true end and profit*...

<margin>B</margin> <note>This is a "signature mark," used by printers to help the bookbinder assemble the pages in the correct order.</note>

<summary>This page serves as the introduction to Geber’s influential work on alchemy, claiming to condense and clarify all previous alchemical knowledge into one comprehensive volume. The author emphasizes that success in the "art" requires a deep understanding of natural principles and "causes."</summary>

<keywords>Geber, alchemy, Summa Perfectionis, Magistery, natural principles, alchemical philosophy, Jabir ibn Hayyan</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction from the previous page. The text begins mid-sentence, completing the thought regarding the practitioner's proximity to the "true end" of alchemical mastery based on their level of knowledge.</meta>

->->BOOK<-<-

...[attained] of this most occult art, he nevertheless has an easier access to the principles of the art, even if ignorance weighs upon his intention regarding the method of this our work; and such a man is but a little removed from the entrance of the art.

But he who shall have known the principles of nature, the causes of **minerals**, and the manner of **generation** <note>In alchemical philosophy, "generation" refers to the natural process by which metals grow and mature within the earth.</note> which consists in the intention of nature: truly, little is withheld from him regarding the completion of the work, without which our science cannot be perfected: since art cannot imitate the nature of the work in all things, but imitates it as best it can.

Therefore, most dear son, we reveal a secret to you: because in this, craftsmen err, for they desire to imitate nature in every single difference of its properties of action. Labor, therefore, to study in our volumes: and revolve them very often in your mind so that you may acquire a true understanding from our manner of speech. In these books you will find that upon which you ought to found your mind, and you will learn how to repel those errors from yourself in which you might [mistakenly] try to imitate nature in the **artifice** <note>Here, "artifice" refers to the human skill and technical craft required to complete the alchemical process.</note> of your work.

Let us therefore first briefly set down all the impediments by which the craftsman is hindered in the work, lest he should fail to reach the true end. We shall speak of those qualities which the craftsman of this art ought to possess within himself: and first we shall argue against the ignorant and the <term>Sophists</term> <note>In alchemy, "Sophists" were practitioners who used deceptive language or false logic to hide their lack of genuine results or to dismiss the art entirely because of their own failures.</note>, who, because of their ignorance and inability to find profit in the <term>magistery</term> <note>The "magistery" is the masterful process of transformation that results in the alchemical goal, such as the creation of the Elixir.</note> of this art, destroy the art and claim it does not exist. We shall therefore set down all their arguments, and afterwards we shall most clearly demolish them: so that it may be made plain enough to the wise that none of their clever arguments contain the truth. Secondly, we shall dispute...

<summary>This page outlines the relationship between natural philosophy and alchemical art, emphasizing that while art imitates nature, it cannot replicate it perfectly. It also introduces a plan to refute the "Sophists" who deny the reality of alchemy due to their own failures.</summary>

<keywords>natural principles, minerals, generation, imitation of nature, Sophists, magistery, refutation of skeptics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory framework of the treatise, moving from the philosophical requirements of the practitioner to the specific plan of the book and the physical and mental obstacles (impediments) that can prevent a craftsman from succeeding in the alchemical art.</meta>

->->FIRST BOOK<-<-

->B ij<-

...[arguing] upon the natural principles, which belong to the intention of nature; and in that same place, upon the manner of their **generation** and **mixing** with one another by the work of nature. Furthermore, we shall subsequently discuss the effects according to the opinion of the ancient philosophers. Fourthly, we shall recount the principles which serve the intention of this our work: in which we are able to imitate nature and the manner of mixing and altering, according to the course of nature with its causes to be brought back to the intention of our work.

## Concerning the impediments by which the
## craftsman of this work is hindered.

### Chapter 1.

**T**HERE ARE therefore two impediments occurring in this work: natural inability, and the lack of necessary expenses, or the preoccupation of labor <note>The author identifies three practical hurdles: lack of innate talent, lack of money for materials, or being too busy with other work to focus on the "Great Work."</note>. We say, however, that natural inability is manifold. Namely, on the part of the craftsman's own <term>organ</term> <note>original: "organi"; in medieval philosophy, this refers to the physical body and the sensory tools (like eyes or hands) through which the soul interacts with the world.</note>, and on the part of the soul itself. On the part of the craftsman's organ, it is manifold: specifically because the organ may be weak or entirely corrupted.

Regarding inability from the soul, it is likewise manifold: either because the soul is perverse within the organs—possessing nothing of rectitude or reason in itself because of those organs—such as the soul of an insane person or a fool. Or because it is "fantastic" <note>original: "fantastica"; this refers to an overactive imagination (phantasia) that is not governed by logic, leading the person to jump to wild, unfounded conclusions.</note>, unduly prone to receiving contrary forms; and suddenly stretching from one thing known to its opposite, and from one universal to its opposite with apparent likeness.

<summary>The author outlines the structure of the upcoming chapters and identifies the primary obstacles to alchemical success, focusing on physical disability, poverty, and mental instability or lack of focus.</summary>
<keywords>natural principles, generation, mixing, impediments, natural inability, organ of the craftsman, soul, imagination, insanity</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the practitioner’s qualifications, moving from the general concept of "impediments" introduced at the end of the previous page to specific physical and mental limitations that prevent success in alchemy.</meta>

->->BOOK<-<-

<meta>catchword: naturalia (natural things)</meta>

## Concerning the impediments on the part of the body.
### Chapter 2.

**W**E have already defined for you in general terms the impediments to this work. But now, we address you in a more specific discourse in this chapter, and more clearly, and we shall recount to you all those impediments most fully and in order. We say, therefore, that if one does not possess his complete <term>organs</term> <note>original: "organa"; in this context, it refers to the physical limbs and senses necessary for lab work.</note>, he will not be able to reach the completion of this work; such as if he were blind, or mutilated in his extremities, because he is not assisted by the limbs by which this art is perfected, which serve as the ministers of nature. If, however, the body of the craftsman should be weak and sick—such as the bodies of laborers or lepers whose limbs fall away, or those struggling in the final stages of life, and the feebleness of an already decrepit age—they do not reach the completion of the art. Therefore, by these natural incapacities of the body, the craftsman is hindered in his intention.

## Concerning the impediments on the part of the soul.
### Chapter 3.

**W**E have set before you one chapter in which we recounted in straightforward language the manifest impediments depending on the body of the craftsman. It remains now to recount the impediments on the part of the soul, which are the greatest impediments against the completion of this work.
    We say, therefore, that he who does not have natural <term>talent</term> <note>original: "ingenium"; refers to innate intellectual capacity or the "spark" of invention.</note> and a soul subtly investigating the principles of na- <note>The text cuts off here, to be continued as "naturalia" (natural things) on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page details the physical and mental obstacles (impediments) to alchemical success, specifically citing physical disabilities, old age, and a lack of innate intellectual curiosity as disqualifying factors.</summary>
<keywords>impediments, body, soul, craftsman, physical disability, natural talent, old age, leprosy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "impediments of the soul" (intellectual and psychological barriers) that prevent a person from succeeding in alchemy. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page's "investigating" (investigantem) natural things.</meta>

->->**BOOK FIRST**<-<-

->**5**<-

<margin>B iij</margin>

...natural things and the foundations of nature, as well as the technical skills that can follow nature in the properties of its action, will not find the true root of this most precious science. Just as there are many who are "stiff-necked" and void of all ingenious insight; who are scarcely able to understand common reasoning, and similarly learn with difficulty how to handle even those works that are common among the people. Among these we also find many who have a soul prone to believing any fantasy whatsoever. <note>The following sentence is a repetition in the original text, a common scribal or printing error known as a "dittography."</note> Among these we also find many who have a soul prone to believing any fantasy whatsoever. 

But what they believe they have discovered to be true is entirely fantastic, deviating from reason, full of error, and remote from natural principles; this is because their brain, being filled with many <term>smoky vapors</term> <note>original: "fumositatibus"; in medieval medicine, vapors from the stomach were thought to rise to the brain, clouding judgment and causing delusions.</note>, cannot receive the true <term>intention</term> <note>original: "intentionem"; here meaning the essential nature or true concept of a thing.</note> of natural things. 

There are also others besides these who have a restless soul, shifting from opinion to opinion and from will to will; like those who at one moment believe a thing and desire it without any foundation of reason, and a little later believe and desire something else entirely. These are so changeable that they can scarcely complete even the smallest part of what they intend, but rather leave the work diminished and unfinished. 

There are similarly others who cannot see any truth in natural things any more than a beast, being as it were deranged, insane, or like children. There are also others who despise the science and do not believe it even exists; these the science likewise despises, and it repels them from the goal of this most precious work. And there are others who are slaves to money, who, desiring this wonderful science...

<summary>The author lists psychological and intellectual barriers to alchemy, including lack of natural talent, a mind clouded by "smoky vapors," a lack of persistence, and a slave-like devotion to money.</summary>
<keywords>impediments of the soul, smoky vapors, fantasy vs reason, intellectual capacity, perseverance, greed</keywords>

they affirm these things to be true, but they fear to incur the <term>expenses</term> <note>original: "dispendia"; refers to the significant financial costs and potential for loss when purchasing materials and equipment for alchemical experiments.</note> themselves. And so, although they affirm the art and investigate it according to reason, they nevertheless do not arrive at the experience of the work because of greed for money. Therefore, our <term>science</term> <note>original: "scientia"; in this context, a systematic body of knowledge or a technical discipline.</note> does not reach these people. For how shall one who is ignorant, or who has neglected to investigate the science, arrive at its completion?

## On the impediments arising from chance circumstances. Chapter 4.

**W**e have reduced the impediments of this art—all those things that delay the goal—into two categories, which arise from fundamental principles according to the nature of the practitioner of this precious business. It is therefore expedient only to recount the impediments arriving from the outside, from accidental occurrences and chance events, by which this glorious work is hindered. For we have seen certain clever and ingenious men, not at all ignorant of the nature of the work, following it where possible in its principles and practices, whose investigation is not based on fantasy in all things regulated by the actions of nature beneath the <term>lunar circle</term> <note>original: "lunarem circulum"; in the medieval geocentric cosmos, the sublunary sphere is the region between the Earth and the Moon where all change, decay, and elemental transformations—including alchemy—take place.</note>. These men, however, weighed down by extreme <term>poverty</term> <note>original: "paupertate"; a common theme in alchemical literature is the high cost of the "Great Work," which often bankrupted practitioners before they could succeed.</note> and a lack of resources, are forced to set aside this most excellent <term>mastery</term> <note>original: "magisterium"; the highest level of alchemical achievement or the successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. There are also many others (besides those just mentioned) who are curious but held back by various cares and anxieties of this world, occupying themselves entirely with every secular business; from these people, our precious science flees. Now, therefore, it is sufficiently clear to you from the preceding chapters what the impediments are that draw one away from this art.

<summary>This page concludes the list of psychological barriers to alchemy—notably greed—and introduces external obstacles such as poverty and the distractions of secular life. It explains that even those who understand the science cannot succeed if they lack the funds or the mental focus required for the work.</summary>

7

## On those things which the practitioner ought to consider.
### Chapter 5.

**W**e conclude therefore from the things already said, that it is necessary for the <term>practitioner</term> <note>original: "artificem"; often translated as "artist" or "artificer," referring to the alchemist as a skilled craftsman.</note> of this work to be learned and advanced in the sciences of <term>natural philosophy</term> <note>original: "philosophia naturalis"; the study of nature and the physical universe, which was the precursor to modern science.</note>: because however much a naturally profound <term>talent</term> <note>original: "ingenium"; natural cleverness or innate intellectual ability.</note> he might possess, and even if he has attained a desire for this craft and a knowledge of natural philosophy, he still shall not acquire its end except through <term>teaching</term> <note>original: "doctrina"; refers to formal instruction, the study of authoritative texts, and the transmission of theory.</note>. This is because that which is not obtained through natural talent is supplemented by the aid of teaching. 

It is also necessary for the practitioner to be assisted by the deepest scrutiny and natural <term>diligence</term> <note>original: "industria"; not "industry" in the modern sense of factories, but the personal hard work, ingenuity, and careful application of the practitioner.</note>. For no matter how much knowledge he has acquired through teaching, unless he is helped by natural diligence, he will not be invited to such precious banquets <note>The "precious banquets" is a metaphorical reference to the successful completion of the Great Work and the rewards of alchemical transformation.</note>. For if he were grounded in teaching alone, he might encounter an error for which he would not know how to apply a remedy; yet, through his own diligence, he could correct that error in an instant. Likewise, he might use the knowledge acquired through teaching to help correct an error in an instant which he could not avoid through natural diligence alone: for the <term>Art</term> <note>original: "ars"; Alchemy was known as the "Sacred Art," combining practical skill with theoretical knowledge.</note> is helped by talent, and talent is helped by the Art likewise. 

It is also necessary for him to be of a constant will in the work, so that he does not presume to try now this, now that; for our Art is not perfected in a multitude of different things. For there is but **one Stone**, **one medicine**, in which the <term>mastery</term> <note>original: "magisterium"; the "Mastery" or "Magistery" refers to the entire process of creating the Philosopher’s Stone and the resulting substance itself.</note> consists. To this we do not add any foreign thing, nor do we take anything away, except that in its preparation we remove what is superfluous.

<summary>This chapter establishes that a successful alchemist requires a balance of innate talent (ingenium), formal study (doctrina), and practical diligence (industria). It emphasizes the alchemical principle of unity, stating that the work involves only "one Stone" and "one medicine" without outside additives.</summary>

<keywords>natural philosophy, alchemist qualities, talent vs teaching, diligence, the One Stone, magisterium, theory and practice</keywords>

<meta>catchword: quae</meta>

It is necessary, therefore, for the practitioner to persist diligently in the work until its completion, so that he does not leave the work unfinished: because he will acquire neither knowledge nor profit from a diminished work, but rather despair and loss. It is also beneficial for him not to be ignorant of the **principles** of this art, and the **primary roots**, which pertain to the very being of the work: because he who is ignorant of the principles will not find the end. And we shall tell you all those principles in a complete discourse, which is also sufficiently open and manifest to the prudent, according to the requirements of our art. 

It is also similarly beneficial for him to be temperate and restrained in **anger**, lest suddenly, due to an impulse of rage, he scatter and destroy what has already been started. Similarly, he should guard his **money**, so that he does not presumptuously distribute it in vain: so that if by chance he does not find the art, he is not left in misery and the despair of poverty. Or perhaps, when he has already approached the end through his <term>investigation</term> <note>original: "indagationem"; a systematic searching or trial-and-error process common in early laboratory practice.</note>, his funds might be consumed, and the wretch is forced to abandon the true end out of poverty—just like those who, being ignorant at the beginning, waste their entire treasure. 

When these people reach their limit, they have nothing further with which to work. From this it is said that such people are "buried in sorrow": both because they wasted their money on useless things, and because they lose that most noble science (which they would have otherwise pursued). For it is not necessary to consume your goods at a low price <note>meaning: wastefully or for little return</note>, since if you are not ignorant of the principles of the art which we have handed down to you, and have understood them correctly, you will arrive at the completion of the <term>mastery</term> <note>original: "magisterii"; the *Magisterium* refers to the successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone or the completion of the alchemical process.</note>. If, therefore, you lose your <term>money</term> <note>original: "aes"; literally "brass" or "copper," but used here metonymically for one's capital or wealth.</note> by not attending to our warnings...

# BOOK ONE

...which we have written for you in this <term>little book</term> <note>original: "libello"; a common term for a technical manual or treatise.</note> are open and manifest: do not criticize us unfairly, nor hurl blasphemies at us, but attribute any failure to your own ignorance and presumption.

Therefore, this <term>science</term> <note>original: "scientia"; here referring to the structured knowledge of alchemy.</note> is not well-suited to the poor or the needy; rather, it is hostile and adverse to them. Nor should you strive to reach a <term>deceptive</term> <note>original: "sophisticam"; in alchemical literature, "sophistry" refers to fraudulent methods that produce "fool's gold" or external appearances rather than true transmutation.</note> goal for the work, but be intent only on its completion. For **our art** is kept in the **power of God**, and He bestows it upon whom He wills and withdraws it from others—He who is glorious, sublime, and filled with all justice and goodness.

For perhaps, as a punishment for a deceptive work, He would deny the art to you and thrust you into the path of error, and from error into perpetual unhappiness and misery. For most wretched and unhappy is he to whom God, after the end of his work and labor, denies the sight of the truth; for he always concludes and ends the span of his life in sorrow. For this man, established in perpetual labor and besieged by every **misfortune** and unhappiness, loses all the consolation, joy, and delight of this world, and consumes his life in sorrow without profit. 

Similarly, strive, when you are engaged in the work, to seal in your mind all the signs that appear in any <term>decoction</term> <note>original: "decoctione"; the process of heating or "cooking" a substance in a liquid, a fundamental operation in the alchemical laboratory.</note>, and to inquire into their causes.

## On the Persuasions of the Sophists.
### Chapter 6.

**S**ince in the summary of this, our book, we have already set forth all the impediments to this work, and have given you sufficient instruction for adhering to the art, it is now expedient for us—in accordance with the intention of our purpose—to dispute against the <term>Sophists</term> <note>original: "Sophistas"; here referring to academic or philosophical critics who used logical arguments to debunk alchemy.</note> and the ignorant. First, by laying out their own reasons, just as we promised in the beginning that we would determine; and finally, by refuting them all and demonstrating through clear proof to the wise that their arguments contain no truth.

There are, moreover, various people who deny and seek to destroy the art. Some do so simply; others, however, strive to reject it based on premises derived from an underlying cause. For there are some who assert that the art does not exist as we have described, strengthening their <term>sophistical</term> <note>original: "sophisticè"; refers to reasoning that is clever but fallacious or deceptive.</note> opinion in the following manner: They argue that the species and diversities of things are distinct because the <term>proportions</term> <note>original: "proportiones"; the specific ratios of the four elements (fire, air, water, earth) that were believed to define the nature of every physical substance.</note> of the elements to one another in their mixture are diverse and distinct. For example, a donkey is different in species from a man because it possessed a much different proportion of elements in its composition; the same can be inferred for all other diversities of things, and therefore also for <term>minerals</term> <note>original: "mineralibus"; in this context, the term encompasses all metals and ores.</note>. 

Therefore, if the proportion of the ingredients—by which the form and perfection of a thing is attained—is unknown, how shall we know how to form the mixture or the substance to be mixed? But we are ignorant of the proportions of the <term>Sun</term> <note>original: "solis"; the standard alchemical name for Gold.</note>, the <term>Moon</term> <note>original: "lunæ"; the standard alchemical name for Silver.</note>, and also of the elements themselves; therefore, we must be ignorant of how to form them. From these premises, they conclude that this art is useless and impossible. Similarly, they also argue against the <term>mastery</term> <note>original: "magisteriũ"; the technical term for the successful completion of the alchemical process.</note> in another way...

<summary>This page introduces a formal rebuttal against critics of alchemy, specifically addressing the argument that because humans cannot know the exact elemental proportions of gold and silver, they cannot create them. The author frames this as the "Sophists' persuasion" which he intends to systematically disprove.</summary>
<keywords>Sophists, alchemy logic, elemental proportions, gold, silver, transmutation arguments, minerals, species of things</keywords>

-> FIRST <-

<meta>catchword: Asin- (refers to 'asinum' on the following page)</meta>

destroying our art. For they say: even if you knew the <term>proportions</term> <note>original: "proportionē"; the ratio of elements like fire, air, earth, and water.</note> of the elements, you nonetheless remain ignorant of the manner of their mixing with one another. For nature procreates these things in caverns, in mines, and in hidden places; therefore, since you are ignorant of the manner of their mixing, you are especially ignorant of how to perform this act. Again, they argue in a similar way: even if you knew this properly, you would nonetheless be ignorant, in the act of mixing, of how to equalize the <term>acting heat</term> <note>original: "calorem æquare agentem"; alchemists believed that specific, sustained degrees of heat were required to "ripen" metals over long periods.</note> by means of which this thing is completed. For nature possesses a certain quantity of heat by which it brings metals into being, the measure of which you do not know.

Likewise, you are also ignorant of other differences in the acting causes of nature, without which nature could not achieve its intended goal. Therefore, with these things being unknown, the whole manner of the art's operation will likewise be unknown. Furthermore, I bring before you an argument based on experience: this science has been sought by wise men for so long that if it were possible to arrive at it by any way, they would have completed it a thousand times already. Likewise also, since the **philosophers** <note>original: "philosophi"; here meaning ancient authorities and alchemical masters.</note> have not dared to hand it down in their volumes, nor do we find the manifest truth in them, it is sufficiently probable through this that this science does not exist.

In the same way, many **Princes and Kings** of this world, possessing infinite treasure and a wealth of philosophers, have desired to discover this art; yet they were not able to reach the fruit of this most precious art. This indeed is a sufficiently enough argument that the art is <term>frivolous</term> <note>original: "friuola"; meaning worthless, empty, or lacking a solid foundation.</note>. Likewise also, we cannot follow nature in the subtle mixing of species, just as we are not able to follow nature in forming a **donkey** <note>original: "asinum"; a common philosophical example of a "natural" creature. The argument is: if we cannot create a simple animal, how can we create a "higher" substance like gold?</note> that is organized according to nature.

It uses the impossibility of "creating a donkey" as a logical comparison to the impossibility of creating metals.</summary>

<meta>catchword: rather</meta>

*For do we not know how to shape* <note>original: "fingere"; to fashion or form an object.</note> *and similar things, whose mixtures are weak and manifest to the senses? Therefore, how much more so shall we be ignorant of the mixing of metals, which is very strong, and which is also entirely hidden from our senses and experiences? A sign of this is the difficulty of* <term>resolution</term> <note>original: "resolutionis"; the chemical process of breaking a substance down into its constituent parts.</note> *of metals from their own forms. Similarly, we do not see an ox transformed into a goat, nor any other **species** reduced into another by artifice. How then, since metals differ in species, do you strive to transform them into one another according to their kind, so that you might make one species from another? This seems absurd enough to us, and removed from the established truth and from natural principles. Similarly also, nature perfects metals over thousands of years; how can you, in the art of transmutation, endure for thousands of years, when you are scarcely able to reach the age of a hundred years?*

*If, however, it should be answered in such a way—that what nature cannot perfect in a very great span of time, we fulfill in a short time through our* <term>artifice</term> <note>original: "artificium"; the human skill or craft used to supplement or mimic nature.</note>, *since artifice in many things completes the deficiency of nature—we say that this is impossible in metals, especially since they are very subtle vapors* <note>original: "fumi subtilissimi"; referring to the theory that metals are condensed from subterranean "exhalations" or smokes.</note> *which require a tempered* <term>decoction</term> <note>original: "decoctione"; a slow, steady heating process meant to "ripen" a substance.</note> *so that within them, according to a degree of equality, the* <term>moisture</term> <note>original: "humiditas"; the fluidic principle within metals that grants them malleability.</note> *might be thickened and not flee from them, leaving them deprived of all moisture. Likewise, they undergo striking and extension* <note>meaning they are malleable and can be hammered or stretched.</note>. *If, therefore, through your artifice you wish to shorten nature's time of decoction in mineral and metallic bodies, it will be necessary to do this through an excess of heat, which will not be balanced, but*

*...instead, by dissolving the moisture, it will dissipate and destroy it from their bodies. For only a temperate heat—one that does not exceed the proper limit—is capable of* <term>thickening</term> <note>original: "inspissatiuus"; the process of making a liquid or vapor more solid or dense.</note> *the moisture and perfecting the mixture. Likewise, being and perfection are granted by the stars, as if by the first movers who perfect the nature of* <term>generation and corruption</term> <note>original: "generationis & corruptionis"; the Aristotelian concept of the natural cycle where the birth of one thing is the decay of another.</note> *regarding the existence or non-existence of species. This, however, happens suddenly and in an instant when one or more stars, through their movements, reach a specific position in the* <term>firmament</term> <note>original: "firmamento"; the sphere of the heavens where the stars are fixed.</note>, *from which the perfection of being is granted. This is because every single thing acquires its being in a single moment from a certain arrangement of the stars; and there is not just one such arrangement, but many, all differing from one another.*

*Just as their effects are diverse, we cannot fully grasp the diversity and distinction of these arrangements, for they are unknown and infinite to us. How then will you make up for the deficiency in your work, when you are ignorant of the diverse positions and motions of the stars? And even if you did know the specific arrangement of one or more stars by which perfection is granted to metals, you still would not complete the work you have begun. For the preparation of any work to receive a* <term>form</term> <note>original: "formam"; in medieval science, the "form" is the essential identity of a substance (like "gold-ness") that is impressed upon "matter."</note> *through human artifice is not instantaneous, but* <term>successive</term> <note>original: "successiua"; occurring in a sequence of steps over time.</note>. *Therefore, the "form" will not be given to the work, since the work is not ready at that specific instant [the stars are aligned]. Likewise, this is the order of natural things: it is easier to destroy than to build. Yet we can scarcely destroy gold; how then can we build it so quickly? For these aforementioned* <term>sophistical</term> <note>original: "sophisticas"; referring to arguments that sound clever but are intended to deceive or are based on false logic.</note> *reasons, and others less obvious than these, they believe they can strike down this divine art. All these preceding arguments are those of the sophists...*

...of those simply denying our art. ## On the natural principles upon which nature 
## founds its actions. Chapter 7.

**W**E say, in any case, that the principles upon which nature founds its action are of the hardest and strongest composition: and they are Sulfur and Quicksilver <note>original: "Argentum uiuum"; literally "living silver," the common name for mercury, which alchemists believed was the feminine, fluid seed of all metals.</note> (as certain philosophers say). Therefore, because they are of the hardest composition and the most difficult <term>resolution</term> <note>original: "resolutionis"; the process of dissolving a solid back into its liquid or primary components.</note>, their mutual thickening and hardening occurs in such a way that there is a blending and extension through <term>hammering</term> <note>original: "malleationem"; the quality of malleability that allows metals to be shaped without shattering.</note> rather than shattering. This only happens because the viscous moisture in their mutual mixture is preserved through a gradual and long-lasting thickening, and through a most temperate <term>slow-cooking</term> <note>original: "decoctionem"; a process of heating and ripening within the earth, often compared to digestion.</note> within the <term>ore-vein</term> <note>original: "minera"; the natural geological source or "mine" where minerals are formed.</note>. But we hand down a general rule to you, my son: no thickening of any moisture occurs unless there is first an exhalation of the thinnest parts of that moisture, and a preservation of the thicker parts, so that the moisture in the mixture overcomes the dry parts. In this way, the mixture of dry and moist is balanced, so that the moist is tempered by the dry and the dry by the moist. Thus, this substance becomes one in all its parts...

<summary>This page explains that metals form naturally over thousands of years through a slow, perfect blending of moisture and earth. The author argues that because these elements are so tightly bound, a sudden heat (like in human alchemy) would destroy the mixture rather than refine it, highlighting a major challenge for alchemists.</summary>

# On the solutions to the arguments of the Sophists. Chapter 7.

**O**ur intention, therefore, is not to follow nature in its first principles <note>original: "principijs"; the fundamental starting points or essential building blocks of matter.</note>, nor in the proportion of the mixable elements <note>original: "miscibilium elementorum"; referring to the four classical elements of fire, air, water, and earth.</note>, nor in the manner of their mixing with one another; nor in the balancing of the thickening heat <note>original: "æquatione caloris inspissantis"; the precise temperature required to "thicken" or congeal substances into solid metal.</note>, since all these things are impossible for us and entirely unknown. It remains, therefore, to destroy the arguments of the Sophists <note>original: "Sophistarum"; here used to describe skeptics or critics who use clever but fallacious logic to argue against the possibility of alchemy.</note> who deny that this sublime art can exist. 

If they say, therefore, that we are ignorant of the proportion of the elements, and the manner of their mixing with one another, and the balancing of the heat that thickens metals, and many other causes and <term>accidents</term> <note>original: "accidentia"; in medieval philosophy, these are the non-essential qualities of a thing—like color or texture—that can change without the core substance changing.</note> that govern nature's actions: we certainly grant that to them. But they do not for this reason destroy our divine science <note>original: "scientiam diuinam"; alchemy was often called the "Divine Art" because it sought to complete nature's work.</note>; because we neither wish to know those things, nor are we able, nor can they be relevant to our work. But for this purpose, we adopt a principle for ourselves, and another way of generating metals, in which we *can* follow nature. 

If they also say that the Philosophers and the princes of this world have desired this science and have not found it: we reply that they lie. For we have read that certain princes—though few, and especially the ancients and the wise, and those found in our own time—have already investigated this science through their own industry. But they were not willing to hand it down to such people either by word of mouth or in writing, since they are unworthy. Therefore, because they have not seen anyone possessing this science, an error has fallen upon their minds, so that they judge that no one has found this science. To this they also argue

<meta>catchword: fantastically,</meta>

<summary>The author defends the validity of alchemy by acknowledging that while humans cannot mimic nature's exact proportions, they can use an alternative method of generation. He asserts that the art is real but kept secret from the unworthy by those who have successfully discovered it.</summary>

<keywords>Sophists, skepticism, metallic generation, divine science, proportion of elements, secrets of the philosophers</keywords>

# C

...fancifully, asserting that in our powerlessness we cannot imitate nature even in "weak" mixtures, such as in the mixture of an ass or an ox; therefore, they argue, we cannot do so in "strong" ones <note>The author is contrasting biological life ("weak" because it is perishable) with minerals ("strong" because they are durable).</note>. We say that their error is manifold: because in their way of arguing, there is no logical necessity that forces us to concede that our art is impossible. They bolster their fantasy and error by arguing from similarity, or from the greater to the lesser <note>This refers to the skeptical argument that if a chemist cannot perform the "lesser" task of making a simple animal, they cannot perform the "greater" task of making metal. The author argues this logic is flawed.</note>, which contains no necessity, but only probability at best.

We also show another way, demonstrating that they provide no apparent similarity between the weak composition of animals and the firm, strong forms of minerals. This is because in animals and other living things where the composition is weak, it is not merely the proportion of elements that completes them; nor the things to be mixed, nor their qualities, nor the mixing that results from their mutual action and passion <note>original: "actione... & passione"; in medieval physics, "action" is what one substance does to another, and "passion" is what the other substance experiences or undergoes during a reaction.</note>, which comes from the gathering of those qualities. Instead, it is the <term>soul</term> <note>original: "anima"; here meaning the vital life-force or animating principle that distinguishes living creatures from inanimate matter.</note>, which, according to the opinion of many, comes from the hidden depths of nature—such as from the <term>fifth essence</term> <note>original: "essentia quinta"; the quintessence, a celestial element purer than the four earthly elements of earth, air, fire, and water.</note> or from the <term>Prime Mover</term> <note>original: "primo motore"; the ultimate source of all motion and life in the universe, often identified with God or a divine cosmic force in philosophical contexts.</note>. We also speak according to the consensus of many, yet we remain ignorant of this secret principle.

Therefore, even though there is a weak mixture in such living things, we do not know how to complete them because we do not know how to infuse the "perfecting" element, which is the soul. Thus it is clear: it is not because of the mixture of physical ingredients that we cannot create an ox or a goat, but because of our inability to infuse a soul. However, to create a composition—be it weak or very weak, strong or very strong—according to the natural path and course...

<summary>The author refutes skeptics by arguing that the reason humans cannot create animals is a lack of "soul" (life-force), not an inability to mix materials; since minerals lack souls, they should be easier to replicate than living creatures.</summary>

# C

...so that we might know our craft by imitating nature. In metals, therefore, perfection is lesser than in living things; their perfection consists more in proportion and composition than in other factors. For this reason, since perfection is lesser in them than in the other things we have described, we can perfect them more freely; but the others, we cannot. For God, the Most High and Glorious, has diversified these perfections among one another in many ways. In those things where the composition according to nature was weak, He placed a greater and more noble perfection, which is according to the <term>soul</term> <note>original: "anima"; here referring to the "animating principle" or life-force that distinguishes living creatures from minerals.</note>. But He gave a stronger and firmer composition to others, such as stones and minerals, yet in them He placed a lesser and more ignoble perfection, which arises from the manner of their mixing. It is clear, therefore, that their comparison <note>The skeptics' comparison between the creation of an animal and the creation of a metal.</note> is not valid. We know that an ox or a goat exists not simply by reason of its composition, but by its perfect form; the perfection in an ox or a goat is more noble and more hidden than the perfection that exists in metals.

Similarly, they argue in another way: that one species cannot be changed into another species. We say, therefore, that they lie once again—as they are often accustomed to say things other than the truth on these matters—because species *is* changed into species in this way, when an individual of one species is changed into the species of another. For we see a worm—both naturally and through natural artifice—be changed into a fly, which differs from it in species. We see a strangled calf be transmuted into bees <note>This refers to the ancient and medieval belief in "spontaneous generation," specifically the "Bugonia" myth that bees could be born from the carcass of a dead ox.</note>; and wheat into darnel <note>original: "lolium"; a weed that resembles wheat, often believed in the Middle Ages to be a corrupted or transformed version of the grain.</note>, and a dog be transmuted into a worm through the boiling of <term>putrefaction</term> <note>original: "putrefactionem"; in alchemy, the process of decay or "blackening" (nigredo) that was believed to be the necessary first step for any transformation of matter.</note>. But we do not do this ourselves; rather, nature does it, and we are merely those who serve nature.

# FIRST.

## FIRST.

*Similarly, we do not change metals ourselves; rather, it is nature for whom we prepare a certain material through our* <term>artifice</term> <note>original: "artificium"; refers to the skilled craft or "art" of alchemy.</note>, *since nature acts by herself, and we are merely her stewards. And if they argue by another reason to bolster their* **sophistical opinion**—*namely, that nature perfects metals over thousands of years, while you cannot live for thousands of years—we reply: we say that nature, acting upon her own principles (according to the opinion of the ancient philosophers), completes her work in thousands of years. But because we cannot follow those specific natural principles, it does not matter whether nature completes this in thousands of years, or more, or fewer, or even in a moment; their persuasion does not hold.*

*This is because we neither can nor wish to imitate nature in her own internal principles. For we see that in thousands of years, nature does not cook stones into* <term>lime</term> <note>original: "calcem"; calcium oxide, produced by heating limestone. This was a common industrial process used as an analogy for alchemical acceleration.</note>, *yet a human being does this in a short time by his own natural ingenuity through* <term>accidental heat</term> <note>original: "accidentalem calorem"; in medieval science, "accidental" means heat applied externally by an operator, as opposed to the "essential" or "innate" heat within a substance.</note>. *Therefore, in the purifying of metals by the ingenuity and artifice of man, they are reduced to a pure and more noble substance in a short time, because nature cannot supply their defects so quickly.*

*We have already determined this from the preceding abbreviated argument, and we will demonstrate it with a more complete discourse in what follows. Nevertheless, according to the opinion of certain clear-sighted and perceptive individuals, nature actually completes her intent suddenly—that is, in a single day, or even a shorter time. And even if this were true, we are still unable to imitate nature in her own principles, which we shall demonstrate more clearly by further proof. As for the rest of the question, we confess that the whole tru[th]...*

He asserts that the alchemist acts as a steward to nature, facilitating her work in a shorter timeframe.</summary>

...[of substances] we believe them to be. And if they say that if a perfection is given to metals by the position of one or several **Stars**—a position which we do not know—we reply that it is not necessary for us to know this position. This is because there is no species of things capable of being generated or corrupted <note>original: "generabilium & corruptibilium"; a standard Aristotelian phrase referring to everything in the earthly realm that is born, grows, and dies.</note> that does not undergo generation and corruption among its individuals every single day. Through this, it is clear that the position of the Stars is every day perfecting and corrupting individuals of every species. Therefore, it is not necessary to wait for a specific position of the stars, though it would be useful; rather, it is sufficient only to prepare nature, so that she, who is wise, may arrange matters with the suitable positions of moving bodies. 

For nature cannot bring anything to perfection without the motion and position of moving bodies <note>In medieval science, "moving bodies" refers to the celestial spheres and planets whose movements were believed to drive all change on Earth.</note>. Thus, if you have arranged the <term>artifice</term> of nature and considered whatever occurs from the contingencies of this <term>magistery</term> <note>original: "magisterii"; the "Mastery" or "Grand Work" of alchemy.</note>, the metals will be duly perfected under the appropriate astrological position by the nature suitable to them, without you having to calculate that position yourself. 

We see an example of this when we desire a worm to be brought into being from flesh or some other putrefying animal <note>This refers to the medieval belief in "spontaneous generation," where life was thought to arise from decaying matter given the right environment.</note>. We do not immediately consider the position of the Stars; instead, we look to the state of the surrounding air and other causes that complete the <term>putrefaction</term>. From such considerations, we know enough to produce worms into being according to nature. Nature herself finds the suitable celestial position, even if we are ignorant of it. 

And if they argue that true perfections are granted in an instant, whereas we do not complete our alchemical perfection immediately, and they conclude from this that it cannot be achieved through the artifice of this art—we say that their heads are empty of human reason, and they are more like beasts than men. ...connection to that which has been brought forward. For this argument holds as much weight—"A donkey runs, therefore you are a goat"—as their own argument <note>The author uses this absurd non-sequitur to mock the logical fallacies of his opponents, who argue that because something is difficult or unknown, it must be impossible.</note>. And this is so because, even if the <term>preparation</term> <note>original: "praeparatio"; the preliminary chemical or philosophical work done on a substance to make it ready for transformation.</note> does not happen in an instant, the <term>form</term> or <term>perfection</term> <note>original: "perfectio"; in this context, the final, completed state of a metal, particularly gold.</note> does not prevent a preparation from being able to be given in an instant. For preparation is not the perfection itself, but rather the enabling of the substance to receive that form.

If they also say that it is easier to destroy natural things than to construct them through <term>artifice</term> <note>original: "artificium"; human skill, technology, or "Art" used to mimic or accelerate natural processes.</note>, and since we can scarcely destroy **Gold**: they therefore conclude that it is impossible to construct it. 

We respond to them in this way: their conclusion is not a logical necessity that should compel us to believe that gold cannot be constructed. For that which is constructed with difficulty is likewise destroyed with difficulty; however, this does not mean it is impossible to construct. 

We have assigned the cause of this: because gold has a strong <term>composition</term>, it therefore has a more difficult <term>resolution</term> <note>original: "resolutio"; the process of breaking a compound back down into its basic elements or its "first matter."</note>, and for that reason, it is destroyed with difficulty. This difficulty is what leads them to think its construction is impossible. But because they are ignorant of the natural composition of gold, they are also ignorant of how it can be destroyed and how an artificial construction can be achieved according to the course of nature.

# On the arguments of those who deny the art on the basis of principles.
## Chapter 11.

**S**o then, my most dear son, we have sufficiently recounted the fantasies of the **Sophists** <note>In this context, "Sophists" refers to false philosophers or skeptics who use clever but fallacious logic to argue against the possibility of alchemy.</note>. It remains, therefore—since we have made you attentive by our promise—to pass, according to the path of that promise, to those matters which must be determined from the reasonings of those who deny the art based on given principles. Whatever principles have been determined, however, belong to the **intention of nature**; we must discuss their essence more fully in what follows. After that determination, we shall also speak of them insofar as they are the principles of this, our **magistery** <term>magisterium</term> <note>original: "magisterium"; the "Mastery" or the Great Work, referring to the entire process of alchemical transformation.</note>. In the first teaching, however, we shall provide a specific discourse on each of the principles; then, in the following part, a universal one. 

For now, however, let us first bring forward the reasons of those who deny the art based on given principles, and their intentions. We have found that those who assume this art exists do so in many ways, based on different intentions. Some indeed seek it in **spirits** <note>original: "spiritibus"; volatile substances like sulfur, mercury, or arsenic that can be vaporized by heat.</note>. Others in **bodies** <note>original: "corporibus"; the solid metals or fixed substances.</note>. Others in salts and **alums**, **nitres**, and **boraxes** <term>bauracijs</term> <note>original: "bauracijs"; an old term for borax or various alkaline salts used in metallurgy as fluxes.</note>. Others indeed affirm that this art and magistery is found in all **vegetable things**. 

Out of all these, some reasoned well in part, and badly in part. Others, however, judging this **divine magistery** poorly in its entirety, handed it down to posterity. From the multiplicity of their errors, we have gathered the truth—though with great difficulty and labor—through cautious conjecture and long, tedious experience, and at the cost of many...

<meta>The catchword "sumptum" indicates the next page begins with "expenses" or "costs," referring to the financial burden of alchemical experimentation.</meta>
