...occurs through the intervention of expenses <note>Alchemical experimentation was notoriously expensive; many practitioners fell into ruin when their costly ingredients failed to produce gold.</note>, and their error has very often campaigned through the reasoning of our mind and brought about despair. Let them therefore be condemned, because through their error they have left behind curses for posterity forever, and they have poured out a malediction upon those practicing philosophy, leaving behind not the truth, but a diabolical instigation after their own deaths. And I also deserve to be condemned unless I correct their errors and hand down the truth in this science, as our art best requires. For this **magistery** <term>magisterium</term> <note>The "Mastery" or Great Work; the complete process of alchemical transformation.</note> does not need hidden speech, but should be entirely manifest.

We shall therefore hand it down in such speech that it will not fail to be understood by the prudent. In this one and the same tradition of ours, our words will be most profound to the mediocre; but for the foolish, it miserably concludes both limits. Returning therefore to the proposal, we say that those who place the art in **spirits** <note>In alchemy, "spirits" are volatile substances that can be vaporized by heat, such as mercury or sulfur.</note> are various and many. Some indeed assert it is necessary to seek the <term>philosopher's stone</term> from **quicksilver** <term>argento uiuo</term> <note>original: "argento uiuo"; liquid mercury.</note>. Others truly from **Sulfur** <term>Sulphure</term> and its relative **Arsenic** <term>Arsenico</term>. And among these, some seek it from **Marcasite** <term>Marchasita</term> <note>A term used historically for various metallic sulfides, often iron pyrites.</note>, some from **Tutia** <term>Tutia</term> <note>Zinc oxide, often collected as a byproduct from furnace chimneys.</note> and **Magnesia** <term>Magnesia</term> <note>A term that could refer to various minerals, including manganese ores or magnetite.</note>; others from **sal ammoniac** <term>sale armoniaco</term>.

Those, however, who place it in **bodies** <note>The "bodies" refer to the solid metals, which were seen as more "fixed" than the volatile spirits.</note>: some say it is in **Saturn** <term>Saturno</term> <note>The alchemical name for lead.</note>. Others say it is in any of the other bodies. Some indeed in **glass** <term>uitro</term>. Others in **gems**. Others in the diversities of **salts**, **alums** <term>aluminum</term>, **nitres** <term>nitrorum</term>, and **borax** <term>baurachium</term> <note>original: "baurachium"; referring to various alkaline salts used as fluxes.</note>. And others in every kind of vegetable things; and for each of these supporters, there is an adversary according to his own supposition. And he who opposes these believes himself simply to be an adversary of the art itself. And for the most part, both sides are empty of clear explanations...

# On those who suppose the art exists within spirits. Chapter 10.

**S**ome, therefore, supposing that the art is to be found in **Sulfur** <term>Sulphure</term> <note>Sulfur was considered one of the primary "parents" of metals, representing the principle of combustion and color.</note>, have expended much labor. However, being ignorant of the true perfection of its preparation, they left that perfection diminished. For they believed that mere cleansing and purification constituted the "perfection" of the preparation, and this is achieved through **sublimation** <term>sublimationem</term> <note>The process of heating a solid substance until it turns into vapor and then collecting it as it cools back into a solid; used by alchemists to "purify" volatile spirits.</note>. 

Thus, they were led by this intent to estimate that sublimation alone in Sulfur is the perfection of its preparation; and they were led to believe the same regarding its companion, namely **Arsenic** <term>Arsenico</term> <note>In alchemy, arsenic was often paired with sulfur due to its volatility and its effect on the color of metals.</note>. Coming therefore to **projection** <term>proiectionem</term> <note>The final stage of the alchemical process, where the "medicine" or stone is cast upon molten base metal to transform it into gold or silver.</note>, which is the intended goal of the transformation, they saw that the substance burned away and vanished. It did not maintain a long-lasting stay within the bodies of the metals; instead, the metals were left by them more unclean than they had been before the projection was even attempted.

Because they saw this delusion at the completion of their work—and because it had revolved in their hearts for a very long time that this science could be found in Sulfur alone—when they failed to find it there, they argued that it could not be found in anything else. Therefore, since they find it neither in this nor in any other substance, they argue that it can never be found at all.

Responding to them briefly, we say that they perceive little in this matter and have very little wisdom. They wrongly suppose that Sulfur alone is our **medicine** <term>medicina</term> <note>Another term for the "Elixir" or "Philosopher's Stone," the substance capable of curing the "sickness" of base metals and turning them into gold.</note>. Even if this were a true supposition, they are nevertheless deceived in their method of preparation, because they believe that sublimation alone is sufficient.

<summary>This page critiques alchemists who mistakenly believe that Sulfur and Arsenic are the sole ingredients of the Great Work and fail because they rely only on sublimation for purification.</summary>
<keywords>Sulfur, Arsenic, sublimation, projection, alchemical medicine, purification of spirits, failure of alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of alchemists who fail to achieve the Great Work because they misunderstand the nature of sulfur and its "companion" (arsenic). ->BOOK FIRST<-

For they are like a child shut up in a house from the beginning of his birth until old age, not thinking that the breadth of the world extends beyond the house or beyond what his eye can see. For these men have not applied their labor to many stones: therefore they could not perceive from what our **medicine** <term>medicina</term> <note>In alchemy, the "medicine" refers to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone, which was thought to "heal" base metals of their impurities, turning them into gold.</note> may be drawn, and from what it may not; and thus they excused their hands even from a bounty of labors, for they deservedly remained ignorant of what constitutes the perfecting of labors. 

But we say why their work was diminished: because they left the nature of **combustibility** <note>original: adurentiæ; the tendency of a substance to burn away.</note> and **volatility** <note>original: fugam; literally "flight," referring to the tendency of a substance to escape as vapor when heated.</note> in the sulfur, which is why they dissipate and destroy the work. Others truly, estimating it necessary that the stone be found in it [sulfur] and its companion <note>Referring to arsenic, often called the "companion" of sulfur in alchemical texts.</note>, and approaching more deeply toward the consummation of the work: not only did they cleanse the combustibility of the sulfur through **sublimation** <term>sublimationem</term>, but they also attempted to remove the volatility remaining in it. To their "perfection" a similar delusion came, because the substance did not adhere stably within the bodies of the metals themselves, but successively and little by little it vanished, leaving the metal in its original state. 

These men also, like the first group, argued destructively against the art; and responding to them as we did to the first, we affirm the art and we know it exists, because we have seen and touched the truth. Others, because they saw more deeply into the matter, cleansed the sulfur and took away its volatility and combustibility, and it became **fixed** <note>Non-volatile; resistant to the heat of fire.</note> and earthy for them. However, it gave no good fusion in the heat of the fire, but only a **vitrifying** <note>Turning into a glass-like substance rather than a metallic one.</note> fusion; and therefore they could not mix it with the metallic bodies during **projection** <term>proiectionem</term>. Therefore they argue like the first ones, and we respond to them as we did to the first, because a diminished work... <note>The text breaks off here and continues on the next page.</note>

<summary>The author critiques alchemists who fail to transform metals using sulfur and arsenic, comparing their limited experience to a child who has never left his house. He explains that their failures result from either leaving the sulfur too volatile (so it evaporates) or making it too "earthy" (so it turns to glass instead of mixing with metal).</summary>

Here, he expands this comparison to other alchemical substances and explains why these failures lead many to falsely believe the science of alchemy itself is a fraud.</meta>

# BOOK

...they carry out, nor did they know how to complete it. For they were ignorant of how to investigate the final goal, which is **penetration** <term>ingressio</term> <note>original: "ingressionem"; the ability of the alchemical spirit to enter and permeate the "body" of a base metal to transform it.</note>. The method of preparation is the same for all **spirits** <term>spiritus</term> <note>In alchemy, "spirits" are volatile substances—like mercury, sulfur, or arsenic—that can be vaporized by heat.</note>, except that with **quicksilver** <note>original: "argento uiuo"; mercury.</note> and **tutty** <note>original: "tutijs"; a mineral substance, usually zinc oxide, collected from the flues of smelting furnaces.</note> we are spared the greater labor of removing "burning," for these do not possess a combustible or inflammable sulfurous nature, but only **volatility** <note>original: "fugam"; literally "flight," the tendency of a substance to evaporate and escape as vapor when heated.</note>.

**Magnesia** <note>A term used broadly in alchemy for various mineral ores, often manganese or a similar white/black mineral used in the Great Work.</note> and **marcasite** <note>An iron sulfide mineral; alchemists valued it for its metallic luster and sulfur content.</note>, however, possess every kind of sulfurous nature; marcasite more so, but magnesia less. Yet they all possess volatility, though quicksilver and **sal ammoniac** <note>original: "armoniacum sal"; ammonium chloride, used to help substances sublimate or to clean metals.</note> have the most; sulfur has less, and its **companion** <note>Referring to arsenic.</note> has even less than that. Fourthly, marcasite has even less; fifthly, magnesia has less still; and sixthly and finally, tutty has the least of all. 

Each, however, partakes in volatility, though some more and some less; and therefore, on account of this "flight," a powerful delusion has come upon certain experimenters in their preparatory operations, and likewise in their **projections** <term>proiectionibus</term> <note>The final stage of the alchemical process where the Philosopher's Stone or Elixir is "cast" onto a base metal to achieve transmutation.</note>. And thus they themselves argue and destroy their work as those who make false assumptions about sulfur, and we respond to those same people as those making such assumptions.

There are also others who, striving in their experiments to **fix** <note>original: "figere"; the process of making a volatile "spirit" stable and resistant to fire so that it no longer evaporates.</note> the spirits into the "bodies" <note>the solid metals.</note> without any other preparation coming first; but for these, the same delusion brought distress and despair, and they were forced by it to believe that this science does not exist and to argue against it. For the cause of their confusion is their own misplaced diligence, since during the melting of the metal bodies, they release the spirits in such a way that they do not bond with them; instead, the spirits flee from the harshness of the fire, while only the solid metals remain in it, because the pressure...

-> of impiety <-

-> BOOK ONE. <-
<page-num>14</page-num>

...they cannot endure the **fire of impiety** <note>original: "impietatis ignis"; likely referring to an unholy or improperly regulated heat that destroys the work</note> because of the **volatility** <note>original: "fugam"; literally "flight," the tendency of a substance to evaporate.</note> which has not been removed from them. A similar **delusion** <note>original: "delusio"; a deceptive result in the lab that leads to false conclusions.</note> also occurs sometimes because the **bodies** <term>corpora</term> <note>In alchemy, "bodies" refers to the solid metals, as opposed to the volatile "spirits."</note> themselves flee the fire along with the spirits. This happens when the spirits are not **fixed** <term>fixi</term> <note>To "fix" a spirit is to chemically stabilize a volatile substance so it remains solid and does not vaporize when heated.</note> and have not adhered inseparably to the depths of the bodies, because the quantity of the volatile substance exceeds the quantity of the fixed substance. 

Whence they argue just as the first critics did, and we respond to them just as we did to the first. Therefore, their entire proof is this: "If you wish to transform the **bodies, O sons of doctrine** <note>original: "corpora filij doctrinæ"; a traditional way of addressing students or adepts of the alchemical art.</note>, then if it is possible for this to happen through any **medicine** <term>medicina</term> <note>The "medicine" or "elixir" is the refined alchemical substance used to transmute base metals into gold or silver.</note>, it must be done through those very spirits. But it is not possible for those spirits that are not fixed to adhere usefully to the bodies; rather, they flee and leave those bodies behind in an impure state. Furthermore, it is not possible for those that are fixed to **enter** <term>ingredi</term> <note>The ability of the alchemical agent to penetrate the surface of a metal to transform its inner nature.</note> the bodies, since they have become **earth** <note>original: "terra"; here meaning a solid, inert state that has lost its fluid, transformative power.</note>, which cannot be poured in. And yet, when enclosed in bodies, they appear to be fixed, but they are not; for they either depart from the bodies while the bodies remain, or both flee the fire together." 

Therefore, the critics argue that since it is in no way possible to find this art in **related material** <note>original: "materia affini"; substances that are naturally close in nature to the desired metal, like quicksilver or sulfur.</note>, it will certainly not be found in more remote material; therefore, it is found nowhere. Our response, therefore, is this:

## On those who suppose the Art is found in bodies,
## stones, salts, alums, boraxes, 
## or growing things.

### Chapter 11.

**C**ERTAIN people, however, placed that art in the **bodies** <term>corpora</term> <note>In alchemy, "bodies" refers to the solid metals, distinguished from the volatile "spirits."</note>, yet when they arrived at the work they were deceived. They judged both types of leaden-colored **tin** <note>original: "stagnum"; here the author uses "tin" as a category for both lead and tin.</note>—namely, **lead** <note>original: "plumbeum"; literally "leaden tin."</note> and **white tin** <note>original: "album"; the metal we call tin today.</note>—to be very similar and close to the nature of the **sun** <note>Gold.</note> and the **moon** <note>Silver.</note> because of their pure whiteness. They believed the leaden one [lead] was much like the sun but little like the moon, while the white one [tin] was much like the moon but little like the sun. 

Therefore, some of these people—judging **tin** <note>original: "stagnum"; specifically white tin.</note> (namely **Jupiter**) <note>Jupiter is the planetary and alchemical name for the metal Tin.</note> to be very much like the moon, and believing it to differ only in its **crackling** <note>original: "stridore"; refers to the "cry of tin," a distinctive sound the metal makes when bent.</note>, its softness, and the speed of its melting—thought it was easily meltable due to an excess of its humidity. Similarly, they explained that it possessed that crackling sound because of the volatile substance of **quicksilver** <term>argentum uiuum</term> <note>Mercury; alchemists believed all metals were composed of varying ratios of Mercury and Sulfur.</note> trapped within its parts. 

Consequently, they exposed it to the fire, and **calcining** <term>calcinantes</term> <note>The process of heating a metal until it oxidizes into a powder (the "calx").</note> it, they held it in the fire for as long as it could endure, until it was made white in its **calx** <term>calce</term>. But afterward, when enveloping it, they were unable to **reduce** <note>original: "reducere"; to turn a metallic ash back into solid metal.</note> it, and they judged that it would be impossible to do so. Even some of these people did manage to reduce something from it, but they found the same crackling, softness, and speed of melting as before. Therefore, they believed this path to be impossible and were led into disbelief, thinking that the art of **hardening** <note>original: "indurationis"; the process of making a soft metal like tin as hard as silver.</note> it could not be found. Some of these, however, calcined and reduced it, and then again by removing its **dross** <term>scoria</term> <note>The impurities or slag separated from metal during smelting.</note> with a greater fire...

# BOOK ONE

through the expression of fire they **calcined** <term>calcinatio</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air to reduce it to a powder.</note> and **reduced** it <term>reductio</term> <note>The process of restoring a powdered substance back into its metallic form.</note>, and so by often repeating the work, they saw it hardened and without its crackling sound. However, because they did not entirely remove its speed of **melting** <term>liquefactio</term>, their minds went astray, and they judged that success could not be reached. And so, some of these people—wishing to grant the same metal hardness and a delay in its melting by adding two other **bodies** <note>In this context, "bodies" refers to other solid metals.</note>—fell into a delusion; for whatever two bodies were mixed with it, it broke them, and their preparation did not help them in this. Therefore, since they could prepare it neither with hard things nor with fire, they excused themselves regarding the long delay in finding the Art, saying it was impossible. Because of this, they argued insistently against the Art, claiming that it did not exist. From this point, and by adding many **medicines** <term>medicamina</term> <note>Chemical additives or reagents used to attempt a transformation.</note>, they saw that these caused no change and were not suitable for them, but rather corrupted the metal and acted contrary to their purpose. Thus, they threw away their books and turned their heads away, finally calling the Art frivolous. To these people, we answer with our first response.

The same manner of deception is also found in **Saturn** <note>Saturn is the alchemical name for Lead.</note>, with only this difference: it does not break other bodies and it returns from its **powdered state** <term>calx</term> <note>The residue left after calcination; "calx" is the root of the modern word "calcium."</note> more quickly than **Jupiter** <note>The alchemical name for Tin.</note>. Nevertheless, they cannot remove its dull, bluish-gray color because they are ignorant. For this reason, they cannot **whiten** <term>dealbatio</term> it with a good whitening, nor could they, through their own imagination, stably associate it with stable bodies without it receding from the mixture when exposed to a strong fire or the expression of fire. And indeed, that which most deceives them in this...

*...preparation, assuming that this science could be found in nothing else but it <note>Referring to lead, the metal under discussion from the previous page.</note>; and because after two **reductions** <term>reductio</term> <note>The process of restoring a metal from its calcined, powdery state back into a solid, metallic form.</note> from its **calx** <term>calx</term> <note>The crumbly, oxide residue left after a metal has been roasted or "calcined" in fire.</note> it receives no hardness, but rather a greater softness than it had before, and they did not see it improved in other qualities similarly. Others, however, composing hard with hard things and soft with soft things on account of their similarity, wished to transmute the **bodies** <note>Alchemical term for the seven base and noble metals.</note> into one another, and they could not do it because of their ignorance; for example, mixing **Gold** <note>original: "solem" (the Sun)</note> or **Silver** <note>original: "lunam" (the Moon)</note> with **Copper** <note>original: "Venere" (Venus)</note> or any other one of the metals, they do not transmute them into Gold or Silver by a firm transmutation, for it happens that by the strong expression of fire each of those is separated from the mixture, or burned, or returns to its former nature. Indeed, some of them last longer in the mixture, while others last less, as has been sufficiently determined by us elsewhere. These delusions, occurring because of ignorance, make such men lose faith in this art and argue that it does not exist. Others, however, inquiring into this more intimately and deeply, thought and devised a way, and wished to find a path, so that hard things joined to soft things might stably harden them: namely, so that things joined and perfected might reduce the imperfect ones to perfection, and generally transmute and be transmuted into one another by a firm transmutation. And therefore they wished to find their likeness and affinity, partly through **medicines** <term>medicamina</term> <note>In alchemy, "medicines" (or elixirs) are substances used to "heal" imperfect metals, transforming them into Gold or Silver.</note>, and partly through the administration of fire...*

# BOOK ONE

...stration <note>This is the conclusion of the word "administration," referring to a specific alchemical process or treatment begun on the previous page.</note>, namely by thinning coarse things like **Venus** <note>original: "Venerem"; the alchemical name for Copper.</note> and **Mars** <note>original: "Martem"; the alchemical name for Iron.</note>, and thickening subtle things, like **Jupiter** <note>original: "Iouem"; the alchemical name for Tin.</note>, and things similar to them. And some of them, believing they could perfect this process, were deceived in the mixing of these two **bodies** <note>In alchemy, "bodies" refers to the solid metals.</note>, either because they made the result entirely fragile, or entirely too soft—unchanged from its hardness—or too hard—unchanged from its softness. And thus they did not find a proper balance; and therefore they denied that the **art** <note>Specifically the "Art" of Alchemy.</note> exists. Others, however, looking even more deeply, wished to alter the bodies by the extraction of their **souls** <term>anima</term> <note>The "soul" in alchemy usually refers to the volatile principle or the "sulfur" of a metal, which carries its color and properties.</note>, and with the soul extracted, to alter another soul. Similarly, their experience could not reach that goal, but they gave up on their intention, judging from this that the art cannot be discovered. Still others, attempting to perfect these things by **fire** alone, were deceived in their opinion, because they could not reach the goal, nor did they know how. And these men thought because of this that the art did not exist, to all of whom we object as we did before. Those who placed their hope in **glass** and **gems** tried to create alterations in the metallic bodies through glass and gems, and they could not, because that which does not penetrate cannot alter. And indeed, glass and gems do not penetrate the metals; therefore they do not alter them. Even if they tried to unite glass with metal—though this can scarcely be done—they still do not achieve their purpose, since they make the glass from the metallic bodies; and because of this error, they assume that error applies to the whole art, and thus they argue it does not exist. To them we respond that they work in an improper manner, and therefore, reaching an improper conclusion, they cannot destroy the validity of this art based on their own mistakes. There are also others who suppose it...

# BOOK ONE

... [find the] **art** <note>Specifically the Art of Alchemy, or the ability to transmute base metals into gold.</note> to be found in **salts** and **alums**, **niters** and <term>boraxes</term> <note>original: "baurachijs"; these are various mineral salts and alkaline substances used as fluxes in early chemistry.</note>: they can indeed experiment with these, but we think it is found in them least of all. And therefore, if after their experience they have found little utility for **transmutation** <note>The conversion of one substance into another, particularly base metal into gold.</note>—namely by **dissolving**, **coagulating**, and **roasting**—they do not thereby destroy the Art, since it is a necessary part of science. It is possible, however, for some alteration to be found in all these things, but it is very remote and extremely laborious. 

Furthermore, in all other growing things <note>Referring to the "Vegetable" or plant kingdom.</note>, it is proven to be even more laborious; therefore, some place the Art in all **vegetables**. They propose it is possible, but not for themselves: because they would sooner fail in the labor than it would be possible for the work to be perfected. Therefore, if such men do not find the Art by their own labors, they argue that the Art can be discovered by no amount of labor at all. 

All the previously mentioned erring ones proposed one single <term>medicine</term> <note>original: "medicinam"; in alchemy, the "Medicine" or "Elixir" is the agent of transformation.</note> of their own, and supposed no other besides that one. And all these men have now been refuted. 

But many others—almost infinite in number—who create a composition of all these things, or of certain ones under a diverse production, proceed ignorantly and unskillfully. Their error extends into infinity, because the diversity of the production of **mixables** is infinite, and the diversity and number of the ingredients themselves is infinite. In both these infinities, it is possible to err infinitely: sometimes from excess, sometimes from lack, and for these people, correction is impossible. 

But we, sparing the length and prolixity of speeches, do not wish to dwell on infinities, since we shall hand down universal knowledge in brief statements,

...which evidently will be able to amend the infinity of their errors and correct them. Now, however, we shall speak of the **natural principles** <note>In alchemical theory, these are the fundamental building blocks—Mercury, Sulfur, and Arsenic—that combine in different proportions to form all metals.</note> according to their causes, as we made you attentive from the beginning in our mention of them.

## Concerning the natural principles of Mercury,
## Sulfur, and Arsenic.
## Chapter 12.

**W**E THEREFORE point out to you, according to the opinion of some who were of the sect of those imitating our Art, that the natural principles in the work of nature are the **fetid spirit** <note>original: "spiritus foetens"; this usually refers to sulfur or sulfurous gases due to their strong, unpleasant smell.</note> and **living water** <note>original: "aqua uiua"; a common name for mercury (quicksilver) because of its fluid, "alive" movement.</note>, which we also concede is called **dry water**. We have divided the fetid spirit: for it is **white** in its hidden state, as well as **red** and **black**. Both are used in the mastery of this work, though in their manifest <note>Visible or external state.</note> form, both tend toward redness. We speak, therefore, in a brief yet complete discourse regarding the sufficient generation of each of these and the manner of their generation. It is necessary for us to expand our discourse and go into detail, providing a specific chapter for each natural principle. In general, however, we say that each of them is of the strongest composition and of uniform substance. This is because, within them, the earthy parts are so united with the airy, watery, and fiery parts—even down to their smallest particles—that none of them can be separated from the others during <term>resolution</term> <note>original: "resolutione"; the chemical process of reducing a substance into its component parts, often by melting or dissolving.</note>. Rather, each is resolved with the other because of the strong union they have toward one another...

-> # BOOK <-

*...is joined in turn through the smallest parts: with a heat in the minerals equal to that hidden in the bowels of the earth, multiplied and balanced according to the proper course of nature, following the requirement of their essence, according to the opinion of certain ancients. Others, however, have said otherwise: that the principle was not **Mercury** <term>original: "Argentum uiuum," literally "living silver"; the liquid metal we know as quicksilver.</term> in its own nature, but altered and converted into its own earth; and **Sulfur** similarly altered and changed into earth. For this reason, they said that the principle was something other than the **fetid spirit** <note>original: "spiritus foetens"; a common alchemical name for Sulfur due to its strong smell when burned.</note> and the **fleeing spirit** <note>original: "spiritus fugiens"; a name for Mercury, referring to its volatility and how it "flees" the fire by evaporating.</note> in the intention of nature.*

*And the reason by which they were moved was this: they do not find in silver mines, or those of other metals, anything that is Mercury in its own nature, or anything that is Sulfur similarly in its own nature; rather, they find each of these by itself, separated in its own mine and nature. Similarly, they also affirm this by another reason, namely:*
<margin>-</margin>
*there is no transition from one contrary to another except through a **middle disposition** <note>In Aristotelian physics, a "middle disposition" is an intermediate state. The argument here is that a soft liquid like Mercury cannot become a hard solid like Iron without passing through a halfway stage.</note>. Therefore, since this is so, they are compelled to confess and believe that there is no transition from the softness of Mercury to the hardness of any metal, except through a disposition that is between their hardness and softness. But in the mines they do not find anything in which this middle disposition is preserved; therefore, by this reason, they are compelled to believe that Mercury and Sulfur in their own nature are not the principle by the intention of nature, but rather something else: which follows from the alteration of their essences at the root of nature into an earthy substance. And this is the method: because first each of them is converted to an earthy substance, and from both of these earthy natu—*

The author uses the philosophical concept of "middle disposition" to argue that nature requires a transitional state between liquid and solid forms.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the theory of metallic generation, explaining how heat within the earth creates a "double vapor" that combines with water and earth. It marks a transition from general theory to specific chapters on the three alchemical principles: Sulfur, Arsenic, and Quicksilver (Mercury).</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
-> # BOOK THE FIRST <-
<sig>D ii</sig>

...vapor is released, a very thin smoke produced by the heat multiplied within the bowels of the earth; and this double vapor is the immediate matter of metals. However, this vapor, cooked by the tempered heat of the mine, is converted into the nature of a certain earth. Therefore, it undergoes a kind of fixation, which the water flowing through the bowels of the mine and the sponginess of the earth dissolves, and it is uniformly united with it in a natural and firm union.

And so, those of this opinion said that the water flowing through the passages of the earth finds a dissolvable substance from the substance of the earth in its bowels, and dissolves it, and is uniformly united with it, until both the substance of the earth dissolved in the bowels and the flowing, dissolving water have become one through a natural union; and all elements come to such a union according to their due and natural proportion, and are mixed through their smallest parts until they make a uniform mixture.

And this mixture, through successive **decoction** <term>decoction: the alchemical process of "cooking" or "digesting" a substance with steady heat to mature it or bring it to a higher state of purity.</term>, is thickened and hardened in the mine and becomes metal. And these theorists also come close to the truth, yet they do not grasp the pure truth. 

Now that we have determined the natural principles of metals in a general discourse, it remains for us to set down a chapter for each principle. Since, therefore, there are three: **Sulfur**, **Arsenic**, and **Quicksilver** <term>original: "Argentum uiuum," literally "living silver"; the liquid metal we know as Mercury.</term>.

First, we will write a chapter on **Sulfur**. Second, on **Arsenic** <note>In medieval alchemy, arsenic was often classified alongside sulfur and mercury as a "spirit" because it could be vaporized by fire.</note>. Third, on **Quicksilver**. Then, for each of the metals which are the effects produced from them, we will set a specific chapter according to how they arise from the work of nature. Then, we must move on to those things which are the foundations of this **mastery** <note>mastery: the "Magisterium," referring to the professional knowledge and practical operations of the alchemist.</note>, and their operations, assigning the causes of all these things.

# THE BOOK
## On Sulfur. Chapter 13:

**WE SAY** therefore, that **Sulfur** <term>Sulfur: in alchemical theory, sulfur represents the "soul" or the active, masculine principle of combustion. It is not just the yellow chemical element, but a spiritualized "fatness" that provides metals with their form and color.</term> is a fatness <note>original: "pinguedo" — a term used by alchemists to describe the oily, flammable substance believed to be found within the earth's minerals.</note> in the mine of the earth, thickened by a tempered **decoction** <term>decoction: the alchemical process of "cooking" or "digesting" a substance with steady heat to mature it or bring it to a higher state of purity.</term> until it is hardened and becomes dry: and when it has been hardened, it is called **Sulfur**. Indeed, **Sulfur** has a very strong composition, and is of a uniform substance in all its parts: because it is homogeneous <note>original: "homogeneum" — meaning it is made of the same kind of matter throughout and cannot be easily separated into different components.</note>: and therefore its oil is not taken from it by **distillation** <term>distillation: a process of heating a liquid to create vapor and then cooling the vapor to collect the condensed liquid, used here to try and extract "oils" from the sulfur.</term>, as it is from other things that possess oil. Therefore, those who seek to **calcine** <term>calcination: the reduction of a solid to a powder by means of intense heat, usually to remove volatile components or "impurities."</term> it without losing anything of its substance worth caring about, labor in vain; for it is not **calcined** except by great industry, and much of its substance is dissipated: for out of a hundred parts, you will scarcely reserve three for yourself after its **calcination**. Similarly, it cannot be fixed <note>original: "figi" — to "fix" a substance means to make it non-volatile, so that it no longer evaporates or changes when exposed to fire.</note> unless it is first calcined; nevertheless, it can be mixed, and its flight <note>original: "fuga" — refers to the substance's tendency to evaporate or "flee" the heat of the furnace.</note> can be somewhat retarded, and its burning can be repressed, and it is more easily calcined when mixed. Therefore, whoever seeks to extract the work from it should not extract it by preparing it by itself, since it is perfected with a mixture, and without that, the **magistery** <term>magistery: from "magisterium," the secret alchemical process or the perfected medicine (like the Philosopher’s Stone) that results from it.</term> is prolonged even to the point of desperation. But with its companion, the **tincture** <term>tincture: a potent alchemical substance capable of penetrating and "coloring" or transforming the nature of base metals.</term> is made, and it gives full weight to each of the **metals** <term>metal: in this context, the author refers to the seven traditional metals (Gold, Silver, Copper, Iron, Tin, Lead, and Mercury) which alchemists sought to perfect.</term>, and purifies it from filth and brightens it, and perfects it with the **magistery**. Without this, it performs none of these things, but rather corrupts and blackens: therefore, do not use it without the magistery itself. Whoever also in the preparation mixes it <insert>with...</insert>

<meta>catchword: to mix</meta>

...knows how to mix it, and unite it with bodies: he will know one of the greatest secrets of nature, and the one way of perfection, though there are many ways to one effect, and to one intention. And whatever body is **calcined** <term>calcination: the reduction of a solid to a powder by means of intense heat, usually to remove volatile components or impurities.</term> from it, undoubtedly gains weight: **copper** <note>original: "æs" — here referring to copper or its alloys.</note> also assumes from it the likeness of **Gold** <note>original: "Sol" — the Sun, representing gold.</note>. When associated with **Quicksilver** <note>original: "Mercurio"</note> and roasted through **sublimation** <term>sublimation: a process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then cooled back into a solid, used to purify substances.</term>, it becomes **cinnabar** <note>original: "usifur" — a medieval term for vermilion or red mercury sulfide, produced by combining sulfur and mercury.</note>. Finally, all bodies are easily calcined by it except for **Gold** and **Tin** <note>original: "Iouem" — Jupiter, the planetary name for tin.</note>. **Gold** indeed is calcined with the greatest difficulty, and **Quicksilver** is not coagulated by it into Gold or **Silver** <note>original: "Lunam" — the Moon, representing silver.</note> in a way that is useful through weak artifice, as some foolish philosophers have thought. 

And so we say, that whatever bodies have less moisture are more easily calcined by **Sulfur** than those which have much. By God Most High, it illuminates every body: since it is light and **tincture** <term>tincture: in alchemy, a substance capable of "dyeing" or transforming the color and quality of a metal, often synonymous with the Elixir.</term>. It is also dissolved with great difficulty: because it does not have salty parts but oily ones, which are not easily dissolved in water. Those things, however, which are easily or with difficulty dissolved in water, we will show clearly enough in the chapter on solution. It is **sublimated** indeed, because it is a **spirit** <term>spirit: a substance that is volatile and can be vaporized by heat.</term>, and if it be mixed with **Copper** <note>original: "Veneri" — Venus, the planetary name for copper.</note> and burned with it, a marvelous violet is produced; likewise it is mixed with **Quicksilver**, and from them through **decoction** <term>decoction: the alchemical process of "cooking" or "digesting" a substance with steady heat to mature it.</term> a celestial and delightful color is produced. Let no one think that **Sulfur** by itself completes any work: for this would be nothing but a vain belief; this however we shall prove clearly enough in the following section. Indeed, that which is thick and clear is to be chosen. And let these words concerning **Sulfur** suffice.

## On Arsenic: Chapter 14

***I***t remains for us to discuss **Arsenic**. For the present, we say that it is made of a **subtle** <note>In alchemy, "subtle" refers to a substance that is very fine, light, or volatile, as opposed to "gross" or heavy matter.</note> matter and is similar to **Sulfur**; therefore, it does not need to be defined differently than Sulfur. However, it differs from Sulfur because it is a **tincture** <term>tincture: a substance capable of "staining" or changing the color of a metal, which alchemists saw as a sign of internal transformation.</term> of **whiteness** <note>original: "albedinis"</note>, and likewise of **redness** <note>original: "rubedinis"</note> quite easily. Sulfur, on the other hand, produces redness easily, but whiteness only with the greatest difficulty. There are two kinds of Sulfur and Arsenic: yellow and red, which are useful for this art; the many other kinds are not. Arsenic is **fixed** <term>fixation: the chemical process of making a volatile substance stable under heat so that it no longer evaporates or "flies" from the fire.</term> just like Sulfur. The **sublimation** <term>sublimation: a process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then cooled back into a solid, used to purify substances from their dross.</term> of both is better when performed from the **calxes** <term>calx: the powdery residue (oxide) left after a metal has been roasted or "calcined."</term> of metals. Yet, Sulfur and Arsenic are not the **perfecting medicine** <note>The "medicine" refers to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone that completes the Great Work.</note> of this work, nor its completion; they are instead a **support for perfection** in certain cases. For use, one should choose the kind that is bright, scaly, and easily split.

## On Mercury: Chapter 15

***Q***uicksilver <note>original: "Argentum vivum"</note>, which is also called **Mercury** by the custom of the ancients, is a **viscous water** found in the bowels of the earth. It is a subtle substance, made of a white earthiness [refined] by a most temperate heat, and united to the whole through a union of the smallest parts, until the moisture is balanced by the dry, and the dry by the moist equally. For this reason, it flees a flat surface easily because of the moisture of its water. It does not stick...

*although it possesses a viscous humidity, because of its dryness—which tempers the humidity—it does not allow it to stick to surfaces. It is also (as some say) the material of metals along with **Sulfur**: it also adheres easily to three minerals: namely, **Saturn** <note>Lead</note>, **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note>, and the **Sun** <note>Gold</note>. However, it adheres to the **Moon** <note>Silver</note> with more difficulty. To **Venus** <note>Copper</note> more difficultly than to the Moon. To **Mars** <note>Iron</note> it does not adhere at all, except through great craftsmanship. From this fact you may indeed draw out a very great secret. For it is friendly and pleasing to metals, acting as a medium for joining **tinctures** <term>tincture: a substance used in alchemy to change the color and quality of a metal, often viewed as its soul or essence.</term>: and no metal is submerged <note>The author is observing density; because gold is denser than liquid mercury, it is the only metal that sinks in it, while others float.</note> in **Quicksilver** except the **Sun** <note>Gold</note>. Nevertheless, Jupiter, Saturn, the Moon, and Venus are dissolved by it and mixed; and without it, no metal can be gilded <note>original: "deaurari" — specifically refers to the process of fire-gilding using a mercury-gold amalgam.</note>. It can be **fixed** <term>fixation: the process of stabilizing a volatile substance so it can endure the heat of a fire without evaporating.</term> and is a tincture of redness, of a most exuberant restoration, shining with a bright glow, and it does not leave the mixture as long as it exists. However, it is not our **medicine** <note>The "medicine" refers to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone in its finished, perfect state.</note> in its natural state, though it can sometimes be helpful in certain cases.*

## On the metallic bodies, which are
## the effects of the principles of nature:
### Chapter 16.

**W**e speak, therefore, of the **metallic bodies**, which are the effects of these principles of nature: they are six in number: **Gold**, **Silver**, **Lead**, **Tin**, **Copper** <note>original: "Aes" — while often meaning bronze, in this list of the six primary metals, it refers to copper.</note>, and **Iron**. We say, therefore, that a metal is a mineral body that is miscible <note>capable of being mixed</note>, fusible <note>capable of being melted</note>, and capable of being extended in every dimension under the hammer. Furthermore, a metal is, as we have said, of a dense substance, of a very strong and firm composition. Metals also have a great affinity for one another: not...

...yet the perfect substance perfects the diminished one through its mixture. For if gold is mixed with lead by melting, the lead does not become gold from this; instead, the lead vanishes from the mixture and is burned away. The gold, however, remains in the testing. In a similar way, in other cases, the result fails according to the common course of nature; but according to our **Mastery** <term>Mastery (magisterium): the specific alchemical process or art used to transform and "perfect" materials.</term>, the perfect substance perfects and aids the imperfect, and we perfect the imperfect in our Mastery by itself without the addition of any foreign thing, and we perfect the perfect by this same Mastery. And by God, they alter each other and are altered, and they perfect each other and are perfected; yet we by no means perfect each of them alone without the assistance of some other thing.

## Concerning the Sun <note>original: "De Sole" — The Sun is the alchemical name for Gold.</note>. Chapter 17.

**W**e have provided you in the general chapter with a summary of the natures of metals, yet we shall now provide a specific discourse on each one. And first concerning Gold. We say, therefore, that Gold is a yellow metallic body, heavy, mute <note>original: "mutum" — In alchemical terms, gold is called "mute" because it does not produce a "cry" or crackling sound (like tin) when bent, and it remains stable and "silent" in the fire without evaporating.</note>, shining, equally digested <note>Alchemists believed metals "ripened" or "digested" in the earth over long periods through subterranean heat.</note> in the belly of the earth, and washed for a very long time by mineral water; it is extendable under the hammer <note>malleable</note>, meltable, and enduring the test of the **cupel** <term>cupel (cineritium): a shallow, porous vessel made of bone ash used in refining to separate precious metals from lead and other base metals.</term> and of **cementation** <term>cementation (cementum): a purification process where gold is heated with "cement" (a mixture of salts and brick dust) to remove silver and other impurities.</term>. From this definition you may certainly conclude that a substance is not gold unless it possesses all the causes and distinguishing features of gold. Nevertheless, whoever yellows a metal from its roots <note>original: "radicitus citrinat" — to fundamentally change a metal's color to the golden hue (citrination) characteristic of the alchemical process.</note>, brings it to equality, and cleanses it, produces Gold from every kind of metal.

<summary>This page defines gold (the Sun) as the most perfect metal, characterized by its weight, color, and ability to survive intense refining processes like cupellation. It also argues that the alchemical "Mastery" can perfect base metals by mirroring these natural qualities.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Gold (the Sun) from the previous chapter. While the previous page defined Gold's physical characteristics, this page explores how nature itself creates gold from base metals and how the alchemist attempts to imitate this natural process.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>
### FIRST [BOOK].

Therefore, through the work of nature, we consider that copper <term>copper (æs): while this can mean bronze or brass, in this alchemical context it refers to the base metal copper, which alchemists believed could be "ripened" into gold.</term> can be changed into gold by its artifice. For we have seen in copper mines, from which water flowed, bringing with it very thin scales of copper, that the water washes and cleanses these scales by its daily and continuous flow. Then indeed, when the water stopped flowing, we saw these scales being "cooked out" by the heat of the sun along with dry sand for a period of three years: among which most true gold was found. We estimated, therefore, that through the benefit of the water they had been cleansed, and through the heat of the sun and the dryness of the sand, the material was equally digested <note>Alchemists viewed the Earth as a kind of stomach where minerals were "digested" or ripened by subterranean heat over centuries.</note> and reached a state of equality. Imitating nature, therefore, when we are able, we similarly alter things, though we are not able to follow nature perfectly in this.

Gold is also the most precious of metals, and it is a tincture of redness <note>original: "tinctura rubedinis" — In alchemy, the highest form of gold or the Philosopher's Stone was often described as a deep red "tincture" that could transform other substances.</note>: because it tints and transforms every body. It is calcined <term>calcined: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder.</term> and dissolved without utility <note>The author suggests that standard chemical dissolution of gold often ruins its specific alchemical potency unless done correctly.</note>: and it is a medicine that gladdens the body in its youth. It is easily broken when combined with Mercury and the scent of lead <note>original: "odore plumbi" — This refers to the vapors or fumes emitted by lead when heated, which make gold brittle.</note>, and it can then be ground down. 

In actual practice, there is nothing that shares more of its nature than Jupiter <note>alchemical symbol for Tin</note> and the Moon <note>alchemical symbol for Silver</note>. In terms of weight, deafness <note>original: "surditate" — Gold and lead are "deaf" because they do not "ring" or resonate as loudly as other metals when struck.</note>, and resistance to decay, it shares with Saturn <note>alchemical symbol for Lead</note>: in color, it shares with Venus <note>alchemical symbol for Copper</note>. In potency, it shares most with Venus, then the Moon, then Jupiter, and then Saturn: and finally Mars <note>alchemical symbol for Iron</note>. And this is one of the secrets of nature. With gold, spirits <term>spirits: volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, or arsenic that "fly" from the fire.</term> are similarly mixed, and they are "fixed" <note>To "fix" a spirit means to make a volatile substance stable so it no longer evaporates when heated.</note> through it with the greatest skill, which cannot be attained by a stiff-necked <note>original: "duræ ceruicis" — A metaphor for a stubborn or arrogant craftsman who refuses to follow the subtle laws of nature.</note> craftsman.

## On the Moon. Chapter 18.

**H**aving previously completed the chapter on the Sun, let us now turn our discourse to the Moon, which is known by the appropriate name of **Silver**. We say that **Silver** is a white metallic body, possessing a pure whiteness; it is clean, hard, resonant <note>original: "sonans" — referring to the clear "ring" silver makes when struck, a test of its purity.</note>, and it endures in the <term>ash-cupel: a porous bowl made of bone ash used in cupellation, a process where precious metals are refined by burning away impurities.</term> Under the hammer it is extendable, and it can be heated and melted. It is, therefore, a tincture of whiteness, and by artifice it hardens <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for the metal tin.</term> and converts it to itself; it mixes with the **Sun** <note>The Sun is the alchemical symbol for Gold.</note> and does not break, but in a test without artifice, it does not remain with it. He who knows how to refine it further, and after refining to thicken it and "fix" it in association with itself, it remains with the other in the "struggle" <note>original: "pugna" — alchemical shorthand for the intense heat and chemical reactions of the furnace.</note> and does not leave it entirely. However, over the vapor of sharp things, such as vinegar, <term>sal ammoniac: a mineral salt (ammonium chloride) used by alchemists to clean metals or initiate reactions.</term> and <term>verjuice: a highly acidic juice made from unripe grapes.</term> a wonderful celestial blue color is produced. It is a noble body, but its nobility is less than that of **Gold**. Its ore is found in specific veins; sometimes it has an ore mixed with other bodies, and that kind is not as noble. It is <term>calcined: reduced to powder through heat.</term> in a similar way and is dissolved with the greatest labor, but with no practical utility.

## On Saturn. Chapter 19.

**L**et us now provide a chapter on **Lead**, and we say that it is a metallic body, livid <note>original: "lividum" — a leaden, bluish-gray color.</note>, earthy, heavy, and mute <note>original: "mutum" — unlike the "resonant" silver, lead makes a dull thud when struck.</note>. It shares in a little whiteness but has much lividity. It flees from the <term>cupel: the ash-vessel used in refining.</term> and from <term>cementation: a process of refining gold by heating it with granulated materials like salt or brick dust.</term> It is soft and easily extended in every dimension with little pressure...

*...malleable, and easily meltable without needing to be heated to redness. As certain fools think, they say that in its nature it very closely approaches Gold. But because they are stiff-necked, devoid of all reason, and unable to recognize any reason in the most subtle things as far as they are able: they judge these things according to their senses. Because they see that it is heavy, and mute <note>original: "mutum" — referring to Lead's lack of resonance or "ring" when struck, unlike silver or copper.</note>, and does not rot, they believe it to be very close to Gold. Through this, therefore, it is clear that **Tin** is more similar to the "perfected" metal <note>In alchemy, "perfected" usually refers to gold or silver.</note>. **Lead** is likewise burned and becomes **Minium** <note>original: "Minium" — red lead, a pigment created by oxidizing lead.</note>, and by placing it over the vapor of vinegar it becomes **Ceruse** <note>original: "Cerusa" — white lead, used as a pigment and in cosmetics since antiquity.</note>. And although it does not closely approach **Silver**, we nevertheless easily form **Silver** from it through our craft; and it does not keep its own weight in the transformation, but is changed into a new weight. And it acquires all of this in the <term>magistery: the master-work or process of alchemical transformation.</term> **Lead** is also the test for **Silver** in the <term>ash-cupel: a porous vessel used to refine precious metals by absorbing oxidized impurities</term>, the reasons for which we shall explain.*

## On Jupiter. Chapter 20.

**N**ot omitting the tradition of **Jupiter** <note>Jupiter is the alchemical name for the metal **Tin**.</note>, *we signify to the sons of doctrine <note>A traditional term for students or initiates of the alchemical arts.</note> that it is a white metallic body, not purely livid <note>original: "liuidum" — a bluish-gray color.</note>, and sounding very little; participating in a little earthiness: possessing by its nature a "cry" <note>original: "stridorem" — a reference to the "cry of tin," a distinct crackling sound heard when a bar of tin is bent.</note>, softness, and a speed of melting without needing to be heated to redness: it does not [endure] the ash-cupel or cement...*

...enduring the hammer, it is malleable. Therefore, **Jupiter** <note>original: "Iupiter" — the alchemical name for **Tin**.</note> is the most perfect among the "diminished" metals <note>In alchemy, metals like tin, lead, and copper were considered "imperfect" or "diminished" compared to gold and silver.</note> because of its root nature; it is related to the **Sun** <note>Gold</note> and the **Moon** <note>Silver</note>, yet more so to the Moon and less to the Sun. This shall be clearly explained in what follows. Because **Jupiter** received a great deal of whiteness from the root of its creation, it therefore whitens bodies that are not white. However, it possesses a defect: it makes every metal brittle <note>original: "frangit" — literally "breaks." It accepts the tincture of redness, shines with an inestimable brilliance, and gains weight in the <term>magistery: the "master-work" or process of alchemical transformation</term> of this art; it is also purified more easily than **Saturn**. Anyone who knows how to remove its defect of brittleness will soon rejoice in its usefulness. For it agrees with the **Sun** and **Moon**, and shall never be separated from them.

## On Venus <note>Copper</note> Chapter 21.

**L**ET our discussion now turn to **Venus** <note>original: "Venus" — the alchemical name for **Copper**.</note>: it is a metallic body, bluish-green and sharing in a dark redness; it is combustible and meltable, malleable under the hammer, yet it flees from the <term>cupel: a refining process using ashes to separate precious metals</term> and from <term>cementation: a process of heating metals with powdered chemicals to refine them</term>. **Venus** certainly (as has been said) possesses the color and essence of gold in the depths of its substance; it can be hammered and heated just like silver and gold. Therefore, you should seek a secret from it, because it is a middle ground between the **Sun** and the **Moon**, and it easily accepts a tincture to be converted to either; it is of good conversion and requires little labor. And it agrees with it...

You should therefore take it before the other imperfect bodies in the minor and middle work; but in the great work, not at all. In this respect, however, it shares a fault with **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note>, since it turns leaden-blue easily and catches a stain from the air, from sour and sharp things; to eradicate that is no easy skill, but rather a profound one.

## On Mars <note>Iron</note>. Chapter 22.

**T**HE account and secret of **MARS** is entirely from the work of nature: for it is a metallic body, very leaden-gray, yet slightly red, participating in an impure whiteness; it is hard, capable of being heated, not meltable by standard melting <note>Iron has a significantly higher melting point than lead, tin, or copper, which made it "stubborn" for early metallurgists.</note>, extensible under the hammer, and very resonant. **Mars** is difficult to work with because of the weakness of its melting; if it is melted without a <term>medicine: an alchemical substance or elixir intended to "cure" a metal's defects</term> that changes its nature, it joins with the **Sun** <note>Gold</note> and **Moon** <note>Silver</note>, and will not be separated by the assay of separation without great effort. But if it is prepared, it joins and is not separated by any artifice, provided its nature of fixation is not changed and only its impurity is removed. It accepts the **tincture of redness** easily, but a good whiteness with great difficulty. And when it is joined without being altered, it does not change the color of what it is joined to, but increases it in quantity. Among all bodies, therefore, **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note> is transformed into a more splendid, clear, and perfect solar and lunar substance...

Therefore, whichever bodies are distant from speed of <term>liquefaction: the process of melting or turning into a liquid state</term> are found to be difficult in the work of <term>transmutation: the alchemical process of changing one substance into another, specifically base metals into noble ones</term> and in handling. Nevertheless, whatever diversities of perfection we determined a little while ago are found in the minor and middle artifice of the work <note>The "minor" or "middle" work refers to partial transmutations or medicinal preparations, as opposed to the completion of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. But in the greater work, all things are of one single perfection, yet they are not of one handling or labor. For there remains that ease or difficulty of handling, and that brevity or length of labor, which were found in the bodies corrupted at their root. 

Now, therefore, we have recorded the natural principles by true tradition—which is according to the intention of the nature of the bodies themselves—and similarly we have truthfully set forth the defining discourses of the bodies themselves in individual chapters, both according to the opinion of those who were able to see into the innermost parts of nature, and according to our own opinion, we who have arrived at these things through persistent labor. Now, however, according to what we have found, it is expedient to supply what is lacking in this art,

There are two things which must be determined: namely, the principles of this mastery, and the perfection of the same. For one method is **Sublimation** <note>The process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then collecting the solid deposit</note>, and another is **Descension** <note>A method of distillation where the extract is forced downward</note>; one is **Distillation**, and one of the methods is **Calcination** <note>Heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder or "calx"</note>, and another is **Solution** <note>Dissolving a solid into a liquid</note>; the sixth is **Coagulation**, the seventh is **Fixation** <note>Making a volatile substance stable so it does not evaporate when heated</note>, and the eighth is **Ceration** <note>Softening a substance until it has a wax-like consistency</note>, about each of which we shall provide an explanation. 

**Perfection**, however, consists of the consideration of the things by which it is attained, and the consideration of the helpful things, and the consideration of the thing itself which ultimately perfects, and the consideration of the thing from which it is known whether the mastery has reached perfection or not. The consideration of those things by which the completion of the work is reached is the consideration of the manifest substance, the manifest colors, and the weight in each of the cleansed bodies, and indeed of those bod- <note>The text cuts off mid-word "corporum" (bodies) to be continued on the next page</note>

## On why sublimation
## was invented.
### Chapter 1.

**C**ONSEQUENTLY, following our purpose, we speak of <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify volatile materials</note>. The reason for its invention was because our ancestors did not find, nor did we, nor those who will come after us, anything that could be united to the [metallic] bodies except <term>spirits</term> <note>In alchemy, "spirits" refers to volatile substances like sulfur, mercury, or arsenic that can be vaporized by fire</note> alone, or something that contains the nature of a body or spirit within itself. They saw that when these spirits were cast upon bodies without being cleansed, they either failed to give perfect colors or they completely corrupted, burned, blackened, and fouled them. This occurs according to the diversity of the spirits themselves. For some are burning, such as sulfur, arsenic, and <term>marcasite</term> <note>A term used for various metallic ores, often iron pyrites</note>, and these corrupt the metal entirely. Others, however, do not burn, such as every kind of <term>tutty</term> <note>zinc oxide</note>, and these give imperfect colors. This is because those that burn also blacken and foul for a twofold reason...

The second cause, truly, is <term>earthiness</term> <note>original: "terreitas," the solid, non-volatile residue or "dross" that does not vaporize</note>, which likewise was not separated from them. In those cases, however, where a perfect color is not produced, the cause is earthiness alone, which creates a <term>livid</term> <note>original: "liuidum," meaning a pale, leaden, or bluish-grey color</note> hue. <term>Adustion</term> <note>original: "adustio," a technical term for scorching or parching by heat</note> can also create a livid color; therefore, we have devised a way to cleanse those that possess it from this burning unctuosity, as well as from all earthy superfluity. Experience indeed makes it clear enough to you through your own sight that they have been freed from it. That scorching is truly removed through sublimation is clear by experiment; for <term>Arsenic</term>, which before its sublimation was impure due to scorching, does not allow itself to be inflamed after its sublimation, but only evaporates without flaming. And you will find this same experiment in <term>Sulfur</term>. Since, therefore, we have seen that nothing but these spirits adheres to the bodies with alteration, we have no choice but to prepare them through their own purification,

# Concerning the diversity of Sublimation, and how 
# it ought to be performed and ordered.

### Chapter 2.

**W**e say, therefore, that **Sublimation** is the elevation of a dry substance by fire with its adherence to its vessel <note>original: "eleuatio cum adhærentia sui uasis" – this refers to the process where a solid is vaporized and then condenses as a solid again on the cooler upper walls of the container</note>. Sublimation is indeed varied according to the diversity of the <term>spirits</term> <note>In alchemy, "spirits" are volatile substances like sulfur, mercury, or arsenic that can be vaporized by heat</note> to be sublimated; for some are done with strong ignition, some with a moderate fire, and others with a gentle fire. 

If, therefore, **Arsenic** or **Sulfur** are to be sublimated, it is necessary for them to be sublimated by a gentle fire. Since they have very subtle parts joined uniformly to coarse ones, their entire substance would otherwise ascend without any purification; indeed, it would be blackened and even burnt. Therefore, so that one might separate the unclean earthy substance, it was necessary to devise methods of two kinds: namely, the proportioning of the fire, and cleansing through the mixing of <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "fæces," referring to inert solid materials like salt, clay, or ash mixed with the chemical to weigh down impurities and prevent them from vaporizing</note>. For the mixing with dregs grips the coarse parts and holds them down at the bottom of the <term>aludels</term> <note>original: "alutelis," technical vessels used specifically for sublimation, often stacked in a column</note>, nor does it permit them to climb. 

From this, it is also necessary for the craftsman to apply a threefold degree of fire to the sublimation. One must be proportioned in such a way that through it, the altered [substances] ascend only...

*...then [they appear] both cleaner and brighter, and through this [the artist] clearly sees them being cleansed from the dreggy earth. The second stage, indeed, is that whatever pure essence remained in the dregs is sublimated with a greater force of fire—namely, by heating the* <term>aludel</term> <note>original: "aludel" – a pear-shaped ceramic pot or series of pots used as condensers in alchemical sublimation</note> *and the* <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "fecum" – the solid residue or impurities left at the bottom of the vessel</note> *themselves until they glow, which the artisan will be able to see clearly with his own eyes. *Therefore, the entire purpose of sublimation is this: that once its earthiness has been removed through the proper application of fire, and the most subtle and smoky part—which causes burning and corruption—has likewise been discarded, we are left with that part which exists in such a state of balance that it simply melts over fire without any burning, yet it flees from the fire without catching flame. That the most subtle part is indeed the cause of burning is proved by the clearest arguments: fire converts everything most akin to its own nature into itself. But of every combustible thing, that which is fine or "subtle" is more akin to fire than the coarse. And that which is even subtler is more akin still. Therefore, that which is most subtle is most akin to fire. This is likewise proven by experience, since* **Sulfur** *or* **Arsenic**, *when not sublimated, catch fire very quickly (Sulfur indeed more easily so). But once sublimated, they do not catch fire directly; instead, they evaporate and thin out if...*

...without inflammation, yet preceded by melting. Thus, it is clear from these things that our discourse is true. The proof, indeed, of the application of the <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "faecum" – the solid, non-volatile material (often a metallic oxide or "calx") added to the vessel to hold onto impurities</note> in their proper proportion is this: that such material should be chosen with which the <term>spirits to be sublimated</term> <note>original: "spiritus sublimandi" – volatile substances like sulfur, arsenic, or mercury that "rise" when heated</note> have a greater affinity, and with which they may be more deeply intermingled. This is because that nature to which they are more closely united is more powerful in retaining the impurities of the things to be sublimated than a nature to which they are not united. The rational proof of this is sufficiently manifest.

The proof, indeed, that the application of dregs is necessary is this: that if the Sulfur or <term>Arsenic</term> <note>original: "Arsenicum" – in alchemy, this usually refers to arsenic sulfides (like realgar or orpiment) rather than the pure element</note> to be sublimated is not joined with the dregs of some <term>fixed thing</term> <note>original: "re fixa" – a substance that resists the fire and does not evaporate or sublimate</note>, then it would necessarily ascend with its entire substance unpurified. Indeed, those who are practiced in sublimations know from experience that we speak the truth. 

The proof that the application of such dregs is necessary—specifically those with which the substances to be sublimated agree and are united in their innermost part—is this: for if the dregs were not mixed down to the smallest particles, then the same thing would happen as if they had no dregs at all; namely, that their whole substance would ascend without any <term>purification</term> <note>original: "mundatione" – the alchemical process of cleansing a substance of its "earthy" or "corrupting" qualities</note>. For if they ascend without dregs with their entire substance, then likewise it is necessary that they ascend when sublimated from dregs with which they are not united. 

Moreover, he who has seen and known this knows by experience that it is necessary; for when he has sublimated from a foreign thing, outside of their nature, he has sublimated in vain, so that he would find the substances in no way purified after their ascent. But if he has sublimated with the <term>calx</term> <note>original: "calce" – a powder produced by heating a metal or mineral (calcination); here used as a stabilizing base to catch impurities</note> of some body, then he has sublimated well, and was able to purify it perfectly and with ease. Therefore, the purpose of the dregs is that they be applied from...

# BOOK

...with the <term>calxes</term> <note>original: "calcibus" – powders or oxides produced by heating a metal until it loses its form</note> of metals. For among them, the process of sublimation is easy, whereas with other things it is most difficult. Therefore, there is nothing else that can take their place. We do not say that sublimation is impossible without the calxes of the <term>bodies</term> <note>original: "corporum" – in alchemical texts, "bodies" almost always refers to the seven traditional metals: gold, silver, mercury, copper, iron, tin, and lead</note>, but rather that it is most difficult, involving a very long labor and a delay leading even to despair. 

But it [sublimation without calxes] has only this much benefit: that what is sublimated without <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "fecibus" – the solid additives or residues that stay at the bottom of the vessel</note>, or without the calxes of the bodies, is of a great quantity. If, however, it is done with dregs, the quantity is less; and even more so, that which is done with the calxes of the bodies results in the smallest amount, but with the easiest and shortest labor. 

That, however, which is most effective as a substitute for the dregs of the bodies is every kind of <term>prepared salt</term> <note>original: "salis præparati" – salts like common salt or sal ammoniac that have been purified or chemically altered for use</note> and things similar to it in nature; and with us, sublimation with salts yields a great quantity, and it is possible to separate the dregs from these same salts by the <term>dissolution</term> <note>original: "solutionem" – dissolving the salts in water to leave the impurities behind</note> of the salts, which does not happen with any other things. 

The proportion of the dregs is such that the quantity of dregs should be proportioned to the quantity of the things to be sublimated. In this even an unskillful craftsman cannot err; but it is sufficient for an average craftsman to provide half the weight of the things to be sublimated from the dregs, and in this he ought not to err. However, for one who is practiced and especially expert, the smallest part of them suffices. For the smaller the quantity of dregs, the greater the abundance of the sublimation must necessarily be—provided that the reduction of the fire is made proportional to the reduction of the dregs. For with a small quantity of dregs, a small fire for the perfection of the sublimation...

->S E C O N D<-

# BOOK

...be produced: in a great [quantity], great; in a greater [quantity], greater. But since fire is not a thing that can be measured, it happens that he who is not practiced in this work very often errs in it, both from the variety in the proportion of the <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "fæcum" – the solid residues or additives used to control the reaction</note>, and also from the variety of the furnace and the burning wood, and similarly of the vessels and their fitting together—concerning all of which it is beneficial to urge the attentive <term>artisan</term> <note>original: "artificem" – the alchemist or chemical practitioner</note> to be diligent. 

But we bring forward a general rule, since first of all it is helpful, with a small fire, to drive out only the wateriness from the substances to be sublimated. Once that moisture is removed, if anything rises through it, then at the beginning the fire should not be increased here, so that by a very weak fire the most subtle part may be removed and set aside separately, which is the cause of <term>adustion</term> <note>original: "adustionis" – scorching or charring, which alchemists believed corrupted the purity of the medicine</note>. 

When, however, nothing or very little now rises—which can be tested by inserting <term>cotton</term> <note>original: "bombacis"</note> or a <term>wick</term> <note>original: "licinij" – a piece of lint or cord used as a probe</note> into the lower opening of the <term>aludel</term> <note>original: "aludel" – a series of pear-shaped pots used as condensers in the sublimation process</note>—let the fire be strengthened under it. The experience with the wick will show how strong the fire ought to be. For if a small amount of the substance to be sublimated comes out clean, the fire is still small; let the fire be increased. If indeed it is much and unclean, then it is excessive; let the fire be withdrawn. But if the proportion is much and clean, then it has already been found. 

Clean or unclean, much or little, can be found by extracting the wick that was placed in the opening of the aludel; for according to the quantity and the cleanliness or uncleanness of the sublimated material the artisan sees adhering to the wick, he will similarly be able to reason out the necessary proportional fire for the whole sublimation. From this, he will find the <term>rectification</term> <note>original: "rectificationem" – the correct adjustment or "straightening" of the procedure</note> of the fire without error. But better...
