## Concerning Dregs and the Furnace. Chapter 3.

**I**t is therefore necessary for us to correct the <term>artisan</term> <note>original: "artificem" – the alchemist or chemical practitioner</note> in those matters where everyone happens to err through ignorance in the <term>sublimation</term> <note>the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again</note> of these two spirits <note>the "spirits" here refer to sulfur and arsenic</note>. We tell him, therefore, that if he uses many <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "fæces" – the solid residues or additives used to control the reaction</note>, and then does not increase the fire proportionally, nothing of the substance to be sublimated will rise; however, how he may find that [proportion] has already been sufficiently told to him. And if he uses a small quantity of dregs, or if they are not made from the <term>calx</term> <note>original: "calce" — a metallic powder or oxide produced by roasting a metal</note> of bodies, then even if he does not find that proportion, the substance to be sublimated will rise with its whole substance <note>meaning it will rise too quickly or without proper purification</note>, and we likewise provide a sufficient discovery of this. 

Errors also happen similarly concerning the furnace. For a large furnace gives a great fire, and a small one a small fire, provided the wood and the <term>ear-holes</term> <note>original: "foramina auricularum" — small ventilation holes or air inlets on the sides of the furnace used to regulate draft</note> are proportioned to them. If, therefore, he puts a large quantity of the thing to be sublimated into a small furnace, it will not be able to provide sufficient fire for its elevation. If, however, he puts a small amount in a large furnace, he will ruin the sublimation because of the excess of fire. Similarly, a thick-walled furnace gives a dense and strong fire; a thin-walled one, however, gives a sparse and weak fire; in which matters it also happens that people err.

If the vents clear themselves freely and well of the smoke and flame, and if the heat passes freely through the whole furnace...

## On the Thickness of the Furnace. Chapter 4.

**T**HE *discovery of the thickness of the furnace is this: if you require a great fire, its thickness should be to the measure of the span of a palm with its thumb <note>original: "extensionis palmæ cum pollice suo" — a measurement of roughly 8 to 9 inches</note>: if however a moderate one, to the measure of a palm alone: if indeed a smaller fire, let it be formed to the thickness of two fingers.*

## On the Proportion of Wood. Chapter 5.

**L**IKEWISE *let the proportion be observed from the wood, because solid wood gives a strong fire, spongy wood a weak one, dry wood a large and quickly ending one, and green wood a small and long-lasting one. Similarly, solid wood gives a long-lasting fire: but spongy wood gives a fire that is abundant and easily ended. Therefore, by considering the distance of the <term>aludel</term> <note>a pear-shaped pot or condenser used to collect vapors during sublimation</note>, and the size and smallness of the vents <note>original: "auricularum" — literally "little ears," referring to the draft holes or flues</note>, and the thickness and thinness of the furnace, and the diversity of the woods, it happens that all variations of fires may be sought through one's own true experience. From the greater or lesser width of the vents or of the furnace window <note>the opening or door of the furnace</note> through which the wood is introduced, and the management of the different types of wood, and the addition and subtraction of them, it happens that a determined space of time for the fire's duration is found—namely, so that certain knowledge may be known of how long each fire can last in its own grade and with steadiness. And this investigation is most useful and necessary to you.*

## On the form of the aludel. Chapter 6.

**T**HE discovery of the <term>aludel</term> <note>a pear-shaped vessel used as a condenser in the process of sublimation, often stacked in series</note> vessel is that it should be fashioned from thick glass; for it would not be effective if made from any other material, unless perhaps it were of a substance similar to glass. For only glass and materials like it—since they lack <term>pores</term> <note>microscopic openings in the material that would allow vapors to leak out</note>—are able to hold the <term>spirits</term> <note>volatile substances or vapors produced during heating</note> so they do not flee and are not driven out by the fire. Other materials are not suitable because the spirits are gradually diminished and vanish through their pores. Nor are metals effective in this, because the spirits, through their "friendship" <note>original: "amicitiam" — refers to chemical affinity or the tendency of substances to bond or react together</note> and their similarity, penetrate into the metals and are united with them; for this reason, they vanish while passing through them. This is clearly proven by necessity through the principles we have established, and experience has found that we have spoken the truth here; therefore, we are not excused from the use of glass in the construction of the aludel by any other material.

Let there be fashioned, then, a round glass bowl, the bottom of which should have a slight curvature, and in the middle of its <term>spondylium</term> <note>original: "spondilium" — likely referring here to the neck or the central joint of the vessel</note> let a glass band be formed, surrounding it on the outside. Upon that band, let a round wall be cast, equidistant from the wall of the bowl, matching the thickness of the cover of the bowl itself, so that the wall of the cover may fit into this gap easily and without pressure. The height of this wall...

## SECOND BOOK

The entire purpose of the vessel is therefore this: that the **lid** can be moved according to the worker’s will, and that the **joint** is so cleverly made that the <term>spirits</term> <note>original: "spiritibus" — in alchemy, these are volatile substances that vaporize when heated</note> cannot find an exit, even without any <term>luting</term> <note>original: "lutatione" — the practice of sealing joints with clay or airtight paste</note>. Whoever can be even more ingenious in this regard should not feel restricted by our tradition. The specific intention here is that the **lower shell**, along with its <term>rims</term> <note>original: "spondilibus" — refers to the ridges or joints where the sections of the aludel meet</note>, should fit halfway into its lid. Since it is the nature of fumes to rise rather than descend, we have found through this invention that the spirits have no way to escape and be lost; this design surpasses other methods we have sought in our research. By experimenting with this, we have seen that our assessment of it was correct.

One objective is to frequently empty the **head of the aludel**, lest the excessive buildup of <term>sublimates</term> <note>original: "sublimandorum" — the purified solid substances that have reformed from vapor at the top of the vessel</note> causes them to fall back down to the bottom. This would keep you occupied for a long time with the need to repeat the sublimation over and over.

Similarly, another objective is that the material which rises as a **powder** near the hole at the head of the aludel should always be separated from that which is found further down. This lower material is often found to be melted, dense, clear, and bright, clinging to the ridges of the vessel. This is because the material at the bottom has suffered less <term>scorching</term> <note>original: "adustione" — over-heating or charring which can spoil the purity of the chemical</note> than that which is found to have climbed near the top opening. This fact is clearly proven by both reason and experience in the preceding sections.

The proof of the quality and perfection of the **sublimation** has already been mentioned: it is that which is found to be clear and bright and does not char when exposed to flame. This, then, is the perfection of the goals of sublimation, specifically regarding **Sulfur** and—

## On the sublimation of Mercury.
### Chapter 7.

**N**OW indeed, let us determine the entire intention regarding the sublimation of **Quicksilver** <note>original: "Argenti uiui" — literally "living silver," the common name for mercury</note>. Its complete aim is therefore the thorough cleansing of its <term>earthiness</term> <note>original: "terreitatis" — the solid, impure, or non-volatile components of a substance</note> and the removal of its wateriness. For we are excused from the labor of removing its combustibility, since it possesses none, but only volatility and superfluous earthiness. We say, therefore, that the ingenious method for separating its superfluous earth is to mix it with things with which it has no affinity, and to repeat its sublimation from them many times.

And the kind of materials used for this are such as the <term>calx</term> <note>original: "calx" — a powder produced by roasting a substance to remove moisture and volatile parts, often called "lime"</note> of eggshells, and white marble likewise, and glass ground very finely, and every kind of prepared salt. For by these it is cleansed; but by others with which it has an affinity—unless they are bodies in a state of perfection, such as <term>Gold</term> <note>original: "Sol" — literally "the Sun," the alchemical name for gold</note> and <term>Silver</term> <note>original: "Luna" — literally "the Moon," the alchemical name for silver</note>—it is not cleansed, but rather corrupted. This is because all such things possess a sulfurous quality which, ascending with the mercury during sublimation, corrupts it. 

And you can see the proof of this in experience: for if you sublimate it from **Tin** <note>original: "Stagno"</note> or **Lead** <note>original: "Plumbo"</note>, you will observe it infected with blackness after the sublimation. Therefore, its sublimation is better performed with those things with which it does not agree. With which...

As this moisture departs, it takes with it the wateriness of the **Quicksilver**, provided the fire is so gentle that the entire substance of the **Quicksilver** does not ignite <note>original: "accendat" — here meaning to be driven off or destroyed prematurely by excessive heat</note>. Therefore, through multiple repetitions of imbibition, grinding, and light roasting, the greater part of that wateriness is destroyed; the remainder of which is removed by repeating the sublimation.

And when you see it becoming extremely white—surpassing snow in its whiteness—and adhering to the segments of the <term>aludel</term> <note>original: "aludel" — a series of pear-shaped pots used as a condenser in the sublimation process</note> like something dead, then repeat its sublimation upon itself without the <term>dregs</term> <note>original: "fæcibus" — the leftover solid residue or impurities</note>. For its **fixed** <note>stable under heat</note> part remains with the dregs and could never be separated from them by any kind of ingenious method. Afterwards, you must fix a part of it; we will clearly deliver the method of its <term>fixation</term> <note>original: "fixionis" — the process of making a volatile substance stable so it no longer evaporates when heated</note> to you in the following sections.

And once you have fixed it, then repeat the sublimation of the remaining part over it, so that it may be fixed in the same way. Observe what you test over the fire: if it yields a good fusion, then you have repeated the sublimation sufficiently for that purpose. But if not, add some part of **sublimed Quicksilver** to it and repeat the sublimation until it becomes <term>fusible</term> <note>original: "fusibile" — capable of being melted</note>. If it then possesses a bright, extremely white, and pure color, you have cleansed it well; if...

## On the sublimation of Marcasite.

### Chapter 8.

**S**ufficiently, therefore, has the main purpose of the sublimation of **Quicksilver** been handed down: let us now proceed to the sublimation of **Marcasite** <note>original: "Marchasitae" — a term used in this period to describe various metallic ores, often iron pyrites or similar shimmering minerals</note>. There are, however, two sublimations of it. The first indeed is completed without <term>ignition</term> <note>original: "ignitione" — the application of strong, direct fire</note>. The other truly with ignition. And that is because it possesses a dual substance. One, namely, is pure **Sulfur** in its matter. The other substance, however, is <term>mortified quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argentum uiuum mortificatum" — mercury that has been "killed" or chemically fixed so it no longer flows or evaporates easily</note>. The first is useful like **Sulfur**; the second is truly useful like mortified quicksilver—namely, that which is moderately prepared. Let us therefore take this latter one, because through it we are spared from the taking up of quicksilver, and from the labors...

...times of its <term>mortification</term> <note>original: "mortificationis" — in alchemy, the process of "killing" or stripping away the external, volatile qualities of a substance to reach its fixed essence</note>. Therefore, the entire method of its **sublimation** is that it should be ground and placed in an <term>aludel</term> <note>original: "aludel" — a pear-shaped earthenware bottle used as a condenser in the sublimation process</note>, and its **Sulfur** should be sublimed without <term>ignition</term> <note>original: "ignitione" — literally "lighting on fire"; here meaning the sulfur should be driven off by heat without actually catching fire</note>: always removing that which is sublimed from it very frequently, for the reason already stated: and by increasing the strength of the fire until the aludel itself glows: and its first sublimation of **Marcasite** should be done in a vessel used for the sublimation of **Sulfur**, until the Sulfur has retreated, and afterward in that [vessel] successively and in order, until everything that consists of Sulfur within it has exited, which you will be able to prove by these manifest experiments. 

For when all of its Sulfur has been lifted, you will see the color of that which is sublimed after it change into a most brilliant white mixed with <term>sky-blue</term> <note>original: "cælestino" — a pale, celestial blue color</note>—the same color being most clear and pleasant. Otherwise, that which is of the nature of Sulfur will be consumed and give off a flame like Sulfur, but that which is truly sublimed second after it will neither be set on fire, nor will it show any properties of Sulfur, but rather those of <term>mortified Quicksilver</term> <note>original: "uiui Argenti mortificati" — mercury that has been processed into a solid, non-reactive state</note> in the repetition of the sublimation. 

Therefore, let us collect that by its own method of sublimation, which is: that a very solid and well-baked earthen vessel be made, to the length of half a man's height: and of a width through which a hand can enter: and let a bottom be made which can be separated and joined, in the likeness of a very deep <term>platter</term> <note>original: "parapsidis" — a dish or bowl; here serving as a detachable base</note>: and let it be measured from the opening of that vessel to near the bottom, to the measure of the length of one hand with its fingers: and from that place up to its head, let the vessel be glazed on the inside with a very thick <term>glazing</term> <note>original: "uitrificatione" — a glass coating applied to ceramic to make it impermeable to vapors</note>, and upon its head let an **alembic** with a wide spout be placed: for in such a vessel...

<meta>catchword: turn</meta>

*The furnace is indeed designed for high heat, the supports of which should be at the height of two cubits* <note>original: "cubitorum duorum" — a cubit is an ancient unit of measurement, roughly the length of a forearm from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger (about 18–20 inches)</note>*, and in the middle let there be a circular grate* <note>original: "rota" — literally "wheel," referring here to the perforated plate that holds the fuel</note> *pierced with very small and very frequent holes, firmly attached with clay* <note>original: "luto" — refers to "lute," a specialized airtight clay used to seal alchemical joints</note>*, but the openings of the holes should be narrow at the top and wide at the bottom, so that ash and coals may fall more freely from them, leaving the holes constantly open for the intake of free air. For the free and abundant intake of air through the lower holes is the sole cause of great heating throughout the furnace; you should practice with this and you will find it to be so. The reason for the length of the vessel* <note>referring to the aludel or sublimation pot</note> *is so that a large portion of it may extend outside the fire and be cooled: so that the vapors of the sublimation* <term>sublimation: the process where a solid substance is heated until it turns into vapor and then collects as a solid again on a cooler surface</term> *may find a place of cooling and adhere to the sides, rather than finding a path of escape and loss. This is known by the one who, while attempting sublimation in a short aludel* <term>aludel: a pear-shaped ceramic pot or series of pots used as receivers in the sublimation process</term>*, found nothing of the sublimated material, because due to the shortness of the aludel, the heat was equal throughout. Therefore, the substance to be sublimated remained constantly <gloss>con-</gloss>turned into the state of vapor and did not stick, but instead vanished successively <gloss>through</gloss> the pores of the vessel. Therefore, in all things to be sublimated, the vessel should be extended for its greater part into a place of cooling. The reason for the glazing* <note>original: "uitrificationis" — referring to coating the ceramic with a glass-like finish to make it non-porous</note> *of the vessel is so that the rising vapors do not find the wall of the aludel to be porous and escape by penetrating through it. Indeed, the place of their ascent is glazed so that their path of flight is blocked; however, the bottom of the aludel is not glazed, because that bottom sits in the great fire, which would melt the glazing; if that were melted, both the bottom and the substance to be sublimated would likewise melt and become glass. For it is the property of glass to melt everything and <gloss>to</gloss> turn everything into itself.*

<note>Technical Terms: furnace, aludel, sublimation, glazing, fire, vessel</note>

And if any of the substance to be sublimated sticks to the hole, it has not yet fully sublimated; but if nothing sticks, it is sublimated. By this same practice, you will be able to be certain in all your sublimations.

## Chapter 9. On the sublimation of magnesia and tutia.

**T**he purpose of the sublimation of **magnesia** <term>magnesia: in alchemy, this usually refers to a specific mineral substance or a "stone" that does not melt, often manganese or a variety of pyrite</term> and **tutia** <term>tutia: also known as tutty, a crude zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces</term> is the same as the purpose for the final sublimation of **marchasite** <term>marchasite: a term for iron pyrites or other sulfide minerals used for their metallic-like luster</term>. For all these substances cannot be sublimated without ignition <note>original: "ignitione" — meaning the application of intense, glowing heat</note>; therefore, they all share one purpose with the same causes and trials. They also follow a single general order, because whatever must be sublimated with ignition must necessarily be sublimated without feces <note>original: "faecibus" — the solid dregs or impurities left at the bottom of a vessel after a process</note>. This is because these substances contain sufficient—even excessive—impurities within themselves, and the sign of this is the difficulty of their sublimation.

## Chapter 10. On the sublimation of diminished bodies.

**A**ll bodies diminished from perfection <note>original: "corpora à perfectione diminuta" — this refers to base metals like lead, tin, or copper, which alchemists viewed as "imperfect" versions of gold or silver</note> are sublimated in a similar way and by this same order. No difference occurs except that the fire for sublimation is more vehement <note>intense or powerful</note> for the metallic bodies than it is for magnesia, marchasite, and tutia.

Furthermore, bodies do not differ in their sublimations except that some require the admixture of a substance that helps to lift them, while others do not; this is done so that their elevation might be made easier. However, one specific consideration and proven practice has been found effective in the sublimation of bodies: namely, that there should not be a large quantity of the body to be sublimated in the bottom of the **aludel** <term>aludel: a pear-shaped ceramic pot used as a condenser in the sublimation process</term>. A great quantity hinders the sublimation. Also, let there be a flat surface in the bottom of the sublimation **aludel**, rather than a concavity, so that the body can be spread equally and thinly over the bottom; thus, all its parts may be elevated equally and in great measure.

<summary>This page explains that certain minerals and base metals require intense heat for sublimation and highlights the importance of using a flat-bottomed vessel to ensure the material vaporizes evenly.</summary>
<keywords>sublimation, magnesia, tutia, marchasite, diminished bodies, base metals, aludel, ignition, impurities</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of sublimation, specifically focusing on the preparation of metallic "bodies" like copper and iron, before transitioning to the next major alchemical operation: descension.</meta>

->[BOOK] SECOND.<-

But the bodies that require the addition of a "lifting substance" <note>original: "rei eleuantis" — a reagent used to help a non-volatile substance sublimate or vaporize</note> are **Venus** <term>Venus: the alchemical name for copper</term> and **Mars** <term>Mars: the alchemical name for iron</term>, because of the slowness of their melting. **Venus** especially requires **Tutia** <term>tutia: zinc oxide impurities gathered from furnace flues</term>, while **Mars** requires **Arsenic**. With these agents, they are easily raised <note>sublimated into vapor</note> because they agree with them most greatly. Therefore, after considering these things, let the sublimation be performed as described for **Tutia** and similar substances, and their sublimation shall be arranged in this same order, following its specific causes and trials.

## Chapter 11. On Descension.

**N**ow that the purposes of Sublimation have been explained along with all their causes, it remains for us to show the method of **Descension** <term>descension: a process of downward distillation or filtration where a substance is melted and flows downward through a filter or hole into a receiving vessel to separate it from impurities or protect it from heat</term>, along with its causes and its determined and complete order. The reason for its invention was threefold.

The first reason is so that when a certain medicine <note>original: "medicina" — in alchemy, this refers to a substance being processed or the "elixir" itself</note> is enclosed in that vessel called a **Descensory** <term>descensorium: a specialized vessel used for downward distillation</term> or **Chimia**, it may flow down through its opening after melting; thus we know for certain that it has undergone fusion. 

The second cause is so that "weak" bodies <note>metals that oxidize or "burn" easily when exposed to prolonged heat, like lead or tin</note> may be preserved from combustion by this descension after they are reduced from their **calxes** <term>calx: the powdery oxide left after a metal has been burned or "calcined"</term>. For when we attempt to reduce weak bodies from their calxes back into metallic form, we cannot reduce every part of them at the same time. If that part which was first reduced back into a body were to wait for the rest of the reduction to finish, a greater quantity of it would vanish <note>vaporize or oxidize</note> due to the fire. It was therefore necessary to devise a way so that as soon as a part is reduced, it is removed from the heat. This is achieved by the **Descensory**.

There is also a third cause for this invention, which purifies bodies from... <margin>The text breaks off here at the end of the page.</margin>

<summary>This page explains how copper and iron are sublimated using additives and introduces the process of "descension," a downward melting technique used to prevent metals from burning away during refinement.</summary>
<keywords>Venus (copper), Mars (iron), tutia, arsenic, descension, descensorium, calx, reduction, fusion, alchemical vessels</keywords>

...from every foreign thing. For the **melted body** <term>corpus fusum: the purified metal or substance resulting from melting</term> descends pure, and leaves every foreign thing in its cavity.

Because of these necessities, now that **Descension** <term>descensio: an alchemical process where a substance is melted and flows downward through a filter or hole into a receiving vessel to separate it from impurities</term> has been discovered, let its method be determined along with its instrument (as previously noted) and its causes.

We say, therefore, that its form should be such that its bottom is sharp, and its walls should end equally and without rough spots into the sharp point of the bottom, so that everything may descend freely without sticking to the hole at that bottom.

And its lid, if necessary, should be shaped like a flat **platter** <note>original: "parapsis" — a shallow dish or bowl used here as a lid</note>, and it should fit it well. It should be made of good and **firm earth** <note>original: "terra firma" — a heat-resistant or refractory clay that does not crack or melt under high heat</note> that does not easily melt under the pressure of the fire.

Therefore, let the substance intended for descension be placed into it upon round rods made of earth, so that it is closer to the upper bottom, and let it be covered with the lid, and the joint be sealed. Then let it be placed in a charcoal fire, and let the fire be blown upon until the entire aforementioned substance descends into the vessel placed beneath it.

However, if the substance is difficult to melt <note>original: "difficilis fusionis"</note>, it can be placed on a flat plate, or one with a small cavity, from which it can easily descend with a slight tilt of the head of the **descensory** <term>descensorium: the specialized funnel-like vessel used for downward distillation</term>. In this way, it will stand more firmly, endure the fire longer, and provide a better **melting** <term>fusio: the act of liquefying a solid by heat</term>. One can test very often by tilting the head of the **descensory** to see if it has melted.

For **bodies** <note>in this context, metallic substances</note> are **purified** <term>purificatio: the chemical process of removing "dregs" or impurities from a substance</term> by this means. But they are purified even better by means of a **pastil** <term>pastillus: a small cake or pellet of material, likely used here as a porous filter or a specific refining mass</term>; the method of purification for this is the same as the method for the **descensory**. Therefore, we are excused from a separate explanation; for the **descensory** holds back the dregs <note>original: "faeces" — the solid impurities or waste left over after melting</term> of the bodies even better. Therefore, we describe its method along with...

Then we mix that powder with something that is not intended to melt, such as **egg white** <note>original: "albumen oui"</note> and **soap** <note>original: "sapo"</note>, and afterwards we melt it.

Instead, it is a useful purification, so that when the **medicine of perfection** <term>medicina perfectionis: a high-level alchemical agent, like an elixir, used to transmute or "perfect" base metals</term> is eventually applied, the bodies may be transformed more effectively and perfectly through it; for this is a treatment that precedes the primary cause.

However, we shall sufficiently describe every treatment to you in the following sections.

# On the threefold distillation, namely by Alembic, Chimia, and Filter.
## Chapter 12.

**I**t is fitting, therefore, as we follow our intended purpose, that we deliver a discourse on <term>distillation: the process of purifying a liquid by heating it into vapor and then condensing it back into liquid</term> along with its underlying causes. Distillation, then, is the rising up of watery vapors within their vessel. Distillation is certainly varied in its practice. For some is done by fire, while some is indeed done without fire. 

That which is performed by fire is found to be of two kinds. One is by "elevation" (rising up) in an <term>alembic: a traditional alchemical still consisting of a boiling flask and a cooling cap</term>. The other is by the "descent" (dripping down) of <term>Chimia: in this context, a specific method of downward distillation</term>, by means of which oil is drawn out from plant matter. 

The general reason for the invention of any kind of distillation is the purification of a <term>liquid: original "liquorosi"</term> from dregs and its own cloudiness, and its preservation from <term>putrefaction: the process of decay or rotting</term>. For we see that a distilled substance, by whatever method of distillation it is prepared, is made purer and is better guarded against decay. 

The specific reason for the invention of distillation by "ascent" (rising) in an alembic is the search for pure water without "earth" <note>In alchemy, "earth" refers to the solid, heavy impurities or sediment that remain at the bottom of a flask</note>. The proof of this is that you can see for yourself that distilled water contains no sediment. The reason for seeking this pure water was for the <term>imbibition: the process where a solid substance soaks up a liquid</term> of many clean medicines. This ensures that whenever we need to soak a substance, we have pure water that does not leave behind dregs after its <term>dissolution: the process of a substance being dissolved into a liquid</term>. Without this purity, the dregs would prevent our medicines and <term>spirits: the volatile, refined essences of a substance</term> from being cleansed; instead, they would be infected and corrupted. 

The reason for the invention of distillation by "descent" (downward flow) was the extraction of pure oil in its natural state, because through the rising method, oil—due to its combustible nature—is often... <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page</note>

<summary>This page defines distillation as the purification of liquids through vapor, categorizing it into methods of "ascent" (rising) and "descent" (dripping). It explains that these processes are essential for creating pure water and oils for medicinal use without the risk of rot or contamination from sediment.</summary>

<keywords>distillation, alembic, chimia, purification, putrefaction, imbibition, spirits, medicinal chemistry, extraction</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the author finishes explaining why certain distillation methods are chosen, moving from the quest for pure water to the specific technical differences between heating methods like ash baths and water baths.</meta>

->BOOK<-

...could not be had; and the inquiry into this was, namely, so that its color, which is mixed with its substance, might be retained, for this can be helpful in certain cases. But for <term>distillation: the process of purifying a liquid</term> which is performed through a <term>filter: a porous material or cloth used to strain liquids</term> without fire, the reason for its invention was the simple clarity of the water. Let us therefore describe every mode of distillation along with its causes.

Of that distillation which occurs by "ascent" <note>This refers to vapor rising upward into the head of the still</note>, there is a twofold mode. For one is performed in an earthen pot full of ashes; the other is indeed done with water in its own vessel, arranged in its proper order with grasses or woolly material, so that the <term>cucurbit: the lower, gourd-shaped vessel of a still</term> or the distillation <term>alembic: the upper cap or "head" of the still</term> is not broken while being brought to completion. 

That distillation which is done with ashes is performed with a greater, stronger, and sharper fire; however, that which is done with water uses a fire that is gentle, manageable, and even. For water does not absorb the sharpness of the fire in the same way that ashes do. Therefore, through the process done with ashes, it happens that colors and coarser "earthy" parts <note>In alchemical theory, "earthy" parts are the heavy, solid, or cloudy impurities of a substance</note> are elevated. But with the process done with water, the parts are more subtle and colorless, approaching the nature of simple wateriness. 

A more refined separation, therefore, occurs through the process performed with water than through that which is drawn out by ashes. Experience has well proven this to be true; for one who had distilled oil by means of ashes received the oil into the receiving vessel barely altered. Desiring, therefore, to separate its parts, he was forced by necessity to distill it by means of water; and then, through the repetition of this process, he separated the oil into its purest <term>elementary: relating to the four fundamental elements (earth, air, fire, water) that compose all matter</term> parts, such that he extracted the whitest and clearest water from the reddest oil, while the redness of that oil remained entirely in the bottom of the alembic. Through this <term>mastery: the Latin "magisterium," referring to the specialized skill or the completed work of an alchemist</term>, therefore, toward the vegetable... <note>original: "uege[tabilis]"</note>

<summary>This page explains the technical differences between distilling via an ash bath (dry heat) and a water bath (moist heat), noting that water baths allow for a more delicate separation of pure, colorless elements from heavy, colored oils.</summary>
<keywords>distillation by ascent, water bath, ash bath, alembic, cucurbit, purification, alchemical oil, magisterium</keywords>

->## BOOK TWO<-

[...vegetable] matter, and that which has proceeded into existence from such vegetable matter, it is necessary to arrive at a precise separation of all the elements. Truly, through the method of distillation by descent <note>original: "per descensum"; a method where the vapor is forced downward into a receiver, often used for extracting oils</note>, one can specifically obtain the oil of any thing, namely of all vegetable substances and those similar to them. Truly, through the method involving a <term>filter: a piece of cloth or porous material used to strain liquids</term>, one can likewise arrive at the clarity of any liquid substance. Now, all these things become necessary and manifest to those who possess even a little knowledge; but whoever is ignorant of this knows nothing of this <term>mastery: the alchemical art or "magisterium"</term>. Therefore, let him practice, and he will easily discover these things.

The arrangement, then, for the distillation performed with ashes is as follows: take a strong pot made of earth and fit it into a furnace similar to a <term>sublimation furnace: a furnace designed to heat solids until they vaporize and collect as purified solids</term>, keeping the same distance from the furnace supports, following the same design, and using similar projections <note>original: "auriculis," literally "little ears," referring to handles or brackets used to suspend the pot</note>. Upon the bottom of this pot, place sifted ashes to the thickness of a finger, and upon these, place the distillation vessel. Cover it all around with those same ashes up to nearly the neck of the <term>alembic: the cap or "head" of the still where vapors condense</term>. 

Afterwards, pour into the vessel the substance intended to be distilled in this manner. Finally, cover it with the alembic, whose neck should receive the neck of the lower vessel inside it tightly, so that the substance to be distilled finds no way to escape. Then, seal the alembic and the lower <term>cucurbit: the gourd-shaped base of the still</term> together <note>original: "lutetur," referring to the use of "lute," a sealant made of clay, dung, or lime to prevent leaks</note> up to the hollow of the alembic's own spout, so that their joint is made firm. Then apply fire beneath it until it distills. The alembic and its cucurbit should be made of glass, and the fire should be increased according to the requirements of the distillation, until everything appears to have been distilled through the strong application of heat.

Now, the arrangement for the second type of distillation, which is done through water <note>This is the "Balneum Mariae" or water bath, providing a gentle, indirect heat</note>, is similar to the first in its use of the vessel and alembic. It differs from it, however, in this: that one takes an iron or bronze pot and fits it to the furnace as previously described; and after...

-># BOOK<-

...on the bottom of that pot, a layer of grass or wool, or something similar, should be built up to a thickness of two or three fingers, so that the <term>cucurbit: the bottom flask of a distillation apparatus, often shaped like a gourd</term> is not broken. The cucurbit should be covered all around with the same grass or similar materials up to nearly the neck of the <term>alembic: the cap or head of the still that collects and directs vapor</term>. Upon these straw-like materials, thin rods should be scattered, and upon the rods, heavy stones should be placed, which by their weight press down the cucurbit, the alembic, and the straw itself. These stones should keep them held firmly and stably against the bottom of the pot, lest they float up when lightened <note>The text suggests the glass vessel becomes buoyant or unstable when surrounded by water.</note> by the water; for this floating would be a cause of the vessel's breaking and the loss of the substance to be distilled. After this, water should be poured among the straw until the pot is full, and a fire should be placed underneath until the whole is distilled.

<margin>☞</margin> Now, the arrangement for the distillation which is performed through descent <note>original: "per descensum"; a method where the substance is heated from above and the extract flows downward</note> is as follows: a glass <term>descensory: a specialized vessel for downward distillation</term> should be made with its cover, and both should be <term>luted: sealed with "lute," a clay or paste used to make joints airtight</term>. Whatever is intended to be distilled in this way is put inside, and a fire is made above the head of it; then its distillation will descend. 

Now, the arrangement for that which is performed through a filter is as follows: the substance to be distilled is placed in a stone <term>basin: original "concha," literally a shell, used here for a shallow vessel</term>, and the wider part of a well-washed and dampened filter is placed into the said liquid down to the bottom of the basin. The thinner part of it should hang outside from the opening of the basin, and beneath the end of that filter, a receiving vessel should be placed to catch the distilled liquid. When the filter begins to drip, first the water with which it was dampened will distill; when that has ceased, the liquid to be distilled will follow it. If this liquid is not clear, it should be returned to the basin as many times as necessary until it distills perfectly clear. Because all these things are easy, they do not require great proof; therefore, we have remained silent regarding their proof <note>The author implies these are standard laboratory procedures that do not need formal logical demonstration.</note>. Now, let the description and the manner of all vessels for any kind of distillation be handed down by us.

## On Calcination Chapter 13.

**A** **FTER** *the account of distillation, then, let us deliver a discourse on Calcination* <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical state or chemical composition, typically reducing it to a powder.</note>. *Calcination is, therefore, the reduction of a dry substance to powder by means of fire, through the removal of the moisture that binds its parts together. The reason for its invention is so that the burning, corrupting, and defiling sulfurousness* <term>Sulphureitas: in alchemy, this refers to the volatile and flammable principle within metals that was believed to cause impurity and decay</term> *may be destroyed by fire. It is varied, however, according to the diversity of things to be calcined. For bodies* <note>metals</note> *are calcined, and spirits* <note>volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, or arsenic</note> *are calcined, and other things foreign to their nature, though with a different purpose. Since there are imperfect bodies of two kinds: the hard ones, namely Venus* <note>Copper</note> *and Mars* <note>Iron</note>, *and the soft ones, namely Jupiter* <note>Tin</note> *and Saturn* <note>Lead</note>, *all of which are calcined, it was necessary for them to be calcined with different purposes, both specific and general. However, the one purpose for which they are calcined is that that burning sulfurousness, which corrupts and defiles, might be destroyed; for every thing containing burning sulfurousness is consumed by fire, which cannot be destroyed without calcination. Because the body itself is solid, and due to the solidity and the hiding of the sulfurousness within the continuity of the substance of Quicksilver* <note>original: "Argenti uiui"; Mercury</note>, *it is protected by it from burning. Therefore, it was necessary to break its continuity* <note>by grinding or initial heating</note>, *so that the fire, reaching any minute part of it more freely, might be able to burn the sulfurousness out of it; and the continuity of the Quicksilver within it should not protect it. And there is similarly a common purpose in it for the purification of earthiness* <term>Terreitas: the "earthy" impurities or dross found in metals</term>. *For it has been found that by the repetition of calcination and their reduction* <note>returning the powdered ash back into a solid metal</note>, *the bodies are cleansed*

*as we write just now in what follows. But the specific* **calcination** <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its physical state, typically reducing it to a powder.</note> *of soft bodies* <note>In alchemy, the "soft" metals are Jupiter (tin) and Saturn (lead).</note> *is such that, along with these two purposes, there is a goal through that calcination to harden and fire them: which is achieved with an ingenious repetition of calcination upon them, concerning which it is expedient for us to define in the following tradition. For we find that by this ingenious method they are manifestly hardened. But Jupiter* <note>Tin</note> *is more manifestly and more quickly hardened. The reason for the invention of the calcination of spirits* <term>Spirits: volatile substances such as mercury, sulfur, or arsenic that "fly" from the fire unless "fixed."</term> *is so that they may be better fixed* <note>rendered stable and non-volatile so they no longer evaporate when heated</note> *and more easily dissolved in water: since every kind of calcined material is more fixed than the uncalcined, and is more easily dissolved, because the calcined parts, having been more refined by the fire, are more easily mixed with waters and converted into water: he who is experienced knows this. But the calcination of other things was a requirement for the preparation of spirits and bodies, about which we will define more broadly in the following. However, none of these things is yet the final stage of perfection. Therefore, the mode of calcination is diverse because of the diversity of the things to be calcined. For bodies* <note>metals</note> *are calcined differently than spirits or other things, and even the bodies differ among themselves. Similarly, they are calcined in different ways. For soft bodies have one general method for the purpose of calcination, namely, that both can be calcined by fire alone, and both similarly whether prepared or not prepared by the sharpness of salt* <note>original: "salis acuitatem"; refers to using salt as a corrosive agent to help break down the metal</note>. *Therefore, it is first accomplished through fire alone in this way: an iron or earthen vessel is taken, formed in the likeness of a platter* <note>a shallow dish or bowl</note>, *the structure of which is firm, and it is fitted to the furnace of calcination in such a way that under it the coals...*

...to be thrown out and blown. Indeed, let the shape of the furnace be the same as the form of the furnace of "great ignition," of which we have already spoken, and of which we shall speak similarly with a fuller discourse.

Therefore, let a fire be lit under that furnace beneath the <term>calcination</term> <note>The process of heating a metal in air until it oxidizes into a powder.</note> vessel, powerful enough to melt the body being calcined. And when the molten body has created a black skin upon itself by the heat of the fire, let it be drawn away from it with an iron or stone shovel—one which does not allow itself to be burned away and thus contaminate the <term>calx</term> <note>The powder or "lime" remaining after a metal has been calcined.</note>. 

Let this scraping continue for so long until the body itself is converted into powder. If it be <term>Saturn</term> <note>The alchemical name for Lead, associated with the slow-moving planet.</note>, let it be placed in a greater fire until its calx is changed into a very bright yellow color. But if it be <term>Jupiter</term> <note>The alchemical name for Tin.</note>, let it be exposed and left until its calx is changed into a complete whiteness. 

In this matter, however, we make the craftsman diligent and careful, because <term>Saturn</term> easily returns from its **calx** back to its metallic body. <term>Jupiter</term>, however, does so with great difficulty. Therefore, lest he happen to err in the exposure of <term>Saturn</term> after its first powdering—namely, at a moderate fire—he must ensure it does not revert to metal before its **calx** is perfected. This is because it requires a moderation of the fire and a succession of gradual increases with caution, until it is fixed in its powdered state, so that it does not easily return to a metallic body; thus, a greater fire can then be administered to it for the perfection of its calx. 

Similarly, let him not happen to err because of the difficult <term>reduction</term> <note>The process of reversing calcination to bring a metal back to its original "body" or metallic state.</note> of **Jupiter**: so that if at any time he should set it for reduction...

...its <term>calx</term> <note>The powder remaining after a metal has been burned or oxidized.</note>, it may happen that he does not reduce it; but if he finds it in its prior state, or reduced into glass, let him then judge its reduction to be impossible. For we say that if, in the reduction of <term>Jupiter</term> <note>The alchemical name for Tin.</note>, one does not apply a great fire, he will not reduce it; but if he applies a great fire, it is not certain that he will reduce it, for it is possible to lead it instead into vitrification <note>The process of turning into glass. Alchemists noticed that overheated metal oxides could fuse with furnace materials or contaminants to form a glassy slag.</note>. And this is because <term>Jupiter</term> has a fugitive substance of <term>Quick-silver</term> <note>Latin: *Argenti uiui*; Mercury. Alchemical theory held that all metals were composed of varying proportions of Sulfur and Mercury.</note> enclosed in the depths of its nature; if it endures a long delay in the fire, this substance will flee, and leave the body deprived of its humidity. It will then be found to be converted to a vitrifying fusion rather than to the fusion of a metallic body. For everything deprived of its own proper humidity gives no fusion except a vitrifying one. And therefore it is necessarily required that he should reduce it quickly with the greatest force of fire and hasten speedily; for otherwise it is not reduced. Therefore, let him who wishes to know this practice himself in it.

## On calcination with salts. Chapter 14.

**B**ut the **M**ethod of Calcination of these two bodies <note>Lead and Tin.</note>, which is perfected by the sharpness of salt, is this: let a quantity of salt be thrown upon their surface, time after time during their fusion, and let them be mixed with much stirring by an iron rod over the fire, until by the mixture of salt the metal is turned into ash. And afterwards, by the same method of perfection, let their <term>calx</term> be perfected according to its requirements. But in this there is also a difference in the calcination of these two bodies, because <term>Lead</term> <note>Latin: *Plumbum*</note> is converted to powder from the first labors of calcination more easily than <term>Jupiter</term> <note>Tin</note>; but not per—

...-ing <note>Completing the word "mantes" or "meditantes" from the previous page's catchword, likely referring to those practicing or "meditating" on these arts.</note> the cause of this depends on the fact that <term>Saturn</term> <note>The alchemical name for Lead.</note> possesses a humidity that is more fixed than <term>Jupiter</term> <note>The alchemical name for Tin.</note>, and a greater earthiness <note>Latin: *terreitatem*; the dry, solid, or "stony" component of a metal.</note> than Jupiter. The method is that they are to be placed as thin plates into a strong firing <note>Latin: *ignitionem*; heating the metal until it glows.</note>. The intention of this is not to melt them, because of the great quantity of their earthiness and the great measure of burning and fleeting sulfurousness <note>The volatile, combustible principle in alchemical theory.</note> they contain; in this way, they are easily brought into a <term>calx</term> <note>The crumbly powder left after a metal is oxidized by heat.</note>.

And this is the reason: since a great deal of earthiness is mixed into the substance of the <term>Quick-silver</term> <note>Latin: *Argenti uiui*; Mercury. Alchemists believed all metals were composed of Mercury and Sulfur in varying degrees of purity.</note>, the continuity of the Quick-silver is disturbed, and therefore a porosity is created within them. Through this porosity, the sulfurousness passing through is able to fly away, and the fire reaching it for that reason is able to burn it and lift it up. By this process, the metal is left behind, its parts become smaller, and it is converted into ash through the disruption of its density. 

The experience of this is manifest: for when a plate of Venus is exposed to firing, it will give off a sulfurous flame and will cause a pulverizable scale to form on its surface. This is because it is necessary for a easier combustion of the Sulfur to occur from its outermost parts. 

Now, the design of the furnace for this calcination is the same as the design of the furnace for distillation, except that it ought to have only one hole at the top, from which it may freely release its fumes. The placement of the materials to be calcined should be in the middle of the furnace, so that they may receive the fire freely from all sides. The vessel itself is made of clay, fashioned in the shape of a <term>platter</term> <note>Latin: *parapsidis*; a shallow, wide bowl or dish.</note>. Thus, the method for the calcination of spirits <note>Volatile substances like sulfur, arsenic, or mercury that "flee" the fire.</note> is such that by them approaching toward fixation...

## On solutions. Chapter 15:

**O**ur discourse having been expanded concerning dissolution, it suggests that the solution of a dry thing is its reduction into water, since every perfection of solution is brought about with subtle waters, and those that are especially sharp, piercing, tart, and bitter, having no dregs—such as distilled vinegar, sour grapes, and pears of great sharpness, and similarly distilled pomegranates, and things similar to these. Now, the reason for the invention of this process was the refinement of those things which have neither melting nor <term>entry</term> <note>Latin: *ingressionem*; the ability of an alchemical substance to penetrate and transform the "body" of a metal.</note>, through which great usefulness was being lost—namely, that of the <term>fixed spirits</term> <note>Substances that have been chemically treated so they no longer evaporate or "flee" when exposed to fire.</note> and those things which are of their nature. For it is necessary that everything which is dissolved has the nature of a salt or an <term>alum</term> <note>A class of mineral salts; in this context, it refers to substances that can bridge the gap between solid metals and liquid solvents.</note>, or something similar to them. Now, it is their nature that they grant melting before their <term>vitrification</term> <note>The process of turning into a glass-like substance under intense heat.</note>; therefore, dissolved spirits will likewise provide a similar melting. Since, therefore, they agree greatly by their nature with the <term>bodies</term> <note>The seven primary metals: Gold, Silver, Copper, Iron, Tin, Lead, and Mercury.</note> and with one another...

# BOOK III

<meta>catchword: in</meta>

Once <term>fusion</term> <note>original: *fusione*; the process of melting a substance into a liquid state.</note> has been achieved, it is necessary for it to penetrate through those bodies, and by penetrating, to <term>transmute</term> them. Truly, reaching the point where they penetrate and transmute cannot be done without mastery <note>original: *magisterio*; referring to the "Magistery," or the expert skill and secret knowledge of the alchemist.</note>. This means that after its <term>sublimation</term> <note>Heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification.</note> and <term>coagulation</term> <note>The thickening or solidifying of a liquid substance.</note>, it should be treated with one of the <term>spirits</term> <note>Volatile substances, like sulfur or mercury, thought to be the "soul" of the material.</note> that has been purified and cleansed, but not <term>fixed</term> <note>A "fixed" spirit is one that has been rendered non-volatile so it no longer evaporates when heated.</note>. It should be sublimated by that spirit many times, until the spirit remains with it, providing it with a swifter fusion and preserving it from <term>vitrification</term> <note>original: *uitrificatione*; the process of turning into glass. In alchemy, this was often seen as an undesirable "dead end" where the material became inert.</note> during its melting. For it is the nature of spirits and bodies prepared in this way not to be vitrified, and to save the mixture from vitrification as long as they remain within it. 

Therefore, a spirit that better preserves its "spiritual" (volatile) nature will better defend against vitrification. A spirit that is merely purified will preserve its nature better than one that is purified, fixed, <term>calcined</term> <note>Reduced to powder by heat.</note>, and dissolved. Therefore, it is necessary to mix it in. From these, a good fusion results, as well as an "entry" <note>The way one substance penetrates the pores of another.</note> and a stable, standing <term>fixation</term>. 

From the works of nature, we can prove that only those things which preserve the nature of <term>salts</term>, <term>alums</term> <note>A specific group of astringent mineral salts.</note>, and similar substances are soluble. In observing all of nature's works, we find no other things that are dissolved except these. Therefore, whatever things are dissolved must be dissolved according to the nature of those substances. But because we see that all truly calcined things are dissolved through the repetition of calcination and <term>solution</term>, we prove by this that all calcined things approach the nature of salts and alums. Therefore, it is necessary that they follow the same properties. 

There are two modes of solution: namely, by **warm dung** and by **boiling water**. These two have one intention and one effect. The method by dung is this: the calcined material is placed...

It introduces the two primary methods of dissolving substances: using the gentle heat of fermenting manure or boiling water.</summary>

...in a glass <term>flask</term> <note>original: *ampulla uitrea*; a rounded glass vessel with a narrow neck, common in alchemical laboratories.</note>, and over it is poured a quantity of distilled vinegar, or twice as much of something similar. Let its head be sealed well so that it does not <term>breathe</term> <note>original: *respiret*; refers to preventing the escape of vapors by making the vessel airtight.</note>, and let it be left under warm <term>manure</term> <note>original: *fimo tepido*; horse manure was frequently used as a natural, steady heat source for "digestion" or slow cooking in alchemy.</note> for three days. Afterward, let the portion that has been dissolved be removed by <term>filter-distillation</term> <note>original: *destillationem filtri*; a method of purification where liquid is drawn through a wick or cloth by capillary action.</note>. That which is not dissolved, however, should be <term>calcined</term> <note>original: *calcinetur*; heated to high temperatures in air to reduce it to a powder or oxide.</note> again, and after calcination, it should be dissolved again in the same way until the whole of it is dissolved through the repetition of the work. Truly, the method that is done by means of boiling water is swifter; it is done so that the calcined material is prepared in the same way with vinegar, with the opening sealed so the substance does not breathe. This should be buried in a <term>cauldron</term> <note>original: *caldario*</note> full of water and straw, just as we have given instructions for the method of distillation by water in its proper order. Afterward, a fire is lit beneath it until the water boils for an hour. Then, let the dissolved part be distilled through a filter and kept aside; that which is not dissolved should be calcined again and dissolved again in the same order, and distilled through a filter as above. Or, let these dissolved solutions be saved and mixed together so that a single distillation may be performed instead, until it is all dissolved through the repetition of the calcinations. The description of what has just been said was already provided above.

## On the coagulation of Mercury and dissolved things. Chapter 16.

**C**OAGULATION, therefore, is the reduction of a liquid thing to a solid substance through the removal of moisture. There are two reasons for its discovery: the first is the hardening of <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: *Argenti uiui*; literally "living silver" or Quicksilver.</note>; the other is the liberation of dissolved medicines from the wateriness mixed into them. It is differentiated according to...

<header>BOOK</header>

# BOOK

...the multiplicity of those things that must be <term>coagulated</term> <note>original: *coagulandorum*; the process of turning a liquid or fluid substance into a solid or semi-solid state.</note>. For one process requires the coagulation of <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: *Argentum uiuum*; mercury</note>, while another requires a dissolved <term>medicine</term> <note>original: *medicina*; in this context, a reagent or a substance prepared to transform other metals.</note>. There is, moreover, a twofold coagulation of Quicksilver itself. One, indeed, is by the removal of all the innate moisture from it. The other, however, is by the thickening of that same moisture until it is hardened. To coagulate it, however, is most difficult and will occur only with great labor; it is a task approached laboriously and with a depth of sharp industry, and we shall describe every <term>technique</term> <note>original: *ingenium*; refers to a clever method, device, or technical skill.</note> of that coagulation.

Indeed, some have thought the technique of coagulation would be through keeping it in a tempered fire; but when they thought they had coagulated it, they found it to flow just as before after removing it from the fire. Because of this, they were struck with stupor and great wonder, arguing that this goal could not be reached. 

Others, however, assuming by necessity from natural principles that any moisture is converted into dryness by the heat of fire, attempted to continue its preservation in the fire with persistent urgency. Through this continuation, they reached the point where some of them converted it into a **white stone**, and others into a **red stone**. Still others converted it into a **yellow** stone which had neither <term>fusion</term> <note>original: *fusio*; the ability to melt.</note> nor <term>penetration</term> <note>original: *ingressio*; the alchemical property of a substance to "enter" and transform the internal nature of a base metal.</note>; and because they could not determine the cause of this diversity, they threw it away. 

Others, attempting to coagulate it with "medicines," did not achieve their goal; instead, a deception occurred, either because they did not coagulate it, or because it was imperceptibly wasted away from them, or because their coagulation did not take the form of any solid body, but rather the form of Quicksilver mixed with **lead**—and they were similarly ignorant of the cause of these things.

<summary>This page discusses the difficult alchemical process of "coagulating" mercury (quicksilver), detailing various failed attempts by practitioners to solidify the metal through heat or chemical additives.</summary>

<header>SECOND</header>

# BOOK SECOND

...reached. Others, however, composing artificial <term>medicines</term> <note>original: *medicinas*; alchemical reagents or prepared substances used to transform matter.</note>, coagulated it in <term>projection</term> <note>original: *proiectione*; the act of casting a transformative substance onto a base metal.</note>; but their coagulation was not useful, since they converted it into an imperfect body and similarly were unable to see the cause of this failure. It is therefore expedient for us to relate the causes of these errors, so that the craftsman may find the mastery of that coagulation.

As we have already sufficiently explained, the substance of **Quicksilver** is uniform; therefore, it is not possible in a short space of time to remove its wateriness by keeping it in the fire. Too much haste, therefore, was the cause of the first error, for since it is of a subtle substance, it recedes from the fire. Thus, an excess of fire was the cause of error for those from whom it flees. 

It mixes easily with **Sulphur**, **Arsenic**, and **Marcasite** <note>original: *Marchasita*; a common mineral, often iron pyrite, used in alchemical operations.</note>, because of a commonality in its nature. Therefore, through these substances, it appears coagulated—not in the form of a solid body, but as Quicksilver mixed with lead. Since these substances are <term>fugitive</term> <note>original: *fugitiua*; volatile materials that vaporize or "flee" when heated.</note>, they cannot hold the Quicksilver in the **battle of the fire** until it reaches the nature of a solid body; instead, they flee along with it through the burning of the fire. This is the cause of error for those who coagulate in this manner.

Likewise, Quicksilver has a moisture very closely united to it, which cannot be separated from it except through the "cautious violence" of fire, or by preserving it in its own fire. Its "own fire" is that which, when increased according to the requirements of what the substance can tolerate, removes its moisture, but without leaving enough of a part for metallic <term>fusion</term> <note>original: *fuſionẽ*; the ability of a metal to melt and flow.</note>. Once that part is removed, it cannot be melted, and this is the cause of error for those who coagulate it into that **stone** which is infusible. Similarly, Sulphur has parts naturally mixed within it...

<summary>This page discusses the common failures in solidifying mercury, explaining that excessive heat causes it to vaporize and that adding volatile minerals like arsenic or sulphur results in an unstable or infusible mass rather than a true metal.</summary>

<header>BOOK THIRD</header>

<meta>catchword: De</meta>

...some more, however, and others less, which it is not possible to remove by **art** <note>original: *artificium*; the skill or practical application of alchemical techniques.</note>. Since, therefore, it is the property of **Sulphur** <note>original: *Sulphur*; in alchemy, the active, masculine principle representing heat and combustion.</note>, when combined with **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Argentum vivum*; the passive, feminine principle representing moisture and metallic essence.</note>, to create a red or yellow color according to the measure of its quantity, the effect of its removal will be to give whiteness to the Quicksilver through fire. 

This is therefore the cause of the variety of its colors after its <term>coagulation</term> <note>original: *coagulatio*; the process of turning a liquid or volatile substance into a solid form.</note> into a stone. It likewise has the earthiness of Sulphur mixed in, by which all its solidifications must necessarily be stained; and this is the cause of error for those who coagulate it into an **imperfect body** <note>original: *corpus imperfectum*; any metal other than gold or silver, such as lead or tin, which was considered "diseased" or incomplete.</note>. From the diversity of the <term>medicines</term> <note>original: *medicinae*; specifically prepared alchemical substances or "elixirs" used to trigger transformation.</note> used for its coagulation, it happens that different bodies are created in the solidification, and similarly from the diversity of that which is to be coagulated. 

For if the medicine—or the substance itself—has **Sulphur** mixed in that is not <term>fixed</term> <note>original: *fixum*; a substance that has been rendered stable and non-volatile, so that it no longer evaporates when heated.</note>, a soft body must necessarily be created from it. But if it is fixed, the body will be hard. If it is white, the result is white; if red, red; and if it is a pale shade of white or red, a pale result must likewise follow. If it is earthy, it will be stained; if not, it will not. Likewise, anything not fixed creates a leaden color; but that which is fixed does not, as far as it is within its power. 

Therefore, a pure substance creates a pure body; an impure one, on the contrary, creates an impure one. This same diversity also occurs in **Quicksilver** alone, without the admixture of Sulphur, because of the different ways it is cleansed and prepared with medicines. 

And so, due to the diversity of the medicine, a deceptive appearance occurs during its coagulation; through it, sometimes **Lead** <note>original: *Plumbum*; the metal associated with the planet Saturn.</note>, sometimes **Tin** <note>original: *Iupiter*; the alchemical name for tin, associated with the planet Jupiter.</note>, sometimes **Copper** <note>original: *Venus*; the alchemical name for copper.</note>, or sometimes **Iron** <note>original: *Mars*; the alchemical name for iron.</note> is produced. This happens because of impurity. But sometimes **Gold** <note>original: *Sol*; the alchemical name for gold, the "Sun" of metals.</note> or **Silver** <note>original: *Luna*; the alchemical name for silver, the "Moon" of metals.</note> is produced, which must occur from purity and the careful observation of colors.

# On the Coagulation of Mercury by precipitation.
## Chapter 17.

*It is coagulated*, therefore, from its frequent precipitation with violence by the harshness of the fire. For the harshness of the fire easily removes its wateriness, and this is done by means of a vessel whose shape is of great length, in which, finding cooling, [the mercury] may find a place for clinging and rest in its <term>spondyles</term> <note>original: *spondilibus*; likely referring to the segments, joints, or ridges in the long neck of the alchemical vessel where vapors condense.</note> along its length, and let it not find a path to escape until, by repeated turns, it is precipitated to the bottom of the vessel by the great heat of ignition, with much reiteration, until it becomes **fixed** <note>original: *fixum*; a substance that has been rendered non-volatile, meaning it no longer evaporates when exposed to heat.</note>. 

It is similarly coagulated by long-lasting retention in its own fire within a glass vessel, the neck of which is of great length and having the shape of a flask in its belly, with the neck kept continually open so that its humidity may afterwards evaporate. 

But it is coagulated through a **medicine** <note>original: *medicina*; in alchemy, a reagent or "elixir" added to a substance to trigger a transformation or "cure" its volatility.</note> suitable to it, and we shall describe that more clearly in the following section. Here, however, we will likewise set forth our full intention regarding it, according to what we have found through our own **experiment** <note>original: *experimentum*; here meaning both a practical laboratory test and the empirical knowledge gained from it.</note>. And that medicine is the one which clings to it most deeply and is mixed through its smallest parts before its "flight" <note>Referring to the mercury's tendency to evaporate (sublime) before it can react.</note>. Therefore, it is necessary to gather [that medicine] from things suitable to it. 

These are, moreover, all its "bodies": **Sulphur** or **Arsenic** <note>original: *Arsenicum*; in this context, it likely refers to arsenic sulfides like orpiment or realgar, which were closely associated with mercury in alchemical theory.</note>; but because we do not see any body in its own nature coagulate it, but rather we see it flee from them—no matter how much they might be of its affinity—we therefore consider that no body clings to it in its depths. Therefore, a medicine of more subtle substance and more liquid fusion is necessary...

-> SECOND [BOOK] <-

...that it is the same substance as that which exists in the metallic bodies themselves. We also observe that a firm and stable **solidification** <note>original: *coagulatio*; the process of turning a liquid or vapor into a solid.</note> of [Mercury] cannot be achieved from **spirits** <note>original: *spiritibus*; in alchemy, these are volatile substances like sulfur, arsenic, or sal ammoniac that vaporize easily when heated.</note> while they remain in their original state. Instead, such a mixture is fleeting and highly contaminated. This happens for two reasons: first, because the spirits flee from the heat; and second, because of the mixture of their earthy and combustible substance.

Therefore, it clearly follows that from whatever **medicine** <note>original: *medicina*; the alchemical agent or reagent used to "cure" or transform a metal.</note> this substance is drawn, that medicine must be of an extremely subtle and pure substance. It must naturally cling to the Mercury with great ease and melt into a very thin liquid—like "fixed water"—when it faces the struggle of the fire. For this very substance will solidify the Mercury and convert it into a **Solar** <note>original: *Solarem*; gold-like or gold-producing.</note> or **Lunar** <note>original: *Lunarem*; silver-like or silver-producing.</note> nature.

We have provided you with the ingenious methods for this medicine through which you can reach your goal, and we have defined the medicine in its own proper terms. Therefore, practice these methods diligently, and you will find it.

But so that you cannot criticize us for providing insufficient detail, we say this: this medicine can be extracted from the metallic bodies themselves when prepared with their own **Sulfur** or **Arsenic**, and likewise from the bodies alone. However, it is found more easily, more directly, and more perfectly from **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Argento uiuo*</note> alone. This is because nature embraces its own nature more kindly and rejoices in it more than in something foreign; furthermore, it is easy to extract that subtle substance from Mercury, since it already possesses an extremely subtle substance in its active state.

The methods for discovering this medicine of Mercury are through **sublimation** <note>original: *sublimationem*; the process of heating a substance until it becomes vapor and سپس collects as a purified solid.</note>, as we have already described sufficiently. The method for its **fixation** <note>original: *fixionis*; making a volatile substance stable so it no longer evaporates under heat.</note> has likewise been described in the chapter dedicated to fixation. Finally, the method for the **solidification of dissolved substances** <note>original: *coagulationis solutorum*</note> is to use a flask placed in ashes up to its neck over a moderated fire, until all the watery moisture evaporates.

## On fixation and its methods, Chapter 18.

**FIXATION** <note>original: *fixio*; the essential alchemical process of making a volatile substance stable so that it no longer evaporates or "flees" when exposed to high heat.</note> is the suitable adaptation of a thing that flees from fire. The reason for the goal of fixation itself is so that every **tint** <note>original: *tinctura*; the coloring principle or agent used to transform the appearance and nature of a metal.</note> and every alteration may be made permanent in the substance being changed and not be lost. This process is diversified according to the variety of things to be fixed: namely, certain **bodies diminished from perfection** <note>In this context, these are "imperfect" metals that have not yet reached the "perfection" of gold.</note> such as **Saturn** <note>Lead</note>, **Jupiter** <note>Tin</note>, and **Venus** <note>Copper</note>; and according to the diversity of the **spirits** <note>original: *spirituum*; volatile substances that vaporize easily.</note> themselves, which are **Sulfur** and **Arsenic** in one degree, and **Quicksilver** <note>Mercury</note> in another. In a third degree are **Marcasite**, **Magnesia**, and **Tutty** <note>original: *Tutia*; an impure form of zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces.</note>; all the aforementioned are dissolved, solidified, and fixed, as are things similar to them.

Therefore, these bodies diminished from their perfection are fixed through their **calcination** <note>original: *calcinationem*; heating a substance to high temperatures in air to reduce it to a powder or "calx."</note>, since they are released by that process from a volatile and corrupting sulfurousness. We have sufficiently shown the method of this calcination in its own discourse. 

However, Sulfur and Arsenic are fixed in two ways: namely, by the repetition of **sublimation** <note>original: *sublimationis*; the process where a solid turns into a gas and then back into a solid, used here to purify and stabilize the material.</note> over them in an **aludel** <note>original: *uase aludel*; a specific pear-shaped alchemical pot or a series of stacked pots used for sublimation.</note> until they "stand" <note>i.e., become stable and cease to vaporize.</note>. From this, the goal of hastening their fixation is achieved, so that you may work toward the discovery of multiple ways of repeating this in a short time. This is done with two aludels, with their double covers, in this order: that they never cease from the act of sublimation until they have been fixed. Therefore, when the substance has ascended into one aludel, let it be cast into the other, and let it be done in alternating turns. Nor should the substances ever be allowed to stick idly to the **joints** <note>original: *spondilibus*; literally "vertebrae," referring here to the connecting ridges or sections where the alchemical vessels fit together.</note> of the aludel, but they should be in a continual rising of the fire.

# BOOK

...until their elevation <note>original: *elevatio*; the process of vapors rising within the flask during heating.</note> ceases: and by however much shorter time you can multiply the frequent repetitions of **sublimation** <note>original: *sublimatio*; the alchemical process of heating a solid until it vaporizes and then collecting the purified solid that deposits at the top of the vessel.</note>, so much more quickly will it be possible to shorten the time of the **fixation** <note>original: *fixio*; the essential alchemical goal of making a volatile substance stable so that it no longer evaporates or "flees" when exposed to high heat.</note> itself.

And for this reason, a second method of fixation was found, which is through the preparation of the substance to be sublimated to its color <note>original: *ad colorem*; likely referring to the specific color stages of the alchemical "Great Work," such as the whitening or reddening, which indicate the material's maturity.</note>, so that it may remain continually in that state until it is fixed: and this is done through a long vessel.
