<header>SECOND BOOK.</header>

[Fixa]tion <note>The word begins with "xio," completing "fixio" from the previous page.</note> truly of **Marcasite** <note>original: *Marchasitæ*; a term used in alchemy for various sulfide minerals, often iron pyrite or bismuth.</note>, **Magnesia** <note>original: *Magnesiæ*; a general term for various mineral substances, not necessarily modern magnesium.</note>, and **Tutia** <note>original: *Tutiæ*; also known as "tutty," this was zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces.</note> is such that when, after their first **sublimation** <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again on a cooler surface.</note>, we have acquired whatever we desire from them, and after throwing away their dross, we should repeat the sublimation upon them. We do this by repeatedly returning that which ascended to the top back to that which remained at the bottom, for each of these substances, until they are **fixed** <note>Stabilized so they no longer vaporize when heated.</note>. The description of the vessels has already been handed down.

## On Ceration. Chapter 19.

**W**AXING <note>original: *Ceratio*; the process of bringing a hard substance to a soft, wax-like consistency so it can be more easily melted or combined.</note>, therefore, is the softening of a hard, non-fusible thing into a liquid state. From this, it is clear that the reason for its invention was so that a substance which lacked the ability to penetrate <note>original: *ingressionem*; the ability of an alchemical agent to enter into and transform a metal.</note> a body—due to its lack of liquidity—might be softened so that it flows and may have entry for the purpose of alteration. Some have therefore thought that **ceration** ought to be performed using liquid oils and waters. But that is erroneous, and entirely removed from the natural principles of this **mastery** <note>original: *magisterij*; the art or "Great Work" of alchemy.</note>, and refuted by the manifest works of nature. For we do not see that nature has placed in metallic bodies themselves a moisture that is quickly ended, but rather one that lasts a long time for the necessity of their softening and melting. For if she <note>Nature</note> had given them such a fleeting moisture, it would necessarily follow that by a single **firing** <note>original: *ignitione*; heating to the point of redness.</note>, the bodies would happen to be totally deprived of moisture very quickly; wherefore it would follow from that, that after one firing, any body could neither be hammered nor melted. Therefore, as we are imitators of the works of nature as much as we can be, it is necessary for us to follow nature's way in **waxing** [ceration].

<summary>This page concludes the instructions on fixing volatile minerals like marcasite and tutia before defining "ceration" as the process of softening hard materials. The author argues that ceration must mimic nature's long-lasting moisture rather than using fleeting oils or water, which would evaporate too quickly during heating.</summary>

# BOOK <note>Likely referring to the Second Book as indicated on the previous page.</note>

It was, indeed, at the very root of the **waxing** <note>original: *cerationis*; the process of softening a substance to a wax-like consistency.</note> of fusible things in moisture, which is above all other moistures in its waiting for the heat of fire. Therefore, it is also necessary for us to use a similar moisture in the process of waxing. In no other things is this moisture found more effectively or more potentially for waxing than in these: namely, in **Sulfur** and **Arsenic** closely, but more closely and better still in **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Argento uiuo*; literally "living silver," the common alchemical name for mercury.</note>. We do not see the moisture of these substances leave their "earth" <note>In alchemy, "earth" refers to the solid, stable component of a substance.</note> because of the strong union they possessed in nature's own work of mixing. 

In all other things possessing moisture, you will find by experiment that the moisture is separated from its earthy substance during dissolution; and after that separation, the substance is deprived of all its moisture. In the aforementioned **spirits** <note>Sulfur, Arsenic, and Quicksilver were called "spirits" because they could be vaporized by heat.</note>, however, this happens least of all. Therefore, there is no other reason by which we might be excused from accepting them for the purpose of waxing. The method of waxing, therefore, is through them: so that their **sublimation** <note>The process of heating a substance into vapor and collecting the solidified deposit.</note> is repeated over the thing to be waxed as many times as necessary,

->perfect **cleansing**,<-

# SUMMARY OF THE THIRD BOOK
## OF GEBER THE MOST INSIGHTFUL PHILOSOPHER
### Preface on the consideration of the things from which the perfection of the mastery is achieved.

**T**herefore, having handed down the discussion of the principles of this **mastery** <note>original: *magisterij*; the "Great Work" or the chemical process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, it is necessary, according to our promise, to pursue the goal of our art completely, in language suited to the task. This consists of the consideration of every thing through which the perfection of this work is more aptly demonstrated; and the consideration of the necessity of the perfection of the **medicine** <note>original: *medicinae*; in alchemy, the "medicine" is the Elixir or Stone that "cures" the "illness" or imperfection of base metals to transform them into gold.</note>—namely, to discuss from what substance that medicine may be more effectively and directly extracted for the perfection of every imperfect thing. We will also consider the chemical operations <note>original: *artificiorum*</note> through which we may recognize whether the perfection is complete. Once these matters are delivered, the entire knowledge of perfection will have been shared, according to the requirements of our art.

### On the essence of Sulfur and Arsenic.
### Chapter 1.

**I**t is truly not possible to understand the transmutations of bodies or of **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Argenti uiui*; literally "living silver," the common name for mercury.</note> itself, unless a true knowledge of their nature, according to their foundational roots, reaches the mind of the craftsman. Therefore, we will first make known the principles of bodies: namely, what they are according to their causes, and what

# B O O K

...[whether they] contain good or evil within themselves. Afterward, we will truly show the natures of all those bodies themselves with all their properties: namely, what the causes of their corruption are, along with their practical demonstrations. Proving first, therefore, the nature of the spirits—which are the principles of the bodies themselves—let us bring them forward: namely, <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: *Argentum uiuum*; literally "living silver," the liquid metal mercury.</note>, <term>Sulfur</term>, and its companion <note>original: *suum compar*; in this context, the "companion" of sulfur refers to Arsenic (orpiment), which was treated as a similar "spirit" in medieval alchemy.</note>.

We say, therefore, that **Sulfur** and its companion are the "fatness of the earth," as we have already narrated above in the chapter on Sulfur: from which experience you may draw a manifest proof through its inflammation and its easy liquefaction by heat. For nothing is inflamed unless it is oily, that is, fatty; nor does it liquefy easily through heat unless it possesses that nature.

**Sulfur**, therefore, and its companion possess a cause of corruption: namely, an inflammable substance and an earthy foulness. Yet they possess a perfecting cause that is intermediate between this [foulness] and that [perfection]. There is, therefore, a cause of corruption in it through this: that it possesses neither <term>fusion</term> <note>original: *fusionem*; the ability to melt or flow when heated.</note> nor <term>ingression</term> <note>original: *ingressionem*; the ability of an alchemical substance to penetrate into the heart of a metal to transform it.</note>.

Likewise, the cause of corruption is an inflammable substance which neither stays <note>original: *stat*; remains "fixed" or stable under heat.</note> nor causes other things to stay, and which provides a blackness from every kind of it. Therefore, the cause of perfection in them is their moderate substance: because, through its earthiness, it is not disturbed from ingression, which is perfected through good fusion; and through its subtlety, its impression is not easily removed due to flight <note>original: *fugam*; volatility or evaporation.</note>. However, their moderate substance is not the cause of the perfection of bodies or of **Quicksilver** unless it be fixed; for since it is not fixed, although its impression may not be mo... <meta>catchword: ueatur (be moved)</meta>

# BOOK THREE

## On the Essence of Mercury. Chapter 2.

**I**n **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Argento uiuo*; also called Mercury.</note> it is likewise necessary to remove what is superfluous. For it contains a cause of corruption—specifically, an earthy and <term>combustible</term> <note>original: *aduſtibilem*; capable of being burned or charred by heat.</note> substance—existing apart from the burning of its wateriness; it is from these impurities that it is purged during <term>sublimation</term> <note>original: *ſublimando*; the process of heating a substance until it becomes a vapor and then collects as a solid on a cooler surface, leaving heavy impurities behind.</note>. Some have thought, however, that it contains no superfluous earth or uncleanness. But their thoughts are in vain. For we see that it possesses much <term>lividity</term> <note>original: *liuiditatis*; a leaden-gray or bluish-black color, considered an impurity compared to the desired "whiteness."</note> rather than whiteness. Furthermore, through a simple artifice, we see black and foul-smelling earth emanate from it through washing. We shall describe the method for this in the chapter on the *Washing of Mercury*; and we have spoken similarly in the chapter on the *Sublimation of Mercury*, and likewise on the *Solution of Mercury*.

But because there is a twofold goal—to draw out its perfection and to actually perfect it—it is therefore necessary to prepare it with a twofold degree of cleansing. Indeed, two cleansings of **Mercury** are necessary. One is through <term>sublimation</term> for the purpose of the <term>medicine</term> <note>original: *medicinam*; the alchemical elixir or "philosopher's stone" in its liquid or powder form used for transformation.</note>, for this is what is hinted at here. The other is through washing for <term>coagulation</term> <note>original: *coagulationem*; the process of turning the liquid mercury into a solid form.</note>, and that is hinted at in the chapter on the medicine of Quicksilver, from which these processes are derived. 

For if we wish to create the "medicine" from it, it is necessary to cleanse it from the foul substance of its earthiness through sublimation; otherwise, during <term>projection</term> <note>original: *proiectione*; the final stage of alchemy where the "medicine" is cast upon a base metal to transmute it into gold or silver.</note>, it will produce a leaden, livid color. It is also necessary to destroy its "fugitive" <note>original: *fugitiuā*; volatile, or likely to evaporate and disappear when exposed to heat.</note> wateriness, lest it makes the entire medicine vanish during projection. One must preserve the tempered substance of the Mercury for the medicine; it is the nature of this substance not to be burned, to defend other things from burning, to not "flee" the fire, and to make other substances <term>fixed</term> <note>original: *fixum*; stable in heat and no longer volatile.</note>. Thus, we prove...

It explains that two types of cleansing—sublimation and washing—are required to prepare Mercury for use in the alchemical "medicine" so that it remains stable and provides the correct color during transmutation.</summary>

# BOOK THREE

*...that it is a perfecting agent is shown by clear experience. For we see that **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Argentum uiuum*; also known as Mercury.</note> adheres most to Quicksilver, and is most friendly to itself; after that, it favors **Gold** <note>original: *Aurum*</note>, and then **Silver** <note>original: *Argentum*</note>. Therefore, it follows that the **Sun** and the **Moon** <note>original: *Solem & Lunam*; the alchemical names for Gold and Silver respectively.</note> share more of its nature. Indeed, we do not see other bodies have such a great conformity to it; thus we truly suggest that they participate less in its nature. Furthermore, whatever we see is better saved from <term>combustion</term> <note>original: *aduſtione*; the process of being charred or oxidized by fire.</note>, we consider to possess more of its nature. Therefore, it follows that Quicksilver itself is perfecting and a savior against burning, which is the ultimate state of perfection.*

*The second degree of its <term>cleansing</term> <note>original: *mū dationis*</note> for its <term>coagulation</term> <note>original: *coagulationem*; the process of solidifying the liquid Mercury.</note> is this: that a mere washing of its <term>earthiness</term> <note>original: *terreitatis*; the heavy, non-metallic dross.</note> through purification should suffice. The method for this is as follows: take a glass or earthen pan, and into it place the Quicksilver. Pour over it a small quantity of **vinegar** <note>original: *robes*; likely a variant of *robs* or a transcription error for a sharp liquid, as the text immediately follows by calling it *acetum* (vinegar).</note> or some similar substance, enough to cover it. Then place it over a slow fire so that it does not boil, and stir it continually with your fingers <note>The author assumes the reader will use their hands; medieval alchemists were often unaware of the extreme toxicity of absorbing Mercury through the skin or inhaling its vapors.</note> against the bottom of the pan, until the Quicksilver is divided into the likeness of a very fine white powder, and until all the vinegar evaporates. When the Quicksilver returns to its liquid form, you will see that a foul and black substance has emanated from it; wash this and throw it away. Repeat this process many times until you see the color of its earthiness change into a bright hue, mixed with a heavenly white color <note>original: *albo cæleſtino*; a "celestial white," indicating a state of high purity.</note>, which is the sign of a perfect washing. When it has reached that state, let the medicine of its coagulation be cast upon it, and it will be solidified into a **Sun-maker** <note>original: *Solificum*; a substance capable of producing Gold.</note> or a **Moon-maker** <note>original: *Lunificum*; a substance capable of producing Silver.</note>.*

<summary>This page describes Mercury's natural affinity for Gold and Silver and details a "washing" procedure using vinegar and gentle heat to remove "black" impurities. This process prepares the Mercury to be solidified into a transmuting "medicine" that can create Gold or Silver.</summary>

...according to how it has been prepared, and we will set forth the account of it in what follows. Therefore, in these matters, it is not possible to follow nature naturally, but rather through our natural artifice.

## On the essence of Marcasite, Magnesia, and Tutty. Chapter 3.

**I**t is necessary for us to provide the natural tradition regarding the other **spirits** <note>In alchemy, "spirits" are volatile substances that can be vaporized by heat.</note>: which are, namely, **Marcasite**, **Magnesia**, and **Tutty** <note>A crude form of zinc oxide collected from the flues of smelting furnaces.</note>, which make a great impression upon metallic bodies. And so, let us state what they are, along with their proofs, in this chapter. We say, therefore, that Marcasite has a twofold substance in its creation: namely, **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Argenti uiui*</note> that has been <term>mortified</term> <note>original: *mortificati*; "killed" or chemically altered so it loses its fluid, metallic form.</note> and is approaching <term>fixion</term> <note>original: *fixionem*; the state of being stable under heat and no longer volatile.</note>, and a burning **Sulphur**. We find from clear experience that it possesses this sulphurous quality. For when it is <term>sublimated</term> <note>original: *sublimatur*; the process of heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then solidifies again.</note>, a clear, burning, sulphurous substance emanates from it; and even without sublimation, its sulphurous quality is similarly perceived. For if it is placed in the fire, it does not receive the heat before it is ignited and burns with the flame of Sulphur. But that it possesses the substance of Quicksilver is sensibly manifested; for it provides the whiteness of pure silver to **Venus** <note>The alchemical name for Copper.</note>, just as Quicksilver itself does, and it provides a celestial color and a manifest brightness in its sublimation...

And therefore, since it is the smoke of white bodies, it does not <term>citrinate</term> <note>original: *citrinat*; to turn yellow or golden-colored.</note> white bodies, but red ones. For yellowness is nothing other than a determined proportion of red and white. Therefore, on account of its subtlety, it penetrates more deeply into the depth of a body, and thus alters it more than its own [original] body does, and it clings more firmly in the small <term>goldsmith’s assay</term> <note>original: *examine aurificio*; a test to determine the purity of gold.</note>, which has already been described to you. For whatever things are altered by the power of Quicksilver or Sulphur, or things similar to these, must necessarily be altered, because these alone communicate in nature with those bodies.

->### THE THIRD [BOOK] ###<-

...[operatio]n of **Sulphur**, a <term>fixed</term> <note>original: *fixa*; stable and resistant to the action of fire.</note> and non-combustible matter, all of it is the essential matter of **Gold** itself. Therefore, a greater quantity of **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Argenti uiui*; mercury.</note> than of **Sulphur** is proved to be in it, because of the easy entry of Quicksilver into it <note>Alchemists believed that because gold dissolves easily in mercury, they must share a common "subtle" substance.</note>. Therefore, whatever things you wish to <term>alter</term> <note>original: *alterare*; to transform or transmute a base metal into a noble one.</note>, alter them according to this example, so that you may bring them to this same equality: and we have already given the method for this. And because it has subtle and fixed parts, those parts were able to be greatly condensed. And this was the cause of its great weight. Truly, through much and tempered <term>decoction</term> <note>original: *decoctionem*; a process of prolonged heating or "digestion" used to refine substances.</note>, a gradual resolution was made from it, and a good <term>thickening</term> <note>original: *inspißatio*; the process of a liquid becoming dense or solid through heat.</note>, and an ultimate mixture, so that it melts when heated.

*From the preceding points, therefore, it is clear that a large quantity of **Quicksilver** is the cause of perfection: and a large quantity of **Sulphur** is the cause of corruption. And uniformity in substance is the cause of perfection, which occurs through thorough mixing during natural decoction: and diversity in substance is the cause of corruption. Furthermore, hardening and thickening, which is perfected through long and tempered decoction, is the cause of perfection; the opposite, indeed, is the cause of corruption. Therefore, if Sulphur should fall upon Quicksilver itself in an improper way, it is necessary that various corruptions be brought about according to the diversity of that Sulphur. For the Sulphur that falls upon it can be entirely fixed and non-combustible, or entirely combustible and **"fleeing"** <note>original: *fugiens*; volatile, or easily evaporated by heat.</note> in the nature of Sulphur; or it may hold part of the fleeing and part of the fixed; holding the nature of Sulphur in one part, but not in another; or it may be entirely clean or unclean, or of a small or large quantity—either dominating in the mixture, or a small amount being dominated within it; or neither dominating nor dominated, or white*...

<summary>This page explains that gold's high density and "perfection" result from a high proportion of Mercury and a long, tempered heating process (decoction). It establishes that while Mercury brings perfection, Sulphur is often the source of "corruption" or impurity in metals depending on its type and quantity.</summary>

## On the Essence of Luna. Chapter 5.

*NOW therefore it is clear from what has been said, that if clean,*
*<term>fixed</term> <note>original: *fixum*; stable and resistant to the action of fire.</note>, clear red **Sulphur** should fall upon a pure,*
*clean, fixed, clear substance of **Quicksilver** <note>original: *Argenti uiui*; mercury.</note>, not over-*
*powering it, but rather being of a small quantity and thus overpowered, pure*
***Gold*** <note>original: *Aurum*</note> *is created from this. If truly it should happen that clean,*
*fixed, white, clear **Sulphur** falls upon a clean substance of **Quicksilver**,*
*pure **Luna** <note>original: *Luna*; the alchemical name for silver.</note> (if it does not exceed in quantity) nonetheless*
*has a diminished purity compared to the purity of **Gold**, and a*
*thicker density than **Gold**. The sign of which is,*
*that its parts are not condensed to such an extent that it would weigh as*
*much as **Gold**: nor does it have as fixed a substance as that. The*
*sign of which is its diminution through fire: and its **Sulphur**, which*
*is not fixed, nor incombustible, is the cause of that. And*
*that the **Sulphur** in it is of this disposition is proved by its*
*preliminary inflammation <note>original: *inflammationem*; the process of catching fire or oxidizing during heating.</note>. Moreover, that a substance is*
*fixed or not fixed in relation to one thing or another is*
*not to be considered impossible. For the sulphureity of*
***Luna*** *when related to the sulphureity of **Gold**, is not fixed and*
*is combustible. But when compared to the bodies of other*
*metals, it is fixed and non-combustible.*

## On the essence of Mars: Chapter 6.

**I**F *truly there should be <term>fixed</term> <note>original: fixum; refers to a substance that has been rendered stable and resistant to the action of fire.</note> earthy **Sulphur**, mixed with earthy **Quicksilver** <note>original: Argento uiuo; mercury.</note>, and both of these are not of a pure whiteness, but are leaden in color: and if the quantity of the excess of fixed **Sulphur** is at its greatest: **Iron** <note>original: ferrum; referred to here by the planetary name "Mars".</note> is created from these, because the excess of fixed **Sulphur** prevents <term>melting</term> <note>original: fusionem.</note>. And therefore from this it follows that the **Sulphur** loses its haste for melting through the work of fusion more quickly than the **Quicksilver**. Indeed, we see that what is not fixed melts more quickly than **Quicksilver**. From these things, therefore, the cause of the speed of melting and its slowness in each of the metallic bodies is made clear. It is not that which has more fixed **Sulphur** that melts more slowly, but rather that which has more of the <term>combustible</term> <note>original: adurente; literally "burning" or "scorching" sulphur.</note> substance that undergoes melting more easily and quickly, which remains for us to prove clearly enough. Moreover, that fixed **Sulphur** makes melting slower is shown by this: that it is never fixed unless it is <term>calcined</term> <note>original: calcinetur; reduced to a powder by high heat.</note>, and once calcined it yields no melting. Therefore, it must impede melting in all bodies. That it is not fixed unless it is calcined is truly evident from the experiment of one who tries to fix it without calcining: for he finds it always "fleeing" <note>evaporating or escaping as a gas</note> until it is turned into earth: the likeness of which is true <term>lime</term> <note>original: calcis; calcium oxide or a similar powdery mineral residue.</note>. This, however, happens least of all in **Quicksilver**, because it can be fixed without being turned into earth, and it can similarly be fixed through its conversion into earth. For by haste toward its fixation, which is accomplished through <term>precipitation</term> <note>original: præcipitationē.</note>, it is fixed and changed into earth. Truly, through its successive and repeated <term>sublimation</term> <note>original: sublimationē; the process of vaporizing a solid and collecting the deposit.</note>...*

<summary>This page explains the chemical composition of iron (Mars), attributing its high melting point to an excess of earthy, "fixed" sulphur. It contrasts the behavior of sulphur and quicksilver (mercury) during the processes of calcination and fixation.</summary>

## On the Essence of Venus. Chapter 7.

**RETURNING** therefore to our subject, we say that if there should be an unclean, <term>fixed</term> <note>original: fixum; a substance that remains stable and does not evaporate when exposed to heat.</note>, and coarse **Sulphur** in its greater part—but in its lesser part a non-fixed, leaden-red, and red Sulphur—and if as a whole it neither overpowers nor is overpowered, and it should meet with coarse **Quicksilver**: then it is inevitable that <term>copper</term> <note>original: æs; while sometimes meaning bronze or brass, in alchemy this specifically refers to the metal of Venus: copper.</note> will be created from it. It is necessary to provide proof for all these things through the data given by their own nature. For when that substance is set to <term>inflammation</term> <note>original: inflammationem; the act of catching fire or being heated to the point of burning.</note>, you will be able to distinguish a sulfurous flame from it; this is a sign of non-fixed Sulphur, and of the loss of its substance through <term>exhalation</term> <note>original: exhalationem; turning into vapor and escaping.</note>. Fixed Sulphur, however, is indicated in that substance by its frequent burning. For from this comes the slowing of its melting and the hardening of its substance: which are signs of a great amount of its fixed Sulphur. Moreover, that it is red and unclean, joined to unclean **Quicksilver**, is shown by the senses; therefore it needs no other proof. Elicit the secret, therefore, by experiment: for you see that everything changed into earth by the action of heat is easily dissolved and returned to the nature of water. This happens because of the <term>refinement</term> <note>original: subtiliationē; the process of making the particles of a substance finer or more "subtle" through alchemical processing.</note> of the parts by fire. Therefore, a thing that is more refined, having been reduced in its own nature to this earthy state, is even more refined, and thus it is more easily dissolved; and so, that which is refined the most, dissolves the most. From these things, therefore, the cause of the corruption and "infection" of these two is clear: namely of **Mars** <note>original: Martis; the metal iron.</note> and **Venus**. This is because it is through a large quantity of fixed and non-fixed Sulphur—a small amount in Venus, but the very least in Mars. Since therefore...

*Fixed sulfur reaches a state of melting through the heat of fire and the refining of its parts by it; seek the suitability of the substance’s dissolution: the sign of which is the exposure of these two bodies to the vapor of vinegar. For by this, the <term>alum-like quality</term> <note>original: aluminositas; a property resembling alum, implying an astringent or salt-like character on the metal's surface.</note> of their sulfur flourishes on its surface, created through the refining produced by the heat within it. And if you place these two bodies into a <term>sharp liquid</term> <note>original: liquorem ponticum; a "pontic" or astringent liquid, usually an acidic or vitriolic solution like vinegar or mineral acid.</note>: many of their parts will be easily dissolved by boiling within it. And if you look into the mines of these two, you will find a clear substance of dissolved alum-like quality dripping from them and clinging to them: which alum-like quality is proven by its <term>sharpness</term> <note>original: ponticitatem; astringency or bitterness typical of salts and acids.</note> and easy dissolution in water. For nothing else is found to be sharp and easily soluble except alum and things of its nature.*

The blackness indeed in each of these two bodies is created in the fire by the <term>non-fixed sulfur</term> <note>original: Sulphur non fixum; sulfur that evaporates or reacts easily, rather than remaining stable under heat.</note>, of which much is enclosed in them, as in **Venus** <note>original: Venere; the alchemical name for copper.</note>; but in **Mars** <note>original: Marte; the alchemical name for iron.</note> there is little, approaching the nature of the fixed: and therefore it is not easy to remove such an impression of blackness from Mars.

Now, therefore, it is clear that from non-fixed Sulfur, melting occurs and is aided; while from fixed Sulfur, melting does not occur and is hindered. He knows this is a necessary truth who, by no cleverness of melting, could preserve Sulfur in a state of fusion after it has been fixed. Truly, he fixed **Quicksilver** <note>original: Argentum uiuum; mercury.</note> by the frequent repetition of <term>sublimation</term> <note>original: sublimationis; the process of heating a substance until it becomes a vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify or "fix" the mercury.</note>, and it thereby took on a good melting property. From this, therefore, it is manifest that those bodies are of greater perfection which contain more **Quicksilver**, and which are less...

## On the essence of Jupiter. Chapter 8.

**H**aving proposed this investigation, and following our purpose, we suggest that if Sulfur exists in the root of the mixture with little <term>fixation</term> <note>original: fixione; the ability of a substance to resist the fire without evaporating.</note>, participating in a whiteness that is not pure—not dominating but rather dominated by Quicksilver that is partially fixed and mixed with an impure white—the result will be **Tin** <note>original: Stagnum; also referred to by the planetary name "Jupiter".</note>. Therefore, a double <term>sulfurousness</term> and a double substance of Quicksilver is proven to exist in Tin. One sulfurousness is that which, when calcined, stinks like Sulfur. ...also another by the continuation of the metal itself at its base to the fire which it holds, because once it is more fixed, it does not smell. Indeed, a double substance of **Quicksilver** <note>original: Argenti uiui; Mercury</note> is proven to be within it. One of these is fixed, because before its <term>calcination</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air to change its properties or turn it into a powder.</note> it does not crackle; but after its triple calcination it does not crackle, which is because the fugitive substance of that Quicksilver that causes the crackling <note>original: stridorem; this refers to the "cry of tin," a distinctive creaking sound made when bending tin, which alchemists attributed to volatile mercury.</note> has evaporated. 

That the substance of Quicksilver is within it is proven by the washing of **Lead** <note>original: Plumbi</note> with Quicksilver: for if Lead is washed with Quicksilver in this way, and after its washing you melt it through that process, with the fire not exceeding the heat of its melting point, there will remain with it a part of the Quicksilver which will bring a crackling to the Lead and convert it into **Tin** <note>original: Stagnum</note>. 

Conversely, you can consider this by the mutation of Tin into Lead. For through multiple repetitions of calcination upon it, and by applying more of the appropriate fire for the administration of <term>reduction</term> <note>The process of returning a calcined powder back into a solid metal.</note>, it is converted into Lead. This happens especially when it is calcined with a great fire through the removal of its **dross** <note>original: scorij; the impurities or slag that separate from metal during smelting.</note>. 

You will be able to be certain of these differences in substances through the methods of their preservation, using proper instruments and the method of fire that divides them; to which we have arrived with persistence, and we have seen with our own certification that we have judged the truth through this. 

Therefore, it is necessary for us to make you certain of what remains after the removal of the two non-fixed parts from **Jupiter** <note>The planetary and alchemical name for Tin.</note>—namely, **Sulfur** and **Quicksilver**—so that you may completely understand the composition of Jupiter itself. ### THIRD BOOK

...and in it there is an equality of density of the two components, namely **Quicksilver** <note>original: Argenti uiui</note> and **Sulfur**, but not an equality of quantity; for in the mixture, Quicksilver prevails. A sign of this is the ease with which Quicksilver enters into it according to its nature. Therefore, if there were not a greater quantity of Quicksilver within it, it would not so easily bond with [additional] Quicksilver in its natural state. And for this reason, Quicksilver does not bond with **Iron** <note>original: Marti; literally "to Mars"</note> except through the most subtle technique, nor with **Copper** <note>original: Veneri; literally "to Venus"</note> due to the scarcity of Quicksilver in their own mixtures. 

From this it is evident that it bonds to Iron with the greatest difficulty, but to Copper more easily, because of the greater abundance of Quicksilver in the latter. A sign of this is the easy melting <note>original: fusio</note> of Copper, whereas the melting of Iron is extremely difficult. 

The <term>fixation</term> <note>The process by which a volatile substance is rendered stable and resistant to heat.</note> of these two substances approaches a firm state, but it is not permanently firm. The proof of this is the <term>calcination</term> <note>Heating a metal until it turns into a powder (oxide).</note> of the body itself: for after calcination and exposure to a strong fire, no separation occurs; instead, the entire substance will ascend <note>original: ascendet; meaning it will sublimate or vaporize</note>, yet it will be more highly purified. 

We see, therefore, that the substance of Sulfur clinging within **Tin** <note>original: Stagno</note> is more easily separated than in **Lead** <note>original: Plumbo</note>. You can see the evidence of this through the easy hardening, calcination, and improvement of the luster of **Tin** <note>original: Iouis; literally "of Jupiter," the planetary name for Tin.</note>. 

And so we consider that these corrupting elements <note>Impurities that cause the metal to degrade or "corrupt."</note> were not present in its root <note>The fundamental or original state of the metal.</note>, but arrived later. And because they were not strongly joined to it in the first mixing, they can therefore be easily separated. For this reason, the alterations in it—namely its cleansing, its hardening, and its fixation—are tasks of quick work. You can clearly consider the causes of these things through what has already been handed down by us.

*And because after these operations, namely <term>calcination</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to a high temperature, but below its melting point, to bring about thermal decomposition or to remove volatile fractions.</note> and reduction, we considered it in its smoke raised by the great application of fire, and we saw that it pertained to yellowness <note>original: citrinitatem; a yellow color often associated in alchemy with the presence of sulfur or a specific stage of transmutation.</note>, since this is a property of calcined **Sulfur**, we judged with a true judgment, of which we are certain, that it contains much of the nature of fixed Sulfur within itself. Therefore, whoever wishes to see the investigation of all these things in this our science, let him study with diligence of work, until he finds the principles of the bodies themselves and the properties of the <term>spirits</term> <note>In alchemy, "spirits" refers to volatile substances like sulfur, mercury, or arsenic that can be vaporized by heat.</note>, with a certain discovery, not a conjectural one, which we have sufficiently handed down in this book according to the requirements of the art.*

## On the Essence of Lead <note>original: Saturni</note>. Chapter 9

**I**T REMAINS for us, therefore, to set down a description of **Lead** <note>original: Saturno; the metal associated with the planet Saturn</note>, and we say that its reduction to its nature does not differ from **Tin** <note>original: Ioue; literally "Jupiter"</note> through the repetition of calcination, except because it has a more impure substance mixed from two coarser substances, namely **Sulfur** and **Quicksilver** <note>original: Argento uiuo</note>, and because the burning Sulfur in it is more adhesive to its own substance than to that of the Quicksilver, and that it has more of the substance of fixed Sulfur in its composition than Tin [Jupiter] does, and we shall bring forward proofs of these things through manifest experiences; therefore, that it consists of a greater part of foul earth than Tin [Jupiter] is shown by sight and by its washing from Quicksilver in its natural state, because more foulness emanates from it through washing than from Tin [Jupiter], and because it undergoes the first degree of calcination more easily than...

**Tin** <note>original: Iupiter</note>, which is a sign of much <term>earthiness</term> <note>In alchemical physics, "earthiness" (terreitas) refers to the presence of the element Earth, which allows a metal to be more easily reduced to a dry powder.</note>. For we find that bodies with more earthiness are easier to <term>calcine</term> <note>The process of heating a metal until it oxidizes and turns into a powder.</note>, while those with little earthiness are more difficult to calcine. Because it has little earthiness, it is difficult to pulverize; the proof of this is the extremely difficult complete calcination of **Gold** <note>original: Solis; literally "of the Sun"</note>. 

And because the foulness <note>The impurities or "crude" elements within the metal.</note> of **Lead** is not corrected by the repetition of calcination in the same way as it is in **Tin**, it is a sign of a greater foulness in its principles—by its very nature—than is found in **Tin**. A similar sign is found in what remains at the bottom of the vessel, which necessarily remains as a sign of one of three things: either that there is no combustible **Sulfur** in it, or very little, or that it is very closely joined to the "root" of the mixture in the proximity of its principles. However, having verified the smoke through its odor, we know it to be of some quantity—and not a small amount, but rather much—because its sulfurous nature is not driven off in a short time. 

Therefore, by the consideration through which the smoke is made certain, we consider that its burning **Sulfur**, together with its non-burning **Sulfur**, approaches the nature of <term>fixed Sulfur</term> <note>Sulfur that has been "fixed" is no longer volatile and does not evaporate when heated.</note>, being uniformly mixed into the substance of **Quicksilver**. Therefore, when its smoke rises, it necessarily rises with the non-burning **Sulfur**, whose property it is to create yellowness. That a greater quantity of non-burning **Sulfur** exists in **Lead** than in **Tin** has been truly demonstrated by us through this...

### BOOK

we see its whole color changed into yellow <note>original: citrinum; referring to the "citrination" stage of alchemy where the substance turns golden-yellow</note>. But that of **Tin** <note>original: Iouis; literally "of Jupiter"</note> [changes] into white, in its <term>calcinations</term> <note>The process of heating a metal to high temperatures to drive off volatile elements and reduce it to a powder or "calx."</note>. And therefore in this a way is opened for us for the investigation of the cause of this work, by which in calcination **Tin** is changed more easily into a hard substance than **Lead** <note>original: Saturnus; "Saturn"</note>. However, it does not [surpass Lead] in the speed of melting; and that is because the cause of true hardness is fixed **Sulfur** and fixed **Quicksilver** <note>original: Argentum uiuum; literally "living silver." In alchemy, "fixed" means a substance that remains stable and does not evaporate when exposed to fire.</note>.

But the cause of melting is twofold: namely, **Quicksilver** and combustible **Sulfur**, either of which suffices for the perfection of fusion <note>melting</note> in each degree of its heating, specifically regarding the **Quicksilver**. Because, therefore, there is a great quantity of **Quicksilver** in **Tin** that is not truly fixed, the speed of its melting remains high, and it is not easily removed from it. 

The cause of softness is likewise twofold: namely, **Quicksilver** and combustible **Sulfur**. Because the burning sulfurousness is removed more easily from **Tin** than from **Lead**, once one of these causes of softness is removed, it must necessarily harden quickly. **Lead**, however, is not easily hardened because it has the causes of both types of softness strongly joined together. 

There is, nevertheless, a difference in softness caused by **Quicksilver** and that caused by **Sulfur**; for softness through **Sulfur** is "yielding" <note>original: cessiua; meaning it yields to pressure or breaks easily</note>, but softness through **Quicksilver** is "extensible" <note>original: extensiua; referring to malleability or the ability to be hammered out into sheets</note>. And this is necessarily proven by this: we see that bodies with much **Quicksilver** possess great extension, while bodies with little [Quicksilver] have little. And therefore **Tin** is extended more easily and finely than **Lead**; **Lead** more easily than **Copper** <note>original: Venus</note>; **Copper** more than **Iron** <note>original: Mars</note>; and **Silver** <note>original: Luna; "the Moon"</note> more finely than **Tin**.

### THE THIRD [BOOK]

I will resolve [this] more subtly than the **Moon** <note>original: Luna; the alchemical name for Silver</note>. It is clear, therefore, that since the cause of hardening is **fixed Quicksilver** <note>original: Argentum uiuum fixum. "Fixed" refers to a substance that has been rendered stable in fire and does not evaporate.</note> or **fixed Sulfur**, the cause of softness will be the opposite. The cause of melting, however, is twofold: namely, **unfixed Sulfur** and **Quicksilver** of any kind. 

But **unfixed Sulfur** is necessarily the cause of melting without ignition <note>melting at low temperatures before the metal glows red</note>, and you can clearly see the experience of this matter through the projection of **Arsenic** <note>original: Arsenici</note> upon bodies that are difficult to melt; for it makes them easy to melt and without ignition. Similarly, **Quicksilver** is the cause of easy melting. However, the cause of melting that requires ignition is **fixed Quicksilver**. 

Therefore, the cause of the hindrance of any melting is **fixed Sulfur**. By this you can certainly elicit a great secret: since bodies with the greatest quantity of **Quicksilver** are found to be of the highest perfection, and those with a large quantity of **Quicksilver** that are nonetheless diminished from perfection must approach perfection more closely. And thus, it happens that bodies with high sulfurousness are of the greatest corruption. 

For this reason, it is clear from what has been said that **Tin** <note>original: Iouem; literally "Jupiter"</note> approaches the perfect state the most, since it partakes more of perfection. **Lead** <note>original: Saturnum</note> partakes less, **Copper** <note>original: Venerem; literally "Venus"</note> even less, and **Iron** <note>original: Martem; literally "Mars"</note> the least, because it depends on the perfecting agent. 

Otherwise, however, regarding the <term>medicine</term> <note>Referring to the "Elixir" or "Philosopher's Stone," the substance used to complete the transmutation of base metals into gold.</note> that completes [the work], supplying what is lacking, thinning the imperfect, and cloaking the thickness deep within the bodies and the dark color beneath the substance of their shining brightness, the situation is different. In this regard, **Copper** <note>Venus</note> is the most capable of being perfected; **Iron** <note>Mars</note> is less so; **Tin** <note>Jupiter</note> even less; and **Lead** <note>Saturn</note> the least.

# BOOK [THREE]

<meta>catchword: bodies</meta>

From these things, therefore, it has been found through a truthful investigation of our labor that, due to the diversity of bodies <note>metals</note>, it is necessary to find various **medicines** <note>original: medicinas; in alchemy, a "medicine" or "elixir" is a substance that "heals" an imperfect metal, turning it into gold or silver</note> through preparation. For a hard body requiring heat <note>original: ignibile; literally "ignitable" or requiring the fire to melt</note> needs one kind of medicine, but a soft and not ignoble body needs another. This medicine indeed works by softening and thinning to the depths, maintaining the substance in its own equality. And that other medicine works by hardening and thickening what is hidden. From these things, therefore, it is expedient for us to pass to the medicines with clear experiences, which set forth for us the causes for the invention of different medicines, and what they leave diminished, and what they lead to completion.

### On the dual medicine of any imperfect body and of Quicksilver, namely the white and the red. Chapter 10.

**W**E PROVE, therefore, that **spirits** <note>original: spiritus; volatile substances like mercury, sulfur, or arsenic that can "fly" from the fire</note> are more assimilated to bodies <note>solid metals</note> because they unite with bodies more closely and more amicably than other things in nature; and nature attracts nature, and like applauds its like. Through this, therefore, it has been brought to us by the first discovery that these spirits are the true medicine for the alteration of bodies. And therefore, with every kind of ingenuity we could muster, we exercised ourselves so that through them we might transform each one of the imperfect bodies, by a firm mutation, into a perfect **Lunar** <note>original: Lunare; Silver</note> and **Solar** <note>original: Solare; Gold</note> body.

Wherefore it happens that a different medicine must be created from the same things, according to the different intention of the things to be altered. Since, therefore, there is a twofold kind of alterable **Quicksilver** <note>original: Argentum uiuum</note>, namely that which can be perfectly **coagulated** <note>original: coagulabile; the process of turning liquid mercury into solid metal</note>, and that which is diminished from perfection in bod...

*...bodies, and these are indeed manifold. Some are hard and lowly <note>original: ignibilia; referring to "base" metals of lesser value</note>, while others are soft, but not lowly. The hard and lowly ones are such as **Iron** <note>Mars</note> and **Copper** <note>Venus</note>. The soft ones are such as **Tin** <note>Jupiter</note> and **Lead** <note>Saturn</note>. It is necessary that the perfecting medicine be similarly manifold. For <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: Argentum vivum; mercury</note> requires one kind of perfecting medicine, while other bodies to be transformed require another. And furthermore, the hard, lowly metals like **Copper** and **Iron** require one kind, while the soft, not lowly ones like **Lead** and **Tin** require another. And since both of these within each genus differ from one another in nature, it necessarily follows that they also require diverse medicines. For **Iron** and **Copper** are of one genus—namely, that of hardness—yet they differ in a specific property. For this one <note>Copper</note> is fusible <note>melts easily</note>, but that one <note>Iron</note> is not fusible <note>Iron required much higher temperatures than early furnaces could easily achieve</note>. Therefore, **Iron** is perfected by one medicine, and **Copper** by another. Also, this one <note>Iron</note> is entirely unclean, while the other <note>Copper</note> is not. And the one partakes of a certain dark whiteness, while the other partakes of redness and greenness <note>likely referring to the green patina or ores of copper</note>; these qualities similarly necessitate a diversity in the medicine. For the bodies of the other genus, characterized by softness, are **Tin** and **Lead**. Since these differ in a similar way, it is necessary that they likewise require diverse medicine. For here **Tin** is clean, but **Lead** is not. Moreover, all four of these mutable <note>changeable</note> bodies are made into perfected bodies, sometimes **Lunar** <note>silver</note> and sometimes **Solar** <note>gold</note>. Therefore, it is necessary that for each there be a double medicine: one that is **citrine** <note>yellow-gold</note>, changing it into a **Solar** body, and the other **white**, changing it similarly into a white **Lunar** body. Since, therefore, for each of the four imperfect bodies there is a double medicine...*

### On the administration and <term>purification</term> <note>original: mundificatione; the process of cleansing a substance of impurities</note> of bodies, varying according to the diversity of their essences. Chapter 11.

**F**ROM the words already spoken by us, it appears what nature leaves behind as superfluous or diminished in each of those bodies which are imperfect, at least in part. However, regarding their full completion—which we omitted in the previous sections—we shall here finish that account entirely with sufficient discourse. First, then, since it happens that there are two kinds of imperfect bodies: [namely] those that are changeable and soft, and not resistant to fire <note>meaning they melt easily</note>, such as Saturn <note>lead</note> and Jupiter <note>tin</note>; and those that are hard and not meltable, or meltable only with intense heat, such as Mars <note>iron</note> and Venus <note>copper</note>. This one [iron] is indeed not meltable <note>In the medieval period, iron was considered "non-fusible" because standard furnaces could not reach its high melting point</note>, while that one [copper] melts with intense heating. Nature, in its forming, has necessarily taught us that because of the diversity of their <term>essences</term> <note>the fundamental nature or "soul" of the metal</note> at the root of nature, we must apply different preparations according to what each requires.

Therefore, there are two necessary bodies of one kind of imperfection to be transformed: namely, Lead, which is called <gloss>black</gloss> <note>original: "melan"; Greek for black</note>, and by the <term>art</term> <note>alchemy</note> called Saturn; and "shrieking lead," which is called "white," and in this science called Jupiter. These are different from one another in the depths of their hidden nature, yet this difference is innate and manifest from their root; for Saturn is dark, leaden-colored, heavy, and black—completely "mute," without any shriek <note>Tin is called "shrieking lead" because it emits a crackling sound, known as the "tin cry," when bent; lead, being softer, is "mute."</note>.

<header>THIRD BOOK</header>

...the other <note>referring to the second type of impurity mentioned at the end of the previous page</note> is impossible to be removed by a <term>medicine</term> <note>original: medicinam; in alchemy, a "medicine" is a substance or elixir used to "heal" or transform base metals into perfection</note> of the first order by any kind of skill. The other, however, happens to be removed with only a small amount of assistance. This first type indeed happens to be impossible to remove because, in the principles of the proper nature of this class of bodies, the impurities were mixed into their true essence and became part of that true essence. And therefore, since it is not possible to remove the true essence of any thing in nature while the thing itself remains, it was not possible for them to delete these corrupting factors.

For this reason, some of the philosophers thought that, because of this, the <term>Art</term> <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy (the Great Work)</note> could not be attained. But we ourselves, searching for this science in our own time, arrived at this same conclusion: that similarly, by no method of ingenious preparation, were we able to brighten the bodies with the completion of their clear luster without it happening that they were entirely stained and completely blackened. 

Because of this, we were likewise led into a stupor and for a long space of time lurked under the shadow of despair. Returning then to ourselves, and torturing ourselves with the immense afflictions of deep thought and meditation, we observed that bodies diminished from perfection <note>i.e., "imperfect" metals like lead</note> exist as foul in the depths of their nature, and nothing bright is found in them, since according to nature it is not in them. For that which is not in a thing cannot be found in it. 

Since, therefore, nothing of perfection is found in them, it necessarily follows that in these same things nothing superfluous <note>unnecessary or extra material</note> is left to be found during the separation of different substances within them, in the depths of their nature. And therefore, through this we found that something had been lacking in them, which necessarily happens to be completed by a medicine...

<sig>K</sig>

<vocab>medicine, essence, nature, bodies, of the philosophers, science, perfection</vocab>

<summary>The author explains that some metallic impurities are so deeply "mixed" into a metal's essence that they cannot be removed without destroying the metal itself. This realization initially led him to despair of the Alchemical Art, until he realized that the "imperfection" is actually a lack of something that must be added via a "medicine."</summary>

# BOOK THREE

## On the specific preparation of Saturn <note>original: Saturnus; the alchemical name for the metal lead</note>. Chapter 13.

**S**aturn and Jupiter <note>original: Iupiter; the alchemical name for the metal tin</note> are therefore prepared through multiple preparations, according to the necessity of bringing them closer to greater perfection, using both a common and a specific method of preparation. The common method proceeds through multiple stages of approaching perfection.

Indeed, one stage of this approach is **Sulfur** <term>original: Sulphur; in alchemy, this refers to the active, combustible principle of a metal, rather than just the chemical element</term> from a clean substance. Another is hardness combined with the ignition of its **melting** <note>original: fusio; the process of liquefying a metal with heat</note>. The third is **fixation** <term>original: fixio; the process of making a volatile substance stable so it can withstand fire without evaporating</term> through the removal of the fugitive <note>volatile</note> substance.

They are therefore cleansed and made shining in three ways: either by cleansing agents, or by the method of **calcination** <term>original: calcinatio; heating a metal until it turns into a fine powder or oxide, known as "lime"</term> and **reduction** <term>original: reductio; the process of turning a powder or "lime" back into a solid metal</term>, or by **solution** <note>dissolving the metal in a liquid</note>. 

Through purifying agents, they are cleansed in two ways: either when reduced into **lime** <note>original: calx; the powdery residue left after burning a metal</note>, or in their natural metallic state. When reduced to lime, they are purified in this manner: either through **salts** and **alums** <term>original: alumina; a group of minerals used for their astringent and purifying properties</term>, or through **glass**. 

The process is this: when the metallic body—namely, Jupiter and Saturn—has been calcined, then water of alums or salts is poured over their lime; or, crushed glass is mixed into it, and it is reduced back into a body. This should be repeated over these bodies alternately until they show themselves to be completely clean. For since salts, alums, and glass melt by a different fusion than the metals, they are separated from them, and they carry the earthy substance away with them, leaving only the purified metallic body behind. 

In their natural metallic state, they are purified similarly and by the same method. It is done by filing these two bodies into very fine shavings; after this, they are treated with the same alums, salts, and glass, and afterward reduced back into a metallic body.

# BOOK THREE

And thus, let the process be repeated in alternating turns until they appear cleaner. They are also cleansed by a wash of **Quicksilver** <note>original: Argentum vivum; mercury</note>, the method of which we have already provided. Truly, these bodies of both kinds <note>referring to lead and tin</note> are similarly cleansed by repeating the process of **calcination** <term>original: calcinatio; heating a metal until it turns into a powder</term> upon them, and likewise by **reduction** <term>original: reductio; restoring the metal from its powdered state back into a solid mass</term> with a sufficient amount of fire, until they appear cleaner.

For by this means, these diminished bodies are cleansed of a double corrupting substance: one which is inflammable and **fugitive** <note>volatile; easily evaporated by heat</note>, and the other which is foul and earthy. This is because fire lifts up and consumes every fugitive substance, and likewise, in the process of reduction, the fire separates all the earthy substance according to its proportion. 

We have written about this proportion in our other volume, entitled *The Investigation of Perfection* <note>original: De investigatione perfectionis; a foundational alchemical text often attributed to Geber</note>, which precedes this book in the proper order. For in that volume, we wrote whatever we investigated according to the reasoning of our own mind; but here, we have fully defined what we have seen and touched according to the order of the science.

They are also certainly cleansed through the **solution** <note>dissolving the metal into liquid</note> of their substance, the method of which we have already stated, and likewise by the reduction of that which was dissolved from them. For by this same method of preparation, the resulting metal is found to be cleaner and more perfect than by any other kind of preparation. No other method can be compared to this one, except that which is perfected through **sublimation** <term>original: sublimatio; the process of heating a substance into vapor and collecting it again as a purified solid</term>, and therefore that method is of equal value to this one.

There is also a similar preparation for these metals: the hardening of their soft substance through the ignition of their **melting** <note>original: fusio</note>. This is done so that we may contrive to mix into them the fixed substance of Quicksilver in the depths...

## On the preparation of Mars. Chapter 16.

The preparation of **Mars** <term>Iron</term> is likewise manifold. For some is completed through **sublimation** <term>the process of vaporizing a substance and collecting the solid deposit</term>, while some is done without sublimation. That which is performed through sublimation is done with **Arsenic**, the method of which is this: let us devise a way, as deeply as we can, to unite non-fixed <note>original: non fixum; refers to a substance that is volatile and evaporates easily when heated</note> Arsenic with it, so that it may melt together with it during fusion. Afterward, let it be sublimated in a vessel appropriate for sublimation; this preparation is found to be the best and most perfect among the others.

There is also another preparation of this **Mars** <term>Iron</term>, by means of sublimated **Arsenic**, [repeating] many times until some quantity of the Arsenic itself remains within it. For if this is reduced <term>returned from a powder or oxide back to its metallic form</term>, it will emerge white, meltable, clean, and prepared. There is similarly a third way of preparing it, by melting it with **Lead** and **Tutia** <term>zinc oxide collected from furnace flues</term>; for from these it flows forth clean and white.

And indeed, all these alterations are of the first order, without which the **Mastery** <term>the Great Work or "Magisterium" of alchemy</term> is not completed.

*...[of] superfluous sulfurousness <note>original: sulphureitas; in alchemy, this refers to the active, combustible, and often "corrupting" oily principle within a metal</note>, and all unclean earthiness <note>original: terreitas; the solid, dross-like impurities</note>, so that, specifically, it may be separated from the mixture in melting <term>fusion</term>, after the projection <note>The act of casting a small amount of the "medicine" or elixir onto a larger mass of molten base metal to effect transformation</note> of the medicine that alters those things. And when you have indeed found this, you now possess one of the differences of perfection. Likewise, therefore, unless the medicine illuminates and alters [the metal] into a white color, or into a lemon-yellow <term>citrinum</term> color, according to the intention you seek—which brings forth a clarity of pleasantness by the brightness of its glow—those bodies diminished from perfection are not completely brought to their fulfillment. Furthermore, unless it brings about a specific Lunar <note>Referring to silver</note> or Solar <note>Referring to gold</note> fusion, it is not alterable in its completion, because it does not remain stable in the tests <note>original: iudicijs; literally "judgments," referring to the trials by fire or acid used to test the purity of metals</note>, but is entirely separated from the mixture and departs. This, moreover, which has been more broadly determined by us, is shown in the chapter on ash-testing <note>original: cineritio; also known as cupellation, a refining process where metals are heated in a vessel of bone ash to separate gold and silver from base impurities</note>. Furthermore, unless the medicine is made permanent with a firm alteration of its impression, its change is of no value: because the impression does not remain but vanishes. Furthermore, also, unless the weights of perfection are brought forth, it does not change under a firm and true completion of nature, in which there is no fraud through the error of belief. For the weight of nature is one of the signs of perfection. It is clear, therefore, that since there are five differences of perfection, it is necessary for the medicine of our mastery <term>magisterij</term> to bring about these five differences in perfection. Through this, therefore, it is clear from what things our medicine is drawn. For it is drawn from those things which are most closely joined to the bodies, and, being altering substances, adhere to them in their depths in a friendly manner. And so, seeking it in other things, we have not found in our discovery any other thing more than Quicksilver* <note>original: Argentum uiuum</note>

Geber identifies Quicksilver (Mercury) as the ideal substance for this work because of its unique ability to bond with other metals.</summary>

->BOOK FOUR<-

...[quicksilver] <note>original: uiuum</note>, is befriended by the natures of bodies in its own nature, from which it is mixed with them, and afterward is fixed with a slow fire. By expending our work upon this, we find it to be a true medicine of alterations in its fulfillment, providing a true and remarkably precious transformation.

## On the preparation of the substance of Quicksilver in the projection of the medicine. Chapter 1.

IT REMAINS, therefore, for us to describe its substance alone, the properties of that substance, and its differences in a specific manner. And since we have not found it to change without the management of its natural alteration, we have likewise found that it must necessarily be prepared, as it cannot be mixed in the depth <note>deeply or thoroughly within the atomic/spiritual structure of the metal</note> without that specific method of preparation. 

And the goal is, namely, that its substance becomes such that it may be mixed in the depth even to the hidden parts of the body to be altered, without ever being separated again. This, however, does not happen unless it is greatly refined <term>subtilized</term> through the specific and certain preparation described in the chapter on sublimation <note>The process of heating a substance into a vapor and then cooling it back into a solid to purify it</note>. 

Likewise, its impression <note>the transformative effect or "stamp" it leaves on the base metal</note> does not remain unless it is fixed <term>fixation</term>, nor does it illuminate unless the most radiant substance is drawn out from it, through the skill of its own method and its manner of operation by a suitable fire. 

Furthermore, it does not provide a perfect melting <term>fusion</term> unless caution is applied during that melting, since it has the power to soften hard things and harden soft things. It is such, however, that it preserves the measure of its proportioned moisture <note>original: humiditas; in alchemy, this refers to the "metallic seed" or fluidity that allows metals to flow and bond</note> with sufficiency, according to the requirements of the fusion being sought...

<summary>This page introduces Mercury (Quicksilver) as the essential "medicine" for alchemical transformation, explaining that it must be purified through sublimation and stabilized through fixation to permanently alter other metals.</summary>
<keywords>quicksilver, mercury, sublimation, fixation, fusion, projection, medicine, alchemy, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the preparation of Quicksilver (Mercury) as a transmuting "medicine." It focuses on how the craftsman must use fire to manipulate the "moisture" of a substance to achieve the proper density and weight required for alchemical perfection.</meta>

# BOOK

Through this, it is clear that such a preparation must be managed so that the most radiant and purest substance is created from it, then fixed <term>fixation</term>. Great care must be shown so that the craftsman is practiced in the management of fire and in the method of its melting <term>fusion</term>, ensuring he can remove the substance of moisture <note>original: humiditas; in alchemy, this refers to the volatile or liquid-like qualities of a metal</note> to such a degree that it is sufficient to complete a perfect melt.

Namely, if you seek to soften bodies for melting through this process, a slow fire should be applied at the beginning of its creation. For a slow fire preserves moisture and brings the melting to perfection. If, however, you seek to harden soft materials, a strong <note>original: uehemens; intense or violent</note> fire should be prepared; for such a fire consumes moisture and disrupts the melting. 

Indeed, a wise craftsman must necessarily consider all these rules, and many others, in every medicine <note>In this context, "medicine" refers to the transmuting agent or Elixir</note>. Similarly, it is necessary to consider the changes in weight, along with their causes and proper order. The cause of great weight is the refinement <term>subtlety</term> of the substance of bodies and their essential uniformity. Because of this, their parts can be made more dense since nothing falls between them <note>The author argues that pure substances have no "pores" or air gaps, making them heavier and more "perfect" like gold</note>. The densification of parts leads to an increase in weight and its ultimate perfection.

It is clear, therefore, that one must seek this refinement <term>subtlety</term> through the skill of the work, both in the preparation of the base metals and in the method of the perfecting medicine itself. For the greater the weight of the transmuted bodies, the greater is their perfection, as discovered through investigation by the art. Thus ends this useful discourse on the medicines. But let us now recount the differences between all the medicines.

->FOURTH<-

## On the threefold order of medicines.
### Chapter 2.

**W**E *have indeed indicated that a threefold distinction of medicines necessarily occurs. For one is of the first order, another of the second, and another of the third. I call a medicine of the first order every preparation of minerals which, when projected <term>projection</term> <note>The act of throwing the alchemical catalyst onto the molten base metal</note> onto bodies diminished from perfection <note>i.e., base metals like lead, tin, or copper that are not gold or silver</note>, impresses an alteration that does not bring about a sufficient completion; rather, it happens that the altered substance is changed and corrupted, with the total evaporation of that medicine's impression. Such is every whitening sublimation <term>sublimation</term> of Venus <note>original: Veneris; Copper</note> or Mars <note>original: Martis; Iron</note> which does not undergo fixation <term>fixation</term> <note>The process of making a substance resistant to fire so it does not evaporate</note>. And of this kind is every addition of the color of the Sun <note>original: Solis; Gold</note> and Moon <note>original: Lunae; Silver</note>, or of mixed Copper, placed over the cementing furnace <term>cementation</term> <note>A process of purifying gold or coloring metals using heat and dry additives</note>, and of zimar <note>original: zimar; a term for vitriol or copper-based salts used in coloring</note> and similar things. For here it changes in a mutation that is not lasting, but rather diminishes itself through exhalation <note>The effect literally "breathes out" or evaporates when subjected to further heat</note>.*

*But we call a medicine of the second order every preparation which, when it is projected onto bodies diminished from perfection, alters them into some degree of completion, though some modes of corruption are left entirely behind; just as is the calcination <term>calcination</term> <note>Reducing a metal to a powder/ash through high heat</note> of bodies, by which everything "fugitive" <note>volatile elements that flee the fire</note> is destroyed. And of this kind is the medicine that perpetually turns the Moon <note>Silver</note> yellow, or even perpetually whitens Venus <note>Copper</note>, while other types of corruption remain in them.*

*However, I call a medicine of the third order every preparation which, when it arrives at the bodies, removes every corruption with its projection, and with every completion* <note>The text continues on the next page to describe the perfect medicine, often equated with the Philosopher's Stone, which achieves total transformation</note>

<summary>This page defines the first two "orders" of alchemical medicines: the first being temporary surface alterations that evaporate, and the second being permanent but incomplete transformations like coloring.</summary>
<keywords>three orders of medicine, projection, Venus, Mars, Sun, Moon, sublimation, fixation, calcination, zimar</keywords>

...completes the difference. This, however, is the only one, and therefore through it we are excused from the labors of discovering ten medicines. Therefore, the work of the first order is called the **lesser**. That of the second, the **middle**. That of the third, indeed, the **greater**. And this is the sufficient distinction of all medicines.

## On the distinction of all medicines.
### Chapter 3.

**F**ollowing, therefore, from the promise of the determined order: since indeed one medicine is for bodies <note>In alchemical theory, "bodies" refers to the solid metals, such as lead, tin, copper, iron, silver, and gold</note>, and another is for Quicksilver <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: Argenti uiui; Mercury, which was often treated as a unique "spirit" rather than a "body"</note>. And among the bodies, one is of the first order, another of the second, and another of the third; and similarly for Quicksilver. Let us therefore recount the differences of all medicines of the first order, then of the second, and then of the third. First, those of the bodies. Afterward, we shall deliver our account regarding the medicine of Quicksilver with a complete discourse and in proper order.

We say, then, that of the first-order medicine for bodies, one is for hard bodies, and another for soft ones <note>Hard metals include iron and copper; soft metals include lead and tin</note>. Among the hard ones, one is for Venus <term>Venus</term> <note>original: Veneris; Copper</note>, another for Mars <term>Mars</term> <note>original: Martis; Iron</note>, and another for the Moon <term>Moon</term> <note>original: Lunae; Silver</note>. For Venus and Mars, the medicine is a pure whitening <term>whitening</term> <note>original: dealbatio; the alchemical process of making a "base" metal look like silver</note> of their substance. For the Moon, however, it is a reddening <term>reddening</term> <note>original: rubificatio; the process of giving silver a golden hue</note> with a lemon-yellow coloring <term>citrinitas</term> <note>A stage in the alchemical process between whitening and reddening, associated with the appearance of gold</note> of pleasant brilliance.

Since reddening does not occur for Venus and Mars with a first-order medicine accompanied by the appearance of brilliance—because they are entirely impure and not fit to receive the brilliance of redness before a preparation arrives that brings forth brilliance—we shall therefore recount first all the medicines of Venus, and afterward those of Mars, which fall into the first order.

## On the medicines of Venus in the first order. Chapter 4.

**T**here is, therefore, a medicine for whitening Venus <term>Venus</term> <note>original: Venerem; the alchemical name for Copper</note>, one by means of Quicksilver <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: Argentum uiuum; Mercury</note>, and another by means of Arsenic. Indeed, the whitening medicine for it is completed through Quicksilver in this way: First, precipitated Quicksilver is dissolved, and the calcination <term>calcination</term> <note>original: calcinatio; the process of heating a metal until it oxidizes into a fine powder</note> of Venus is dissolved in a similar manner. Both of these solutions are mixed into one; then they are coagulated <note>turned from a liquid into a solid or semi-solid state</note>, and their medicine is cast upon the body of Venus itself. This treatment whitens and cleanses the metal, yet it does not bring about a complete transformation, for it still happens that the altered metal may change back or be corrupted.

Furthermore, and in another way, precipitated Quicksilver is dissolved, and Litharge <term>Litharge</term> <note>original: Litargirium; lead oxide, often used in refining processes</note> is also dissolved. These two solutions are joined into one, and the calcination of the body of Venus—which is intended to be whitened—is dissolved and joined with the aforementioned solutions. They are then coagulated, and afterwards, they are cast upon its body; for it is whitened by this process.

In another way, a quantity of Quicksilver is sublimated <note>heated so it turns into vapor and then solidifies again</note> by turns from its body, until a part of the Quicksilver remains with it, with the ignition <note>intense heating</note> completed. Thereafter, let it be ground by frequently soaking it with distilled vinegar <note>original: aceto distillato</note>, so that the mixture may be better incorporated into its very depths. From this point, it is roasted, and finally, Quicksilver is sublimated from it in a similar fashion; then it is soaked and roasted again. And let the work be repeated upon it so many times until a great quantity of Quicksilver rests within it, with the ignition completed.

For this is a good whitening <term>whitening</term> <note>original: dealbatio; the alchemical process of making a "red" metal like copper appear "white" like silver</note> of the first order. Alternatively, over precipitated Quicksilver <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: Argentum uiuum; mercury</note>, let Quicksilver in its own nature be sublimated <term>sublimated</term> <note>original: sublimetur; the process of heating a substance to a vapor so it collects as a solid in the upper part of a vessel</note> as many times as necessary until it is fixed within it and allows for melting. It is also whitened by means of sublimated Arsenic <term>Arsenic</term> <note>original: Arsenicum</note>.
