But unless you apply yourself skillfully to the methods of sublimation, the Arsenic will not persist within the metal through any such alteration. It is done like this: after the first stage of sublimation, you should repeat the second stage just as we described in the sublimation of Marcasite <term>Marcasite</term> <note>original: Marchasitæ; a crystalline mineral, usually iron pyrite, used here for its sulfur or arsenic content</note>.

It is also whitened in another way: cast sublimated Arsenic into Silver, and then cast this whole mixture upon Venus, for it whitens it remarkably. Or first mix Litharge, or burnt Lead <term>burnt Lead</term> <note>original: Plumbum ustum; lead that has been heated until it forms a powdery oxide</note> that has been dissolved, with Silver; then, let Arsenic be thrown over this, and the whole mixture cast upon Venus will whiten it; this is a good whitening of the first order.

Alternatively, let sublimated Arsenic be cast only over dissolved and reduced Litharge, and then throw all of this upon the molten Venus, because it whitens the metal with a bright appearance. Or let Silver and Venus be mixed together, and over these let every whitening medicine be cast. For Silver is more "friendly" to Arsenic <note>original: amica; the author uses the metaphor of friendship to describe how well certain chemical substances bond or react with one another</note>...

Let us therefore first bring forward all the methods of medicines that arise from <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: Argentum uiuum; mercury</note>. Then, accordingly, those that come from <term>Sulfur</term> <note>original: Sulphur; brimstone</note> or from the mixture of both. Finally, those from copper gum <note>likely a reference to verdigris or a copper salt</note> or similar substances. The method for that which is accomplished through <term>Quicksilver</term> is as follows: Let the precipitate be taken, and through its <term>precipitation</term> let it be made <term>mortified</term> <note>original: mortificatum; rendered non-reactive or "dead" through chemical processing</note> and fixed <note>made resistant to heat so that it does not evaporate</note>.

Then indeed, let it be placed in a furnace of high heat, in the manner of the <term>preservation of calxes</term> <note>original: conseruationis calcium; the process of keeping metallic oxides/powders under heat without melting them</note> which we have taught, until it reddens to the likeness of <term>Vsifur</term> <note>original: Vsifur; an archaic term for cinnabar or red mercuric sulfide</note>. If indeed it does not redden, take a part of un-mortified <term>Quicksilver</term>, and with <term>Sulfur</term> repeat the <term>sublimation</term> <note>the process of heating a substance until it vapors and then solidifies again</note> of it. Let the Sulfur, however, be cleansed of all impurity, and the Quicksilver likewise.

And after you have repeated its sublimation twenty times upon the precipitate itself: dissolve it with the <term>sharpness of dissolving waters</term> <note>original: aquarum acumine; a reference to strong acids or mineral solvents</note>, and <term>calcine</term> <note>heat to a powder</note> it again, and dissolve it again, until it is abundantly sufficient. After this, dissolve a part of <term>Moon</term> <note>original: Luna; the alchemical name for silver</note>, and when it has been dissolved, mix the solutions together and coagulate them. Then cast them upon molten <term>Moon</term>, for it will turn yellow with a very peculiar yellowness <note>this refers to "citrination," the goal of giving silver the golden hue of sun/gold</note>. 

If however the <term>Quicksilver</term> has reddened during precipitation to the perfection of its <term>projection</term> <note>original: proiectionis; the act of casting the alchemical medicine onto a base metal to transform it</note>, the said procedure suffices without the mixing of a substance to tint it. By <term>Sulfur</term> indeed it is reddened, but its <term>reddening</term> is immensely difficult and laborious. The necessity of the work leads us to this: that we first calcine it, and afterwards fix it, which requires a great deal of labor. Then indeed, let us manage it by this same preparation, and pour the same <term>projection</term> upon the substance of <term>Moon</term>, and yet its yellowing <meta>catchword: yellowing (trinatio)</meta>

...[ci]trination, shining, or rather dark and livid with a deadly earthiness.

Indeed, **Silver** <note>original: Luna; the alchemical name for silver</note> is also **citrinated** <note>original: Citrinatur; the alchemical stage of turning a metal yellow or gold-colored</note> in a similar way with the solution of **Iron** <note>original: Martis; literally "of Mars," the alchemical symbol for iron</note>.

Truly, the method of that citrination which is accomplished through **Vitriol** <note>original: Vitriolum; a sulfate of a metal, usually iron or copper</note> or **Copperas** <note>original: Cuperosam; another term for metallic sulfates used as pigments or reagents</note> is as follows. Let a certain quantity of each of these be taken, and let that part of it which allows itself to be **sublimated** <note>heated into a vapor and then solidified as a purified powder</note> be sublimated, until it is totally sublimed with pressure. After this, however, let that which was sublimated be sublimated again with the type of fire appropriate to it, so that piece by piece it becomes **fixed** <note>original: figatur; rendered stable and resistant to evaporation by heat</note>, until the greater part of it is fixed. Afterwards, let it be administered with careful attention to the fire, so that a stronger fire can be applied to it for its perfection. 

After this, let it be dissolved into a **most deep red water** <note>original: aquam rubicundissimam; likely a highly concentrated acidic solution</note>, to which there is no equal. Then you shall devise a way to show it the entrance into the **Lunar body** <note>original: Lunare corpus; silver</note>, and these devices have been sufficiently shown to you, if you have been a diligent seeker of the perfect work which we have written about. 

And because we see these things adhere amicably to the depth of Silver, we have therefore considered it certain that these are of their same root, and thus it happens that they are altered by them. These, indeed, are all the medicines of the first order which we have presented; however, many more ways of them can be multiplied, while the essence of the **pigmented substances** <note>original: pigmentalium rerum; coloring agents or chemicals used for dyeing and transmutation</note> remains the same through the variety of methods. 

Some, however, have invented more medicines, but one of two things must necessarily happen: either it is necessary for them to create the medicine from the same things, or from things having the same nature. Or they compose a medicine which is equivalent to that which does not exist, along with its alteration, and which contributes neither to the world nor to the parts of the world, until the **prime mover** <note>original: motor in sublimi; a philosophical reference to the ultimate source of motion and change in the universe</note> in the heights of mobile nature rests incorruptible.

<meta>This page continues the technical classification of alchemical "medicines" (agents of transformation). # BOOK
## Concerning medicines of the second order.
### Chapter 7.

*I*T remains now to move on to the medicines of the second order, providing a full and true account supported by clear proofs and practical experience. Since there is one type of medicine for cleansed bodies <note>metals that have been purified of surface impurities</note> and another for **Quicksilver** <note>original: Argenti uiui; Mercury</note>, <term>namely</term> <note>original: scilicet; literally "that is to say"</note> the perfection of its **coagulation** <note>the process of turning liquid mercury into a solid metallic state</note>: let us first describe the medicines for all bodies in full.

After that, we shall turn to those medicines that **coagulate** Quicksilver into true **Gold-making** <note>original: Solificum; the power to transform into or act as gold</note> or **Silver-making** <note>original: Lunificum; the power to transform into or act as silver</note> agents. A medicine of the second order is therefore one which completes imperfect bodies by adding a single specific quality of perfection.

Indeed, there are many causes of corruption in each of the imperfect bodies. For example, in **Lead** <note>original: Saturno; literally "Saturn," the alchemical name for lead</note>, there is a volatile **sulfurousness** <note>original: sulphureitas; the presence of sulfur which was believed to cause metals to blacken or decay</note> and the "flight" of Quicksilver <note>the tendency of mercury to evaporate when heated</note>, which necessarily causes corruption, as does its **earthiness** <note>original: terreitas; the presence of non-metallic, impure mineral matter</note>. 

Therefore, let a medicine be prepared that either removes one of these defects entirely or disguises it through a **"coating"** <note>original: palliando; literally "cloaking" or "veiling," referring to a surface-level or partial transformation rather than a fundamental change</note>, while leaving the other causes of imperfection behind. Because there is something unchangeable within these bodies—something innate in their very "root"—it cannot be removed by a medicine of the second order. Therefore, every medicine that removes such deep-seated impurities from the mixture is called a medicine of the third and higher order, not the second. 

And since the excess of "fleeing" <note>volatile</note> elements and the aforementioned **earthiness** <note>original: terreitas</note> can be removed through the methods of **calcination** <note>heating a metal until it turns into a powder or oxide</note> and **reduction** <note>restoring a powdered metal back to its solid metallic state</note>, it was necessary to discover a medicine of the second order. This medicine indeed disguises what is innate, and softens the hard, and hardens the soft, and in hard [substances]...

<summary>This page defines "second-order medicines" as alchemical agents that fix specific defects in metals—like "earthiness" or "sulfurousness"—without changing the metal's fundamental nature. It distinguishes these from "third-order" medicines, which achieve total transformation.</summary>
<keywords>second order, medicine, quicksilver, gold-making, silver-making, lead, sulfurousness, earthiness, calcination, reduction</keywords>

On the previous page, the author introduced medicines of the second order as agents that provide specific improvements to metals. Here, the text specifies how these medicines correct the fundamental density and melting points of "soft" (low-melting) and "hard" (high-melting) metals.</meta>

->BOOK FOUR.<-

namely, and for soft bodies, according to a completion that is not deceptive <note>original: sophisticum; refers to a fake or surface-level imitation</note> but perfectly constitutes a true **Gold-making** <note>original: Solificum; able to produce the qualities of gold</note> or **Silver-making** <note>original: Lunificum; able to produce the qualities of silver</note> body.

---

## On the administration of medicines
## of the second order.
### Chapter 8.

**C**onsequently, since it is clear that in soft bodies <note>metals like lead and tin that melt at low temperatures</note> alone, through the skills of this craft's work, the haste of their **liquefaction** <note>the speed at which they melt</note> cannot be removed, nor the impurity innate in the root of their principles, it necessarily follows that a medicine must be sought. This medicine, when used in **projection** <note>the act of casting the alchemical agent onto the molten base metal</note>, would thicken their thinness <note>increase the density/stability of the metal</note>, and by thickening them, would provide them with sufficient resistance to the heat of the fire during their melting. In hard bodies <note>metals like iron or copper that melt at high temperatures</note>, it should thin their thickness <note>reduce their density or resistance to melting</note>, and by thinning them, lead them to a sufficient speed of **fusion** <note>melting</note> along with the proper quality of heat; and by masking the darkness of both types of bodies, it should beautify them, transforming the latter into white <note>silver</note> and the former into the most perfect yellow <note>gold</note>. 

However, this medicine does not differ from a medicine of the third order <note>the highest and most transformative class of alchemical agents</note> except by the imperfection of a lesser preparation. Nor does it differ from itself in what pertains to its projection onto various bodies and the acceptance of **pigments** <note>original: pigmentorum; here referring to coloring agents or specialized additives</note>, but rather in the manner of its preparation. For the medicine for soft bodies requires one type of industrious preparation to thicken their thinness, while the medicine for hard bodies requires another to thin their thickness; the latter indeed by the method of consuming fire, whereas the former requires the administration of preserving humidity.

<note>original vocabulary: Gold-making, Silver-making, medicine of the second order, liquefaction, fusion, coloring/pigment, preparation</note>

<summary>This page explains how "medicines of the second order" work to balance the physical properties of metals—thickening soft metals to make them heat-resistant and thinning hard metals to help them melt faster—to prepare them for transformation into gold or silver.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, second order medicine, soft bodies, hard bodies, liquefaction, fusion, projection, Solificum, Lunificum</keywords>

## On the correction of defects following the administration of medicines of the second order. Chapter 9.

**L**et us therefore bring forth a universal discourse concerning the medicines <note>original: medicinae; in alchemy, these are the elixirs or substances used to transform base metals into noble ones</note> of this second order, complete with a certain and true determination. We shall first speak of all the **Lunar** <note>original: Lunares; refers to silver-making medicines</note> agents for all bodies that fall short of perfection, including the different ways they are prepared. After that, we shall discuss the **Solar** <note>original: Solares; refers to gold-making medicines</note> agents with their own similar differences.

We have already proven in our previous discourses that **Sulfur** of any kind is corruptive to perfection. **Mercury** <term>Mercury (Argentum vivum)</term> <note>literally "living silver"</note>, however, is the perfecting agent in the work of nature when the controlled processes are complete. Therefore, imitating nature as closely as we are able to follow her works, we likewise adopt **Mercury** in the mastery <note>original: magisterium; the professional practice or "Great Work" of alchemy</note> of this work for every medicine of perfection—namely the Lunar and the Solar—both for the imperfect bodies <note>base metals like lead or tin</note> and for the **coagulable Mercury** <note>Mercury that has been treated so it can be solidified</note> itself.

Since we have stated in our most recent discourses that there is a two-fold difference in these medicines—one for the bodies <note>metals</note> and another for coagulable Mercury—we shall first provide a certain account of the medicines for bodies, and thereafter for the medicine of Mercury.

Therefore, the matter of this medicine is of one kind for every type, and it is that which is already sufficiently known <note>a common alchemical trope referring to the "First Matter," which the author assumes the reader can identify from previous chapters</note>. Take it, then, and if you wish to work toward the Lunar medicines according to the order previously set before you, make yourself experienced; prepare it, therefore, through the known methods of this mastery, the intention of which...

He emphasizes that while Sulfur is corruptive, Mercury is the essential agent for perfecting metals, imitating the natural process of metallic growth.</summary>

## On the medicines of Quicksilver, and their penetration. Chapter 10.

**ACCORDING**, therefore, to the order of what has been set forth before, it remains—following the completion of the work which we showed we would determine from the beginning—to pass into the narrative concerning the **medicine** <note>original: medicina; the alchemical elixir used for transformation</note> of **Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: Argenti uiui; Mercury</note> that coagulates it. We say, therefore, that the medicine is drawn out from that same **Stone** <note>original: lapidis; likely the Philosopher's Stone or the base material of the Great Work</note> known from the many chapters of this work. The reason for this is that since Quicksilver is **fugitive** <note>volatile; it evaporates easily when heated</note> and escapes without any burning, it requires a medicine that suddenly, before its flight, adheres to it in its depths and joins with it in its smallest parts. This medicine must thicken it and, by its own melting, preserve it in the fire until it gains a tolerance for a greater fire that consumes its humidity. Through this benefit, the medicine converts it in a moment into a true **Solar-maker** <note>original: Solificum; a substance that creates gold</note> and **Lunar-maker** <note>original: Lunificum; a substance that creates silver</note>, according to the purpose for which the medicine was prepared.

Since, therefore, nothing is found to share more with it than that which is of its own nature, we have judged to complete its medicine with itself; and we have devised a way to provide the form of that medicine through **ingenious methods** <note>original: ingenia</note>. This is, namely, that it should be prepared in its aforementioned ways through the persistence of long labor, by which all of its subtle and purest substance may be perfectly displayed—white indeed for **Silver** <note>original: Luna</note>, but an intense yellow for **Gold** <note>original: Sole</note>. This indeed is not completed—that is, creating the yellow color—without the mixture of the thing that tints it, which is of its own nature. Thereafter, however, with this purest substance of Quicksilver, let it be perfected with the ingenious methods of this **mastery's** <note>original: magisterij</note> work...

<summary>This chapter discusses the preparation of a "medicine" for Mercury (Quicksilver) that prevents it from evaporating (fixation) and allows it to be transmuted into gold or silver. The text emphasizes that Mercury is best treated with substances of its own nature to achieve these transformations.</summary>

->FOURTH<-

...ingenious **medicines** <note>original: medicina; the alchemical elixir used for transformation</note>, which most closely adhere to **Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: Argento uiuo; Mercury</note>, and are melted very easily, and coagulate it, and convert this into a true **Solar-maker** <note>original: Solificum; a substance that creates gold</note> or **Lunar-maker** <note>original: Lunificum; a substance that creates silver</note>, with its preparation previously completed. But it is often asked from what sources this substance of Quicksilver can best be drawn out. In response, we say that it is drawn out from those things in which it already exists. For it is found both in the "bodies" <note>the seven classical metals: gold, silver, copper, iron, tin, lead, and mercury</note> themselves, as well as in Quicksilver itself according to nature, since they are discovered to be of a single nature. But it is more difficult to extract from the metallic bodies. In Quicksilver, however, it is closer at hand, though not more perfect. Therefore, of whatever kind the medicine may be—whether found in the metals or in the substance of Quicksilver itself—the medicine of the **precious stone** <note>original: lapidis præciosi; referring here to the Philosopher's Stone or the fundamental transformative agent</note> is sought after.

But because it happens sometimes that the medicine is mixed and sometimes it is not, we will describe the method of mixing: namely, how each substance may acquire the deepest entry into a metal body, or why a medicine might fail to enter. The method is through the **dissolution** <term>dissolution</term> <note>original: dissolutionem; the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state to make it more reactive</note> of that which does not enter, and through the dissolution of that which does enter, and through the mixing of both solutions. For any substance that is joined to another in its smallest parts <note>an alchemical concept of "per minima," referring to the most fundamental level of matter</note> is made capable of entering it. This entry is perfected by dissolution; and by dissolution, **melting** <note>original: fusio</note> is achieved in things that are otherwise not meltable. For this reason, they become more fit to enter, penetrate, and alter the metal. This is also the reason why we **calcine** <term>calcine</term> <note>original: calcinamus; to reduce a substance to a powder or "ash" through prolonged, high heat</note> certain things that are not naturally of this type: namely, so that they may be better dissolved. They are dissolved for this reason: so that the metallic bodies may better receive an "impression" <note>a change in their fundamental qualities or appearance</note> from them, and be prepared and cleansed by them in the same way.

It highlights the importance of dissolution and calcination in making materials reactive enough to "impress" their qualities upon base metals.</summary>

For through this benefit, it necessarily gains **entry** <note>original: ingressum; the ability of the elixir to penetrate the physical structure of a metal to change its nature</note> into that body alone, and this does not necessarily happen in just any other body.*

## On the Medicines of the Third Order, Chapter 11.

**T**he medicine of this third order is twofold: namely, **Solar** <term>Solar</term> <note>original: solaris; referring to the medicine used to produce gold</note> and **Lunar** <term>Lunar</term> <note>original: lunaris; referring to the medicine used to produce silver</note>. It is, however, one in essence and acts in a similar manner; for this reason, it is called a "single medicine" by our ancients, whose writings we have studied. There is, however, an addition of a **citrine** <term>citrine</term> <note>original: citrinitatis; a lemon-yellow color, which in alchemy represents a specific stage of maturity or "digestion" in the work</note> color, because it is perfected from the purest substance of fixed **Sulfur** <term>Sulfur</term> <note>In this context, sulfur refers to the alchemical principle of "fixity" and color, rather than the common chemical element</note>. The difference between the Lunar and the Solar is that the latter contains this [sulfur] within itself, while the former does not.

This third order is called the order of the **Greater Work** <term>Greater Work</term> <note>original: maioris operis; the Magnum Opus, the highest level of alchemical achievement</note>, and it is named so because it requires the industry of greater wisdom in its administration and its preparation for perfection, as well as a longer labor to reach the fulfillment of truth. Therefore, the medicine of this order is not fundamentally different in its essence, but only in the most subtle degrees of preparation during its creation, and through a more persistent application of labor in these stages. We shall describe all of these stages at the conclusion of this discourse, along with the complete manner of action, its underlying causes, and clear experiments. From there, we will likewise describe the many steps of administration for this third order.

For the Solar medicine requires one specific degree in the complete preparation of **pigments** <note>original: pigmentorum; here referring to the coloring agents or tinctures that change the metal's appearance</note>, while the Lunar requires another; the Solar indeed requires the administration of the Sulfur that tints it, while the Lunar does not. Therefore, let us first describe the method of administration for the Lunar medicine. It is as follows: take its known **stone** <term>stone</term> <note>the "lapis," the starting material or the base of the elixir</note>, and through the method of separation, take its purest part...

*Then indeed, you should fix a certain portion of its purest part and leave another portion of it [unfixed]. Therefore, repeat all these orders of preparation—calcination, dissolution, coagulation, and preservation in a tempered fire by light roasting—four times upon it. If this does not occur, let the quantity of the unfixed part be added to it again in turn, until it [is lifted] to the...*

## On the management of the Solar and Lunar medicine
### Chapter 12.

Therefore, the preparation of this **Solar** <note>original: Solaris; referring to Gold, which was associated with the Sun</note> medicine (which is non-adhering) is performed by the addition of **Sulfur** <note>In alchemy, Sulfur represents the active, combustible, and masculine principle of matter</note>, through a fixing and calcining method, with the industrious skill of perfect management and a manifold way of dissolution with much repetition, until it becomes pure. This is preceded by perfect management, which is accomplished through sublimation. And the method of this addition is through the repetition of joining the non-fixed part of the stone by the smallest amounts <note>original: per minima; a technical term meaning to mix substances at the most fundamental, almost atomic level</note> during sublimation through skill, until it is elevated with it, and is again fixed with it so that it remains stable. And the more the order of this completion is repeated, the more the abundance of this medicine is multiplied, and its quality is increased, and the increase of its perfection is greatly multiplied. And we, lest we be bitten by the impious <note>A common alchemical trope: the author claims to use "brief speech" or "hidden notes" to prevent the unworthy from discovering the secret</note>, shall relate the whole completion of this **mastery** <term>mastery</term> <note>original: magisterij; the "Magistery" is the successful completion of the alchemical Great Work</note> using a complete note with brevity of speech.

And its intention is that, by the method of **sublimation** <term>sublimation</term> <note>original: sublimatio; the chemical process of heating a substance into vapor and collecting the purified solid that forms upon cooling</note>, the **stone** <term>stone</term> <note>original: lapis; the "Philosopher's Stone," the goal of the alchemical Great Work</note> and its addition may be most perfectly purified; and from here, by means of various skills, the **volatile** <term>volatile</term> <note>original: uolatile; a substance that easily evaporates or vaporizes when heated</note> parts among them may be **fixed** <term>fixed</term> <note>original: fixum; a substance that has been made stable and resistant to the heat of fire</note>. Thereafter, let the fixed become volatile, and again let the volatile become fixed. Perform this so many times—making the fixed volatile and the volatile fixed—until it yields an easy fixation when ignited. 

And in this sequence, the most precious **secret** <term>secret</term> <note>original: archanum; a hidden mystery or profound chemical truth</note> is completed: which is above every secret of the sciences of this world, and is an incomparable treasure. You should indeed apply yourself to it with the greatest persistence of labor and with the duration of immense meditation; for with those things you will find it, and without them, you will not. 

And for this medicine, the repetition of the quality of its administration—with the industry of such caution—can result in the preparation of the stone, until it changes **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: Argentum uiuum; mercury</note> into an infinite amount of true **gold-making** <term>gold-making</term> <note>original: Solificum; literally "Sun-making," as the Sun was the symbol for gold</note> and **silver-making** <term>silver-making</term> <note>original: Lunificum; literally "Moon-making," as the Moon was the symbol for silver</note> substance. This does not depend on anything except its multiplication, or the frequent repetition of its preparation. 

Now, therefore, let the sublime God of natures be praised, blessed, and glorious, who has revealed to us the sequence of all medicines through experience; which, by the goodness of His inspiration and the persistence of our labor, we have sought out, seen with our eyes, and touched with our hands—the completion of it having been investigated by our mastery. 

But even if we have veiled it, let the **son of the doctrine** <term>son of the doctrine</term> <note>original: doctrinae filius; a term for an initiate or dedicated student of alchemy</note> not wonder. For we have not veiled it for him, but for the wicked and the dishonest. We have delivered it in such speech that it must necessarily remain hidden from the foolish, yet it allures the prudent to the search for its discovery. Therefore, sons of the doctrine, seek it out, and you will find this most excellent gift of God reserved for you alone.

Sons of foolish wickedness, and of immense malevolent depravity, flee from this **science** <term>science</term> <note>original: scientia; here meaning the sacred knowledge of alchemy</note>, because it is an enemy and adversary to you, and it will place you in the misery of poverty; for this gift of God is entirely hidden from you by the judgment of divine providence, and is denied to you altogether. Therefore, having thoroughly examined the methods of all medicines, and following the beginning of our proposed task, we must now move on to those things which make known the industry and perfection of this **mastery** <term>mastery</term> <note>original: magisterium; the "Great Work" or the completed Philosopher's Stone</note>, along with the reasons for their trials.

## Concerning the examinations by which it is known whether the mastery is in perfection.
### Chapter 13.

**P**assing over the obvious experiments, of which we make no account since they are known and certain to everyone—namely, those without the skill of cleverness in weight, color, and extension by the hammer—let us test with caution through the experiences of the arts whether the **projection** <term>projection</term> <note>original: proiectio; the final stage of alchemy where the "Stone" is cast upon base metal to transmute it into gold or silver</note> of the administration of this art brings about its fulfillment in truth. These tests are: the **ash-test** <term>ash-test</term> <note>original: cineritium; also known as cupellation, a process of refining ores or alloys in a hearth made of bone ash</note>, **cementation** <term>cementation</term> <note>original: cementum; a process of heating metal with corrosive salts to remove impurities</note>, ignition, melting, exposure to the vapors of sharp substances, the quenching of burning **sulfur** <term>sulfur</term> <note>original: Sulphur; a key alchemical element representing combustibility</note> in a mixture, the trial of **calcination** <term>calcination</term> <note>original: calcinationis; heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</note> and the repetition of reduction, and the easy or difficult reception of **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: Argentum uiuum; mercury</note>. Therefore, we must begin from the first things according to their order, and then let us seek to complete the others according to our promise, following that same order,

## On the examination by the Ash-test.
### Chapter 14.

**W**e speak, therefore, regarding the discourse on the **Ash-test** <term>Ash-test</term> <note>original: Cineritium; a process now known as cupellation. A sample of metal is melted in a "cupel" made of bone ash. Base metals oxidize and are absorbed by the ash, while gold and silver remain as a pure bead.</note>, along with all its manifest causes and its method of operation. It is only the substance of the **Moon** <note>The alchemical symbol for Silver.</note> and the **Sun** <note>The alchemical symbol for Gold.</note> that endures in the examination of the Ash-test. We shall therefore investigate the true differences in the substance of these perfect bodies, together with the reasons for the Ash-test—namely, why certain things endure more and others less in this kind of examination of the mastery, compared to the perfection of those things which are diminished.

It has already been sufficiently described by us, the secret of these two bodies in the depth of their substance. And it is this: that their primary root was a great quantity of **Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: Argentum vivum; mercury. In alchemical theory, all metals were thought to be composed of mercury and sulfur in varying states of purity.</note>, and its essence was first most pure and most subtle, but was afterwards thickened until it could undergo fusion with the application of fire. Therefore, whatever things are diminished from perfection and possess more **earthiness** <term>earthiness</term> <note>original: terreitas; the impure, non-metallic, or "drossy" elements within an ore that do not survive the refining fire.</note>, they endure less in this examination; whereas whatever things have less [earthiness] endure more. For these [perfect metals] adhere together more strongly because of the subtlety of their parts, which mix and unite themselves to the highest degree.

And those which are similarly of greater thinness <note>Refers to metals that are "weak" or easily melted and vaporized, such as tin.</note>, like the bodies of the two **Leads** <term>Leads</term> <note>original: Plumborum; alchemists often categorized both lead (black lead) and tin (white lead) under this term.</note>, or on the contrary, those bodies which are of greater thickness <note>Meaning denser or more stubborn in the furnace.</note>, such as **Mars** <term>Mars</term> <note>The alchemical name for Iron.</note> and **Venus** <term>Venus</term> <note>The alchemical name for Copper.</note>, more so than those which consist in perfection, must necessarily be separated entirely from the mixture: because they do not have the same melting point, and for that reason they are separated. Furthermore, those things which participate in a smaller quantity of **Quicksilver** are more easily separated from the mixture. It is clear, therefore, that since much **earthiness** <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the word "terreitatis" on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page explains the theory behind cupellation (the ash-test), asserting that only gold and silver survive because they are composed of pure, "subtle" mercury. Base metals like iron, copper, and lead are purged during the process due to their "earthiness" and differing fusion points.</summary>

...of earthiness <note>The text begins with the suffix of the word "terreitatis" (earthiness) from the previous page.</note> Saturn <note>The alchemical name for Lead.</note> is, and of a small quantity of **Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: Argentum uiuum; mercury. Alchemists believed all metals were composed of mercury and sulfur.</note>, and of easy melting, and also of the greatest thinness—all of which are qualities opposed to the perfection of the **Ash-test** <term>Ash-test</term> <note>original: Cineritij; cupellation. A process where metals are melted in a porous bowl of bone-ash; base metals oxidize and sink into the ash, leaving pure gold or silver behind.</note>. Therefore, Saturn, among the other bodies, lasts the least in the mixture during the craft of the Ash-test; rather, it is separated and yields most quickly. 

And therefore, since it yields more than the other bodies diminished from perfection, it is for this reason more suited to the examination of this mastery. And this is why: because it yields faster, it draws each of the other imperfect things out of the mixture along with itself. Because of this, a greater quantity of the perfect [metal] <note>Gold or silver.</note> is saved from the strong combustion of the fire’s examination, because the perfect metal does not remain subject to the consumption of the test for a long duration of time. Therefore, through the examination by lead, it is burned less and purified more easily.

Indeed, some [observe that] the substance of Jupiter <note>The alchemical name for Tin.</note> consists of a greater quantity of **Quicksilver**, and of less earthiness, participating in a greater purity and a more subtle substance. Therefore, it is preserved in a mixture longer than Saturn or Venus, because it adheres more deeply within the mixture. And this is the reason why a great quantity of the perfect metal is destroyed before the substance joined to it <note>In this case, the tin.</note> can be separated. 

Venus <note>The alchemical name for Copper.</note>, however, provides fusion with ignition <note>The metal glows or "burns" as it melts.</note>. But because its melting is slower than that of the perfect metal, it is separated from the mixture more slowly than Saturn, owing to the ignition of its own fusible substance. Yet, because it contains a smaller quantity of Quicksilver than Jupiter, and possesses greater earthiness and a thicker substance, it is more easily removed from the mixture than Jupiter; for Jupiter adheres more deeply [to the gold or silver] than Venus does.

But **Mars** <note>The alchemical name for Iron.</note> indeed does not possess the quality of melting, and for that reason, it is not mixed [with other metals]; this happens because of the deprivation of its humidity. But even if it should happen to be mixed because of the vehemence of the fire: since it does not have the humidity of **Silver** <note>original: Luna</note> or **Gold** <note>original: Sol</note>, it is united to it through the smallest parts by drinking it in. And therefore, although it has much earthiness and a small amount of **Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: Argentum uiuum; mercury.</note>, and a lack of melting, it is not separated from them by any easy artifice. For through this, the industry of the craftsman is expanded toward the true rectification <term>rectification</term> <note>The process of purifying a substance to its "right" or most potent state.</note> of any body, if he rightly knows the efficacy of that which we have written. If, however, he applies his understanding to it in a fantastic <note>Meaning "imaginary" or "unscientific" in this context.</note> manner, he will know nothing of the truth of it. There are, however, two bodies of perfection that endure in this examination: namely, **Gold** <note>original: Sol</note> and **Silver** <note>original: Luna</note>, on account of their good composition, which results from a good mixture and their pure substance.

Let us therefore relate the method of that [test], since it is most necessary to us for certain knowledge in the mastery of this perfection. The method of it is thus: that sifted ash be taken, or lime, or the powder of burnt animal bones, or a mixture of all these, or of some of them. From there, let it be moistened with water, and let the hand be pressed over it, and let it be made into a firm and solid layer; and in the middle of the layer let there be made a round, solid, and polished pit, and upon the bottom of that pit let a certain quantity of crushed glass be sprinkled. Then indeed let it be allowed to dry, and when it has been dried, let that which is intended to undergo the examination be placed in the said pit, and let a strong fire of coals be kindled over it, and let it be blown upon the face of the body to be examined until it melts; and once it is melted,

...of **Lead** <note>original: Saturni; the alchemical name for lead.</note>, let us throw it part by part into that vessel, and let it be blown upon with a flame of strong ignition. And while you see it agitated and moved with a strong shaking motion, it is not yet pure. Wait, therefore, until all the **Lead** <note>original: Plumbum</note> has vanished; for if it vanishes and that motion does not cease, it is not yet purified. Therefore, throw **Lead** upon it again, and blow upon its surface again until the **Lead** is separated. If it does not become still, repeat the throwing and blowing of the **Lead** and examine its surface until it rests, and you see it clean and clear on its surface.

After this, open the coals, dissipate the fire, and pour water upon its surface. For you will find this perfectly refined. And if at any time during the blowing of this test you throw in glass, it will be purified better and more perfectly, because the glass takes away the filth and ensnares <note>Meaning it traps impurities in a molten slag.</note> them. However, in place of glass, salt can be thrown, or **borax** <term>borax</term> <note>original: baurach; an alkaline mineral used as a flux to help metals melt and to remove oxides.</note>, or some alum. Similarly, this **ash-test** <term>ash-test</term> <note>original: cineritij; also known as cupellation, where base metals are oxidized and absorbed into a porous cup of ash, leaving pure gold or silver behind.</note> can be performed in an earthen crucible, and the air can be blown around it and upon its surface in the same way, so that what must be examined is melted together as described above. These things having been sufficiently related, let us pass to the **examination of cement** <note>Cementation was a process used to part gold from silver or copper by heating it with salts or acids.</note> with its causes and its manifest and known experiments.

# B O O K

*...containing a quantity of combustible material; those with more [burn] more, while those with less, [burn] less. Because the **Sun** <note>original: Sol; the alchemical name for Gold.</note> therefore, among other bodies, possesses the smallest quantity of **Sulfur**, for that reason among all other mineral bodies, it is least consumed by the inflammation of fire. The **Moon** <note>original: Luna; the alchemical name for Silver.</note> indeed, after the Sun, participates in a smaller quantity of Sulfur than all remaining bodies, though it contains more than the Sun. Therefore, according to this principle, it is able to tolerate the heat of inflammation for a shorter space of time than the Sun, and by its nature tolerates things that burn in a similar way to a lesser degree. **Venus** <note>original: Venus; the alchemical name for Copper.</note> however, because it has more Sulfur than the Sun and Moon, and a greater **earthiness** <term>earthiness</term> <note>original: terreitatis; in alchemy, this refers to the solid, dross-like, or impure part of a substance that lacks the "spirit" of the noble metals.</note>, therefore tolerates inflammation less than they do. **Jupiter** <note>original: Iupiter; the alchemical name for Tin.</note> indeed participates in less [sulfurousness] than Venus, but possesses more **sulfurousness** <note>original: sulphureitatis; the quality of containing sulfur, which alchemists believed was the principle of combustion and color.</note> and earthiness than the Sun and Moon; for this reason, it is burned less than Venus by inflammation, but more than the Sun and Moon. **Saturn** <note>original: Saturnus; the alchemical name for Lead.</note> indeed has preserved more earthiness and sulfurousness by its nature in its mixture than the bodies just mentioned, and therefore is inflamed more quickly and easily than all the bodies just named, and is consumed more swiftly by inflammation. This is because it has the greatest amount of sulfurousness joined [within it], and it is more **fixed** <note>In this context, "fixed" refers to how strongly the sulfur is bound to the metal's substance, though lead remains easily melted.</note> than in Jupiter. **Mars** <note>original: Mars; the alchemical name for Iron.</note> however, is not burned by its own nature, but **by accident** <note>In medieval philosophy, "by accident" means a property that is not essential to the thing itself, but occurs due to external circumstances.</note>. For when it is mixed with bodies of great humidity, it drinks them up <note>original: combibit; literally "drinks in" or absorbs.</note> because of its own lack of humidity; and therefore, when joined, it is not inflamed nor burned if the bodies united to it are not themselves inflammable or combustible. If, however, the bodies mixed with it are combustible according to the nature of their combustion, it is necessary that Mars also be burned and inflamed.*

<summary>This page explains the varying resistance of the seven planetary metals to fire based on their internal proportions of sulfur and "earthiness." Gold (the Sun) is the most resistant, while Lead (Saturn) is the most easily burned due to its high sulfur content.</summary>

M iiii

*Since, therefore, **cement** <term>cement</term> <note>original: cementum; in alchemical practice, this refers to a corrosive powdered mixture used in a "dry" refining process to strip impurities from noble metals.</note> is composed of inflammable things, the reason for its necessary invention is clear: namely, so that all combustible things might be burned away. Since there is only one single incombustible body <note>The "Sun" or Gold.</note>, only that body, prepared according to its nature, is preserved within the cement. However, certain things endure longer in the cement, while others endure less; which ones last more and which last less is known from the causes already mentioned. Thus, the **Moon** <note>original: Luna; Silver.</note> lasts longer, while **Mars** <note>original: Mars; Iron.</note> lasts less, **Jupiter** <note>original: Iupiter; Tin.</note> even less, **Venus** <note>original: Venus; Copper.</note> less still, and **Saturn** <note>original: Saturnus; Lead.</note> the least of all.*

*Let us therefore describe the method of the cement, as the knowledge of it is most necessary to us in the examination of perfection <note>The "examination of perfection" refers to testing whether a metal has been successfully transmuted or purified into gold.</note>. We say, then, that its composition is made from inflammable things. All things of this kind are substances that blacken, and flee, and penetrate, and burn—such as **Vitriol** <note>original: Vitriolum; sulfate minerals.</note>, **Sal ammoniac** <note>original: Sal armoniacus; ammonium chloride.</note>, and **flower of copper** <note>original: æris flos; likely copper oxide or verdigris.</note>, and old crushed **potter's stone** <note>original: lapis figuli; ground-up ceramics or clay used to provide bulk and withstand heat.</note>, and a very small quantity of **Sulfur** (or none at all), and **male urine**, and similar sharp and penetrating substances. All these things are mixed into a paste with male urine <note>Urine was a common source of nitrogen and ammonia in early chemistry.</note>, and spread upon thin plates of whichever metal is intended to be tested by this judgment.*

*Then, these plates should be laid out upon an iron grate inside a closed earthenware vessel, yet in such a way that no plate touches another, so that the power of the fire may run between them freely and equally. In this way, they should be kept in the earthenware vessel for three days with a strong fire; however, caution must be exercised so that the plates are heated to redness, but not melted. After the third day, you will find the plates clean of all impurity, if the body of the metal was of a perfect nature. But if it was not, they will be found entirely corrupted and consumed by **calcination** <term>calcination</term> <note>original: calcinatio; the process of heating a metal until it oxidizes and turns into a powdery dross.</note>.*

<summary>This page describes the alchemical process of "cementation," a dry refining method used to test the purity of metals by burning away "combustible" impurities using a corrosive paste and prolonged heat. It ranks the durability of various metals (Silver, Iron, Tin, Copper, and Lead) when subjected to this intense purifying fire.</summary>

*Some, however, place plates <note>Metal plates or sheets used in the refining process.</note> for heating without the mixture of **cements** <term>cement</term> <note>original: cementum; a corrosive powdered mixture used to strip impurities from metals.</note>, and they are purified in a similar way, provided they are of a "perfect body" <note>In alchemy, the "perfect bodies" are Gold (the Sun) and Silver (the Moon).</note>. If, however, they are not burned away entirely, a longer period of combustion is required; in this final test, it is necessary that the heating of the fire alone accomplishes the work, rather than the process of those things examined by the judgment of the cement.* 

*But since there is not a great difference between the **Moon** <note>original: Luna; Silver.</note> and the **Sun** <note>original: Sol; Gold.</note>, with only a slight difference in the method of treatment, it [Silver] stands with it [Gold] in this judgment. Neither does a separation of these bodies from each other occur in these two types of testing, except because of the diversity in the composition of their substances. From this results a difference in their melting, their density, and their porosity—which are indeed the causes of separation. Because of their strong composition, their substance is not corrupted by the substance of an external body, since their mixing does not occur at the level of the smallest particles <term>per minima</term> <note>original: per minima; an Aristotelian and alchemical concept meaning that the substances are not unified at a molecular level, allowing them to be separated without losing their essential nature.</note>.* 

*And therefore it is necessary for those things to be separated from the mixture without the total destruction of their essence. Wherefore, the complete treatment of imperfect bodies is recognized when the methods of melting, ignition, and solidity of treatment are discovered through ingenuity.*

administration <note>original: [administra]tionem; refers to the controlled chemical or heat treatment of the metal.</note> of them, for it is necessary to bring the treated bodies to their melting point <term>fusion</term> <note>original: fusionem; the process of liquefying a metal through heat.</note>. And this is to say: before the "bodies of perfection" <note>In alchemy, the "perfect bodies" are Gold and Silver, which do not rust or perish in fire.</note> are melted, they should undergo heating <term>ignition</term> <note>original: ignitionem; heating a substance until it glows, but before it liquefies.</note> with a flame of a celestial color <note>original: cœlestini coloris; a sky-blue or bright blue flame, often associated in early chemistry with the burning of specific impurities or the presence of certain salts.</note> and beauty, before their heating reaches the "whiteness of the fire," in which the eye can see nothing. It is clear, therefore, that their perfect ignition is completed before melting, with an intense redness, and not with a whiteness that the eye cannot behold. For if the treated bodies are melted before they are properly heated to a glow, they are not in a state of completion. If, however, they are heated with great effort and by a strong blast of fire, their treatment is not true. And this indeed occurs in soft metals, whereas that [the latter case] is gathered in **Mars** <note>original: Marte; the alchemical name for Iron.</note> alone. For things that are not easily heated do not readily undergo the ignition of preparation; nor do fusible things undergo the proper melting which we find in the perfect metals according to nature. And if, during this heating, the treated bodies do not project a beautiful flame of celestial color, their treatment is not complete. And if any of the quality of the various preparations found by skill is diminished, the craftsman’s <note>original: artificis; the alchemist or metallurgist.</note> investigation was not sufficient. Therefore, let him search by repeating the process, until he finds it through the methods granted by divine goodness.

## On the Examination of Melting, Chapter 17.

**W**e shall therefore provide a sufficient account of melting, according to how the examination of all these bodies leads to a certain knowledge of them. We say, therefore, that

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section regarding the visual cues—specifically the color of the glow (ignition)—that indicate the quality and "perfection" of a metal during the heating process. Despite the jump in page numbering in the source manuscript (from 85 back to 28), the thematic continuity regarding the "administration" of metals is clear.</meta>

# BOOK

There is only one melting of perfection, specifically with heating <term>ignition</term> <note>original: ignitione; the stage where a substance glows from heat but has not yet liquefied.</note>, but not with just any kind of heating. Rather, it must be with a heating in which the body does not turn entirely white, and with a heating in which no darkness <note>original: fuscedo; a dull, dusky, or muddy appearance in the glow.</note> comes upon the fire, and in which the body is not melted and liquefied so that it flows immediately after the heating. 

Therefore, when a body melts weakly from too much pressure of the fire, either without a glow or with a dark glow, this preparation must belong to one of the imperfect bodies <note>Base metals like lead, tin, or copper which were considered "imperfect" compared to gold and silver.</note> resulting from an inferior craft. And if, after melting, it is allowed to cool, and its glow suddenly turns entirely into blackness, and it loses its glow before it hardens, the body is not in a state of completion, whatever kind of metal it may have been. 

However, it is useful to judge by its softness that such a body belongs to the types of imperfect bodies. And if indeed, with the laborious exertion of the fire, the glow becomes strong and violent before melting, and turns entirely white with a ray of immeasurable brightness, then the body has been altered not by perfection, but by hardness. 

And for this reason, if after its melting it is removed from the fire and suddenly hardens so that it does not flow, while its bright glow remains, the body—whatever kind or preparation it may have been—is no longer of the **Lunar** <note>original: Lunaris; the alchemical term for Silver.</note> or **Solar** <note>original: Solaris; the alchemical term for Gold.</note> perfection; rather, it should be categorized under the nature of the varieties of **Mars** <note>original: Martis; the alchemical name for Iron, noted here for its high melting point and intense white glow.</note>. 

It is clear, therefore, from what has been said, that three degrees of heating can be gathered by experiment in meltable substances before their liquefaction. One, namely, is a dark <note>original: fuscam</note> heating. The second is a clear red. The third is an extremely white heating, shining with a ray...

It distinguishes "perfect" metals (Gold and Silver) from "imperfect" ones and specifically identifies Iron (Mars) by its intense white glow and rapid hardening.</summary>

...ity. The first is of soft bodies. The second is of perfect bodies. The third is of hard bodies, as is proven by the trial of reason. But whoever desires to investigate the degree of all these <term>ignitions</term> <note>original: ignitionum; here referring to the stages of glowing heat a metal reaches before melting.</note>, let him melt all fusible bodies and consider the sufficiency of the fire for a complete perfection of the melting; and by observing, let him gather the difference in the degrees of all the signs of melting. In this way he will find the truth, but otherwise he will not. And let this be brought to you as a model in all the modes of examination determined or yet to be determined by us. And so, let these things said concerning melting be sufficient.

## On the examination by exposing bodies to sharp vapors. Chapter 18.

**P**ursuing, therefore, the completion of our discourse, let us speak of the exposure of bodies over the vapors of sharp substances. We say, then, that we see bodies of perfection <note>The "perfect" metals, gold and silver.</note> exposed over the vapors of sharp things—namely those that are pungent, harsh, and vinegary—either bloom not at all, or emit a most beautiful <term>celestial flower</term> <note>original: caelestinum florem; a sky-blue surface oxidation or patina often used to identify the presence of copper or the purity of the metal.</note>. 

But we find that the purest <term>Sun</term> <note>original: Solem; the alchemical name for Gold.</note> does not bloom. However, we find that <term>the Moon</term> <note>original: Lunam; the alchemical name for Silver.</note>, or impure Gold, when exposed to sharp vapors, does bloom and emits a most lovely celestial color—the Sun more beautifully than the Moon. And for this reason, imitating nature, we likewise create a celestial color in the prepared bodies, which is perfected through the goodness of <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: Argenti uiui; Mercury.</note>, as has been sufficiently told by us in the preceding parts of our discourse.

*Therefore, whatever perfect bodies were held over the vapors of sharp substances, and did not create a pleasant <term>celestial color</term> <note>original: caelestinum colorem; likely a sky-blue or turquoise patina like verdigris.</note>; they are not in the total completion of perfection. And there is a certain one among the bodies, which claims a dark red, or a dark yellow mixed with green, a color that blooms on its surface from the sharpness of harsh things, and of this kind is <term>Mars</term> <note>The alchemical name for Iron.</note>. Truly another blooms a dark green on the surface mixed with a cloudy celestial blue, and of this is <term>Venus</term> <note>The alchemical name for Copper.</note>. Truly another is a dark white, and of this kind <term>Saturn</term> <note>The alchemical name for Lead.</note> is found. Truly another is a clear white, and of this is <term>Jupiter</term> <note>The alchemical name for Tin.</note>. Therefore, because the most perfect body blooms the least, or not at all—and if anything blooms in the slowest space of time, indeed Jupiter blooms the least and slowest among the bodies diminished from the completion of perfection—its <term>gumminess</term> <note>original: gummositatem; in alchemy, this refers to the viscous, oily, or sulfurous quality that holds the metal together.</note> blooms. Therefore, by this examination of the <term>magistery</term> <note>The "mastery" or the successful completion of an alchemical process.</note>, we consider Jupiter to most closely approach perfection in the work of the <term>greater order</term> <note>original: maioris ordinis; refers to the "Great Work" or the creation of the Elixir/Philosopher's Stone to transmute metals into gold.</note>. Through this examination, therefore, it can be investigated in what state the tempered kind of bodies will persist through the treatise of perfection, if you have correctly considered their order, which we have narrated in this chapter: but if not, attribute it to the foolishness of your own rashness.*

## On the testing by the mixing of Sulfur.
### Chapter 20.

**W**hether the <term>magistery</term> <note>original: magisterium; the successful completion of an alchemical process or the "Mastery" of the art.</note> consists in perfection is similarly proven by the mixing of **Sulfur**. For we have found through our experience that Sulfur, when mixed with [metallic] bodies, burns some more and some less; and we have discovered by our craft that some return [unharmed] from that burning, while others do not. From this, therefore, a difference can be noted between the bodies themselves that have been prepared with a diminished perfection in a <term>sophistical</term> <note>refers to "sophistication" or counterfeiting—metals made to look like gold or silver through surface treatments or alloys rather than true transmutation.</note> completion. 

Therefore, when comparing the various bodies of every kind, we find that **Gold** <note>original: Sol; literally "the Sun."</note> is burned the least by Sulfur. After this comes **Tin** <note>original: Iupiter.</note>, then **Silver** <note>original: Luna; literally "the Moon."</note>, and after that **Lead** <note>original: Saturnus.</note>. Indeed, **Copper** <note>original: Venus.</note> is burned more easily than all of these, and **Iron** <note>original: Mars.</note> most easily of all, by the <term>oiliness</term> <note>original: oleaginitatem; in alchemy, sulfur was thought to contain a "fatty" or oily principle that was responsible for its flammability and its ability to "stain" or corrode metals.</note> of the Sulfur. By this test, it is noted which metals approach closer to or further from perfection. 

Furthermore, from the diversity of colors appearing after the burning of the bodies, it can be investigated in what genus the body consists, having been altered from the root of its nature. For **Gold** (Sol) extends an intense yellow-citrine or a clear red color from the burning of Sulfur. **Silver** (Luna) produces a black mixed with sky-blue. **Tin** (Jupiter) produces a black mixed with a modest tincture of redness. **Lead** (Saturn) produces a dark, dusky black mixed with much redness and lividness <note>a grayish-blue or leaden color.</note>. **Copper** (Venus) produces a black mixed with greenness when a great deal of the burning of Sulfur is present; but when little burning is present, it produces a very bright green and a pleasant violet...

<summary>This page describes how to use sulfur to test the purity of metals, noting that gold is the most resistant to sulfur's "burning" while iron and copper are the most reactive. It also provides a guide to the specific colors produced by different metals when subjected to this chemical trial.</summary>
<keywords>sulfur test, metallic perfection, gold, silver, tin, lead, copper, iron, alchemical colors, sophistical metals</keywords>

...[and it] produces color from the combustion of **Sulfur**. **Iron** <note>original: Mars</note>, however, in every kind of combustion, creates a very black-brown color. Furthermore, from the **reduction** <note>The process of attempting to restore a metal to its metallic state after it has been "burned" or calcined.</note> after the combustion by **Sulfur**, a similar diversity is noted in the [metallic] bodies. For some, such as **Gold** <note>original: Sol; "the Sun"</note> and **Silver** <note>original: Luna; "the Moon"</note>, return [to their original state]; others, however, by the application of fire during the reduction with **Sulfur**, vanish—either totally or in their greater part. And [of the] bodies [subjected] to the combustion of **Sulfur**, some return to a body of their own nature, while others return as something other than their own nature. 

The bodies of **Gold** and **Silver** return to their own metallic nature after the combustion of **Sulfur**, but **Tin** <note>original: Iupiter</note> and **Lead** <note>original: Saturnus</note> vanish. **Tin** does so either totally, or in its greater part. **Lead**, however, does not vanish entirely, but sometimes a greater and sometimes a lesser part of it is destroyed. A difference exists between these two—namely **Tin** and **Lead**—due to the nature and the treatment of their bodies mentioned in the preparation of the work; for it happens that from a sudden application of fire during reduction, **Tin** is destroyed. From a successive and gradual application, however, both **Lead** and **Tin** are saved. Yet their reduction results in the nature of a foreign body rather than their own. Our experience has found that the reduction converts **Tin** into a bright increase <note>likely referring to a white or bright metallic dross or alloy</note>, but converts **Lead** into something dark. 

**Copper** <note>original: Venus</note>, indeed, is diminished by the force of the reducing fire. It happens that **Iron** is diminished even more. But the reduction of **Copper** is heavy and yellow-brown <note>original: citrina, fusca; "citrine" usually refers to a dark yellow or orange hue in alchemy</note>. That of **Iron**, however, is white, shining, dark, soft, and partaking in blackness, with an increase of its weight. From these signs, therefore, the nature of all altered bodies can be investigated.

## On the examination of the calcination and reduction of bodies. Chapter 21.

*Next, we must investigate the examination involving the repetition of <term>calcination</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder.</note> and <term>reduction</term> <note>The chemical process of returning a powder back into its solid metallic state.</note>. We have found, therefore, that when bodies of perfection are discovered, they lose nothing of their color, weight, or quantity—at least not enough to be of great concern—nor do they lose their luster during the repetition of <term>calcination</term> and <term>reduction</term>, no matter how many times these multiple operations are repeated upon them. And therefore, if through the repetition of the methods of <term>calcination</term> and <term>reduction</term> from a powder <note>original: calce; "calx," the ash-like substance left after a metal is burned.</note>, any of the qualities of goodness are lost from any kind of altered metals, you should judge that the seeker has investigated the art in a deceitful <note>original: sophistice; meaning the result is a "sophistication" or a fake rather than true alchemy.</note> manner. For this reason, you should practice other methods so that you may know them.*

## On the examination of the easy and difficult mixing of Quicksilver. Chapter 22.

And the sign of this is the easy acceptance of <term>Quicksilver</term> by a <term>Solar</term> <note>Gold</note> or <term>Lunar</term> <note>Silver</note> body of perfection. # The entire work in one summary
## Recapitulation.

**S**ince we have therefore thoroughly treated the known experiences of the sufficiency of the causes of this <term>mastery</term> <note>original: magisterium; the highest skill or the complete alchemical process.</note> according to the requirements of our proposed discourse, it remains for us to reach the completion of the entire divine work in one chapter, and to contract into a summary the mastery that was dispersed through abbreviated chapters. 

We say, therefore, that the sum of the goal of the whole work is nothing else than that the <term>stone</term> <note>A symbolic term for the primary material being transformed, often called the Philosopher's Stone.</note> known from the [previous] chapters be taken; then indeed, with persistent effort, the operation of the first degree of <term>sublimation</term> <note>Heating a substance until it turns to vapor to purify it from solid dross.</note> should be applied to it, so that by this it may be cleansed from corrupting impurity. This is the perfection of sublimation: the stone is sublimated until it reaches the ultimate purity of the process and finally becomes <term>volatile</term> <note>Able to evaporate or turn into gas when heated.</note>. 

From this point, indeed, it should be <term>fixed</term> <note>original: fixio; the process of making a substance stable under heat so it no longer evaporates.</note> through various methods until it remains stable even in the harshness of the fire. This is called the second degree of preparation, and in this lies one of the milestones of perfecting the work. 

But the stone is also treated in a third degree, which constitutes the final completion of the preparation: that is, you must make the already fixed stone volatile again through sublimation, and then make the volatile fixed, and the fixed dissolved, and the dissolved volatile once more, and then the volatile fixed again, until it flows and transforms into a certain <term>gold-making</term> <note>original: Solifico; literally "Sun-making," as the Sun was the alchemical symbol for gold.</note> or <term>silver-making</term> <note>original: Lunifico; literally "Moon-making," as the Moon was the alchemical symbol for silver.</note> completion. From the repetition, therefore...

<summary>This page summarizes the alchemical "Great Work," outlining three main stages: purifying a substance through sublimation, fixing it to withstand fire, and repeatedly alternating its state between solid and vapor. The ultimate goal is to create a perfected substance capable of transmuting metals into gold or silver.</summary>
<keywords>alchemical stages, sublimation, fixation, transmutation, gold-making, silver-making, purification, Philosopher's Stone</keywords>

# THE BOOK OF THE INVESTIGA-
## TION OF THE MASTERY BY GEBER,
### the most sharp-sighted philoso-
### pher, the Prologue.

**I** SHALL hand down to you the investigation of this noble
science, derived from the continuous and frequent diligence of work,
and from excessive study of this work, as well as from our
deepest and various connected thoughts, so that the subsequent volumes 
may be better and more clearly understood by you, and so that, 
once understood, and through the creation and scrutiny of the same, 
they may be more easily and promptly brought to fulfillment. 
And because it is one thing of the art to investigate through reason, and 
another to attempt and test the <term>subtleties</term> <note>original: subtilitates; refers to the intricate details or refined, hidden aspects of alchemical processes.</note> and <term>methods</term> <note>original: ingenia; the clever techniques, "engines" of thought, or specialized procedures used by the alchemist.</note> of these things, 
until, by working, searching, and experimenting, they have arrived at the 
complete intent. Therefore, in this book of mine, whatever we have 
investigated through the things recounted, we have written 
according to the reasoning of my mind—I mean the things that perfect the art. 
Let no one think, however, that [we intended] this investigation, or our book 
which is titled **The Sum of Perfection of the Mastery** <note>original: "Summa perfectionis magisterij"; Geber's most influential alchemical text.</note>, 
in which we have completely finalized according to the order of the 
science whatever we have seen and touched, [to be based on anything other than] 
experience and certain thought, which we noted through our 
scrutiny of the various transforming effects of natural and mineral 
substances appearing in the work; and we have devised our 
science, previously composed, [to be paired] with this investigation

<summary>This page serves as the prologue to "The Investigation of the Mastery," where the author (Pseudo-Geber) explains that this work is a practical companion to his "Summa Perfectionis." He emphasizes that alchemical success requires both theoretical reasoning and hands-on experimental validation of mineral transformations.</summary>

->OF INVESTIGATION.<-

# INVESTIGATION.

*...assembly than that which we proposed for clarification regarding the science itself; therefore, by right, this [book] precedes that one, since through this book I seek to investigate the thought of the perfecting thing. Since, therefore, this science treats the imperfect bodies of minerals insofar as it is fitting to perfect them:* In the first place, therefore, we have considered these two things: namely, imperfection and perfection. Around these two we base our intention. Regarding the things that are perfecting and corrupting—according to what we have investigated through our experience—we compose this book, because opposites placed next to each other shine more clearly. 

Now, the thing which perfects in minerals is the substance of <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argenti uiui"; Mercury, considered by alchemists to be the "mother" or passive principle of all metals.</note> and of <term>Sulfur</term> <note>original: "Sulphuris"; considered the "father" or active principle of metals.</note> mixed together in due proportion by a long and temperate <term>decoction</term> <note>original: "decoctionem"; a process of steady heating or "cooking" within the earth that matures base metals into gold.</note> in the bowels of the clean earth; thickened and fixed with the preservation of its <term>radical humidity</term> <note>original: "humiditatis radicalis"; the essential, "rooted" moisture that allows a metal to be melted and hammered without shattering.</note>, which is not corrupting, but produced into a solid substance that is fusible under proper heat and extendable under the hammer. 

Through the definition of this perfecting nature, we can more easily arrive at the knowledge of the corrupting thing. And it is that which is to be understood in the opposite sense: namely, the impure substance of <term>Quicksilver</term> and <term>Sulfur</term> mixed without due proportion, or decocted either too little or too much in the bowels of the clean earth; neither correctly thickened nor fixed; possessing a combustible and corrupting humidity; and of a thin and porous substance; or having fusion without the proper heat, or none at all, and not sufficiently enduring the hammer. I found the first definition, however, ingrained in these two bodies, namely in the <term>Sun</term> <note>original: "Sole"; the alchemical symbol for Gold.</note> and the <term>Moon</term> <note>original: "Luna"; the alchemical symbol for Silver.</note>.

<summary>Geber defines the chemical composition of metals as a balance of mercury and sulfur, explaining that "perfection" (gold and silver) results from proper "cooking" in the earth, while "imperfection" results from impure ingredients or improper heat.</summary>
<keywords>Quicksilver, Sulfur, Decoction, Radical Humidity, Gold, Silver, Malleability, Mineralogy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of metals and their perfection. While the previous page (numbered 91 in the previous sequence) introduced the primary roles of Quicksilver and Sulfur, this page (numbered 42 in this specific printing) shifts focus to the "imperfect" metals—Tin, Lead, Copper, and Iron—and the necessity of "preparation" to transform them.</meta>

<header>BOOK</header>

...according to the perfection of each. But secondarily in these four—namely in **Tin**, **Lead**, **Copper**, and **Iron**—according to the imperfection of each. And because these imperfect bodies cannot be reduced to health and perfection unless a contrary process is worked within them—that is, that what is manifest <note>Visible or outward qualities.</note> be hidden, and what is hidden be made manifest—and since this operation or "contrariety" is achieved through <term>preparation</term> <note>original: "præparationem"; the chemical processing of a substance to remove impurities or change its state.</note>, it follows that preparation must be applied to them. To prepare is, therefore, to take away what is superfluous and to supply what is absent, and thus to impart a known perfection into them. Perfect bodies <note>Gold and silver.</note> do not require this kind of preparation. These [imperfect metals] require a preparation such that their parts are further refined and reduced from their "corporality" to a fixed "spirituality." <note>Alchemists sought to make metals "spiritual" (subtle or volatile) yet "fixed" (stable and resistant to the fire).</note> The intention of this is to make from them a **fixed spiritual body**: that is, to thin and refine it much more than it was before. Regarding the preparations of all these, according to our investigation, we shall treat them sufficiently in their own place in this book; after they have been sufficiently prepared, they will be fit so that from them the great **White** or **Red Elixir** <note>The "Elixir" or Tincture was the substance used to transform metals; the White produced silver, and the Red produced gold.</note> may be made. For we find that the moderns <note>The author's contemporary alchemical writers.</note> write to us of only a single **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note> for the complete White or Red, which we concede to be true. For from whatever thing the White or Red Elixir is fashioned, there is nevertheless nothing else there than <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>Mercury.</note> and <term>Sulfur</term>, of which one acts nothing without the other, nor can it even exist. And therefore it is called "one Stone" by the philosophers, although it may be extracted from many bodies or things. For to believe one could extract it from a thing in which it does not exist would be a foolish and vain thought, as certain fools have believed...

<summary>The author explains that imperfect metals like lead and iron must undergo "preparation"—a process of refining and balancing hidden and manifest qualities—to become the "fixed spiritual body" necessary for alchemy. He confirms the traditional view that despite various methods, the ultimate Elixir is composed solely of the unified principles of Quicksilver and Sulfur.</summary>

<keywords>imperfect metals, preparation, fixed spirituality, White Elixir, Red Elixir, Philosopher's Stone, Quicksilver, Sulfur, alchemy theory</keywords>
