they thought: which was never the intention of the philosophers. 
Yet they say many things through similarity <note>original: "per similitudinem"; meaning they speak metaphorically or through analogies to hide their secrets.</note>. And because all metallic bodies are composed of **Quicksilver** and **Sulfur**, [being] pure or impure by accident <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, an "accident" is a property that can change without changing the essential nature of the thing (like the color of a shirt).</note> and not innate in their primary nature. Therefore, it is possible for these [impurities] to be removed through suitable preparation. For the stripping away of accidental qualities is not impossible. Preparation is, therefore, to remove what is superfluous and to supply what is lacking in imperfect bodies, which cannot be done without the assistance of [manual] work and purifying substances. For preparation varies according to the diversity of the things in need [of it]. For experience has given us the modes of acting, namely **Calcination**, **Sublimation**, **Descension**, **Solution**, **Distillation**, **Coagulation**, **Fixation**, and **Ceration**. <note>These are the core chemical processes of alchemy: Calcination (burning to ash), Sublimation (turning solid to vapor), Descension (distilling downwards), Solution (dissolving), Distillation (purifying liquids), Coagulation (solidifying), Fixation (making a substance resistant to fire), and Ceration (softening a substance to a wax-like state).</note> Of each of these we have made a sufficient account in the *Summa perfectionis magisterij* <note>original: "The Summary of the Perfection of the Mastery," the most famous work attributed to Geber (the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan).</note>. For these are the operations that assist in preparation. Moreover, the things assisting the preparations are these: namely, all kinds of salts, alums, vitriols <note>original: "atramentorum"; literally "inks," referring to the sulfate minerals used to make black pigment.</note>, and also glass, borax, and things of this nature, as well as very sharp vinegar and fire. ## On the preparation of common salt.

**SALT** <note>original: "SAL commune"; common table salt or sodium chloride</note> should be burned; once burned, let it be dissolved in common water or clear, warm spring water. Once dissolved, let it be distilled <note>In this context, distillation refers to filtration through a cloth or paper filter</note> through a filter. Once distilled, let it be congealed <note>evaporated until solid</note> over a slow fire in a glazed dish or a lead-glazed earthen vessel, and not in metal. Once congealed, let it be calcined <note>heated to a high temperature to remove volatile substances</note> for a day and a night in a moderate fire, and keep it thus sufficiently purified.

<margin>A pointing hand</margin>
## On the preparation of salt of glass.

**SALT**-peter <note>original: "Sal petræ." Curiously, the heading specifies "Salt of Glass" (Sal uitri), but the recipe calls for saltpeter. Salt of glass was often the scum or dross collected from glass furnaces.</note> is dissolved in spring water; once distilled through a filter, it is congealed in a glass vessel until it reaches its own crystalline fusion.

## On the preparation of salt of nitre.

**SALT** of nitre is prepared thus: dissolve glass-fat <note>original: "sagimen uitri"; likely the alkaline dross or "sandiver" that floats on top of molten glass during manufacture</note> in strong water <term>aqua forti: nitric acid, a powerful solvent</term>, distill it through a filter, and congeal it in a glass vessel, and thus it is most excellently clarified.

## On the preparation of rock salt.

**ROCK SALT** <note>original: "Sal gemmæ"; a pure, transparent form of mineral salt</note> is ground, dissolved, and congealed in a glazed earthen vessel, just like common salt.

## On the preparation of alkali salt.

**ALKALI SALT** <note>original: "Sal alcali"; usually potash or soda ash derived from plant remains</note> is made from dissolved soda, distilled through a filter, and boiled down to a third [of its volume]; in time the salt will descend to the bottom of the vessel in the manner of a crystal, and it is [then] prepared. Si-

<meta>This "Si-" is the beginning of the word "Similiter" (Similarly), which continues on the following page.</meta>

<summary>This page provides specific chemical recipes for the purification of five essential salts: common salt, salt of glass, saltpeter, rock salt, and alkali. These processes involve dissolution, filtration, and evaporation to ensure the reagents are "mundatum" (cleansed) for further alchemical work.</summary>
<keywords>common salt, salt of glass, saltpeter, rock salt, alkali salt, purification, aqua fortis, crystallization, calcination</keywords>

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of specific recipes for the preparation of chemical salts. The first word "similarly" (partially appearing as "militer") follows the instructions for rock salt on the preceding page. This section covers alkali salt, ammoniac salt, and salt of tartar, emphasizing purification through distillation, sublimation, and calcination.</meta>

-># ON INVESTIGATION<-

Similarly, **alkali salt** <note>original: "sal alcali"; an alkaline substance, typically potassium carbonate or sodium carbonate, used as a flux or reagent</note> is prepared by some in this way: they take five or two parts of **potash** <note>original: "cineris clauellati"; literally "clubbed ashes," the residue of burnt wood from which lye is leached</note> and one part of **quicklime** <note>original: "calcis uiuæ"; calcium oxide, which makes the lye more caustic</note>. They extract the whole **lye** <note>original: "lixiuium"</note>, then distill and congeal <note>evaporate to a solid</note> it. They repeat this once, and it is prepared. Alternatively, just as with common salt, it may first be ground entirely, dissolved in warm common water, then distilled through a filter, congealed, and calcined with a slow fire.

## On the preparation of ammoniac salt.

**AMMONIAC SALT** <note>original: "Sal armoniacus"; ammonium chloride, highly valued in alchemy for its ability to sublimate and its role in "whitening" metals</note> is made from five or two parts of human urine, one part of the **sweat** <note>original: "sudoris"; while it literally means sweat, in chemical texts it can sometimes refer to a specific liquid distillate</note> of the same, one part of common salt, and one and a half parts of **wood soot** <note>original: "fuliginis lignorum"</note> or twigs. When these have been boiled together until they are consumed in this manner, sublime <term>sublimation: a process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then collected again as a purified solid on a cool surface</term> a true and useful ammoniac salt. Dissolve this again into "sweat" <note>likely referring to its liquid or deliquesced state</note>, congeal it, and sublime it once more from common salt, and it is prepared.

Alternatively, let it first be ground along with the preparation of cleansed common salt; afterward, let it be sublimed in a tall **aludel** <term>aludel: a pear-shaped ceramic pot or a stack of pots used as condensers in the sublimation process</term> until it has been entirely extracted in a pure state. Afterward, let it be dissolved upon a **porphyry stone** <note>original: "porphydum"; a hard, reddish stone used for grinding or as a cool surface for substances to absorb moisture from the air</note> in the open air if one intends to make its "water" <note>a concentrated liquid or oil-like form of the salt</note>; otherwise, let the sublimed and pure substance itself be sufficiently kept.

## On the preparation of salt of tartar.

**SALT OF TARTAR** <note>original: "Sal Tartari"; potassium carbonate, obtained by burning the crusty deposits—tartar—found in wine barrels</note> is made from the calcined and distilled **dregs of wine** <note>original: "fæcibus uini"; the lees or sediment</note>, or from calcined tartar that has been dissolved and congealed, and thus it is prepared.

<summary>This page provides practical recipes for preparing alkali salt, ammoniac salt, and salt of tartar, focusing on purification through lye extraction and sublimation.</summary>
<keywords>alkali salt, potash, quicklime, ammoniac salt, wood soot, sublimation, aludel, salt of tartar, wine dregs, calcination</keywords>

## On the preparation of the salt of urine.

**S**alt of urine is made from the dregs <note>original: "fætibus"; likely a variant spelling or transcription for "fæcibus," meaning the dregs or solid residue</note> of distilled urine which have been calcined <term>calcination: a process of heating a substance to a high temperature, but below its melting point, to bring about thermal decomposition or remove volatile fractions</term> and dissolved again in its own water and congealed <note>evaporated into a solid state</note>. It is of great benefit: many people treat the preparation of salts in various ways. However, through our own investigation, we have found these preparations of salts to be shorter and more useful for achieving our purpose. Since salts are extracted through dissolution from all things that are truly calcined, they are required to be prepared by the aforementioned path; it is enough for us to pass over these with the usual techniques <note>original: "ingenijs consuetis"</note>.

## On the preparation of ice alum or rock alum.

**A**lum of ice or rock <note>original: "Alumen glaciæ uel rocæ"; these terms refer to high-purity potassium alum, which often forms clear, ice-like crystals or large rock-like masses</note> has a two-fold method of preparation: one for washing calcined bodies, and another for sublimating spirits <term>sublimation: a technique where a solid substance is heated until it turns into a vapor and then collects as a purified solid on a cooler surface</term>. 

**The first method.** Dissolve the alum in clear spring water, filter it through a cloth, and boil it until only a third of the volume remains. Place it in glazed dishes, and the prepared crystalline alum will settle around the sides of the vessel and at the bottom. 

**The second method** is to cook the alum in an earthenware vessel until the moisture has vanished; you will then find a white, spongy, and light alum, prepared for sublimations and various other works. As for the dregs remaining at the bottom of the vessel, let them either be dissolved upon a stone <note>placed on a stone slab to absorb moisture from the air and liquefy</note>, or in some moist place, or in the water extracted from it, or let them be re-boiled.

<summary>This page describes the chemical preparation of urine-derived salt and two methods for purifying alum for use in alchemy and metallurgy. The text emphasizes efficiency and the different physical forms of alum required for specific chemical operations.</summary>
<keywords>salt of urine, ice alum, rock alum, calcination, sublimation, purification, chemical reagents, distillation</keywords>

-># OF THE INVESTIGATION<-

## On the preparation of Yemen alum.

**Y**EMEN alum <note>original: "alumen iameni"; a high-quality alum traditionally sourced from Yemen, prized for its purity in medieval chemical arts</note> is prepared in the same manner as ice or rock alum is prepared, except that in this art it possesses a greater power.

## On the preparation of feather alum

**F**EATHER alum <note>original: "alumen plumosæ"; also known as plumose alum, this refers to a naturally occurring fibrous form of aluminum sulfate or sometimes asbestos, named for its hair-like or feather-like crystals</note> is dissolved and prepared just like ice or rock alum. Now it remains to investigate the preparations of vitriols <term>vitriols: a group of sulfate minerals (atramenta) known for their glassy appearance and corrosive properties, used as reagents in alchemy</term>, since they provide us with much that we gather for use in tinctures <note>the process of changing the color or essential nature of a metal</note> and in the bindings of spirits <term>binding of spirits: the process of "fixing" volatile substances so they can withstand heat without evaporating</term> and other things which pertain to the elixir.

## On the preparation of black vitriol.

**B**LACK vitriol <note>original: "atramentum nigrum"; likely an impure iron sulfate or a mixture used specifically for making dark dyes and inks</note> should be dissolved in clean vinegar or in boiling water, then passed through a filter and congealed <note>evaporated back into a solid form</note>, and thus it is prepared. Alternatively, let it first be placed in an alembic <term>alembic: a two-part distillation apparatus consisting of a boiling flask and a cooling cap with a snout</term>, and the total moisture should be extracted. The dregs <note>the solid residue left after distillation</note> from this distillation are to be calcined upon a porphyry stone <note>a very hard, purple-red stone used by alchemists for grinding materials to an extremely fine powder</note>, or dissolved in their own water and congealed; or the water itself may be saved according to the will of the craftsman.

## On the preparation of copperas.

**C**OPPERAS <note>original: "cuperosa"; also known as green vitriol or ferrous sulfate</note> must be dissolved in distilled vinegar, clarified through a filter, and congealed, and in this way it is clean.

## On the preparation of Roman Vitriol.

**R**OMAN vitriol <note>original: "Vitriolum Romanum"; usually refers to iron(II) sulfate or copper(II) sulfate, valued for its ability to produce acidic vapors when heated</note> *requires no other preparation, except that it be held in some ashes over a moderate fire until it turns red.* <note>The process of heating until red is calcination, which dehydrates the vitriol and changes its chemical state.</note> *It is certain that from imperfect bodies* <term>imperfect bodies: base metals, such as lead, tin, or copper, which alchemists believed could be purified or "perfected" into gold or silver</term> *(as we have considered further) various things necessary to us are extracted, and they are aids of perfection in cases where they require preparation.*

## On the preparation of Ceruse.

**C**ERUSE <note>original: "Cerusa"; white lead, a basic lead carbonate used as a pigment and alchemical reagent</note> *must be dissolved in vinegar and thereafter purified from its coarser parts; and that which has emanated like milk is to be congealed in the sun, or over a slow fire, and thus it is prepared.*

## On the preparation of Spanish White.

**I**N THE same way as above, it is done with Spanish white <note>original: "albo hispanico"; while often referring to bismuth or high-quality lead, here the text specifies a tin-based preparation</note>: *white tin is to be dissolved in distilled urine in the manner of ceruse: and thus prepared.* <note>Distilled urine was a common source of ammonia and salts used to react with metals.</note>

## On the preparation of Verdigris.

**V**ERDIGRIS <note>original: "uiride æris"; literally 'green of copper,' the green pigment or crust formed by the action of acetic acid (vinegar) on copper</note> *dissolved in distilled vinegar, and clarified until limpid, is to be congealed by a very slow heat: and it is excellent.*

<summary>This page details the specific alchemical preparations for Roman vitriol, white lead (ceruse), Spanish white (derived here from tin), and verdigris. These processes emphasize dissolution in acidic mediums like vinegar or distilled urine followed by slow evaporation to achieve purity.</summary>

-># RESEARCH<-

### On the preparation of Crocus of Iron.

**C**ROCUS *of iron* <note>original: "Crocus ferri"; a reddish-brown powder, usually iron(III) oxide, named for its saffron-like color</note> *must be dissolved in distilled vinegar and clarified; and this reddish, saffron-colored water, once congealed* <note>in this context, "congealed" refers to evaporating the liquid to leave behind a solid residue</note>*, yields a suitable crocus, and it is finished.*

### On the preparation of Litharge.

**L**ITHARGE <note>original: "Litargirium"; lead(II) oxide, a yellowish-red mineral obtained during the refining of silver from lead ores</note> *should be dissolved in distilled vinegar, clarified, and congealed; for that which is well prepared you can again dissolve, just like the other substances named before, and use them in their dissolved and congealed forms. In this lies deep investigation, and this preparation is completed very quickly.*

### On the preparation of Antimony.

**A**NTIMONY <note>original: "Antimonium"; usually refers to the mineral stibnite, which was processed to extract the metal or used in medicinal and alchemical recipes</note> *is calcined, dissolved, clarified, and congealed, and it is prepared.*

### On the preparation of Lapis Lazuli.

**L**APIS *Lazuli* <note>a deep-blue metamorphic rock used as a semi-precious stone and for the pigment ultramarine</note> *is calcined, ground and washed, and thus it is purified from its earthiness.* <term>earthiness: the crude, non-essential mineral impurities or "dross" within a substance that must be removed to reach its pure essence</term>

### On the preparation of Hematite.

**H**EMATITE <note>original: "Lapis Ematitis"; a heavy, reddish iron ore whose name derives from the Greek word for "blood"</note> *is heated in the fire and quenched repeatedly in bull’s gall and spleen.* <note>Quenching hot minerals in organic fluids like bile was a common alchemical technique believed to imbue the material with specific medicinal or transformative properties.</note>

### On the preparation of Bole.

**B**OLE <note>original: "Bolus"; refers to medicinal clays, such as Armenian Bole, which were used as pigments and in pharmacology</note> *is ground, dissolved just as ceruse* <note>white lead</note> *is, and congealed.*

## On the preparation of Cinnabar.

**C**INNABAR <note>original: "Cinabrium"; a bright red mineral consisting of mercury sulfide, prized as a pigment and as the primary source of mercury</note> is to be prepared once with common salt, and it is very good.

## On the preparation of Tutty.

**T**UTTY <note>original: "Tutia"; a crude form of zinc oxide that collects as a crust in the chimneys of smelting furnaces, often used in medieval eye salves and metallurgy</note> is calcined <note>heated to high temperatures to drive off impurities</note> and dissolved in distilled vinegar, and thus it is prepared.
Various types of Salts, Alums, and Vitriols <note>original: "Atramentorum"; literally "blackening agents," these are metallic sulfates such as copperas or iron sulfate used in making ink and in chemical transformations</note> are still found, all of which are prepared and cleansed in their own kind as already mentioned.

## On the preparation of Glass and Borax.

**G**LASS and Borax <note>original: "Baurachia"; a mineral flux used to help metals melt and flow more easily during soldering or smelting</note>, if they have been made in the proper manner, do not require preparation.

## On the preparation of Very Sharp Vinegar.

**F**URTHERMORE, all types of very sharp vinegar are refined and purified, and their strength or effect is improved through distillation. We have sufficiently discussed their cleansing and purification, with which imperfect bodies <term>imperfect bodies: in alchemy, this refers to base metals like lead, tin, copper, or iron, which were believed to be "sick" or "incomplete" versions of gold and silver</term> can be prepared, purified, improved, and refined, always with the proper fire mediating— <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword "te" suggests the next page begins with the completion of the word "mediante" (mediating).</note>

<summary>This page outlines the purification of cinnabar, tutty, and various mineral salts, while noting that properly made glass and borax require no further treatment. It emphasizes the role of distilled vinegar and heat in refining "imperfect" base metals.</summary>
<keywords>cinnabar, tutty, vitriol, borax, distilled vinegar, calcination, imperfect bodies, purification, alchemy, chemical preparation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the refinement of metals. The previous page ended with a mention of how imperfect bodies (base metals) can be "prepared, purified, improved, and refined." This page details the specific flaws within these metals—such as "superfluous humidity" and "combustible sulfur"—and explains the methodology for removing these accidental impurities using fire and corrosive agents to reach the pure substance required for the alchemical elixir.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>
<header>OF INVESTIGATION.</header>

# OF INVESTIGATION.

...refined. For they are prepared and purified by these means for the application of fire in this manner. For these imperfect bodies <term>imperfect bodies: base metals like lead, tin, or copper, which alchemists believed were "diseased" versions of gold and silver</term> possess superfluous humidities and a combustible sulfurousness, which generates a blackness when mixed within them, corrupting the aforementioned bodies. They also possess an unclean, foul, and combustible earthiness, which is too coarse, hindering penetration and melting. These and such things are the superfluities in these aforementioned bodies, which have been discovered in them by our experience through certain and ingenious investigation. 

And because these superfluities come upon these bodies accidentally, and not fundamentally <note>original: "radicaliter"; at the root or essence of the metal</note>, and since the stripping away of accidental qualities is possible, we must, with artificial fire, cleanse these aforementioned things and remove all accidental superfluities, so that only the substance of Quicksilver <note>original: "Argenti vivi"; mercury</note> and radical Sulfur remains. And this is the complete preparation of the imperfect [bodies], and their perfect purification, improvement, and refinement. 

The treatment of these, or of this remaining pure substance, is done in many ways according to what the elixir <term>elixir: the sought-after transformative substance or "Philosopher's Stone" capable of perfecting metals</term> requires. Therefore, the general method of preparation and purification is as follows: 

First, all superfluous and corrupt humidity within their essence must be raised up <note>i.e., evaporated or driven off</note> with a proportional fire; likewise, the subtle and burning superfluity must be removed, and this is done by calcining <term>calcining: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder, known as a calx</term>. Then, the total remaining corrupted substance—within their calx of burning superfluous humidity and blackness—is to be corroded with these aforementioned sharp or very biting cleansed corrosives <note>likely strong acids or mineral salts</note>, until the calx <note>the powdered residue</note> becomes white or red, or colored according to the nature and property of the body, and is cleansed and pure from all superfluity.

<summary>This page explains that base metals contain accidental impurities like excess moisture and "foul earthiness" that must be removed through fire and calcination. By stripping away these qualities, the alchemist recovers the pure mercury and sulfur necessary to create the elixir.</summary>
<keywords>purification, imperfect bodies, calcination, quicksilver, radical sulfur, elixir, corrosives, alchemy</keywords>

<header>BOOK</header>

# BOOK

...or the corruption previously mentioned: and these [bodies are treated] with these corrosives and cleansing agents by grinding, soaking, and washing. Afterward, however, the entire unclean earthiness, and the foul, combustible, and coarse [matter] must be destroyed or cast off. This is done by using the aforementioned cleansed things—or pure substances that do not melt like metals—mixed and well-ground with the aforementioned <term>calx: a powder or dry residue left after a metal has been roasted or "calcined"</term> which has been purified in the manner previously stated. During fusion or the <term>reduction: the process of restoring a metal from its powdered "calx" form back to a solid metallic state</term> of the calx, these additives will retain the coarse and unclean earthiness within themselves, while a pure body remains, cleansed of every corrupting superfluity. From this, the general method for the descent, improvement, and refinement of this pure substance is as follows:

First, this purged and reduced body is to be <term>calcined: heated to a high temperature to remove volatile substances without melting the metal</term> again with fire and the aforementioned cleansing aids. Then it is to be dissolved with those things that are resolutive, for this water is "our stone" <note>original: "lapis nofter"; a reference to the Philosopher's Stone in its liquid or "mercurial" phase</note>, and "Quicksilver from Quicksilver" <note>original: "Argentum uiuum de Argento uiuo"</note>, and "Sulfur from Sulfur," a spiritual body extracted and refined or thinned. This can be improved by strengthening the elemental virtues within it using other preparations made from its own kind: and by increasing the color, <term>fixion: the state where a substance remains stable and does not evaporate when exposed to heat</term>, weight, purity, fusion, and all other things pertaining to the perfect elixir. 

And this is the method, investigated by us alone, for the preparation, purification, refinement, and improvement of mineral bodies in general. Now let us pass to the special or particular preparation of any imperfect body with all its methods, and also of the perfect mineral body—and first concerning Jupiter <note>original: "Ioue"; in alchemical shorthand, the planet Jupiter represents the metal Tin</note>.

## Concerning the preparation of Jupiter [Tin]. <insert>Chapter 2.</insert>

**T**he preparation of <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for the metal Tin</term> in particular is performed in many ways, but best by this method. It is as follows: let it be placed in a <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to a high temperature to remove volatile impurities or to oxidize it</term> furnace in a vessel suitable for this purpose, and let fire be applied beneath it until the body itself is well melted. Then, stir the liquefied body with a perforated iron spatula, removing the <note>original: "spolia"; refers to the dross or oxide film that forms on the surface of molten metal</note> "spoils" that are created upon it. Continue stirring while the heat of the fire remains steady until a good quantity of powder has gathered on the surface. Let that powder then be set aside, and let the metal be stirred again until, by turns, the entire body has been reduced into a very fine powder. 

Therefore, let that powder be sifted and placed back in the furnace, applying a strong fire to it—one that does not exceed its melting point—and stirring it many times. Let it remain in the fire of its calcination in this way for a <note>original: "diem naturalem"; a "natural day" refers to a full 24-hour period</note> natural day or thereabouts. Finally, once all accidental moisture and superfluity have been destroyed, along with the combustible and corrupting Sulfur, then let the cleansed <term>white calx: the white powder or oxide left after tin has been thoroughly roasted</term> be removed. 

For the fire raises up and consumes every fleeting and flammable substance from the aforementioned things; specifically, by washing it many times with cleansed common salt and alum, and with purified and very sharp vinegar. Then, let it be placed in the sun or in the air. Let it be ground, washed, and dried again, and let this be repeated in turn until, through the sharpness of the salts, the alum, and the vinegar, all of its moisture, blackness, and impurity have been consumed, eroded, and destroyed. Then, let some crushed glass be added with these pre-

We ought to honor this water, for it is exactly what we seek for the <term>white work: the stage of the alchemical process intended to produce silver</term>. ## Concerning the Greater Perfection of Perfect Bodies.

**P**erfect bodies <note>In alchemy, the "perfect bodies" are gold and silver, which do not rust or perish in fire.</note> indeed do not require preparation as far as their perfection is concerned, since they are already perfect; rather, they are prepared so that they might be further perfected, refined, and thinned: for such a preparation must be employed. Let <term>Sol: the alchemical name for gold</term> be taken, drawn out into thin plates, and placed with very well-prepared common Salt, layer by layer <note>original: "solariatim," likely referring to the arrangement of the plates or a specific solar-aligned preparation</note>, into a vessel of <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</term>, and let it be placed in the furnace to be well calcined for three days, or until the whole has been consumed and finely calcined; then let it be extracted, ground very well, washed with vinegar, and dried in the sun: afterwards let it be ground well with half its weight of cleansed <term>Sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, used as a powerful solvent</term>, and let it be set to dissolve, until the whole, by the benefit of the common Salt and the Sal ammoniac, has been dissolved into a most clear water. This <term>ferment: a substance that initiates a transformative process, like yeast in bread</term> is a perfect, precious elixir, and a true <term>spiritual body: a solid metal that has been rendered liquid or volatile</term> made manifest.

BOOK I

## On the Refining and Perfection of Silver.

**S**ILVER is refined and thinned, and reduced to a spiritual state <note>In alchemy, "spiritual" refers to a substance that has been purified and processed into a volatile or highly refined form, often through sublimation or dissolution.</note> through the method already mentioned. Therefore, perform each and every step in this refining just as you did for <term>Sol: the alchemical name for gold</term>. And the water of dissolved <term>Luna: the alchemical name for silver</term> is the ferment for the white spiritual <term>elixir: the transformative substance sought by alchemists; the "white" version was intended to transmute metals into silver</term> that has been produced.

### Recapitulation of the Whole Work.

**N**OW we have sufficiently described the preparation of the <term>imperfect bodies: base metals such as lead, tin, copper, and iron</term> and the refining of the perfect ones, so that any discerning person may fulfill their purpose regarding them. Therefore, let one pay attention to the modes of the property of action, or the composition of the greater elixir. For we seek to create a single substance, though aggregated and joined together from many, which is <term>fixed: a substance that is stable when heated and does not evaporate or fly away from the fire</term>; so that when it is placed upon the fire, the fire does not drive it out, but rather it mingles with melted things and melts along with them. It should do this by means of that which is in it of a penetrating substance, and be mixed with that which is of a miscible <note>capable of being mixed</note> substance, and be consolidated with that which is of a consolidating substance, and be fixed with that which is of a fixing substance. It must not be burned by those things which do not burn Gold and Silver, and it should bring about consolidations with a proper and perfect ignition. However, do not understand "in a short time" to mean that it can be established in just a few days or hours on the first attempt. Rather, it is so called because, in comparison to other me[thods of the authorities...] <note>The sentence ends abruptly at the catchword "dicorum," likely referring to "medicorum" (the doctors or philosophers of alchemy).</note>

<summary>This page concludes the refining process for silver (Luna) and begins a summary of the Great Work, defining the ideal properties of the Alchemical Elixir as a stable, penetrating, and fire-resistant substance.</summary>
<keywords>silver, Luna, gold, Sol, elixir, fixation, alchemical properties, refining, white elixir, spiritual state</keywords>

*...of the modern doctors, also in respect to the operation of nature,*

*cleansed by natural proportion, and joined by such a union*
*that it cannot be separated from itself. To these a third thing must be added to*
*shorten the work: that is, a thinned <term>perfect body: gold or silver that has been processed into a fine or "spiritualized" state</term>. From the*
*foregoing, therefore, the things in which the truth is near are evident, and*
*those completing the work itself. Indeed, by our investigation, by which*
*we are certain, and through manifest experience, we have considered*
*all these words to be true, which have now been written by us alone in our*
*volumes, according to what we have seen through experiment*
*and reason reduced into these volumes. Indeed, those things which we*
*have extracted with our fingers through our experience, and*
*seen with our eyes, and touched with our hands, we have written in the* Summa...

Through this consideration and through our own practice, we have found that the corruption of imperfect things has occurred accidentally,* <note>In alchemical philosophy, "accidental" means a quality that is not part of the essential nature. This implies that base metals can be "cured" of their impurities to become gold.</note> *which gave the matter a new and corrupt form.*

# THE TESTAMENT OF GE-
## BER, THE MOST PERCEPTIVE PHILOSOPHER
### and King of India. Concerning the Salts of Animals, Fish, Birds, Vegetables, and others. Chapter 1.

**F**rom all things, even from animals, fish, and birds, a fixed salt <note>In alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that remains stable in the fire and does not evaporate or "fly" away.</note> can be made by burning them and reducing them to ashes: in the same manner that salt is made from the ashes of wood, or from the lime of stone. And if the matter was naturally red, such as blood, its salt will naturally be red. However, the combustion of these things must be performed in a closed vessel. If the matter is blood, let it first be dried; it is also not a bad thing if, in eight parts of the matter, you dissolve two parts of common salt <term>common salt: sodium chloride, which was often calcinated (heated to high temperatures) to remove impurities before use in alchemical recipes</term> calcinated in any furnace. Then dry it, and afterwards burn it in a glassmaker’s or potter’s furnace, or in a reverberatory furnace <note>A furnace that reflects or "reverberates" the flame and heat down onto the material being heated, rather than heating it directly from below.</note> for a day and a night, or intermittently for two days without the nights. But if it should be the flesh of some animal that cannot be dried, then burn it for two natural days without any salt in a vessel well-luted so that it cannot breathe <note>To "lute" a vessel (original: "lutato") means to seal it with a paste of clay, dung, or other materials to make it airtight and heat-resistant.</note>. After the combustion, boil it in four times its weight of rainwater until two and a half parts are consumed. Then let it settle and distill it through a filter <note>Alchemical "distillation through a filter" often refers to a process of wicking or gravity filtration to clarify a liquid.</note> until the water becomes clear without any cloudiness. Finally, congeal <note>original: "congela," here meaning to crystallize or solidify by evaporating the liquid.</note> it over a slow fire, or what is better, in the sun; you will then have the salt of the animal, which surpasses others in its power of fusion <note>original: "virtute fusiva," the ability to melt or to act as a flux to help other substances melt.</note>. Then resolve it again in twice its volume of water...

<insert>
Above all else, I desire that all the recipes of the Testament be revealed. <note>original: "sopra a l'altro desidero si scuopri no, tutte le ricette del Testamento." This Italian insertion appears to be a later reader's or owner's note expressing a wish to see the full contents of this secret work.</note>
</insert>

rainwater, and place this kind of Salt under dung <note>Dung was used by alchemists as a source of gentle, steady "moist heat" for fermentation or slow digestion.</note> for a week and it will become sharp. Finally, congeal it as has been said, or do it without dung, and once placed upon a metal, see if it enters <note>In this context, "entering" refers to the salt penetrating or permeating the metal to transform its properties.</note> it. Animal salt is good, but if it does not work, repeat its solution and congelation, and its effectiveness will be declared to you through experience. Know also that the Salt of a whole burnt mole congeals Mercury <term>Mercury: the metal quicksilver, representing the principle of volatility</term>, and converts Venus <note>original: "Venerem" — the alchemical name for Copper</note> into the Sun <note>original: "Solem" — the alchemical name for Gold</note>, and Mars <note>original: "Martem" — the alchemical name for Iron</note> into the Moon <note>original: "Lunam" — the alchemical name for Silver</note>. ## On the Salts of Metals.

**F**rom metals also, Salts are made after their calcinations <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the air to reduce it to a powder or "lime"</term>, and if these Salts have a penetrating and fixing power, it is by reason of their refinement and sharpness, because they are made from fixed things and have been long calcinated in the fire. But because metals are calcinated in the fire in different ways, we must first look at the calcination of those that lead to the red <note>The "Red" refers to the Great Work or "Rubedo," the final stage in creating the Philosopher's Stone that makes gold.</note>, such as the Sun, Venus, Saturn <note>original: "Saturnus" — the alchemical name for Lead</note>, and Mars. Finally, we look at those that are effective for the white <note>The "White" or "Albedo" is the stage of the work that produces silver.</note>, such as the Moon and Jupiter <note>original: "Iupiter" — the alchemical name for Tin</note>.

## On the Calcination of Gold.

**T**he calcination of the Sun <note>Gold</note> is performed in an open vessel in a glassmaker’s furnace for ten continuous days, or for twenty days; or in a reverberatory furnace for three days. Finally, extract its Salt with distilled rainwater through an alembic <term>alembic: a classic distillation apparatus consisting of a boiling flask and a cooling cap to collect vapors</term> once or twice by boiling it in a copper vessel; let the water be four times the amount of the lime <note>The "lime" (calx) is the powder left over after calcination.</note>, and finally congeal it: and if you again <margin>calcinate it</margin>

Do not wonder at this, since every red thing, once its tincture <note>In alchemy, a "tincture" is the concentrated essence or color of a substance that has the power to transform other things.</note> is removed, remains white: because under the redness, whiteness remains hidden, but it is not converted. And for that reason, because whiteness is beneath the redness, I say that if for ten days you calcinate those dregs <note>original: "fæces" — the solid residue left after a distillation or extraction</note> alone without any mixture, and finally as was said of the Salt, you extract the Salts, and afterwards you again calcinate their dregs remaining from the Salt into earth, they will be converted into whiteness. And if you reduce <note>To "reduce" a metal means to return it from its powdered/calcined state back into its solid, metallic form.</note> them: it will be true living silver <note>original: "Argentum uiuum" — Quicksilver or Mercury</note>. Therefore I say that if you return the Salt of the Sun <note>original: "Salem Solis" — Salt extracted from Gold</note> to its dregs that have been converted into whiteness, an elixir is made, as much for tinting as for converting true silver into gold, and for tinting prepared copper <note>original: "æs" — here referring specifically to Venus or copper</note> and Lead.

## On the Calcination of Venus:

**V**ENUS <note>The alchemical name for Copper</note> is calcinated in an open vessel for 35 days, or rather for two months in a glassmakers' furnace: and then only its red tincture remains in the appearance of blood. From this, extract the Salt as was said of the Sun [Gold], and it will become an elixir. And know that the Salt of any metal, if made in the proper way, fixes and tints Mercury and changes it from color to color, and from state to state by reason of its sharpness <note>In this context, "sharpness" refers to the acidity or corrosive power of the salt.</note>.

## On the Calcination of Saturn:

**S**ATURN <note>The alchemical name for Lead</note> is calcinated into a lemon-yellow color in a reverberatory furnace, and it is better done in a closed vessel, or a glassmakers' furnace. Which indeed in 15 or 35 [days]...

## On the Calcination of Mars.

**M**ARS <note>Mars is the alchemical name for Iron.</note> is calcinated <term>calcination: the process of heating a metal to a high temperature in the presence of air to reduce it to a powder or "calx"</term> if it has been filed, soaked with red vinegar, and dried by a good fire or intermittently. Afterwards, let it be burned in a reverberatory furnace <term>reverberatory furnace: a furnace that reflects or "reverberates" heat from the roof onto the material, avoiding direct contact with the fuel</term> or a glassmaker's furnace for thirty days. From this, make salt as has been said of the others. The salt extracted from the calx of Mars, and congealed in the sun, and again dissolved by itself under dung <note>Alchemists used the gentle, steady heat produced by fermenting horse dung for slow dissolutions.</note>, and afterwards congealed again in the sun—if one part of it is placed over two parts of Mercury that has been well-washed, inside a well-sealed flask <note>original: "ampulam"</note> for half a day in hot ashes, it congeals the Mercury into a powder. This powder will become redder if a stronger fire is applied. It also "kills" <note>original: "mortificat" — to "mortify" a metal meant to strip it of its metallic appearance or "living" fluidity, as with quicksilver.</note> other living Mercury [quicksilver] in a similar way. This is the Mercury you can soak with the water in which you dissolved the calx of Mars, and finally the calx of Gold.

## On the Calcination of Luna.

**L**UNA <note>Luna is the alchemical name for Silver.</note> is calcinated <term>calcination: the process of heating a metal to a high temperature to reduce it to a powder</term> for five days in the aforementioned furnaces, and finally with alkali salt; however, it must first be purged by melting <note>original: "fusionem" — the process of melting or liquefaction</note>. After you have performed this, it tints Mercury into a fixed white <note>In alchemy, a "fixed" substance is one that remains stable under heat and does not evaporate.</note> substance. With this Mercury, operate as previously described upon any metal, and it will whiten it with a fixed and permanent whiteness. You can, moreover, "multiply" <note>Multiplication is the process of increasing the quantity or potency of the alchemical medicine.</note> upon Mercury more effectively toward the "white" stage than toward the "red," since one part of this salt tints forty parts of Mercury through multiplication by projecting the tinted Mercury upon that which is not tinted. But this is not the case for the "red" stage <note>The "white" stage of alchemy (Albedo) aims for silver, while the "red" (Rubedo) aims for gold.</note>, because it requires a greater medicine to be transmuted by nature than to be added to nature. And one part of this Mercury, [when placed] upon ten parts of whitened and softened copper <note>original: "æris" — copper or bronze</note>, converts it into Luna [Silver].

## On the Calcination of Jupiter.

**J**UPITER <note>Jupiter is the alchemical name for Tin.</note> is benevolent among the other planets. It is calcinated in a closed vessel in the aforementioned furnaces—namely those of the glassmakers, or of reverberation—and in the same manner as described above. Moreover, salt is made from it with rainwater, or other water distilled through an alembic <term>alembic: a distillation apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</term>. You will find this salt to be much sharper than the others for congealing Mercury, and of greater efficacy than the salt of Luna, on account of the greater decoction <note>original: "decoctionem" — a long process of boiling or heating</note> and because it contains "living" Mercury <note>Quicksilver or raw mercury.</note>. For this reason, the greater part of it is sublimated <term>sublimation: heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again</term>; and with common salt, and salt of nitre <note>Saltpeter, often used as a flux or purifying agent.</note>...

## On the Moon.
<note>original: "Luna" — the alchemical name for Silver.</note>

**S**ILVER in its inner depth is Gold, but it seems to contain within itself a tincture <term>tincture: in alchemy, a substance or "dye" capable of imbuing another material with its properties, such as color or stability</term> of whiteness however long it remains in the fire, unless perhaps it is calcinated for many days. Finally, once its Salt has been extracted, if its dregs <note>original: "fæces" — the solid residue left after a chemical process</note> were calcinated, it would perhaps not be impossible for them to be turned into red <note>Redness usually signaled the final stage of the alchemical work, indicating the presence of Gold.</note>. However, it is changed by an external tincture, not by its own power, but through the aid of other things.

## On Jupiter.
<note>original: "Iupiter" — the alchemical name for Tin.</note>

**T**IN, indeed, calcinated for seven days and finally reduced <term>reduction: the chemical process of returning a metal from its oxide or salt form back to its metallic state</term> with salt of nitre <note>Salt of nitre: potassium nitrate or saltpeter.</note>, or alkali, and egg white, and sal ammoniac <note>Sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, a salt used to clean metals or as a flux.</note>, and soft soap, and rock borax, will be true Silver. This is because tin is itself Silver, but "cooked" <note>original: "decoctū" — alchemists believed metals "ripened" or cooked in the earth over time; tin was seen as undercooked silver.</note> for only a short time. Therefore, without any other medicine <note>In alchemy, a "medicine" refers to the elixir or agent used for transmutation.</note>, it will be able to stand in the fire long enough to be found turned into true Silver. Concerning this, note also that you should reduce the calx <note>The powder produced by calcination.</note> intended to be converted into Gold with egg yolks, or their oil and red tartar <note>Tartar: a potassium salt (cream of tartar) found in wine barrels; red tartar was used for gold-making, white for silver.</note>, because tartar is of great value in the reductions and fusions of bodies. Alum <term>alum: a mineral salt used for many purposes, including purifying or fixing colors</term> and white tartar should be used in reductions to white <note>i.e., for making Silver.</note>. Also, note that every perfect medicine contains these five virtues: it penetrates through the smallest parts, it softens, it tints, it truly fixes <term>fixation: making a substance stable in heat so that it no longer evaporates or changes</term>, and it purges. These virtues Mercury <note>Mercury: quicksilver.</note> contains with Salt...

->TESTAMENT<-

Fixed Auliar. <note>original: "Auliar fixatus" — likely a term derived from Arabic alchemy referring to a specific alkaline salt or "the salt of alkali" used as a flux.</note> Likewise, all salts, whether of animal or metallic origin, have the same efficacy, especially when joined with Mercury <note>In alchemy, Mercury (Quicksilver) was considered the "spirit" or "mother" of all metals.</note>. It has been said that every metal, when it has been well calcinated <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to a high temperature in the air to remove volatile parts and reduce it to a powder</term>, produces a Salt just as ashes, stones, and limes do. And with that salt, Mercury is perfectly tinctured <term>tincture: a substance or "dye" capable of imbuing another material with its properties, such as color or stability</term>. And it is that Mercury which transmutes all bodies, just as it is itself transmuted, which is the greatest secret. And it appears that the calcination of bodies is the shorter path to perfection.

However, concerning Jupiter <note>original: "Iouem" — the alchemical name for Tin.</note>, it should be noted that it is deficient in itself, not indeed by the fault of sulfurousness, but because it shares in a non-fixed humidity <note>In the alchemical worldview, metals were composed of "Sulfur" (dryness/combustibility) and "Mercury" (humidity/fluidity). "Non-fixed" means it evaporates too easily.</note> which it contracted from Mercury, its father. Hence, in calcination for its perfection, it requires a tempered fire, because otherwise it would be consumed too much. Now, a tempered decoction <term>decoction: the process of boiling or heating a substance over time to extract or concentrate its essence</term> thickens the humidity, and this thickening consumes [the excess moisture]. Therefore, let this decoction be done with wood shavings, and it ought to be placed underground with ash on top. And afterwards, let a fire be made on top. Keep this a secret.

## On Saturn.
<note>original: "De Saturno" — the alchemical name for Lead.</note>

**I** say the same regarding Saturn <note>Lead</note> if you wish to Lunify <note>original: "Lunificare" — to transform into silver (Luna/The Moon).</note> it. But it is otherwise if you wish to Solify <note>original: "Solificare" — to transform into gold (Sol/The Sun).</note> it, because this is not done except through the greatest fire, and then it is made red and transmuted.

## On Mars.
<note>original: "De Marte" — the alchemical name for Iron.</note>

**M**ARS <note>Iron</note>, because it has a fixed substance, is better among the other bodies; yet it is a work of long labor on account of its excessive dryness. But if you were able to...

## Concerning the calx of Saturn, Venus, and Mars

**N**OTING regarding the calx <term>calx: the powdery metallic oxide formed after a metal has been roasted or "calcinated"</term> made of Saturn <note>Lead</note>, Venus <note>Copper</note>, and Mars <note>Iron</note>: they must be calcinated so thoroughly that they can in no way be reduced back into a metallic body, nor be diminished further, nor be changed from one color to another, and let it be a most sharp calx. And then their salts remain; dissolve these often under dung <term>under dung: placing a vessel in a pile of fermenting horse manure to provide a steady, gentle heat for digestion</term>, and coagulate them in the Sun until it becomes like an oil. Then it will "fix" <term>fix: to turn a volatile substance, like Mercury, into a solid that can withstand heat without evaporating</term> all Mercury, and in that Mercury, another Mercury, even unto infinity.

## Concerning the Salts of metals

**N**OTE that if you mix the Salts of metals with Mercury through sublimation <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then collects as a solid on a cooler surface</term>, and Sal ammoniac, and finally dissolve it under dung, and congeal it in the Sun, and repeat the dissolution and congelation three or more times, you will congeal crude, washed Mercury, and this you will be able to multiply unto infinity.

## Also concerning the Salts of metals.

**S**ALT of metals, therefore, you should extract with distilled fresh water or with "water of life" <term>water of life: aqua vitae, or concentrated alcohol, used as a powerful solvent</term> by boiling; congeal it in the sun, and dissolve it again into water under dung <term>under dung: a common alchemical method of providing gentle, steady heat by burying a vessel in fermenting horse manure</term>, and distill it through wool <note>a method of filtration where liquid is drawn through wool fibers via capillary action</note>, and congeal it. One part of this perfectly tints <note>original: "tingit" — to color or transform the nature of a metal, a key step in creating silver or gold</note> one hundred parts of Moon <note>the alchemical name for Silver</note> or Saturn <note>the alchemical name for Lead</note>.
Likewise, one part of it fixes <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance, like Mercury, stable and resistant to fire</term> five parts of raw Mercury, and any part of that [mixture] tints a hundred.

## Also concerning the Salts of metals.

**S**OME say that the Salt extracted from metals must be calcined <note>reduced to a fine powder through high heat</note>. Also note that it is better for the Salt to be extracted from calcined bodies with distilled urine that has been previously boiled and skimmed of its froth, or with water of life made from white wine for "white" calxes <note>likely referring to silver-producing materials</note>, and for "red" calxes <note>gold-producing materials</note> with distilled vinegar. Then combine the Salt with Mercury sublimated from sal ammoniac <term>sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride, used to help volatile substances rise or "sublimate" during heating</term>. Also note that regarding the Salt of metals and their calxes, you have no other work to do except to give them "entry" <note>original: "ingressum" — the ability of the alchemical medicine to penetrate and merge with a base metal</note>.

Also note that whether you extract the Salt from red calxes with distilled water or with distilled vinegar, you should congeal it gently in the sun or by fire, and let the water dissolve by itself. With that water, boil washed quicksilver <note>Mercury</note>—which has been dissolved under dung—over a slow fire in a "urinal" <note>a glass vessel shaped like a specimen jar, used for digestion</note> closed at the top; or, with the water of the aforementioned Salt and Mercury, dissolve it in the manner described above with the calx of Gold.

*...each substance having first been sublimated <term>sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification</term> by itself. ### Concerning the Mercury of Jupiter.

**N**OTE *concerning the Mercury of Jupiter <note>Tin</note>, and its sublimation, which is perhaps sublimated with Saltpeter <term>Saltpeter: potassium nitrate, a powerful oxidizer</term>. Try to sublimate Jupiter in this way: Take 3 ounces of it, which you should amalgamate <note>mix into a soft paste with mercury</note> with three ounces of Mercury, and one ounce of sal ammoniac, and 3 ounces of lime made from eggshells or gypsum; sublimate these together for a short time. Then, whatever remains at the bottom of the urinal <term>urinal: a round-bottomed glass vessel used in laboratories, named for its shape</term> after the sublimation, should be reduced back into a metallic body, to which you may join Silver if you wish.*

*Also note that the Salt of Metals, when dissolved, distilled, and congealed in the sun, allows Mercury to be congealed <note>solidified or "fixed"</note> in a crucible <term>crucible: a heat-resistant container used for melting metals</term>.*

*Likewise, the Salt of Metals allows Mercury to be dissolved in water under dung <note>a method of providing gentle, steady heat by burying a vessel in fermenting horse manure</note>. And that mixture, once coagulated, will be a perfect medicine <term>medicine: in alchemy, a "medicine" or "elixir" is the substance capable of "curing" base metals by transforming them into gold or silver</term>.*

# BOOK OF THE THREE WORDS
## of the most sharp-witted Khalid. <note>Khalid ibn Yazid (d. 704 AD) was an Umayyad prince credited in alchemical tradition as one of the earliest practitioners and translators of the art.</note>

### On the quality of the Philosophers' Stone.
### Chapter one.

**T**HIS STONE from which this work is made has within itself all colors. For it is white, red, most intensely red, yellow, most intensely yellow, celestial <note>original: "caelestinus" — a sky-blue or azure color</note>, green, and heavy. In this stone are the four elements <term>four elements: the classical building blocks of matter—Earth, Air, Fire, and Water—which alchemists believed could be rebalanced to transform substances</term>. For it is watery, airy, fiery, and earthy. In this stone, heat and dryness are hidden, and coldness and moisture are manifest; it is necessary therefore for us to hide the manifest, and to make manifest that which is hidden. Moreover, that which is in the hidden—namely heat and dryness—is an oil, and this oil is dry; that dryness colors <note>original: "tingit" — to tinge or dye, meaning to transmute the color and nature of a base metal into gold</note> and nothing else, because the alkali <term>alkale: a fixed salt or alkaline substance believed to provide the "fixing" power that stops spirits from evaporating</term> and nothing else colors. That which is manifest—the cold and the moist—is a watery, corrupting vapor. It is necessary therefore that this moisture and coldness be made equal to the heat and dryness, so that it does not flee from the fire; because between that coldness and moisture there is a single hot and dry particle. It is necessary therefore that the cold and the moist receive the heat and dryness which was hidden, and they become one substance. Moreover, that coldness and moisture is a corrupting and watery vapor of which it is said, that watery and parching <note>original: "adustiva" — burning or parching; a term used for substances that consume or oxidize metals rather than perfecting them</note> moisture corrupts the body and colors it black.

<meta>catchword: these [istus]</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the "Book of the Three Words" by Khalid, defining the Philosophers' Stone as a substance containing all colors and elements. It explains the central alchemical principle of inverting qualities: hiding the "manifest" moisture and coldness while making the "hidden" internal heat and dryness manifest to achieve transmutation.</summary>

The goal is that that which is in the hidden may become manifest, and that which is manifest may become hidden through the power of God and through heat, such as dryness. For the Persian philosophers <note>Alchemical texts often cited "Persian" or "Chaldean" masters to claim a lineage of ancient, exotic wisdom</note> say that watery and burning coldness and moisture are not friendly to heat and dryness, because heat and dryness destroy the cold and moist, watery and burning qualities by divine power. And then this spirit is changed into a most noble body, and it does not flee from the fire but runs like oil, which is a living tincture <term>tincture: a concentrated substance capable of "staining" or transforming the inner nature of a base metal into gold</term> that is multiplying,

## On the hidden heat and dryness existing within the moist and cold. Chapter 3.

**T**HE wondrous **work of the three words** is the work of the **precious stone**, in which there is coldness and a watery and burning moisture <term>burning: (Latin: *adustiva*) in alchemy, this refers to a corrosive or "scorching" quality that must be purified or removed</term>, and in the same substance there is a **hidden heat**. And thus, that which concerns the "three words" is read and understood differently by some, so that not everyone may clearly and openly understand the cause. In these three words, investigation is made into the **moist and the cold**, in which there is hidden **heat and dryness**. It is necessary for us to know this so that we may **make the manifest hidden, and the hidden manifest** <note>This "inversion of qualities" is a core alchemical axiom; the goal is to bring the inner, hidden perfection (gold/heat) to the surface while hiding the outer, imperfect qualities (base metal/cold moisture)</note>. 

And this hidden thing is of the nature of the **Sun and of Fire**, and it is the most **precious oil** of all hidden things, and a **living tincture** <term>tincture: a concentrated substance capable of "staining" or transforming the inner nature of a base metal into gold</term>, and **permanent water** <term>permanent water: (Latin: *aqua permanens*) a metaphorical solvent that does not wet the hands and is capable of dissolving metals without being destroyed by fire</term>, which always lives and remains, and the **vinegar of the philosophers**, and a **penetrating spirit**. It is a hidden thing that stains, gathers, and revivifies; it **rectifies and illuminates** all the "dead" <note>In alchemy, "the dead" refers to base metals like lead or iron that lack the "life" or luster of gold</note> and causes them to rise. Its heat and dryness do not flee from the fire, but the watery and burning coldness flees from the fire and destroys itself.

## On the works of the planets and their images 
## existing in Mercury. Chapter 5.

**I SAY** therefore that in **Mercury** are the works of the planets, and their images in their places, just as they work in the fetus in its parts and times. For in the fetus, in the **first month** when the seed is received from the womb, **Saturn** works, congealing and constricting the matter itself into one mass by its coldness and dryness. In the **second month**, **Jupiter** works by its heat, digesting it into a certain fleshy mass, which is called an **embryo** <term>embryo: (Latin: *embrio*) at this stage of historical science, the embryo was seen as a semi-formed mass of "digested" matter before the limbs were distinct</term>. In the **third month**, **Mars** works, acting upon the matter, and by its heat and dryness, it divides and separates that mass and arranges the limbs. In the **fourth month**, the **Sun**, as lord, sends in the spirit, and thus it begins to live. In the **fifth month**, **Mercury** works, which makes the openings and breathing-holes <note>breathing-holes: (Latin: *spiracula*) the pores and internal passages of the body</note>. In the **sixth month**, **Venus** disposes and orders the eyebrows and eyes, the testicles, and similar things. In the **seventh month**, the **Moon** works by its coldness and moisture to expel the fetus.

# Explanation of the three words.
## Chapter 6.

**R**ETURN *now to the explanation* of the Three *words, in which the whole art consists. For Water is said to preserve the fetus in the womb for three months.* Air *nourishes it for three months. Fire guards it for just as many <note>original: "totidem"; referring to the final three months of a nine-month term</note>. All this is said of* Mercury *by way of analogy. And this word and this discourse, and this obscure term, is open to those who understand the truth. For there is one nature in a pregnant woman, and another in* <term>Mercury: (Latin: "Mercurius") in this context, it refers to the "Philosophical Mercury," the living spirit or "prime matter" of the alchemical work, rather than just the common metal</term>; *nevertheless, through the likeness of the heat found in the womb, the fire is estimated to be thirty-two degrees. For this reason, that third word of which it is said, "fire guards for just as many," is obscure, and many go astray in it; they enter a land of an unknown region because they take two degrees from the three, and from those two degrees the remaining degrees are extracted. The little book is divided into thirty-two particles of the* <term>Grid: (Latin: "Craticula") likely referring to a specialized alchemical diagram or a "grate" used to measure stages of heat in a furnace</term>, *and in these degrees that whole third word is fulfilled, of which it is said, "just as many." The* first degree *completes the water and the air.* The second degree *completes all this which we have mentioned, and "just as many" [degrees]. And this is the* <term>Gift of God: (Latin: "donum Dei") a common alchemical term signifying that the final secret of the Stone is a divine revelation rather than a purely human discovery</term>.

## On the degrees of fire.
### Chapter 8.

**T**HE **P**HILOSOPHER *of the King of the Persians and the Prince of the Romans said: divide the three words into two parts in this way, and let these two parts again be divided into two parts, and over those two let 32 degrees be divided. These are the limits of fire, and they are called* <term>particles of fire: (Latin: "particulæ ignis") specific increments of heat used to regulate the alchemical furnace</term>, *and these are found from the portions of the* <term>ark: (Latin: "archa") likely referring to a symbolic chest or a specific measuring scale used by the author</term> *which is divided into 32 parts and called* <term>almec: an Arabic-derived term referring to the divisions or "steps" on an alchemical scale</term>. *All these degrees are scattered over the first two parts, which are the two limits; and being divided into 4 parts, the 32 degrees are distributed such that the first degree is one particle of fire called* <term>albechir: an obscure term, likely from Arabic, designating the most subtle or initial stage of heat</term>. *It is alone and simple, and is said to be almost nothing, because it is a light fire. With this fire we begin to seize* <term>Mercury to the red: (Latin: "Mercurium ad rubeum") the process of "rubefaction," or turning the philosophical mercury into a red solid or elixir</term>, *when the masters of the Persians lead it to death <note>In alchemy, "death" refers to the stage of putrefaction or the "fixing" of a volatile substance so it no longer evaporates</note> through the subtlety of their skill. In this way, two words are completed in 6* <term>maenchen: a term for a specific unit of time or measure in this particular alchemical tradition</term>. *After this, the third word is fulfilled, which is obscure, and in which many go astray and lose their senses. The Philosopher of the King of the Persians said: let us divide this through the middle. The half consists of three* <term>maenchen</term>, *and this half is ruled by two degrees, which are two* <term>particles of fire</term>. *In this way these three words are fulfilled in 22* <term>maenchen</term>, *and this is the first limit and is without error. The second limit consists of 16* <term>maenchen</term> *and is ruled with eight degrees of fire. The third limit consists of 20* <term>maenchen</term> *and is ruled with 16 degrees, that is,* <term>particles of fire</term>.

# BOOK

The fourth <term>limit: (Latin: "terminus") a specific boundary or stage in the alchemical process</term> consists of twenty-four <term>maenchen: an alchemical measure of time or weight, likely derived from an Arabic or Persian root</term> and four days, and it is governed with thirty-two degrees of fire, which constitute a particle of fire. Raxit Aedianus <note>Likely a corruption of a Persian name, possibly referring to a specific sage in the lineage of Rhazes or Ardeshir</note> and all the philosophers of the Persians said: "Praised be God, and holy be His name," because this was spoken concerning the <term>tempered fire: (Latin: "temperato igne") a controlled, moderate heat</term> above the "three words" regarding the nature of a pregnant woman—used as an analogy for the heat required for <term>Mercury: (Latin: "Mercurio") here referring to "Philosophical Mercury," the base substance being transformed</term>. All these two limits are divided, each through the middle, because both are twenty-four <term>manahen: a variation of the term "maenchen"</term> and seven <term>diethen: likely a term for "days" (from the Latin "dies")</term>. At the end of the first limit, open the <term>treasure: (Latin: "thesaurum") the alchemical vessel containing the evolving substance</term> and test what you find; if it should leap and emit smoke upon a red-hot metal plate, it is not yet finished. Therefore, return it to a fire of sixteen, which contains eight degrees of fire within it, and again open the treasure and place it upon the glowing plate; see if it leaps and produces smoke, for if so, it is not yet finished. Then return it to a fire of twenty, which contains sixteen degrees. Again, open the treasure, and if it still produces smoke, it is not yet cooked. Finally, return it to a fire of twenty-four maenchen and four <term>diechen: a variation of "diethen," meaning days</term>, which contains thirty-two degrees of fire. Now it will have become a fusible, precious stone—either green, or golden, or yellow <note>original: "ialneus," a medieval term for a hyacinth-like or orange-yellow color</note>, or red. In this hour, let God be praised and His holy name, which is blessed above every name, for this His holy gift.

<meta>This page marks a transition from the previous alchemical instructions on "fixing" substances to a natural philosophical treatise attributed to Avicenna (Ibn Sina). # AVICENNA ON THE
## CONGELATION AND CONGLV-
## tination of stones.
### Chapter 1.

**P**ure earth does not become stone, because it does not create continuity but rather discontinuity; for the prevailing dryness within it does not allow it to be <term>conglutinated: (Latin: "conglutinari") bonded or stuck together into a solid mass</term>. Stones are formed in two ways: by conglutination <note>the sticking together of solid particles like clay</note> and by <term>congelation: (Latin: "congelando") the process of a liquid hardening into a solid, such as minerals precipitating from water</term>. In some stones, earth is the dominant element; in others, however, water is dominant. Sometimes the primary clay <note>original: "lutum primum"</note> is first dried out, and a certain substance is formed that is intermediate between clay and stone, and then it becomes stone. Viscous clay is better suited for this transmutation, for it is cohesive. Also, on the banks of the Geon <note>The Gihon, a river mentioned in Genesis; in medieval geography, it was often identified with the Nile or the Amu Darya in Central Asia</note>, earth has been observed which appears to be converted into stone over a period of 33 years. Stones are also formed from water in two ways: one is when water falling drop by drop solidifies <note>likely a description of stalactites forming in caves</note>; the other is when a certain substance descends from running water and settles on the surface of the bottom of that water, and it becomes stone. For there are certain places where poured waters are turned into stones of various colors; and there are waters which, if taken up from their source, do not solidify, but if they are poured out near their own channel, they solidify and become stones. We know, therefore, that there is in the earth that <term>mineral power: (Latin: "uis mineralis") a formative, "stony" virtue or force thought to exist within the ground that directs the growth of minerals</term> which solidifies waters. Thus, the origins of stones either arise from a clay-like or viscous substance, or from a substance in which [something] prevails, which by a certain mineral power con- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely continuing as "congelatur" or "consolidatur" on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Avicenna's theories on lithogenesis, explaining that stones form either through the bonding of viscous clay or the solidification of mineral-rich waters. It highlights the existence of a specific "mineral power" in the earth that drives these natural transmutations.</summary>
<keywords>Avicenna, Ibn Sina, geology, lithogenesis, conglutination, congelation, mineral power, Geon river, petrification, formation of stones</keywords>

--- Page 240 ---
<meta>This page continues the medieval Latin translation of Avicenna's (Ibn Sina) natural philosophy, specifically focusing on the mineralogical work "De Congelatione et Conglutinatione Lapidum" (On the Congelation and Conglutination of Stones). The previous page introduced the two ways stones are formed—conglutination (sticking together) and congelation (solidifying from liquid)—and mentioned the petrifying properties of certain waters.</meta>

# **OF AVICENNA**

is frozen or prevails in that earth, causing it to coagulate. In the same way, <term>Sal ammoniac: (Latin: "Sal armoniacum") ammonium chloride, a salt found near volcanoes and used in early chemistry</term> is frozen, but the earthy power does not suffice to change it into salt; instead, heat assists it. However, the arriving heat coagulates it by a hidden power: and perhaps it is made from cold, dry earth, and earth is made from water when the qualities of earth overcome it, and vice versa. There is, however, a certain thing used by some ingenious people when they wish to coagulate a dry substance, which is composed of two waters and is called <term>virgin’s milk: (Latin: "lac uirginis") an alchemical term for a milky white liquid or precipitate, often created by mixing solutions of lead or other minerals</term>, which is its most certain effect.

Stones are therefore made from mud through the heat of the sun, or from water coagulated by a dry earthy power, or from hot, dried-out water. Similarly, certain plants and certain animals are converted into stones by a certain <term>mineral lapidifying power: (Latin: "uirtute minerali lapidificatiua") the theorized natural force that turns organic matter into stone, explaining the existence of fossils</term>. And this happens in stony places or through some sudden disharmony of power which exits from the earth at the hour of an earthquake; this power converts into stone whatever is caught in that hour. This transformation of the bodies of plants and animals is similar to the transformation of waters <note>likely referring to the way minerals precipitate from water to form solid structures like stalactites</note>.

However, it is impossible for any complex thing to be entirely converted into one element, because elements and things made of elements, when changed into one another, pass into the dominant substance. Hence, that which falls into salt-works becomes Salt: and that which falls into fire becomes fire: but some things faster and some slower, according to the power of the active forces and the resistance of the passive ones. And there is a place in Arabia which colors all bodies existing in it with its own color. Bread near a <note>The OCR reads "toratem," likely a corruption of "torrentem" (a stream or torrent). Petrified bread in a stream was a common medieval anecdote of natural wonders</note> was converted into stone, yet its color remained. Such things are wonders because they rarely happen...

<summary>This page explains the petrification of plants and animals through a 'lapidifying power' and discusses how substances like Sal ammoniac and 'virgin's milk' are formed or used to harden materials. It highlights the medieval belief that substances eventually take on the properties of the environment that dominates them, such as salt mines or fire.</summary>

Often, however, stones are also created from fire when it is extinguished, because it frequently happens that earthy and stony bodies fall during <term>lightning flashes: (Latin: "coruscationibus") specifically referring to the historical belief in "thunderstones" or meteorites falling during storms</term>, because fire becomes cold and dry upon its extinction. 

And in Persia, bronze-like bodies also fall along with lightning, resembling barbed arrows; these cannot be melted, but are instead forced by fire to evaporate into smoke because of their moisture, until only ash remains as a residue. 

There also fell at <note>original: "Lurgeam"; likely Gorgan or Jurjan in modern Iran</note> a piece of iron weighing one hundred <term>marks: (Latin: "Marcarum") a medieval unit of weight for precious metals, roughly 8 ounces</term>, which was almost unbreakable because of its extreme hardness. Nevertheless, a portion of it was sent to the <note>original: "Regi torati"; likely referring to the Sultan of Khorasan or the "King of the Turks"</note> who ordered swords to be fashioned from it, but the material proved to be unbreakable and impossible to forge. The Arabs say, however, that <term>Alemanic swords: (Latin: "enses alemanici") Germanic or "Almain" swords, highly prized in the Middle Ages for their steel quality</term>, which are the best, are made from this kind of iron. 

When that mass fell, it bounced several times from the ground just as a ball does, because it was composed of tiny pieces clinging to one another, each about the size of a large grain of millet. This phenomenon is consistent with other earthly matters. Thus, therefore, are stones made,
