->AVICENNA<-

# On the Cause of Mountains.
## Chapter 2.

**M**ountains also sometimes arise from an <term>essential cause: (Latin: "causa essentiali") an internal or fundamental force originating within the earth</term>, and sometimes from an <term>accidental cause: (Latin: "causa accidentali") an external or incidental force such as erosion</term>. From an essential cause, as when the earth is elevated by a violent motion of the earth <note>meaning an earthquake</note> and a mountain is formed. From an accidental cause, as when a deep excavation is created by winds or the flow of water, occurring gradually until a vast depth is produced: consequently, a great prominence emerges alongside it, and this is the primary cause of mountains. There are also certain types of earth that are soft and others that are hard. The soft ones are carried away by water channels and winds, while the hard ones remain, and thus a prominence is formed. 

Just as the generation of mountains occurs, so too does the generation of stones, because the flow of water continuously brought them a viscous mud, which over a long duration of time is dried out and becomes stone; and it is not far-fetched <note>original: "non est longe quin"</note> to suggest there is a <term>mineral power: (Latin: "vis mineralis") a formative natural force believed by medieval scholars to transform liquid or mud into solid stone</term> converting waters into stones. And for this reason, in many stones are found certain parts of <term>aquatic animals: (Latin: "animalium aquaticorum") early evidence of fossils</term> and other things. Mountains have been formed, as it were, over many ages, as we said before, provided they are not currently decreasing. 

However, the muddy substances found on these heights are not made of that stony matter, but consist of that which is worn down from mountains, or terrestrial matter, or some other substance that the waters bring along with silt and herbs that are mixed with the mountain mud. Or perhaps it is the ancient mud of the sea, which is of a single substance, where part of it becomes stone and another part does not, but is instead softened and dissolved by some dominant quality. The sea has its retreat and advance <note>referring to the shifting of coastlines or tidal action over long periods</note>; it hollows out certain places and raises others, and whe—

<summary>Avicenna explains that mountains are formed either by internal geological shifts (earthquakes) or external erosion (wind and water), and correctly identifies fossils as remains of sea creatures trapped in ancient mud.</summary>
<keywords>orogeny, erosion, fossils, vis mineralis, sediment, Ibn Sina, medieval geology, aquatic animals</keywords>

<note>original: "quandoque"</note> ...and whenever [water] covered the entire earth, it scraped away certain soft materials from it, left others that were hard, and gathered them together in certain places. These soft materials, which were gathered together when they were cut off by the water, were dried out and turned into mountains.

## On the four species of mineral bodies. Chapter 3.

**M**ineral bodies are divided into four species: namely, into <term>stones: (Latin: "lapides")</term>, <term>liquefiable substances: (Latin: "liquefactiua") materials that can be melted</term>, <term>sulfurous substances: (Latin: "sulphurea")</term>, and <term>salts: (Latin: "sales")</term>. Some of these are of a thin substance and a weak composition, while some are of a strong substance; some are <term>ductile: (Latin: "ductibilia") capable of being hammered or shaped without breaking</term>, and some are not. Among those of a weak substance, some are <term>salts: (Latin: "sales")</term>, which are those that liquefy quickly from moisture, such as <term>Alum: (Latin: "Alumen")</term>, <term>Vitriol: (Latin: "Calcamentum") a metallic salt often used in dyes and inks</term>, and <term>Sal Ammoniac: (Latin: "Sal Armoniacum")</term>. Others are oily and do not liquefy easily by moisture alone, such as <term>Sulfur: (Latin: "Sulphur")</term> and <term>Orpiment: (Latin: "Auripigmentum") a yellow mineral used as a pigment</term>.

However, <term>Quicksilver: (Latin: "Argentum uiuum") mercury</term> belongs to the second category <note>the liquefiable substances</note>, although it is the constituent element of ductile metals, or similar to certain ductile things. All ductile things are capable of being melted; for the most part, stones are neither ductile nor liquefiable, and they cannot be softened except by great force. The material of ductile things is an aqueous substance mixed with an earthy substance in a strong mixture, such that one cannot be separated from the other. The aqueous substance is congealed by cold after the action of heat upon it, a process called <term>epthesis: (from Greek: "hepthēsis") a "cooking" or "digestion" by heat that brings a substance to maturity</term>. An example of this can be seen in wine, which has not yet frozen because of its oily nature.

<summary>This page describes how water erosion and sediment accumulation create mountains, and then introduces a four-fold classification of minerals: stones, liquefiable substances, sulfurs, and salts.</summary>
<keywords>geology, mineralogy, Avicenna, mountain formation, classification of minerals, salts, sulfur, mercury, ductility, epthesis</keywords>

->### OF AVICENNA<-

<meta>catchword: subtle</meta>

...and therefore it is not <term>ductile: (Latin: "ductile") capable of being hammered thin or stretched into wire</term>. Stones, however, among mineral substances, are materially made of water, but they are not solidified by water alone, but also by dryness, which transforms the "wateriness" into "earthiness." There is no excessively oily moisture in them, and for this reason, they cannot be hammered or shaped. Because their <term>coagulation: (Latin: "coagulatio") the process of turning from a liquid to a solid</term> comes from dryness, they are not easily dissolved, except by clever natural methods of dissolution. 

<term>Alum: (Latin: "Alumen")</term> and <term>Sal Ammoniac: (Latin: "Sal armoniacum")</term> belong to the genus of Salt, because the element of fire in Sal Ammoniac is greater than that of earth. For this reason, the whole substance is <term>sublimated: (Latin: "sublimatur") turned directly from a solid into a vapor by heat</term>; it is essentially water mixed with an extremely subtle, hot smoke of great fieriness, which has been solidified by dryness.

The "wateriness" of <term>Sulfurous substances: (Latin: "Sulphureorũ")</term> is mixed with earth in a powerful combination through boiling heat, until they become oily, and they are afterwards solidified by cold. 

<term>Vitriols: (Latin: "Atramenta") literally "inky" or "blackening" substances, used to describe metallic sulfates</term> are composed of Salt, Sulfur, and stones. Within them is the "mineral power" of certain liquefiable bodies that are produced from them. For example, <term>green vitriol: (Latin: "calcamentum")</term> and <term>alathar: (Latin: "alachar") a red mineral deposit or red vitriol</term> are generated from the larger grains of these "inky" minerals. Only their saltiness is dissolved along with the sulfurous part within them, and then they solidify, having already received a mineral power from other bodies. Therefore, that which receives the power of iron will be red or saffron-colored, such as *alathar*. That which receives the power of <note>original: "æream," referring to copper or bronze</note> copper will be green. For this reason, it is possible to create these two minerals artificially. 

Quicksilver <note>Mercury</note> is like water mixed with an extremely subtle earth in a powerful sulfurous mixture, so much so that it will not rest on a flat surface; this is due to the great dryness inherent in it, and thus it does not stick to what it touches. Its whiteness comes from the clarity of that water and from the whiteness of the earth.

<summary>Avicenna explains the chemical composition of stones, salts, and vitriols, focusing on the balance of fire, water, and earth. He describes how different metallic "powers" (iron or copper) give vitriols their distinct red or green colors.</summary>

# MINERALS

...nature which is subtle within it. It is characteristic of <term>Mercury: (Latin: "Argentum vivum") literally "living silver," the common name for quicksilver</term> that it is <term>coagulated: (Latin: "coaguletur") solidified or thickened</term> by the vapor of <term>Sulfur: (Latin: "Sulphur") regarded by alchemists as the active, masculine principle of combustion and color</term>, and perhaps in this way it is solidified through <term>Lead: (Latin: "Plumbum")</term>, or easily by the vapor of Sulfur. 

It appears, however, that Mercury and substances similar to it are the fundamental element of all <term>liquefiable: (Latin: "liquabilium") substances that can be melted or fused by heat</term> things; this is either because all liquefiable things turn back into it when they are melted—though they do not melt until they are heated—and once melted, they appear red. But Lead, without doubt, when it is melted, is Mercury; yet it does not melt unless it is heated, and when it has been melted, it turns to a color common to all liquefiable things, namely a fiery redness. For this reason, Mercury mixes with these bodies, because it is part of their very substance. 

Yet these bodies differ in their composition from Mercury, just as Mercury differs from things similar to itself, and by the various mixtures that are blended with them until they are solidified. And if the Mercury is pure, the power of white, non-burning Sulfur will coagulate it; this is the best material that those who practice <term>Alchemy: (Latin: "Alchimia")</term> can find, or those who convert it into **Silver**. 

If, indeed, there is pure, excellent Sulfur with a bright redness, and if there is in it a power of simple, non-burning fieriness <note>Alchemists distinguished between "burning" sulfur, which caused impurities, and "non-burning" sulfur, which was the philosophical principle of maturation</note>, it will be the best thing that Alchemists can find to make **Gold** from it; for these qualities transform it. 

And if the Mercury is of good substance but the Sulfur is not pure—meaning it has a burning power within it—it will convert the Mercury into **Copper**. If, however, the Mercury is of poor quality, impure, and earthy, and the Sulfur is unclean, **Iron** is created from it. **Tin**, on the other hand, seems to be Mercury of a good... <note>original: "Stannum uerò uidetur Argentum uiuū bo-"</note>

<summary>This page outlines the foundational alchemical theory that all metals—including Gold, Silver, Copper, and Iron—are formed from the combination of Mercury and Sulfur. It explains that the specific metal produced depends entirely on the purity and "burning" quality of these two parent components.</summary>

<header>AVICENNA'S [WORKS]</header>
<meta>catchword: in</meta>

# AVICENNA'S [WORKS]

...to have <note>This sentence fragment likely refers to the "perfection" or "solidification" discussed on the previous page.</note> [perfection]. But the **Sulfur** is truly bad, and these substances are not well-ground, but rather put together as if by small parts, and therefore it does not become such a thing. In coarse **Lead**, the <term>Mercury: (Latin: "Argentum vivum") literally "living silver," the liquid metal quicksilver</term> is of poor quality, heavy, and yellowish; and its **Sulfur** is bad, possessing an evil, stinking, and weak vapor. For this reason, it does not <term>solidify: (Latin: "gelatur") thicken or coagulate into a hard metal</term> well. 

And craftsmen perform a solidification that is almost similar by artificial means, although artificial things are not the same as natural ones, nor do they possess such certain similarities. Therefore, it is believed that natural composition happens in this way, or something close to it. But art is weaker than nature and does not follow it, even though it labors much. 

Let the practitioners of **Alchemy** know that the <term>species: (Latin: "species") the essential nature or "blueprint" of a substance that makes it what it is</term> of things cannot be transmuted. But they can make things similar to them, and tint a red substance with <term>citrine: (Latin: "citrino") a lemon-yellow color</term> so that it appears to be **Gold**, and tint a white substance with whatever color they wish, until it is very similar to **Gold** or copper. They can also afterwards wipe away the impurities of **Lead**. Yet it will always be **Lead**, even though it may look like **Silver**. 

But then other qualities will be excellently present in it, so that men are deceived, such as those who use salt and <term>Sal Ammoniac: (Latin: "Salem Armoniacum") ammonium chloride, a salt used by alchemists to clean metal surfaces and prepare them for tinting</term>. Furthermore, I do not believe it is possible for the <term>specific difference: the unique, defining characteristic that separates one species (like Gold) from another (like Lead)</term> to be removed by human ingenuity, and it is not possible for one <term>complexion: (Latin: "complexio") the specific balance of the four qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) that determines a substance's nature</term> to be converted into another. This is because these sensible qualities <note>Qualities that can be perceived by the senses, like color or smell.</note> are not the "difference" by which a species is changed; rather, they are <term>accidents: (Latin: "accidentia") non-essential properties like color, weight, or scent that can change without the object becoming a different kind of thing</term> and properties. 

However, their true "differences" are not known. Since the difference is unknown, how can it be known whether it is removed or not, or how it could be removed? But the stripping away of "accidents"—such as vapor, color, and weight—or at least their reduction, is not impossible, for reason does not stand against this; but the proportion of these [essential] substances will not be the same...

<summary>Avicenna argues that while alchemists can manipulate the "accidents" of metals (like color and weight) to make them appear like gold or silver, they cannot change their "species" or essential nature. He asserts that the specific differences between metals are unknown to man and therefore impossible to manipulate.</summary>
<keywords>Avicenna, Mercury, Sulfur, Lead, transmutation, species and accidents, alchemy, Sal Ammoniac, specific difference</keywords>

# AN INTERPRETATION
## OF A CERTAIN LETTER
### which circulates under the name of Alexander the Great, King of the Macedonians, explaining most sharply to the wise the hidden composition of the Philosophical Stone.

**A**LEXANDER, treating the subject in his *Letter on the Art of the Sun* <note>The "Art of the Sun" is a common alchemical synonym for the creation of gold.</note> according to the image and likeness of the world, says briefly that you must be grounded in this demonstration; for he shows <gloss>that</gloss> the art is derived from the creation of man, in whom all things are present. This art is not perfected except by those who study through wakeful labor and through its proper moderation. ...the female is cold and moist, and is the purifying water. The <term>quintessence: (Latin: "quinta essentia") the "fifth essence" or spiritual principle that transcends the four elements</term>, however, is life itself; it is unique, being neither hot nor moist, nor cold nor dry, nor masculine nor feminine. Yet, if it is turned toward dryness through the power of these <note>the other four elements</note>, its opposite is destroyed. Therefore, so that you do not err in your work by searching for it in some river-bed <note>The author warns against taking alchemical "water" or "rivers" literally</note>, I shall show you its location.

If, therefore, you wish to dissolve the **water of life** through coldness, you will instead coagulate it. If you wish to make the water cold, you will instead heat it; unless you correct each nature of the powers according to the purpose of your work, you will ruin the operation.

It should also be known that the **appetitive power**, which is hot and dry, is strengthened by **Fire**. The **retentive power**, being cold and dry, gathers heat within bodies and, by softening them, merges with their colors; this is **Earth**. The **digestive power** is masculine and purifying; it cleanses the body because it overcomes nature’s rust <note>original: "rubigine"; referring to the "leprosy" or impurities of metals</note>, and subsequently imprints nature in an accidental way; it is hot and moist, and this is **Air**. The **expulsive power**, which is also purifying, is feminine; it drives out all impurity. While it is poured into the matter, it is transformed into itself, and they can never again be separated—just as when poison is spread through a body, it cannot afterward be removed. This power acts as a heat within bodies, and its nature is arranged such that it gathers their elements into one; yet it is inherently cold and moist, and this is **Water**.

And it is the **quintessence** that directs and completes the entire work; it is the <term>soul joined to the body: (Latin: "anima corpori coniuncta") the union of the volatile spirit with the fixed physical form</term>. Some have performed this conjunction poorly in their work and have erred, choosing the useless instead of the useful. Some also, just as...

# EXPOSITION OF THE LETTER

Those who, while working by the direct path as we commanded, fell short and acted according to their own opinion, have thereby ruined the entire work, and it has become a stone <note>meaning the matter has hardened into an unproductive, inert state</note>. Nay, I tell you that if you desire to perfect your work, you must begin as I commanded. It remains now for us to assign names to the species, and it must not be forgotten that the things with which you work ought to be of a non-luminous nature <note>Latin: "non lucidae naturae"; likely referring to raw, unrefined matter rather than the "light" of the finished product</note>, out of which the **microcosm** <note>Latin: "minor mundus"; the human body, viewed as a miniature version of the universe</note> was created, the properties of which I shall now name for you. 

Know that **Sulphur** is fire, and earth is **Magnesia** <note>In alchemy, "Magnesia" refers to the "prime matter" or a maternal substance from which metals grow, not the modern element magnesium</note>, and air is of the mine, and water is **Quicksilver** <note>Latin: "Argentum vivum"; mercury</note>, which is submerged in the body. The **Quintessence** <note>The "fifth essence" or spiritual principle</note> is the spirit which vivifies and alters all things, and makes every seed sprout, and kindles every light, and makes all fruits flower. 

With such governance indeed it is necessary to direct this work; and know, as has been said, how to join the natures appropriately: the male to the female, the hot to the cold, and the moist to the dry. Then you must nourish the water, the spirit, and the light, because the work is founded upon diverse natures. From its melancholy <note>the "black bile" humor, associated here with the "body" or earth</note> the body is composed, and its quantity arises from the fire of the liver’s defect, from which red choler <note>the "yellow bile" humor, associated with fire</note> arises. 

When this is done, it is necessary that those things which pertain to the spiritual deeds and labor be digested, by which the air is joined; this is the blood <note>the "sanguine" humor, associated with air</note> flowing in all the veins of the body, and it overcomes until it is turned into a red color. Water, however, is the phlegm <note>the "phlegmatic" humor, associated with water</note> which resides in the chest and appears in the form of golden instruments. And so henceforth, by the power of those things I have already told you, every body is made perfect. Strive therefore to rule all these natures prudently, and place also the vessel with its like or its contrary in a secluded place with **tinchar** <note>likely "borax" or a similar flux used to encourage the melting of metals</note>, and

<summary>This page defines the primary materials of the alchemical work (Sulphur, Magnesia, and Quicksilver) and provides a symbolic map linking the four elements to the four humors of the human "microcosm." It emphasizes the need for proper "digestion" and the balancing of opposing qualities like hot/cold and moist/dry to achieve a perfect body.</summary>
<keywords>microcosm, sulphur, magnesia, quicksilver, quintessence, humors, melancholy, choler, phlegm, blood, tinchar</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions attributed to "King Alexander" (a legendary persona often associated with Hermes Trismegistus in alchemical lore). Following the previous page's identification of the four elements and the microcosm, this text focuses on the practical stages of transformation—specifically how to move from "body" to "spirit" through melting and refining the matter.</meta>

# OF KING ALEXANDER

Your work is directed just as I have granted my grace to you, when I described the <term>species: specific substances or materials used in the work</term> to you, which must be governed step by step, until the bodies become spirits <note>This refers to "sublimation," where a solid substance is turned into a vapor or "spirit"</note>, and then the wisdom of the art works so that nature is altered and converted into another nature, and nature appears through nature. 

But after the accidents <note>In Aristotelian terms, "accidents" are the external properties like color or texture, as opposed to the internal "essence"</note> of nature itself, you must join two natures until from them you extract a clear and lucid nature, which when it ascends into the highest places, is held, shines, and is fitted together. 

And know that in this art I speak nothing [merely] philosophically <note>He is emphasizing that these are practical, laboratory instructions rather than abstract metaphors</note>, and in the solution of <term>liquefactio: the process of melting or dissolving a substance into a liquid state</term>, caution must be applied above all else, so that in ascending you do not exceed the measure. For in liquefying consists the whole <term>archanum: a profound secret or "great mystery" of alchemy</term>, which is the alteration of all natures. 

And know how this is changed from nature to nature, until it becomes a substance that is not fleeing <note>Meaning "non-volatile"; it no longer evaporates when heated</note> nor altering, but becomes fixed and constant. From these things, the entrance fits together the black [parts], since the part is discerned. And soon nature appears through nature. Therefore, he fits together the rare quality of fixed things by the nature of liquefaction, and the water which by the Wise is called the **water of wisdom** <note>Latin: "aqua sapientiae"; a specialized solvent or mercurial water used to dissolve the "Philosopher's Stone"</note>. 

Then [there are] the secrets which are tempered and moist, because unless you thin the natures and make them into water, they will not be able to color [the matter]; thus therefore you shall moisten the body by watering. And know that <term>albedo: the "whitening" stage, symbolizing purification and the washing away of impurities</term> is in the <term>coagulatio: the process of solidifying or thickening a liquid into a solid</term>, and that the species are of the <term>spiritus: the volatile or gaseous essence of a substance</term>, and they improve one another. 

I say also to you, my son, who desires to be improved, that you should not put into it raw and disordered species, but those that are putrefied <note>Putrefaction was considered a necessary stage of decay that allowed for new life/form to emerge</note> and fitted together, ascending and descending. I have without doubt discovered certain elevated, lucid, and illuminated ointments, and I have found in them hidden secrets, which if you govern them well, the nature of those things which...

<summary>This page describes the alchemical transition from solid "bodies" to volatile "spirits," emphasizing the importance of liquefaction and the "water of wisdom." It highlights the "albedo" (whitening) stage as the point where the matter becomes purified and fixed.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, liquefaction, coagulation, water of wisdom, albedo, archanum, spirit, King Alexander, transmutation</keywords>

<header>A SMALL WORK</header>

# A SMALL WORK

You have the secrets; it has appeared clearly to you and it will appear [again], that is, the <term>terra alba: white earth</term> <note>This refers to the "Albedo" or whitening stage of the alchemical Great Work, signifying purity.</note> through the <note>original: "Argentū lucidū"</note> bright and known Silver. And know that certain bodies require solution <note>dissolving into liquid</note>, and some require <term>eleuatione: elevation</term> <note>sublimation, where a substance is heated until it rises as vapor</note> so that they may tinge [other metals], and become bright and illuminated, in which all bodies may be dissolved. Know also that according to nature, secrets are hidden within them; for if the hidden nature is governed [rightly], those things which appeared to <note>original: "Hermeti Philosopho"</note> **Hermes the Philosopher** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical founder of alchemy</note> will appear to you, and it is the white earth near to Silver. Prepare therefore the bodies which heal themselves when the earth is white, so that they remain <term>fixa: fixed</term> <note>stable and resistant to change by fire</note>, and place them [over the fire] so that the fire does not burn them with its flame, but melt them just as you would melt wax. And be mindful that this is called the "water entering again." Therefore, in your arts in which you study, believe that there will be two hammers, one of which is owed to the <term>Sol: Sun</term> <note>Gold</note>, and the other to the <term>Luna: Moon</term> <note>Silver</note>; afterwards follow two orders, the governance of which the <note>original: "dubiū nostro capiti"</note> uncertainty [or crown] of our head represents appropriately enough. Behold, I have numbered [the steps], I have revealed the heavenly secret, I have illuminated the difficult and hidden science, and I have confirmed the method of governing with perfect knowledge. I swear, therefore, that I have hidden nothing, nor have I described another or any species with a foreign description: nor have I placed types in foreign places. Therefore, if you read and understand my letters, you will reach your goal, and you will give thanks to God. If, however, there is one whose composition is weak and whose understanding is brief, he will not reach his goal; let him not blame me for his own ignorance, nor say that I have erred, but rather blame himself who does not reach his goal.

->The end of the interpretation of the Letter of<-
->King Alexander.<-

# A SMALL WORK OF AN UNKNOWN
## AUTHOR, METAPHORICALLY DESCRIBING
### THE SECRETS OF THE PHILOSOPHICAL STONE.

**T**HE EXAMPLE OF OUR SCIENCE <note>original: "SCIENTIAE NOstrae"</note> is an old man beyond death; in him the natures are joined with fulfillment. His earth, water, fire, and air. And all these are in <term>Saturno: Saturn</term> <note>In alchemical symbolism, Saturn represents lead, as well as the initial stage of the work characterized by darkness and decay, known as the *Nigredo*.</note>; with him the gates of knowledge are opened, just as **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of alchemy.</note> and his first predecessors say. Take the stone suspended over the sea like an old man [as white as] white wax, over the fountain like the boy of the <term>ypoquistidos: hypocistis</term> <note>The juice of the *Cytinus hypocistis*, a parasitic plant. In this context, it refers to a specific medicinal or alchemical extract used as a metaphor for the substance's state.</note>. Its name is Victor. With it, kill the living and bring life to the dead, for to it belong death and life, both this and that, from the East and the West. With this, you will have the knowledge in which the Arabs <note>A reference to the great medieval Arabic alchemists like Jabir ibn Hayyan (Geber), whose works laid the foundation for Western alchemy.</note> have labored. I have told you the truth by Him who made the author of the stone of paradise, the light of life, to arise. In it are two opposites together, namely fire and water, and the one gives life to the other, and the other kills the one; and both of these are joined together in the <term>leone: Lion</term> <note>The Lion often symbolizes "fixed" sulfur or a powerful acidic solvent, such as the "Green Lion."</note>; by Him who is death and life, I have told the truth, I do not lie. Our <term>Gumen: gum</term> <note>A sticky or resinous substance produced during the chemical process.</note> coagulates our milk, and our milk dissolves our gum. And afterwards the <term>rubedo: redness</term> <note>The final stage of the Great Work, signifying the completion of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> of the East will appear, and the redness of blood; this pure stone without stench is found in the highest places, with which the philosophers have magnified themselves above all others and prophesied future things. I have told the truth by the power of the heavens, to which I have not knowingly lied. Our ancient man is our <term>draco: dragon</term>; he eats his own head with his tail <note>This describes the *Ouroboros*, the serpent eating its tail, a famous symbol for the unity of matter and the cyclical nature of the alchemical process.</note>, and the head and tail are the soul and

Spirit, and soul, and spirit are created from mud, both this and that from the east and the west; by Him who drives away all harm, I do not lie, for what I have said, I saw in the city. Our water washes away filth; you shall cleanse all its blackness and obscurity, and then it will smile, saying, "Who has cleansed me?" He who saw it will smile as he speaks, and I have told the truth, I do not lie.

The Earth in [our] eyes consists of three: the East and the West, and over them white **Saturn** <note>original: "Saturnus albus"</note> has arisen, and the soul of these three is fire; I have told the truth by the family of **Muhammad** <note>original: "Mahometi". You blacken with our **Egypt**, and not with **Persia**; and with **Persia** you dry, because it is the house of dryness; and with **Egypt** you moisten, because it is the house of humidity; and both this and that are whiteness and redness without measure, like the redness in blackberries, or like the blood of ladies <note>A common alchemical metaphor for the "mercurial" or "sulfuric" tinctures.</note>. # MERLIN’S ALLEGORY,
## PERFECTLY CONTAINING THE DEEPEST
### secret of the Philosophical Stone. <note>The "Philosophical Stone" is the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting long life, representing spiritual and physical perfection.</note>

**A** CERTAIN KING, desiring to overcome other powerful rulers, prepared himself for war against them. And when he wished to mount his horse, he commanded one of his soldiers to give him a drink of that water which he loved very much. The soldier, responding to him, said: "Lord, what water is that which you seek?" And the King said: "That water which I seek is a water most desired by me, and it loves me above all others." And the soldier, having weighed this <note>original: *ppendens*, meaning to consider or weigh the meaning carefully.</note>, went immediately and brought it to him. The King, receiving it, drank and drank again, until all his limbs were filled, and all his veins swollen, and then he became greatly discolored <note>This "discoloration" and swelling signifies the "nigredo" or blackening stage, where the material begins to break down and rot before it can be purified.</note>. This having been done, his soldiers said to him: "Lord, behold the horse; mount if it pleases you." He responding sa— <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "xit," which will complete the word "dixit" (said) on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page concludes an anonymous alchemical work and begins "Merlin's Allegory," a parable where a King drinks a beloved water that causes him to swell and change color, symbolizing the initial chemical dissolution of matter.</summary>

...he said. "Know that I am unable to mount <note>original: *ascendere*; the King is unable to mount his horse for the war mentioned on the previous page.</note>." The soldiers said, "Why are you unable?" He replied, "Because I feel myself weighed down, and my head aches, and it seems to me that all my limbs are being separated from one another." I command you, therefore, to place me in a bright chamber, and let it be established in a warm and dry place, continuously tempered through day and night; and thus I shall sweat, and the water which I drank shall be dried out of me, and so I shall be set free. Therefore, they did as the King had commanded them. But when the time was completed, they opened the chamber and found him as if dead.

His relatives <note>original: *parentes*; in this context, it refers to his kin or the members of his court.</note> immediately ran to the Egyptian and Alexandrian physicians—who were to be honored above all others—and brought them to him, telling them what had happened to the King. When they had seen him, they said that he could undoubtedly be set free. And the relatives asked: "Who among you shall be the master?" The Alexandrians replied, "We shall (if it pleases you)." The Egyptians said, "It does not please us; rather, we wish to be the masters. For we are more ancient than you, even though we seem younger." The Alexandrians then agreed. 

Then the said masters took the King and tore him into the smallest pieces, grinding them, and they mixed them with their moistening medicines just a little; and thus they placed him, suitably prepared, in his chamber, in a warm and tempered place, just as before, through day and night. When this was done, they drew him out as if half-dead and still possessing a little bit of life. When his relatives saw him, they cried out, saying: "Alas, the King is dead!" To whom the physicians said: "He is not dead. Do not cry out, for he sleeps, and now he has begun to reach his end <note>The "end" here likely refers to the completion of the first stage of transformation, or the "limit" of his old physical form.</note>." Therefore, they took him again and washed him with sweet water until the taste of the medicine...

# MERLIN

...[physicians] <note>original: [medi]cinarum; the text begins mid-word, likely referring back to the practitioners from the previous page</note> withdrew, and they sent with him again some of that same new medicine, and they placed him in his place just as before. And when they drew him out, they found him completely dead. Then his relatives <note>original: parentes; meaning kinsmen or members of the court</note>, crying out loudly, said: "Alas, the King is dead!" To whom the physicians, responding, said: "We killed him for this reason, so that he might become better and stronger in this world after the resurrection on the day of judgment than he had been before." Indeed, when his relatives heard this, they believed them to be swindlers <note>original: truffatores; deceivers or mountebanks</note>, and they immediately took their medicines away from them and expelled them from the kingdom.

When this was done, they spoke among themselves, considering what they ought to do with this poisoned and dead body. And they agreed that they should bury him, lest he should rot and his stench cause harm. The Alexandrian physicians, however, hearing this, went to them and said: "Do not bury him, for (if it pleases you) we will restore him healthier, more beautiful, and more powerful than before." Then those others began to smile, saying: "Do you wish to swindle us just as the others did? Know that unless you do what you promise, you shall not be able to escape our hands." 

The physicians, however, binding themselves to this, took the dead King just as the others had left him; grinding him, they washed him well until nothing of the medicines of the others remained, and they dried him. Then they took one part of Sal ammoniac <term>Sal ammoniac: Ammonium chloride, a salt used in alchemy to "open" or clean metals for transformation</term>, and two parts of Alexandrian nitre <term>Alexandrian nitre: A mineral salt used for purification, likely sodium carbonate or saltpeter</term>, and they mixed them with the powder of the dead man, and they made it into a paste with a little linseed oil. Then they placed him in a certain chamber, made in the manner of a crucible perforated at the bottom, and beneath the opening they placed another clean vessel in the manner of a cru-

# ALLEGORY

...of the vessel <note>original: eiboli; likely a corruption of "theiboli," a specific type of alchemical vessel or crucible mentioned in other versions of this text</note> they had made, and they left him there for one hour. Then they covered him with fire, blowing with bellows until he was completely liquefied, descending into another crucible <note>term: crucible <gloss>a heat-resistant container used for melting metals or minerals</gloss></note> below through a hole. Then the King, thus rising from death to life, cried out with a great voice and said: *"Where are the enemies? Let them know that I shall kill them all, unless they come to me obediently without delay."* Therefore, everyone hearing this came before him and said: *"Lord, behold, we are ready to obey your commands in all things."* And so, from that hour as before, all the kings and powerful ones of other regions honored and feared him. 

And when they wished to see his wonders, they would place one ounce of well-washed Mercury <note>original: Mercurij; representing quicksilver</note> into a crucible, and they would cast over it as if a single grain of millet from his nails, or from his hair, or from his blood <note>In alchemy, these "body parts" of the King represent small amounts of the highly concentrated Philosophers' Stone</note>, and they would blow lightly with coals, and let it cool with them, and they found a stone such as I know. Of which stone they would cast a little upon purified Saturn <note>original: Saturnum; the alchemical name for lead</note>, and immediately its form was changed, as I know. Of which they afterwards placed one part upon ten of Venus <note>original: Veneris; the alchemical name for copper</note>, and it was all of one color and goodness <note>This describes "transmutation," where the Stone turns base metals into gold or silver</note>. 

Likewise in another way: they took the said ground stone and mixed it with Salt <note>original: Sale</note> and the Sun <note>original: Sole; the alchemical name for gold</note> as before, and they liquefied it, and they threw the said dissolved salts into goat’s whey <note>original: fero caprino</note>, and then it became the best for all things. Guard this treatise, brother, and keep it well, because it is a great deception <note>original: truffa; can mean a joke, a trick, or nonsense</note> among fools, but not a deception among the wise. For this is the Way of the Three-Day Kings, because with little labor...

# RACHAIDIBI, VE-
## RADIANI, RHODIANI, AND KA-
### *nidis, philosophers of the King of the Per-*
### *sians. A Fragment of a most acute*
### *dialogue concerning the matter of the*
### *Philosophic Stone.*

---

**T**HE SON OF RACHAIDIBA SAID,
*Zetheibidae, a philosopher of the King of the Persians and the Prince of the Romans: The seed* <note>original: Sperma; in alchemy, this refers to the "metallic seed" or the primordial starting matter of the Stone</note> *of the stone is cold and moist in its manifest* <note>term: manifest <gloss>the outward, visible, or physical properties of a substance</gloss></note> *state, and in its occult* <note>term: occult <gloss>the internal, hidden, or spiritual essence of a substance</gloss></note> *state it is hot and dry. He says, therefore: make the cold and moist into the occult, and place the hot and dry into the manifest state: and in this way its coldness and moisture will be hidden, so that they shall never be diminished. For if they were diminished, the stone would also be diminished. When, therefore, the coldness and moisture are hidden, act by hiding them, so that you may thus compose that hot and dry thing, which was in the occult and in the manifest: and so, from the spirit let a body be made in two degrees,* <note>note: "degrees" likely refers to the stages of heat or the specific steps in the alchemical process</note> *and it shall become a sphere, and blood, and flesh. Rodianus says: The seed is white and liquid. This liquid first forms a sphere in the place where it belongs for five days: and it receives flesh and blood, and members and an image, and the power of imagination from God most high: and it comes to its birth, and shines and arises in its own time. Which are two degrees and seven. And now we speak of the second*

<summary>This page introduces an alchemical dialogue between Persian philosophers regarding the "Seed of the Stone." It describes the process of transmuting elemental qualities—turning hidden heat and dryness into outward properties—and uses biological metaphors like "blood and flesh" to describe the creation of the Philosophic Stone.</summary>

<keywords>Rachaidibi, Rodianus, Persian philosophers, Seed of the Stone, manifest and occult, four elements, alchemical degrees, Zetheibidae, generation of the stone</keywords>

# RACHAIDIBI ETC.

...seed, Veradianus says concerning the second seed, which is the **Stone alone**, and there is no other like it in this world; it impregnates itself, conceives within itself, and gives birth to itself. By this is understood the **one stone**, which is called the **white and liquid seed**, and afterwards the **red** <note>In alchemy, the transition from white to red (albedo to rubedo) represents the final stages of the Great Work</note>. Rhodianus, however, says that this seed is a certain stone that is <term>fugitive<gloss>volatile; a substance that evaporates or "flees" when heated</gloss></term> from heat and dryness; it is airy and volatile, yet it is also cold and moist, as well as hot and dry. 

And after this living thing dies by the **red death**, having died, it is now equal to heat and dryness; thus it no longer flees from that which is hot, nor does it flee the fire, nor emit smoke, nor make any sound <note>original: "nec sonum facit"; likely referring to the lack of "decrepitation" or crackling when a substance is heated</note>. It melts like wax and is like oil, and it is dry—which dryness colors with a **red and white tincture** <note>term: tincture <gloss>the final alchemical substance capable of "dyeing" or transforming base metals into silver or gold</gloss></note>. 

And so, from the spirit a body is made; then this body must be turned back into a spirit, and afterwards it shall become a **clear water** without any cloudiness from the bottom part all the way to the top. All its particles are distinct in the water, so that the tincture is not hindered—which is penetrative and joining, for the purpose of retaining, reaching, and coloring all bodies of metals. This tincture remains in them forever, and with this tincture all the dead shall be brought to life so that they may live forever. This is the **first elemented ferment**, and it pertains to the **Moon** <note>In alchemical code, "Luna" or the Moon signifies Silver</note>; this is the light of all lights, and the flower and fruit of all lights, which illuminates all things. It is the true and living tincture, both red and white, and the rectifying oil of tinctures. 

Therefore, give to it the **second ferment**, which is equally fermented by every elemented element: which is **Gold**. Give...

<meta>catchword: ...to it a fourth [part]</meta>

...to it a fourth part, which nevertheless should first be calcined <note>original: "calcinatum"; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</note> and dissolved into water. For this is the elemental water <note>original: "aqua elementata"</note> composed equally of all the elements. Give to it also the second ferment <note>original: "fermentum"</note>—when I say "second," I mean the second retainer <note>original: "retinaculum"</note>—and in it is the <term>Sulphurous tincture<gloss>a spiritual essence derived from sulfur, believed to provide color and stability to the alchemical stone</gloss></term>, and it is called the "oil of retainers." This is the greenest, most purified, and clearest <term>Vitriol<gloss>a sulfate mineral, usually iron or copper sulfate, prized for its acidic and transformative properties</gloss></term> which has been turned into water. 

Give to it also the saffron-colored water made from *endacuto* <note>a likely reference to a sharp or acidic mineral compound</note> like the water of verdigris <note>original: "viridis æris"; the green pigment or "rust" that forms on copper</note>, which is the poison of <term>Mercury<gloss>represented here both as the metal and the planetary spirit, often seen as needing "slaying" or "fixing" to become the Stone</gloss></term>. And give to Mercury some of the water worked into its own spirit, which you have extracted from its body, and thus you have mortified <note>original: "mortificasti"; to "kill" or strip a metal of its outward characteristics to reach its inner essence</note> it. 

And this worked water—namely the water of <term>Quicksilver<gloss>Latin: Argentum vivum; another name for Mercury</gloss></term>—is prepared so that the Quicksilver is turned into water that does not flee the impetuous fire, so that it does not form a solid substance [prematurely], but always remains by force. And of each of these, give to it as much as there is of itself. And give to it also <term>Sal Ammoniac<gloss>ammonium chloride, a salt used in alchemy to dissolve metals and "spiritualize" solid bodies</gloss></term>, which is a precious spirit, and is both dissolving and coagulating; let it be turned into water, though first it must be sublimated <note>original: "sublimatum"; purified by heating until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again</note> five or seven times, and let it be double the amount of the first ferment. 

Therefore, give to it a dry, hot, and constant water with strong clarification and subtle imbibition <note>original: "imbibitione"; the process of a solid slowly absorbing a liquid</note>, namely drop by drop; for if you were to give it less of these "drinks," it would be confounded <note>original: "confunderetur"; ruined or failed to transform</note>. And do this in the hot sun or on ashes of gentle heat, or both if possible. And let all these waters first be mixed for 21 days in a very clean and warm place, and let them boil together and form a single substance. Then let it be congealed into a <term>stone<gloss>the sought-after Philosopher's Stone</gloss></term>, and again let it be turned into water. And among all these things it has great efficacy...

### RACHAIDIBI ETC.

...contains <term>Anatron<gloss>a naturally occurring soda or sodium carbonate, used as a flux</gloss></term>, otherwise known as <term>Saltpeter<gloss>potassium nitrate, a powerful oxidizer</gloss></term>, as well as <term>Rock salt<gloss>pure mineral salt</gloss></term>, and <term>Alkali<gloss>a basic, ionic salt typically derived from plant ashes</gloss></term>. But do this with the red substance which suffers a long and great thirst <note>In alchemy, "thirst" refers to a dry state of matter that readily absorbs moisture or "spirit"</note>, until they become double their own volume; and <term>antinkar<gloss>borax, used in soldering and as a flux in refining metals</gloss></term> has great efficacy in these matters. And above all these, let one ounce of this water float on top, along with the water of "living" <term>Sal ammoniac<gloss>ammonium chloride, used to make spirits and volatile substances more soluble</gloss></term>, so that the particles of the fugitive <note>original: "fugitiui"; refers to volatile substances that "flee" or evaporate when heated</note> may be better dissolved.

Second, regarding the red color—or **Yellow** or green, or sky-blue. But the green color shines above all others; put the composition into a round vessel <note>original: "rotumba"; likely a round-bottomed flask or alembic</note> so that the clear water is dissolved from the bottom to the surface, just like strained honey. Then let it be congealed in the place of freezing into a green stone—sometimes into a red one, sometimes **Yellow**, sometimes sky-blue—and let it be dissolved and congealed again two or three times. This is the duplication, triplication, quadruplication, and sublimation, and it is sharper than all else.

The philosophers of the **King of the Persians** have said: Let us take the cold and moist, and the watery <note>traditional qualities associated with the element of Water and the planet Mercury</note>, and let it be cleansed of all superfluities and of **Lead**, and by pure and frequent decoction let it be dried of its moisture in the **Sun** by boiling and with moist cloths: and this is the most beautiful chapter. To those who desire to know a wonder above every wonder concerning the watery, cold, and burnt moisture, **Kanide** <note>likely referring to Al-Kindi (c. 801–873 AD), a prominent Arab philosopher and scientist</note> said that in one single fire, without the tempering of water, all the **distillations**, **sublimations**, **calcinations**, **rubifications** <note>the process of turning a substance red, often the final stage of the Great Work</note>, and the **deaths** of **Mercury** itself were accomplished. And then a friendship was made between it and the fire, and it flows like oil in a single stroke, because oil is something that enters, penetrates, gathers, mixes, and coagulates, performing...

...persevering <note>original: "perfeueratiuum"; in alchemy, this refers to the ability of the substance to remain stable and not evaporate under heat</note> (for it overcomes every subtle thing and penetrates every solid thing), joining, permanent, and purifying. And it is a <term>ferment<gloss>a substance, like yeast, that has the power to transform other matter into its own nature</gloss></term> above every ferment; it impregnates itself, and gives birth to itself. Whence all who seek this <term>science<gloss>the "art" or "science" of alchemy</gloss></term> must know how everything is, and how it is not, and how it can be done, and if they fail, how they can correct it. Above all, they must know the things necessary for the work, and the clear and manifest proofs.

And this is the first of all proofs: namely, when it has reached the end and the "second limit," let a little of it be placed upon **red-hot iron**. If it flows like wax, and produces neither smoke nor noise, but runs through everything and tints a plate of iron, copper, or silver, and fixes itself with them—fearing not the fire, nor being diminished by the fire, nor ever being separated from the metals—then it is perfect. This is the true proof above all proofs. 

But if it does not produce these signs, return it to the fire until it is so. All these proofs ought to be performed between the first and second "solution" <note>dissolving into liquid</note> and "congelation" <note>thickening or freezing into a solid</note>.

After this, one must know about those things which are joined with the "dead" <note>likely referring to "mortified" metals or mercury rendered non-volatile</note>. These conjunctions include the evident saffron-color of iron, the color of <term>vitriol<gloss>sulfate minerals, often green or blue</gloss></term>, and the <term>flower of copper<gloss>verdigris; the green crust that forms on weathered copper</gloss></term> (which is the "poison" of Mercury), and the lime of Salt, and the "water of Mercury" worked so that it does not flee from the fire. This is "living" and permanent water, a rectifying spirit, and the **ancient Sal Ammoniac**. Let all these things be individually dissolved into water. After all this, mix them and...

...congeal <note>original: "congela"; to thicken or solidify a liquid through heat or cold</note> over a slow fire, and thus it becomes a **living body**. And this body is afterwards dissolved into water, and so the mixing <note>original: "commixtio"</note> is accomplished.

# RACHAIDIBI AND OTHERS <note>The name "Rachaidibi" is likely a Latinized corruption of an Arabic name, possibly referring to the alchemist Khalid ibn Yazid or Rhazes, common authorities in medieval Latin alchemy</note>

*All the Persian Philosophers were gathered in one place in the mountainous regions, and it was a secret and fragrant place; and among them, many spoke in many ways regarding the tinctures which tint metals and change them into high and precious **Sun** <note>original: "Solem"; in alchemical texts, "Sun" is the standard term for Gold</note>. And that method of tincture which is more precious above all other tinctures is the one revealed among them through divine grace: namely, that two small parts encompass the whole work of <term>Alchemy<gloss>The ancient art of purifying, maturing, and perfecting certain objects, most notably the transmutation of base metals into gold</gloss></term>. The first part is sufficient in its weight and softness. The second multiplies the first into thousands of thousands of other weights into precious Gold by the grace of God. This happens in such a way that, when this Alchemy is cast with an artificial iron tube onto hard and liquid water in the fire—and there is a certain white water, and another somewhat white and soft, which is quickly spread by the fire and flows—the "daughter" <note>the resulting substance or precipitate</note> is red and hard. This is the **oil of tinctures** of which we speak; it penetrates all those "waters," and by entering into them, it creates a "friendship" <note>original: "amicitiam"; an alchemical term for the chemical affinity or harmonious bonding between two substances</note> and a lemon-yellow <note>original: "citrinatam"</note> unity with them. It is never separated by the fire nor diminished for all eternity. Furthermore, remember what was said above: namely, that the first limit "mortifies" <note>original: "mortificat"; to strip a substance of its outward form or "kill" its base properties</note> the coldness and humidity throughout the whole, and that it turns the boundaries red. The second limit completes the death of the first "dead thing" <note>the initial base matter</note> and increases the redness; it creates a scaly and pine-coned mass—shaped like the fruit of a pine tree—clear and shining like leaves of Gold, and it scatters...*

<vocab>Rachaidibi, Alchemy, Persian Philosophers, tincture, precious Sun, white water, oil of tinctures, lemon-yellow, scaly</vocab>

...itself, and it tints as it should to a yellow color <note>original: "citrinitatem"; the yellowing stage of the alchemical Great Work, often called the xanthosis or citrinitas</note>. This is the purification of Mercury, which is a <term>fixed oil<gloss>an oil rendered resistant to evaporation by heat</gloss></term> and a refining agent that restores all corrupt <term>metals<gloss>base metals such as lead or tin</gloss></term>, bringing them to health and to a better nature. Let us indeed remember the instruments in which all the decoctions <note>the process of boiling down or concentrating a substance</note> of the works of Alchemy must be performed, and of this work which is the first and is natural, which demonstrates all the operations of Alchemy, which are seven. And this is the work which demonstrates all <term>distillations<gloss>separating components of a liquid through evaporation and condensation</gloss></term> and <term>sublimations<gloss>heating a solid to a gas and back to a solid to purify it</gloss></term>, and all <term>calcinations<gloss>heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</gloss></term> and <term>dissolutions<gloss>turning a solid into a liquid</gloss></term>, and <term>congealments<gloss>thickening or solidifying a substance</gloss></term> and <term>mortifications<gloss>the "killing" or chemical breakdown of a substance's outer form to prepare it for a new one</gloss></term> according to the method of the art of fire.

In the art of Alchemy, there is one art that works with five fires. Of these, the first is white; the second is <term>yellow<gloss>original: "ialdus," a medieval term for yellow</gloss></term>; the third is green; the fourth is ruby-red. The fifth fire truly completes the entire work in the <term>athanor<gloss>a specialized alchemical furnace designed to maintain a constant, steady heat over long periods</gloss></term> for seven days and nights. And so it appears to have been inspired from the beginning of the seven days until it is congealed at the bottom; and it appears almost black, yet reddish because of the fullness of that red color. And this is the firmest operation of Alchemy, resisting...

<meta>This page transitions from the technical extraction of metallic spirits to a poetic reflection on the Philosopher's Stone. It features a celebratory alchemical riddle by Faustus Sabaeus (c. 1478–1559), a noted humanist and Vatican librarian, which personifies the Stone and its paradoxical nature.</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>

### FAUSTUS SABAEUS TO THE READER. <note>Faustus Sabaeus was a 16th-century Italian humanist and librarian at the Vatican; he often wrote introductory poems for scholarly works.</note>

Woman, man, neither, a father to itself, and a creator-mother,
Conceiving itself, and also giving birth to itself.
It lies hidden in these papers, although it shines brighter than a star,
And although by its softness it overcomes the <term>Hundred-Handed One<gloss>original: "Centimanum"; referring to the Hecatoncheires of Greek myth, giants with a hundred hands representing invincible strength</gloss></term>.
Suited to all names, and to all colors,
Dry, and flowing water, King, <term>slave<gloss>original: "geta"; a name for a Thracian or common slave in Roman comedy, signifying the humble origins of the alchemical matter</gloss></term>, flame, and stone.
Who, dying, brings forth life and health for others,
While he is pious to us, he is impious to himself.
He suffers the sword and fire like a martyr; suspended from the high
Air, while still alive he is enclosed in a tomb. <note>The "tomb" refers to the sealed alchemical vessel where the substance is heated and "buried" to be transformed.</note>
From martyrdom he finally rises, shining with a crown,
Much stronger and better than he had been before.
Then he is made the victor over Kings, and the tamer of Gods,
And he holds both peace and war in his own hand.
It is no wonder, therefore, if he is sought throughout the whole world—
That honor, that health, that wealth, and that rest.
And it is less a wonder if he hides himself, and fears everyone,
He who just now had been a thief, crafty, and fleeting. <note>This refers to Mercury, traditionally depicted as a thief and a "fugitive" because of its volatile nature and tendency to evaporate.</note>
*Certain to pay the penalty for his flight and theft with death*
*If he is caught; for this reason, he lies hidden in fear.*

->THE END.<-

<summary>A poetic alchemical riddle by Faustus Sabaeus describing the paradoxical nature of the Philosopher's Stone as a self-creating, hermaphroditic substance that must suffer "martyrdom" in the vessel to achieve its perfect state.</summary>
<keywords>Faustus Sabaeus, Philosopher's Stone, alchemical riddle, Rebis, Mercury, transmutation, martyrdom, Renaissance poetry</keywords>

<meta>This page (376) appears to be a concluding leaf, possibly from a different section than the previous page (126). The text identified here is mirrored bleed-through from a preceding page, containing the final stanzas of the "Lauda Sion Salvatorem," a sequence written by St. Thomas Aquinas around 1264 for the Feast of Corpus Christi. The transition moves from the previous page's alchemical metaphors of death and rebirth to a formal liturgical celebration of the Eucharist.</meta>

# THE EUCHARIST UNTO THEE <note>original: "EVCHARISTIA AD TECVM"; likely a devotional heading addressing the reader or the soul in relation to the Sacrament.</note>

<unclear>...</unclear>
<unclear>...</unclear> that this bread is [the body] of Christ <unclear>...</unclear>
<unclear>...</unclear> in communion, and at that very point <unclear>...</unclear>
<unclear>...</unclear>
<unclear>...</unclear>
<unclear>...</unclear>

> Both the good and the bad partake: <note>This stanza reflects the medieval theological view that while everyone physically eats the host, the spiritual effect—life or damnation—depends on the moral state of the recipient.</note>
> Yet with an unequal lot,
> Leading to life or to destruction.
> It is death to the bad, life to the good:
> See how the outcome of a similar
> Partaking is so different.
>
> When the Sacrament is finally broken,
> Do not waver, but remember,
> That there is as much under a fragment,
> As is hidden in the whole. <note>This refers to the doctrine of "concomitance," teaching that the entirety of Christ is present in every small piece of the consecrated bread, not divided by the breaking of the host.</note>
> No tearing of the reality occurs:
> There is only a breaking of the <term>sign<gloss>original: "signi"; referring to the outward appearance or "accidents" of the bread (size, shape, color) which change, while the substance remains Christ.</gloss></term>:
> By which neither the state, nor the stature
> Of the one signified is diminished.
>
> Behold the bread of Angels,
> Made the food of <term>wayfarers<gloss>original: "viatorum"; a traditional term for human beings on their earthly pilgrimage toward eternity.</gloss></term>:
> Truly the bread of the children,
> Not to be cast to the dogs.
> It is prefigured in ancient signs:
> When Isaac is sacrificed, <note>In medieval "typology," Old Testament events like the near-sacrifice of Isaac were seen as "types" or shadows that predicted the sacrifice of Christ.</note>
> When the Paschal Lamb is designated,
> And manna is given to the fathers.
>
> Good shepherd, true bread,
> Jesus, have mercy on us:
> Feed us, protect us,
> Make us see good things
> In the land of the living.
>
> You who know and can do all things,
> Who feed us mortals here:
> Make us your fellow-guests there,
> Co-heirs and companions
> Of the holy citizens.
>
> Amen.

->THE END.<-

<summary>This page preserves the concluding stanzas of Thomas Aquinas's famous hymn "Lauda Sion," which explores the theology of the Eucharist and the Real Presence of Christ in the bread. It serves as a devotional conclusion to the preceding text, emphasizing the spiritual nourishment of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Eucharist, Lauda Sion, Thomas Aquinas, Sacrament, Communion, Bread of Angels, Isaac, Manna, Transubstantiation</keywords>

--- Page 272 ---
<meta>This page serves as the formal colophon for the volume, concluding the text. While the previous page provided a partial glimpse of the printer's information and the conclusion of the "Lauda Sion" hymn, this page provides the definitive publication details for the 1542 edition. It confirms that the work was printed by Peter Schoeffer the Younger, continuing the legacy of his famous father in the city of Venice.</meta>

->At Venice, by Peter Schoeffer:<-
<note>original: "Venetijs apud Petrum Schœffer"; Peter Schoeffer the Younger (c. 1475–1547) was the son of the famous associate of Johannes Gutenberg. He moved his printing operations to Venice, the capital of the book trade, where he produced high-quality editions.</note>

->German, of Mainz.<-
<note>original: "Germanum, Maguntinum"; Mainz was the birthplace of the printing press in Europe. Schoeffer proudly identifies himself as a German from Mainz to signal his connection to the high technical standards and prestigious history of early printing.</note>

->In the year 1542.<-

<summary>This page is the colophon or printer's imprint, identifying Peter Schoeffer of Mainz as the printer of this 1542 Venetian edition. It highlights the migration of German printing expertise to the major publishing hub of Venice during the 16th century.</summary>
<keywords>Venice, Peter Schoeffer, Mainz, printing history, 1542, colophon, German printers</keywords>

--- Page 273 ---
<meta>This page is a blank reverse side (verso) or flyleaf. It contains no original ink or handwriting on this specific surface. Instead, it displays a "ghost image" or mirrored bleed-through from an adjacent page—likely a title page. Notably, the date visible in this ghosting (1583) is several decades later than the colophon of Peter Schoeffer (1542) seen on the previous page, indicating that this volume may have been rebound or stored alongside a later Venetian edition.</meta>

-> [Mirrored Printer's Emblem] <-
<note>This is a mirrored impression of a printer’s mark, a decorative symbol used as a trademark by publishing houses. The ghosting here shows an ornate border surrounding a central shield, typical of the late Renaissance Venetian style.</note>

-> From the shop of Master Lorenzo Basilio <-
<note>original: "Apud Dominum Laurentium Basilium"; Lorenzo Basilio (Latinized as Laurentius Basilius) was a member of a well-known family of printers and booksellers active in Venice during the late 16th and early 17th centuries.</note>

-> Patriarch of Venice. In the year <-
<note>original: "Patriarcham Venetiarum"; This phrase identifies Basilio's location or his status as a printer to the Patriarchate of Venice, the city's highest religious authority. The mirrored text indicates the book's association with the administrative and religious heart of the Venetian Republic.</note>

-> 1583 <-

<summary>This page is a blank leaf featuring a mirrored "ghost image" transferred from a 1583 title page. It identifies the printer Lorenzo Basilio and marks a later period in the history of this volume's binding or storage in Venice.</summary>

-> [Printer’s Emblem] <-
<note>The woodcut depicts the severed head of Saint John the Baptist on a platter, a reference to the printer's name, Giovanni Battista (John the Baptist). He is the patron saint of printers in many regions, and the surrounding text "Go instead to those who sell, and buy for yourselves" from the Parable of the Ten Virgins (Matthew 25:9) serves as a clever invitation to potential book buyers.</note>

->**At the house of Master Giovanni Battista**<-
->**Pederzano of Brescia. In the year**<-
<note>original: "Ioannem Baptistam pederzanum Brixiensem"; Giovanni Battista Pederzano was a prominent printer and bookseller. Though he identifies here as <term>Brixiensem</term> (from the city of Brescia), he was primarily active in the publishing hub of Venice.</note>

->**1542.**<-

<note>This page is an original flyleaf, a protective sheet of paper or parchment found at the beginning or end of a book. The visible "foxing" (small brown spots) and water stains are common in 16th-century volumes, often caused by humidity, mold, or the chemical reaction of iron contaminants in the paper over hundreds of years.</note>

This page has been severely degraded by extensive water staining and moisture damage over the centuries. There is no original text written directly on this side of the paper; instead, the surface is covered with heavy "bleed-through"—a phenomenon where ink from the opposite side of the page seeps through the fibers, appearing as a faint, mirrored image.

<note>Visible in the center of the page is the ghosted, backwards impression of a decorative emblem and several lines of text from the reverse side. Among the fragments, the phrase "To the Very Reverend" <note>original: "AD MODUM REVERENDI"</note> can be identified. This is a traditional Latin honorific used when addressing high-ranking members of the clergy, suggesting the work was dedicated to or authored by a church official.</note>
